# Lectures on the Apocalypse: A Modern Paraphrase

*Lectures on the Apocalypse, or Book of the Revelation of St. John the Divine, by Frederick Denison Maurice, restated paragraph by paragraph in modern English. This paraphrase follows the order and content of the original without adding interpretation. Drafted with claude-opus-4-8; reviewed by a human editor.*

## PREFACE.

These Lectures are not argumentative or scholarly. They do not require the reader to know anything about the various theories about the Apocalypse that have been held in the past or the present, in England or elsewhere. If you have already adopted one of those theories, I hope my words may help you test it against the actual text and the overall aim of the book it came from. If you have been wavering among several of these theories, I hope my Lectures may help you do them all justice, take useful hints from all of them, and find the words of the Prophet more satisfying and more understandable than any of them.

Nor do I ask the reader to bring an extensive knowledge of ancient or modern history — the kind you would need to evaluate most modern commentaries on Prophecy. The more familiar you are with the facts of history, and the more honestly you have sought that knowledge from writers least inclined to argue in favor of the Christian Church — even from writers wholly skeptical about its value — the more, I believe, the Revelation of St. John will help explain those facts and bring your own thoughts about God's government of the world into harmony. But my plan keeps me from trying to find precise matches between particular sentences of the book and particular events that have happened or are yet to happen in one period or another. The main historical references in these Lectures concern the condition of the Roman world in the years just before the fall of Jerusalem. I would like the reader to check these against the Histories of Tacitus, just as he will naturally turn to Josephus for the record of the crimes and disasters of the Jewish people.

The method I have adopted is, I believe, very simple. But I do not therefore claim I discovered it myself. The first hint of it came from a revered friend, a clergyman of the school of Cecil and Venn, who had devoted much of his life to the study of Prophecy, and who, more than twenty years ago, was allowed to leave the school in which he had been learning for the home where his spirit had long dwelt. He is not responsible for any of the particular conclusions I have reached. But I can never be grateful enough that, through his teaching, I arrived at the conviction that the words "The kingdom of heaven is at hand" were used by the Evangelists in their strictest sense; that the Apostles were not mistaken in believing the end of an age was approaching; that they had no exaggerated expectations about the age that would follow it; and that if we accepted their statements plainly, we would understand far better what condition we are living in, what our responsibilities are, what our sins are, and what we have a right to hope for.

I have called these discourses Lectures, because they are not lessons drawn out of separate texts. But they were delivered from the pulpit, like ordinary sermons. They were aimed at what I thought were the needs of a congregation I had been connected with for fourteen years, and to which, throughout all those years, I had often spoken on the subject of Prophecy. I have not had the heart to remove from them references to current events and the days on which they were delivered. I have even taken the liberty of giving them a more pastoral and personal character by adding a sermon on the last verse of the Apocalypse — one written less as an explanation of that verse than as an expression of my wishes for a community I was about to leave. I would not have included it if I had not thought it illustrated the subject of these Lectures, as well as gave me a chance to declare my continued regard and affection for those to whom I preached them.

London, December, 1860.

## LECTURE I. INTRODUCTORY.

*The passage:* This is the Revelation of Jesus Christ, which God gave to him so that he could show his servants what must soon happen; he sent and made it known through his angel to his servant John, who testified to the Word of God and to the witness of Jesus Christ and to everything he saw. Blessed is the one who reads and those who hear the words of this prophecy and keep what is written in it, because the time is near. John writes to the seven churches in Asia: may grace and peace come to you from him who is, and was, and is to come, and from the seven Spirits before his throne, and from Jesus Christ, the faithful witness, the firstborn from the dead, and the ruler of earthly kings. To him who loved us and washed us from our sins in his own blood, and made us kings and priests to God his Father, be glory and power forever. Look, he is coming with clouds; every eye will see him, including those who pierced him, and all the peoples of the earth will mourn because of him. So be it. "I am Alpha and Omega, the beginning and the end," says the Lord, who is, and was, and is to come, the Almighty.

Many people have tried to interpret this book, and one after another has failed; that is the general opinion among educated people. But I suspect that opinion is not broad enough. It ought to be extended from this one book of the Bible to all the books of the Bible, and from the books of the Bible to every book at all that contains truths worth seeking. Those who try to interpret fail; the writer turns out to have expressed thoughts they did not grasp or reach the depth of; then others come with their own rules and measures, overturn their predecessors, and are overturned in their turn. The same thing happens with the simplest Gospel as with the Apocalypse, and with a poem, whether Latin or English, as with the Gospel. Commentators have become a byword; nearly every reader imagines he has caught something in the author they discussed that they missed or twisted.

But perhaps every reader also discovers, sooner or later, that this or that commentator has helped him see something he did not see before. He discovers that he too has the ambition to fence his author in with rules and theories of his own making. He begins to suspect that each of us might help the next person if we were more childlike, more reverent toward the subject we study, and more willing to learn its laws rather than impose our own. The kind of criticism that is not a teacher looking down on a pupil, but a pupil looking up to a teacher, may after all have the most insight in it and bring the greatest reward. Everyone is qualified to practice that kind of criticism. Some will carry away more and some less, but each will be shown something that may make his mind clearer, his heart purer, and his actions more consistent.

1\. The verses I have read to you speak of the Apocalypse of Jesus Christ the Son of God, which God gave to him. So the very opening of the book takes us at once beyond the book. What is revealed is not a doctrine—not anything that can be put into set terms—but a living Person. The letters do not make or give the Revelation; God gives it. We are not belittling the letter when we say this; we hold to the letter religiously. We contradict the book when we put it in the place of the One from whom it comes. In doing so we contradict the plain language, not only of the Apocalypse, but of the whole Bible.

2\. How strictly I mean to follow the letter of this chapter, and of all the later chapters, you will see when I come to the next clause: "to show to his servants things which must shortly come to pass." I would only be repeating myself if I stopped to weigh that clause here. The phrase "the time is at hand," and several other phrases in this passage, will soon bring it to our attention.

3\. "He sent and *signified* it." The word "signified" is not a lazy word for which any number of equivalents would have done. As everyone admits, the Apocalypse is a book of *signs*. That is assumed to be one reason for its obscurity. We think that if we could only translate all the emblems into ordinary speech, we would understand it perfectly. But are we sure that signs and emblems, if they are chosen by God, may not be the best and simplest aids to grasping truths that would stay obscure if they were presented in what we call ordinary speech? Are signs not a common, human way of speaking, one that puzzles the learned man more than the peasant, precisely because the learned man is not in touch with the facts of life the way the peasant is? If the Revelation is really to be the revelation of a Person, may the signs not point to his actual presence, while abstract words could only express certain notions about him?

4\. So in this book, as in all the earlier books of the Bible, God himself is presented as speaking to people through words or signs. Whatever methods or persons are used as the means of the revelation, he is the one who reveals. He removes the veil that keeps us from knowing what is close to us, or the veil over our hearts that keeps them from perceiving it. That must be kept in mind before we turn at all to secondary agents. Here one of them is said to be an angel: "he sent and signified it by his *angel* to his servant John." I take this name, as most people would, to mean a messenger from another world. But I must remind you that it is also used for people living in this world, in every respect like their fellow human beings, committing sins and certain to die. And I must remind you that when it is used that way, the writer does not say, "Now I am using the word in a different sense than before." He plainly wants us to feel that the sense is the same. He believes that whether or not mortal weaknesses are present, this does not disturb the basic fact that the ones he speaks of are messengers of God, receiving light from him and passing that light on to those he means it for. If there is anything spiritual in a human being, it must be able to receive such communications; if there is nothing spiritual in a human being, the Bible would never have been written—there would have been no need for such a book, and no possibility of one. But I think we shall learn from this book, more than from any earlier part of the Bible, how people's dreams about an unseen world and its inhabitants are given substance, and how their thoughts about contact between that world and this may become, not fanciful and superstitious, but calm and orderly.

5\. "He sent and signified it by his angel to *his servant John*." Students have found it very hard to identify the writer of the Apocalypse with the writer of the Gospel and the Epistles. The earliest church historian hints at another John, to whom many have been willing to assign this book. The latest and most advanced school of German Rationalists has rejected that view. Those scholars credit the Apostle himself with the work, in which they can detect nothing but fierce Jewish prejudice and hostility to St. Paul. Yet they too, like those who look for another author, see the sharpest contrast between the Apocalypse and the Gospel. They date the Gospel to the second century. And suppose the heading of this book did *not* match its contents—suppose it were *not* a revelation of Jesus Christ the Son of God but only a collection of predictions about the future—then I do think it would take an overwhelming amount of external evidence to convince us that it could come from the man who wrote of the Light that gives light to every man coming into the world, who recorded the conversations with Nicodemus, with the woman of Samaria, and at the Feasts of Tabernacles and of Dedication. There is an enormous difference between the two books as long as we hold to the popular idea about the Gospel—an idea I do not think we can ever really forget, however much we force ourselves into believing the two books had a common origin. But if we assume the writer is able to define his own purpose, and if that purpose is visible through every vision and prophecy that follows, then I believe no one was more likely to write about the fall of Babylon or the New Jerusalem than the man who testified to "the Word of God and the testimony of Jesus Christ, and all the things that he saw." There is a peculiarity in that description that a clever debater could never have reached. He might have referred to the Son of Thunder, or to the beloved Disciple. He might have referred to the Theologian who wrote about the Divine Word. But this one sentence combines the *opposite* qualities of the writings the Church has received as St. John's. The writer of the fourth Gospel dwells more than the other evangelists on what is lofty and mysterious. He also sets out more than they do what is visible and tangible. He never separates the one from the other. In the plainest language he presents his Master's plainest acts as manifestations of his eternal character and nature. Those two aims which commentators have found so hard to reconcile, and which have led them into the most contradictory theories about the fourth Gospel, are here blended with a childlike skill. How they are blended in the Apocalypse itself we shall see as we go on.

6\. The next sentence: "*Blessed* is he that reads, and they that hear the words of this prophecy, and keep the things written in it: for the time is at hand." Writers who wanted to fix our attention on this book, and to overcome the listlessness or despair with which so many regard it, have quoted this blessing again and again. "Do we not have here a divine promise," they ask, "that we shall be the better for undertaking this study? Should we not rely on that promise and hope for results, even if earlier students have been disappointed ever so often?" The argument is very strong in itself, but in many minds its force has been weakened by the clause that ends the verse. If a teacher of the seventeenth, eighteenth, or nineteenth century could persuade his hearers that the "time" said to be at hand meant the year, the decade, or the half-century following the one in which he was speaking, he would have a good chance of engaging their thoughts, even if they were deeply absorbed in other pursuits. So this has been a common effort of interpreters: *that* date must belong to events that happened just before our own day; *this* one must point to events yet to come out of a not-distant future. But if the seventeenth-century commentator was *not* wrong in his forecast, then it occurs to the hearer or reader that the nineteenth-century commentator must be wrong in his. And if the seventeenth-century commentator *was* wrong, why should his successor be any luckier in his guess—or, at any rate, why should we give up certainties in order to find out whether he is? And there is always another, more serious suspicion behind these. Did the original writer not use words in their plain, natural sense? If he told the readers and hearers of his own day that *the time* was at hand, did he not mean them to understand that it really *was* at hand? Can he possibly have meant that what he states so definitely should be taken indefinitely? Can he have backed a divine promise with an assurance that events proved false—or with what, in an uninspired writer, we would call a pious fraud?

7\. I confess, my brothers, that these questions seem to me deeply serious and even awful. I can find only one answer to them. I take the words in their most direct and straightforward sense. I believe that the time John wrote about was at hand when he wrote. I no more suppose him to have been mistaken about its nearness than I suppose him to have been a deliberate deceiver. Yet I do not admit that the promise is any less good for the seventeenth, eighteenth, or nineteenth centuries, because I hold that events happened in the first century that were of surpassing importance, that marked the end of an age, and that deserved to be described as the Apocalypse of Jesus Christ the Son of God. Before I could hold that this passage is drained of any of its worth or reality for us because the revelation was made centuries ago, I would have to change the meaning of one of the words in it—or at least abandon the scriptural meaning of that word for a vulgar heathen one. St. John speaks of the words of this *prophecy*. If prophecy were merely an announcement of future events—if it had no more force than the Babylonian soothsayers and fortune-tellers gave it—then I would grant at once that once an event has happened, any prophecy about it is used up, except perhaps as evidence of the predictor's accuracy. But the Jewish prophets, far from wanting to be lumped in with those soothsayers and fortune-tellers, regarded them with horror, and protested fiercely against those of their own people who imitated the soothsayers' practices while daring to use the sacred phrases, "The burden of the Lord" or "The Lord has said." Prophecy, as they used and understood it, is the utterance of the mind of him who is, and was, and is to come. Events, days of the Lord, crises in a nation's history were manifestations of his everlasting mind and purpose. The seer was to explain the past and the present, and only in connection with those did he speak of the future. He told what curses people were bringing on themselves by breaking the laws that individuals and nations were created to obey. He told how the purposes of God's will were unfolding in a steady progression despite all the resistance of human self-will. He told how they would go on moving forward until everything God intends for man, for the universe, and for his own glory has been accomplished. That is prophecy, if we take our idea of it from the books we accept as authoritative, and do not shrink and twist them to fit some notion we have picked up elsewhere. But if that is what prophecy is, why should a past event be any less powerful and meaningful for us than one still to come? If we can find an interpreter to tell us what it signifies, may that meaning not be of the deepest interest to one age after another? May each age not catch some glimpse of it that another missed? May it not link that glimpse with events it has itself experienced—events happening in God's world, and so subject to the same law, the results of similar deeds, and heavy with results not unlike those the prophet spoke of? Surely there can be a blessing on the hearers and readers of his oracles, even though we believe those oracles were not ambiguous and that he never played with double meanings. Surely times may be at hand for people in every generation that make it deeply necessary for them to try to enter into the meaning of the times that were at hand in his generation. And surely, as an age—or as we sometimes call it, a dispensation of God—moves toward its completion, the need may grow greater, and greater too the hope that we shall be allowed to profit from past illuminations and past mistakes and confusions.

8\. In trying to set out the idea of prophecy that I find in the earlier prophets of his nation, I have borrowed some words from the next passage in this chapter. At first you might treat that passage as merely a burst of devotion. But the more you reflect on it, the more, I believe, it will explain the whole purpose of the book. "John to the seven churches in Asia: grace and peace to you from him who is, and was, and is to come, and from the seven Spirits before his throne, and from Jesus Christ, the faithful witness, the firstborn from the dead, and the ruler of the kings of the earth. To him who loved us and washed us from our sins in his own blood, and made us kings and priests to God his Father—to him be glory and power forever and ever. Amen. Look, he is coming with clouds, and every eye will see him, including those who pierced him, and all the peoples of the earth will mourn because of him. So be it. I am Alpha and Omega, the beginning and the end, says the Lord, who is, and was, and is to come, the Almighty."

To these seven churches St. John declares the revelation God has made to him. It is the revelation of him who is, and was, and is to come; the revelation of him in his own perfect and absolute nature, as the source from which all the grace and peace ever known by any of his creatures, and all that can ever be known, has flowed. It is the revelation of that one Spirit—seven being the perfect number, the expression of unity within variety—who dwells in their churches, who gives them their distinct graces and gifts, who binds them into one: the Spirit of Grace and Peace. It is the revelation of Jesus Christ, who has these three titles and descriptions: (1) that he is the faithful and true Witness of the Eternal God—the one in whom the Spirit dwells without limit, the one who displays his Father as perfect Goodness, Truth, and Love; (2) that he is the firstborn from the dead, the Conqueror of man's enemy, the Conqueror on behalf of the whole family of God; (3) that he is the Prince of all the kings of the earth, the actual Lord over men, to whom all must at last do homage. What he is, and what he has done for our race, cannot be set out in cold, dry statements. It can only be expressed in thanksgiving to him who has loved us with a love that is, and was, and is to come; to him whose love has shown itself in action and suffering; to him whose actions and sufferings have been for the deliverance of people from the sins that set them at war with him and with each other, and were the cause of their misery; to him who was not content merely to wash us from our sins in his own blood, but has made us kings under him—rulers over ourselves, rulers over the earth to which we are naturally slaves—and has made us priests under him, to offer holy sacrifices to God his Father. To him be glory and praise forever and ever.

9\. I have called *this* the Revelation or Apocalypse. And I believe that as you go on studying the book, you will find it occupied throughout with that divine Name into which the Christian churches are baptized, in which all of us live and move and have our being. Such an Apocalypse must be meant for every age that was to follow, just as every age before it was preparing for it. The full revelation of this Name must be the Revelation of Revelations, the one that throws light on every other. But what about the words that follow? "Look, he is coming with clouds, and every eye will see him, including those who pierced him, and all the peoples of the earth will mourn because of him." Keeping to the order and the letter of the words, I cannot doubt they were meant to tell the seven churches how they should regard the dark events that were to happen in their own time—the time of a tremendous crisis in the Roman Empire, the time of the overthrow of the Jewish nation. In the dark clouds of that time they were to recognize the coming of the Son of Man. The Judge was there, acknowledged by every human conscience, acknowledged even by the consciences of those who had pierced him as a blasphemer and a criminal. The churches would be able to read these signs. They would know what luminous form stood behind the clouds, and would hear, in the mourning of the peoples of the earth, their crying out after the very King and Deliverer they had rejected and now trembled before.

10\. I do not want to anticipate what may be said better on future occasions about these seven churches, or this awful Name, or this coming with clouds. I take these verses only as a series of pointers that the book is going to unfold. In that same light I receive the closing verse, which is repeated in the vision that follows. As some have thought, it may have been shifted here from that later place by accident. But we need to be reminded at the very start, and in many ways, of who the subject of the book is, so that we are never tempted to see in it only a scattering of rays of light with no center from which they came and to which they return. We need to be reminded that it is not enough to trace all things and all people back to their origin, unless we can also find the end for which they exist. We need to know that only a living person can be the Alpha and Omega, the starting-point of Creation and its final Rest.

I shall be glad if I can give you a few hints about this great book that will help your own reading of it. I am sure it will reward the study of the layman just as much as the priest; that it need not tempt him into any fantasies, but may deliver him from a great many; that God may use it to guide us through dark roads and tangled thickets where we are all likely to lose our way; and that it will become clear when we ask its help in practice rather than in speculation. The blessing on the one who hears and on those who read is one of those that will not pass away. May it come down richly upon us from him who is the Author of all blessings!

## LECTURE II. THE SON OF MAN.

*The passage:* John, calling himself the brother and companion of his readers in suffering and in the kingdom and endurance of Jesus Christ, was exiled on the island of Patmos because of the word of God and his testimony to Jesus. On the Lord's day he was in the Spirit and heard behind him a loud voice like a trumpet, calling itself the first and the last, telling him to write what he sees in a book and send it to the seven Churches of Asia — Ephesus, Smyrna, Pergamos, Thyatira, Sardis, Philadelphia, and Laodicea. Turning to see the voice, he saw seven golden candlesticks, and among them one like a Son of Man, in a long robe with a golden band across his chest. His head and hair were white as wool and snow, his eyes like flame, his feet like glowing brass, his voice like the roar of many waters. He held seven stars in his right hand, a sharp two-edged sword came from his mouth, and his face shone like the sun. John fell at his feet as if dead, but the figure laid his right hand on him and told him not to fear, for he is the first and the last, the one who died and now lives forever, holding the keys of death and the grave. He was told to write what he had seen, what is, and what will be; and it was explained that the seven stars are the angels of the seven Churches, and the seven candlesticks are the seven Churches themselves.

Whether or not we accept the common view that St. John had already outlived his fellow Apostles, the way he begins this passage is very striking. He usually lived at Ephesus, among the Christians of Asia Minor, who afterwards turned to him with the deepest reverence as the last living keeper of Christ's own words. Yet he calls himself "your brother, and companion in tribulation, and in the kingdom and patience of Jesus Christ." He makes no claim to apostolic authority, to his great traditions, or to any difference between himself and those he addresses. He is simply one of them, their fellow-worker and fellow-sufferer.

I do not point this out in order to draw any conclusion about the special humility of this divine teacher. It seems to me that St. Paul was just as humble when he was strongly asserting his claim to be an Apostle and denouncing the Judaisers who tried to rank him below the original twelve. Asserting oneself can be as much a duty as belittling oneself; both are wrong only insofar as they are the least bit put on; and one may spring from as deep a sense of personal weakness as the other. They may have the same source and, in different circumstances, lead to the same end. If St. Paul had held back from proclaiming his apostolic title and character, he would have given ground for the conclusion that his Lord, once he was no longer visible, had stopped governing his Church and stopped calling men to his work. The man who said he did not trust in clever words when he declared God's testimony would in fact have been trusting in that cleverness rather than in his calling and in the Spirit that helped his weakness. If St. John had told the Churches of Asia Minor about the voice that once bade him leave his father's nets, he might have led them to think that voice was now silent, or unable to reach them. If he had spoken of his apostolic commission or his special place among the Apostles, he might have made them think that when he left the world they would be stripped of the divine Presence, and only an oral or written memory of it would remain. But the lesson he was appointed to teach his own generation, and all later ones, was exactly the opposite; and so it was fitting that he should describe himself not by titles that set him apart from other men, but as their brother and companion.

1\. He tells us he was an exile on the island of Patmos. Whether the local authorities of Ephesus sent him there, or whether it was a formal deportation ordered by the Emperor, his words do not tell us. Nor does this passage help settle the debated question whether his banishment happened as early as the reign of Claudius, as late as the reign of Domitian, or somewhere in between. On the cause of his banishment he sheds more light. Most of St. Paul's persecutions were stirred up by Jews; the two notable exceptions were at Philippi, where he angered those who traded in magic, and at Ephesus, where a true instinct led Demetrius and his fellow craftsmen to see that he was harming their business, even though he had not attacked their goddess. But the charge the Jews used to stir up the mob at Thessalonica was in the end the effective one: that these men act against the decrees of Caesar, saying there is another King, one Jesus. That charge could not be permanently effective until it was joined to the one on which the rulers of the Sanhedrin had convicted our Lord of blasphemy: that he makes himself the Son of God. When St. John proclaimed the Word of God, who existed before all worlds, who had become flesh and lived among men, who is King of kings and Lord of lords, he struck a blow at both the worship and the political order of the Roman Empire. He set the God-man against the man-God. The best emperors, who tried to hide the rotten foundation of their power under respect for law, acts of mercy, and philosophy, had as much reason to hate this testimony as the worst emperors, who openly embodied the false principle in themselves.

2\. St. John does not tell us what he mostly looked at on that island, or what sounds beyond the roar of the Aegean reached him from the outer world. He says that on the Lord's day, the day of the Resurrection, he "was in the Spirit" — withdrawn from the things the senses register, seeking his eternal and real home. Then a voice came to his heart, a voice like a trumpet loud enough to wake the dead, saying, "I am Alpha and Omega, the first and the last." Like the name "I AM," spoken centuries before to the shepherd in the desert, this was a witness to him that there is a living, personal ground of all things, which existed before temples, cities, earth, sea, and sky, and which will last whatever becomes of them. And like the message to Moses, it was not for himself alone: "What thou seest, write in a book, and send it unto the seven churches of Asia." Those Churches were to learn what their own existence meant, what they had to do, and how they stood in relation to him who is the First and the Last.

3\. "And I turned to see the voice that spake with me." The voice comes from no created thing around me. It is not the echo of my own thoughts. It comes as a command to my spirit from the Ruler of my spirit.

4\. "And being turned, I saw seven golden candlesticks." Our first thought is that we are now entering a region of symbols. Are not these candlesticks like the one of pure gold that Moses was told to place in the Tabernacle, or like the somewhat different one that Solomon had made when he built a house for the Lord God of Israel? Certainly they are like these; and the Apostle loves them for the resemblance; they were meant to remind both Israelites and non-Israelites within the Church of a sacred past, of a worship that was soon to end. But, as we shall soon see, they were also meant to lift them out of symbols, to turn the signs into what they signify, and to show them among what practical realities they were actually living. No stronger testimony could be given, I think, than is given here — that symbols will always have a worth for human beings, but that the world's infancy was over, that now the truths were to throw light on the parables rather than the parables on the truths, and that men were now to study the visions of an earlier day by the revelations of this later one.

5\. For "in the midst of the seven candlesticks was one like unto the Son of Man." That the candlestick pointed to the giving of light; that the light had to be spread out yet remain one; that it stood in the Tabernacle to signify him who was worshipped there as the Source of Light, and the worshippers as the instruments of spreading it abroad — this lesson needed no cleverness to draw out, and must have reached many hearts that would never have grasped it if put into words. But the more deeply it went home, the more questions it will have raised. Only those on whom it made no impression could be content simply to look at the candlestick or to hear the Scribe's explanation of it. "How can this light reach me? How can I show it forth? Is it really a light for all Israel? Is all Israel to be a lamp to mankind? How can this be, when we are so often servants of darkness? And does God really want his light to go out everywhere? Isn't it meant to be kept within his chosen people?" Such thoughts the symbol will have awakened in those in whom it did its work. Nothing they saw satisfied them. It drove them to seek for light, and those who sought found more than they dreamed of, though never enough. They found more and more signs of a Light near them, of a Light that lightens every man. They rejoiced in what was given. But they waited to hear that what they felt must be true really was true — that God's Light was indeed man's Light, that there was One in whom all the scattered rays of light were gathered up, from whom they could be received, and by whose power they could be spread abroad.

To see the Son of Man walking in the midst of the seven golden candlesticks was at once to declare that these candlesticks had a real meaning, and that their meaning was no longer merely Jewish and limited. They were no longer part of the furniture of the Tabernacle or the Temple. The one candlestick, with its six branches and its ten, had been lit long enough in Jerusalem. That flame had burned itself out. He who had kindled it as a witness of himself and his own presence with men was now truly present. It was the Son of Man himself, and not some substitute for him, no mere formal image of him, who was giving light to the sons of men.

6\. Such a revelation must have been exactly what a Jew needed for comfort, if he mourned bitterly over the present state of his people and dreaded their still darker future; and it was exactly what he would have wished everyone to share. But it would have been very incomplete if only the golden candlestick in the Jewish Temple had been given its true meaning and raised to its highest power. In the worship of that Temple, things were not the most important part; the persons who ministered were far more sacred. What was even the holy of holies itself compared with the high priest who entered it once a year?

It is this high priest who is at once brought to mind by the first part of the description of the Son of Man. He was "clothed with a garment down to the foot, and girt about the paps with a golden girdle." There can be no doubt this is priestly clothing. Enough is said to show that the Son of Man claims and fulfills the office once given to the children of Aaron: that he blesses the people in God's name, and stands as their Representative before his Father. The Alpha and Omega takes the place of the man whose work was nearly over, who had ceased to be any witness for God or for man, and had become rather a barrier between them.

But the seer moves quickly from the priest's clothing, from what is merely official, to the person himself. "His head and his hairs were white like wool, as white as snow." What St. John had glimpsed for a few moments on Mount Tabor, when the face of the Man of Sorrows shone like the sun and his clothing became white as light, was now shown to him as a lasting glory. "His eyes were as a flame of fire" — looking into the heart and spirit, exposing whatever is false, and burning it away with their love. The feet bear the marks of endurance and suffering. They have walked over the earth and been scorched and made holy by it. They are "like unto fine brass, as if they burned in a furnace." The roar of the sea is in the ears of the lonely man on Patmos; its strange and varied notes are blended in the voice of the Son of Man, which is "as the sound of many waters."

8\. From the robes that mark the priest we have passed to the form of the Divine Man — a form expressing purity, struggle, victory, and calm reconciling power, but never suggesting mere qualities or attributes apart from a Person in whom they live. Then we return to where we began. This Son of Man is walking among the candlesticks; he is the center of light. It is a heavenly light, not belonging to earth, though reaching into the farthest corners of the earth. The light is no longer in the Tabernacle or the Temple. "He had in his right hand seven stars." The Persian who worships the night, the Greek who worships Apollo, may still see in the forms of nature the witnesses of the true King. "And out of his mouth went a sharp two-edged sword." The forms of nature, beautiful and true as they are, must bow to the word that speaks to man. Out of his mouth comes that which separates soul from spirit, joints from marrow, that which divides the good from the evil, the false from the true. How essential this feature is to the picture of the Son of Man we shall feel more and more. Its effect, joined to the former, is to turn the images of night into images of day. The stars were in his hand, but his "countenance was as the sun shineth in his strength."

9\. "And when I saw him I fell at his feet as dead." Years of dwelling on the Son of Man had not lessened the old disciple's awe, but deepened it. Mere power does not make him tremble. But before the form of Divine Goodness, before eyes that are like flame, he is as weak as a child. Even lesser revelations must be moments of terror. The first glimpse of a discovery in nature, though it sometimes brings the cry "I have found it!," as often crushes the one to whom it comes with a sense of his own smallness and a wonder at what may lie behind it. To be assured that he stood before Him who is the First and the Last — was that not enough to make this poor exile, though the most favored of the Apostles, fall at his feet as if dead?

10\. And what words raise him up? Are they, "Do not fear; I am he whom you knew at Cana or on Tabor; I am he who placed you nearest to myself"? No — but, "Fear not; I am the first and the last: I am he that liveth, and was dead; and, behold, I am alive for evermore, Amen; and have the keys of hell and of death." It could not comfort or revive St. John to be told what had been done for him, how he had been singled out from the rest of his people. What did restore him was the assurance that the face he could not bear to look upon was the face of One who had been under the power of that death and that grave appointed for every criminal as much as for every apostle, and who had risen as the Conqueror of them, and Conqueror for men. Yes — to hear these words ringing in his ears, "I have the keys of hell and of death," the keys of all he fears, of all that man fears, of all that sin has made him fear; to be convinced that these are the words of the Son of Man — this must be a comfort to every apostle and martyr such as no thought of his own life, or witness, or approaching death could ever give. When all such memories are drowned in the memory of evils done and evils present; when death and hell seem as if they might be deeper abysses for him than for any poor outcast who never thought of them at all — then this trust in One who has plunged into those depths because he is the Son of Man, and has found something deeper than they, and holds the keys of that kingdom as well as of heaven's — this message meets him as fully satisfying, precisely because it overturns every selfish confidence and forces him to rest on the Redeemer and King and Lover of the universe.

11\. And because this is so, he is raised up to do a work for his fellow men; and only by doing it will he be able to keep hold of his own trust. "Write the things which thou hast seen, and the things which are, and the things which shall be hereafter." What he has seen are not private illuminations for himself. They are facts that concern all men — the principles of human life and fellowship. The Son of Man is not walking among the candlesticks only at that one moment. He is always doing so. St. John must write of the things that were, in order to write of the things that would be hereafter. He can serve as an interpreter to lands he never saw, and to times of trouble that would come after he had gone to his rest, only by faithfully interpreting the situation of the people closest to him, of those who were his contemporaries and companions.

12\. And how is he to carry out this task? He is told at once to strip the veil from those outward images he had been looking at, the very images that seemed as if they were to take the place of the images of the older dispensation. But those images are not to replace the old ones; the things the images represent are to replace them. The mystery, the inner meaning, of the candlesticks and the seven stars is to be declared: "The seven stars are the angels of the seven churches: and the seven candlesticks which thou sawest are the seven churches." And so this verse follows naturally from the one before. The prophet is to write things he has seen, and that are, and that shall be hereafter. He has seen the candlesticks, and the One holding the stars in his right hand. These Churches existed then, and were to exist afterwards. They were then distinct from one another, each under its own minister. Each minister was an angel or messenger of God. Though distinct, they formed one whole. There was one Church among many Churches. The whole body was sent into the world to spread light through it. Each separate body was to spread light over a sphere of its own. Each minister was to be a star of light to the body he served. Each was to understand that his light came not from himself but from the Center of light. Each would fail in his office if, under one temptation or another, he forgot this condition of his very existence. And each, by failing in his office, would harm the society he was meant to enlighten, infecting it with his own evil. These things were. The future would be shaped by the present; in principle they would be the same. These Churches were formed not for a day or a year, but for an ever-renewing life. They were to last on even as one star after another vanished, because the Son of Man was walking among them. The Church would endure even though particular Churches ceased to exist, because the Son of Man was in the midst of it. If all of them perished, he would not perish. These were not revelations for the apostolic age only; this revelation was especially meant to prepare the way for the ending of that age, so that no one might suppose the Church depended for its stability or life on those who had been its first pillars, and so that no one might suppose any single one of them was its center. The mystery of the seven candlesticks and the seven stars, then, is a mystery that the struggles of that time were to unfold, and that the struggles of all later generations were to make clearer. By the sins of particular angels or ministers, by the sins of one Church or another, no less than by the light one or another was able to spread, every aspect of this mystery would be brought into fuller view. More and more it would be seen that a Church which does not spread light must increase darkness, and gather darkness into itself. More and more it would be known what temptation each Church has to become a servant of darkness, and how great that darkness is. More and more it would be seen what judgments rouse Churches from their sleep, and what the signs of their doom are.

13\. This is only part, and a small part, of the Revelation. But if we open our hearts to receive it, many of the doubts that trouble us in the later parts of the book will be cleared away. We shall be far less tormented by the question, "If these words concerned Jerusalem or Rome in the days of Galba or Vespasian, how can they concern London in the days of Queen Victoria?" The mystery of the seven golden candlesticks must apply to both if it applies to either. If Churches still exist; if the Bible is the book from which we are to learn why they exist, how they may serve that end, and by what sins they may frustrate it — then we need not doubt that we shall find just as many lessons about Medieval Churches and Reformed Churches, about how particular Churches relate to the Catholic Church, and about the domination of Emperors and Popes, as the commentators have worked out who have twisted the whole Apocalypse into a testimony about such things. We shall find these lessons, not by changing present tenses into future ones, but by noticing that the present tenses are tenses of continuance — that the principles worked out at one moment must govern every moment that follows. We shall find them when we are not looking for them; they will press upon us most strongly when we are least trying to make them fit historical events, or historical events fit them. Above all, we shall understand them best, and be most able to use them to link different events and ages in the story of mankind, when we let them bear most directly upon ourselves.

Primitive Churches, Medieval Churches, Continental Churches, seats of Papacy and of Empire — yes, it is good to know what we can of them. But are not *you* a Church, a body of people from whom light is meant to go out? Does it go out from you? Are there not ministers in the Church of England? Will not the Son of Man, who walks among the candlesticks, ask each of them: "Are you uttering your own dreary, secondhand commonplaces? Are you pouring out your own dreams and speculations? Are you strutting your little hour like an actor on a stage, or the oracle of some school? Or do you care to be a messenger of My purpose, a star in My right hand?"

## LECTURE III. THE SEVEN CHURCHES.

*The passage:* Christ, who holds the seven stars and walks among the seven golden lampstands, sends a message to each of seven churches. To Ephesus: I know your works, labor, and patience; you have exposed false apostles and hate the deeds of the Nicolaitanes, but you have left your first love — repent, or I will remove your lampstand; the one who overcomes will eat of the tree of life. To Smyrna (from him who was dead and is alive): I know your tribulation and poverty, though you are rich; be faithful unto death and I will give you a crown of life; the one who overcomes will not be hurt by the second death. To Pergamos (from him with the sharp two-edged sword): I know you dwell where Satan's seat is, yet you held my name even when Antipas was martyred; but you have some who hold the doctrine of Balaam and of the Nicolaitanes — repent; the one who overcomes will receive hidden manna and a white stone with a new name. To Thyatira (from the Son of God with eyes like fire): I know your love, service, faith, and patience, and that your last works exceed your first; but you tolerate the woman Jezebel who seduces my servants — she and hers will be judged; the one who overcomes and keeps my works will receive power over the nations and the morning star. To Sardis (from him with the seven Spirits and seven stars): you have a name that you live but are dead — be watchful; a few have not defiled their garments; the one who overcomes will be clothed in white and his name confessed before my Father. To Philadelphia (from him who is holy and true, holding the key of David): I have set before you an open door; because you kept my word with little strength, I will keep you from the hour of temptation coming on the world; the one who overcomes will be a pillar in God's temple, marked with God's name and the name of the new Jerusalem. To Laodicea (from the Amen, the faithful witness, the beginning of God's creation): you are lukewarm, and think yourself rich when you are wretched and poor and blind — buy from me gold, white raiment, and eye-salve; I stand at the door and knock; the one who overcomes will sit with me on my throne.

Last Sunday our subject was the revelation, or unveiling, of the Son of Man to His servant John. He was walking among seven golden lampstands. He wore the robes of the High Priest. His form was both divine and human. Light was in Him and shone out from Him. He had died, and He was alive — alive forever. He held the keys of death and hell.

What John saw and heard, he was to report; he was to write it in a book for the good of the seven Churches in Asia. Those seven Churches were declared to be the seven lampstands among which the Son of Man was walking. They were to spread abroad the light that lived in Him. The stars He held in His hand stood for their angels, or ministers. So the revelation of Him was also a revelation to them — a revelation of how they were related to each other, and of the purpose for which they existed.

The second and third chapters continue this revelation. They contain a series of messages to these Churches. Readers who find little meaning in the rest of the book, or who despair of finding any, have still accepted these messages as of great practical worth. In every period of Christendom's history they have been felt to speak forcefully to *that* period. Indeed, it has proved so impossible to confine them to the Apostolic age or to the circumstances of Asia Minor that theories have been built up to identify which era of the Church is the Sardian, which the Philadelphian, which the Laodicean. Given what I have said about the principles I mean to follow in studying this book, you will not suspect me of any fondness for such theories. I do not care to ask which is best or worst. I am determined to stay with the Apostle: when he speaks of his own time, I assume he means his own time; when he speaks of Sardis or Philadelphia, I assume he means Sardis or Philadelphia. But I take such theories as evidence that the revelation this book contains is a revelation of what abides — of what does not depend on circumstances, however circumstances may affect or alter its appearance. In this respect the messages to the Churches seem to me no different from the parts before or after them. If we are permitted to understand them, to feel their force on our own lives at all, it will be because the Spirit who spoke them to the ancient Churches is speaking them to us now; because He who was promised to be with the Church forever, and of whose living power the Church is the witness, has not stopped living or working. It will be because He opens our ears to hear what, in these messages or through any other means, He is saying to us and to mankind.

I. 1. Before I mention any special features of these messages, I want you to notice what they all have in common. The first and most obvious of these shared features is one I have already prepared you for. St. John — their companion in the faith and patience of Jesus Christ, St. John the Apostle, dearest to them, who was to outlive all his fellow Apostles — is *not* the one addressing them. To each and all, the Son of Man is speaking Himself. To each and all He declares that He knows them, knows what they are and what they are doing. To each and all He makes known the secret of their own lives: "Unto the angel of the church of Ephesus write; These things saith he that holdeth the seven stars in his right hand, who walketh in the midst of the seven golden candlesticks; I know thy works." And so likewise to Smyrna, from him who was dead and is alive; to Pergamos, from him with the sharp two-edged sword; to Thyatira, from the Son of God with eyes like fire and feet like fine brass; to Sardis, from him with the seven Spirits of God and the seven stars; to Philadelphia, from him who is holy and true and holds the key of David; to Laodicea, from the Amen, the faithful and true witness, the beginning of the creation of God — in each case with the same words, "I know thy works."

The language, you will notice, is uniform in these respects: it identifies the speaker as the Person who was unveiled to the Apostle on Patmos, and He alone is assumed to know what the works of each Church are. The difference between the messages lies in which aspect of this divine Person is shown to which Church — and these disclosures, as we shall see, correspond to each Church's particular tendencies and dangers.

2\. The second general feature is that these words are repeated after each message: "He that hath an ear, let him hear what the Spirit saith unto the churches." When we look more closely at what is said about them, I believe we will see why it was not enough simply to tell them that *the Son of Man* was among them and knew what they were doing. To admit that much is possible. Recognizing Him as in *some sense* the Head of all Churches, as in *some sense* discerning their qualities and ruling them, has not been very difficult in later times, and would not have seemed so to many even then. But to combine that with the belief that He is conversing inwardly with them by His *Spirit* — that by this *Spirit* He is convincing them of sin, of righteousness, and of judgment, that by this *Spirit* He is appealing to what is deepest and most permanent in them, not to what is passing or impulsive — *that*, I suspect, was the difficulty then, and has been ever since. It has taken different forms. At one time the confession of a Son of Man could harden into a merely outward, almost material confession. At another, the confession of a Spirit could be twisted into an excuse for all lawlessness and fanaticism. So it is very significant, I think, that every message should begin with one of these revelations and end with the other; and this is a great reason why, in reading of the Asiatic Churches of eighteen centuries ago, we feel we are reading of the European Churches among which we live.

3\. Another feature is no less obvious — the one I touched on last week in speaking of the seven stars. Each message is addressed to the whole body, in the person of its angel. There were clearly very wide differences in the conduct and moral life of the various people who made up these Churches. Some of these are noted. Yet still a mind and temper is attributed to the whole society, and the minister's mind and temper are taken as a faithful index of it. He represents the particular Church over which he presides. There is no address to its individual members apart from him. With such materials it would be easy to construct a whole system of priestcraft and claim St. John's authority for it. Indeed, such a system is *bound* to arise if *only this* part of St. John's words is noticed, and the part about the Son of Man walking among the lampstands and the Spirit's presence with the Churches is forgotten. Take away those divine revelations, and the light they receive from St. John's refusal to claim any honor for himself, and it takes no elaborate ecclesiastical theory to turn priests into tyrants and Churches into slaves. This will happen naturally, because there is such a craving for unity in the human heart, such an inward sense that we are set under some head, such a consciousness that fathers are responsible for their children, kings for their subjects, ministers for their congregations, that whenever the divine principle explaining these convictions and setting their limits is forgotten, there will be perpetual new outbursts of anarchy opposing priestcraft. If a minister forgets that he is a messenger of God, the only way he can avoid becoming a usurper over men is by becoming their tool.

4\. Once more, all these messages share this feature: every one ends with some words about struggle and victory. To Ephesus: the one who overcomes will eat of the tree of life in the paradise of God. To Smyrna: the one who overcomes will not be hurt by the second death. To Pergamos: the one who overcomes will receive hidden manna and a white stone bearing a new name known only to the one who receives it. To Thyatira: the one who overcomes and keeps Christ's works to the end will be given power over the nations, ruling them with a rod of iron as Christ Himself received from His Father, and will be given the morning star. To Sardis: the one who overcomes will be clothed in white, his name not blotted from the book of life, and Christ will confess his name before His Father and the angels. To Philadelphia: the one who overcomes will be made a pillar in God's temple, never to leave it, marked with the name of God, the name of the new Jerusalem, and Christ's new name. To Laodicea: the one who overcomes will sit with Christ on His throne, just as Christ overcame and sat down with His Father on His throne.

II. 1. I imagine that, even if the first three features have not separated the Apostolic-age Churches from those of later times, the fourth may be thought to belong especially to the age when Christians were persecuted for their faith. These urgent calls to overcome, it will be assumed, must refer to the cross, the stake, the amphitheater, or at least the prison. But if you read through these chapters, you will be struck by how rarely such perils are even mentioned. The most notable case — the only one where the call to struggle and the promise to the victor are directly connected with outward suffering — is Smyrna's: from him who was dead and is alive comes the acknowledgment of their tribulation and poverty (though they are rich), and of the slander of those who claim to be Jews but are the synagogue of Satan; then the encouragement to fear nothing they will suffer, though the devil will cast some into prison and they will have tribulation ten days; and the charge to be faithful unto death, with the promise of a crown of life. Then follows the further promise already quoted: the one who overcomes will not be hurt by the second death.

In this city there was a persecution — apparently not a Roman one at all, but one coming from a synagogue of Jews, men who clung to that venerable name while denying the King of the Jews. Such men, through their influence in particular cities before or after the fall of Jerusalem, could easily raise a violent storm against the sect of Nazarenes and get them thrown into prison. If they themselves were in rebellion (as was shown in the second century, when they followed Barcochba as their Messiah), they were especially willing and able to make the disciples of the Crucified suffer. Or, if the Roman authorities suppressed them by force, Christians might easily be lumped in with them, as sharing a common origin and equally refusing to worship idols. Such persecution would have been just as terrible for the Church of Smyrna as the most general and systematic kind. It had clearly borne the best fruit in the lives of both pastor and flock. Though poor, they were rich. He who had died and become alive and lived forevermore saw in them the blessed result of His agony. Still, they needed strength and comfort. The tribulation would be sharp, though short. The flesh would shrink from it. But let them hold on. The second death, the death of the spirit, would not reach those trusting in the Deliverer from death. There was life in Him. Was not that life for them? Was any royal crown like that?

I have taken this Church first, out of its order. The words spoken to it must have cheered thousands in different generations. They must have felt the message was as truly addressed to them as to the angel of the Church in Smyrna, and that it could only come from Him who had died and was alive. But though it would be an immense loss if this one instance of a body suffering persecution were missing among the seven, it is not typical of the others; it is the exception. Pergamos is called Satan's seat — probably because, as one of the wealthiest cities in Asia Minor, it had been a special resort of Jews who were unusually hostile there. A distant reference is made to an earlier tribulation in which Antipas was a faithful martyr and the Church held fast to Christ's name. But that danger was apparently over; in any case it is not the one the angel is chiefly warned about. In Philadelphia, too, there is a reference to a "synagogue of Satan, which say they are Jews, and are not, but do lie." That synagogue might greatly harass the Christians of Philadelphia. But the chief harassment seems to have come from their claiming to be the people of God, and denouncing those who confessed Jesus as the Son of God as deserters from the old faith. On the whole, nothing could more distort the words of this book than to associate the conquests it speaks of chiefly with external troubles, or to suppose that the seven Churches were all going through such experiences — as if this made them unfit to serve as examples or warnings for those living in ease or safety.

2\. If we are not allowed to build that highly plausible theory about these early Churches, we are even more firmly forbidden to hold another: that they were free from the invasion of immoral doctrines and habits, or that such things, where they appeared, never took root. In the message to Ephesus we hear of the deeds of the "Nicolaitanes, which I hate." There these deeds were discouraged and kept down by the angel of the Church. But in Pergamos they seem to have gained a foothold and to be linked with another doctrine, said to be that "of Balaam, who taught Balac to cast a stumbling-block before the children of Israel, to eat things sacrificed unto idols, and to commit fornication." And the angel of Thyatira is charged with tolerating "that woman Jezebel, which calleth herself a prophetess," in seducing Christ's servants into fornication and eating things sacrificed to idols.

That there is a difference among the three doctrines or states of mind named here, I fully believe. That there was one trait common to all — an indifference to ordinary moral obligations, grounded on the belief that Christians had been raised to a state above them — is equally certain. Two passages in the Stromata of Clemens throw light on the teaching of the Nicolaitanes. From the first (Book II. c. xx. § 118), it appears the sect twisted a saying of the teacher whose name they took, about contempt (or abuse) of the flesh, into a sense opposite to what he meant; what was asceticism in him became libertinism in them. The second passage suggests (c. xxiv. § 25) that the founder's ascetic profession was joined with a practical arrogance and disregard for the most sacred obligations, which might well explain the later conclusions drawn from it. Those conclusions, according to Clemens, amounted to a defense of concubinage, if not of a community of wives. These accounts come from an uncertain tradition and are not entirely clear. But they have an inner probability; the same story has been repeated again and again in different forms.

I do not think we need to assume there was an actual school or sect of "Balaamites." The man who would not curse the children of Israel in words, who could only bless them, and yet did Balak's work far more effectively by setting a temptation before them that undermined their moral strength — he is the forerunner and type of all who accept and proclaim the spiritual lessons of the higher covenant but turn them into excuses for encouraging the fleshly license that saps and destroys the spirit. So far the Nicolaitane and the Balaamite are alike. The Balaamite's distinctive features are two. (1.) He has a real inspiration; he is not a pretended but a genuine prophet, turning an actual gift to a vile use. (2.) His motive for evil is money. Indulgence of lust is the result in his victims. Covetousness has destroyed *him*.

Like the Nicolaitanes and Balaamites, the Prophetess of Thyatira sets herself above law. Like the Balaamite, she enthrones her own spiritual intuitions. But it is power, not liberty, that she aspires to. Her prototype is the Sidonian Jezebel, who broke down the righteous laws of Jehovah, set up the worship of the Baalim (the powers of nature), and turned the Israelites into Phoenician idolaters, and so into a degraded, filthy people. Such female usurpation is as directly connected with sensual worship, and all its consequences, as the doctrine of the male prophet. Indeed it leads *more* directly to tyranny.

3\. The use of names borrowed from the Old Testament for two of these three heresies is proof, I think, that they did not appear to the Apostle as essentially new. He was sure they had existed and borne fruit ages before. They appeared in the Church because the Church was made of men and women; no new race had been created to people it. Even the one heresy with a modern name, of post-Pentecostal origin, was like the others in its nature and effects. Yet it did testify to the fulfillment of the old covenant, to the baptism with the Spirit. In doing so it explained how the conflict between flesh and spirit, present in all earlier times, had now reached its full intensity. Every question now came down to this: what has the illumination of the Divine Spirit done for men? Has it freed them from the restraints imposed on their evil natures, or has it explained the meaning and blessing of those restraints? Has it opened to them visions of heaven that make their earthly conduct a matter of indifference, or has it given meaning to all the common occupations of earth? Has it made those who receive it into tyrants, or made them more obedient — and so more free? If that is so, then instead of confining these Nicolaitane, Balaamite, and Jezebel tendencies to the Apostolic period, we should expect to see them growing from the little seeds in Pergamos and Thyatira into monstrous diseases infecting the Church's life in every region — diseases that, if the Church gained influence over the nations, would infect *them* too, weakening and corrupting *their* powers and threatening *their* existence.

These consequences — which we would expect must follow, if these messages were true and divine — have, it seems to me, actually appeared in the history of Christendom. Nicolaitane communism has kept reappearing in the most varied forms and at the most unexpected moments. The Balaam-prophet — who speaks grand words and sets an example of foul deeds, flattering the spirit with its own greatness until it sinks under the rule of animal or covetous desires — has never been absent from small communities, and has influenced whole countries and generations. The Jezebel, or female form of wickedness, mingling with the others, has given birth to the most widespread idolatries and to systems of spiritual despotism so bewildering, in their variety and apparent opposition, that the successive reformers who fought against them have seemed to others, and even to themselves, to be fighting under different banners for opposite ends. Yet they have been of one heart and mind, in that they all bore witness that morality is unchangeable and that the righteous God cannot dispense with His own laws. And when they have been wise and successful, they have been alike in this too: they never hoped to overcome spiritual delusions by weakening their testimony to a Spirit of Peace and Love and a Sound Mind; they traced the evil around them not to belief in His Presence, but to the denial of it.

III. 1. This last lesson, about how falsehood should be handled, is contained in the message to Ephesus. The angel of that Church had a great horror of religious pretenders: he "cannot bear them that are evil." And he had not only zeal but discernment: he tested those who claimed to be apostles and were not, and found them liars. He also spared no toil: he bore up patiently. And this from real devotion: for Christ's sake he labored and did not faint. As for that sect which has done so much mischief, he hated the deeds of the Nicolaitanes, as Christ Himself does. For all this clarity and vigor of purpose, He who walks among the lampstands and holds the stars in His hand commends His servant. And yet an evil change has been found in him: he has left his first love. The outward activity is still there. The heart is gone. His service has become — is becoming — not dishonest, but mechanical. He who looks within knows it. And He calls him to repent — He whose call, if heeded, is itself the gift of repentance. But if he does not repent, if the coldness and indifference grow deadly, then the punishment is this: "I will remove thy candlestick out of its place." It was set to give light; he is failing to give it. Men see him busy repressing bad opinions and dutifully going through his round of duties. But the life and love of good do not flow from him. So he must not pretend to be what he is not. Ephesus must cease to be a Church if it fails in the one function of a Church. This, then, is the temptation he must overcome — not persecution, not heresy, but dreariness, formality, the worship of decorum. Let him fight against it in the strength of Him who walks among the lampstands. And He will give exactly what is needed: the fruit of the tree of life — the fruit that has power to prevail against every tendency in him toward torpor and death.

2\. In Pergamos it is different. He who has "the sword with two edges," who fights not only outward enemies but the subtle, secret one that undermines the heart within, saw that this Church — brave enough when called to bear witness against Jewish or heathen oppressors — was becoming infected with Balaamite and Nicolaitane corruptions, and that the angel was not resisting them courageously. No doubt they had crept into his own heart. He was not clearly distinguishing *there* between the liberty of the spirit and the license of the flesh; he was not hating the one so as to cherish the other. So he could not fight them in his flock. But the Son of Man is fighting them in both, and can overcome them in both. And if the angel will let that two-edged sword do its work, this shall be his reward: he will eat of the hidden manna that restores the spirit which the flesh-pots of Egypt have weakened. He will have the white stone of Absolution — the true spiritual emancipation which the Balaamite and Nicolaitane emancipation only counterfeited. On the stone will be written "a new name, which no man knoweth save he which receiveth it." The divine liberty is not given in his own name but in Christ's name. The regenerated spirit accepts its freedom in that name; it knows it bears His name, though it cannot make anyone understand how it came to bear it. It stands fast in Christ's liberty — the liberty of doing God's will, of giving up itself.

3\. In Thyatira, again, He "who hath his eyes like unto a flame of fire, and his feet are like fine brass" — He who sees into the heart of things and looks through every appearance, and who Himself passed through the furnace of human temptation — sees how much good there is in the angel and the Church: their love, service, faith, patience, and their last works exceeding the first. There is life; there is growth; evils have been beaten; where there had been failure, there is now success. But He sees evil at work here too — evil of the same inward, penetrating kind as in the other case.

The angel tolerates the woman Jezebel in drawing Christ's servants away to eat things sacrificed to idols and to commit fornication. He is clearly afraid of her spiritual pretensions. He dares not rebuke one who regards herself as a great saint, and whom many of his flock follow as such. He has not learned that the spirit which exalts itself above law is the spirit of Lucifer; that those "depths of wisdom" which lead to moral corruption and to dabbling with idolatry — dignify them with whatever religious names you please — are the depths of Satan. But if the angel is timid, if he dares not exercise discrimination and judgment, there is Another who will lay bare sins that hide under respectable names, who will make it known what gross and open crimes spring from spiritual self-exaltation. That is how I understand the terrible threat to this Church — fulfilled to it, I have no doubt; and fulfilled again and again in particular Churches, and in the Church at large, whenever the Jezebel temper has run rampant.

Can the promise, too, have been fulfilled? "He that overcometh, and keepeth my works unto the end, to him will I give power over the nations. And he shall rule them with a rod of iron; as the vessels of a potter shall they be broken to shivers: even as I received of my Father. And I will give unto him the morning star." The temptation to be overcome is the temptation to covet heathen privileges and heathen power, to tamper with heathen opinions and practices in order to win them. The hook was wonderfully baited; not to nibble at it was very hard in the earliest times, and has been very hard ever since. What is the encouragement to reject it entirely? This: if you do, you will have the very power you were seeking — power over those to whom you were being asked to surrender yourselves; a power they will confess; a power that will break their corruptions and superstitions in pieces; a power the same in kind as that which Christ received from His Father. Now this is a promise I am sure was performed to that Church at Thyatira and its angel, and has been performed to every Church since, insofar as it has struggled with this most subtle evil and conquered it. Every society that has kept its own moral standing-ground, that has refused to win power and influence by stooping to the practices of those around it, has been allowed, in marvelous ways, to lift them up, to reform nations, to make them confess that Christ's kingdom is no dream. In the smallest circle this is verified; it is proved by all the real dominion the Church has exercised over the world, by the conquests it has won from the world. It has been proved just as fully by the Church's own abominable tyrannies over the world, by the tyrannies it has kept alive in the world, and by the defeats it has suffered from the world. Grasping at the promise, "Ye shall rule them with a rod of iron" — but forgetting the clause, "as I have received from my Father" — the Church has misused her rod to crush the very spirits Christ came from the Father to raise, and to protect and worsen the cruelty and oppression He came to break to shivers. And what has been the recompense? The wretched power she wrenched and stole from the nations has been turned against her. She has been forced to grovel to them and beg their help, and they have justly spurned her. She chose to exalt herself like Lucifer, and she has fallen like Lucifer. Had she trusted her Lord, He would have given her the morning star. She should have received from Him what she claimed independently of Him. She should have dispensed light to the world. She should have shouted for joy with all the sons of God.

4\. If we take these messages this way — as applying directly to the Churches of Asia — then, it seems to me, they apply most powerfully to all times, and most stingingly to ourselves. Is there one of these early heresies to which we are not exposed? Are we free from any Antinomian danger — the danger of turning spiritual imaginations, or even spiritual realities, into an excuse for self-indulgence and self-glory?

But perhaps we are even more surprised to read the messages to Sardis and Laodicea — connected as they are with bodies we suppose must have had all the freshness of youth upon them — than to hear of the startling moral corruptions denounced in the others. For here we find all the marks we associate with what is aged and worn out, or with the indifference, softness, and self-satisfaction that ease and luxury breed. The Sardian "has a name that he lives and is dead." He must "strengthen the things that remain, that are ready to die." The Laodicean is "neither hot nor cold." He says he is "rich, and increased with goods," and does not know he is "wretched, and miserable, and poor, and blind, and naked." This language has often been used in pulpits to show the contrast between primitive times and ours, how far below that blessed age of the New Testament we have fallen. And the admonition has been felt to have force; it has struck home to many consciences and stirred some to repentance and reform. Why? I think, brethren, because the language itself is far truer, mightier, and simpler than the preacher's interpretation of it. His golden age never existed. Christ's revelation of facts scatters that dream. But that revelation tells the Sardian that he has the seven Spirits that quicken and the seven stars that give light. And those Spirits can quicken what is ready to die now as well as then; and those stars can light up what is dark now as well as then. Christ's revelation tells the Sardian that all who overcome shall be clothed in white raiment, that what is evil shall be blotted out, that their names shall *not* be blotted from the book of life, but that He will confess them before His Father and the angels. In such a sentence lies an imperishable power. Those white robes of innocence, that power to stand before God and call Him Father — we need them as much as the generations of old. And since it is held out not to those full of faith, energy, and hope, but to those whose works were feeble, who had only a name to live, we may take courage to take it home; in us it may be accomplished. Christ's revelation tells the Laodicean that He is the "Amen, the faithful and true witness, the beginning of the creation of God," and that as many as He loves He rebukes and chastens, and that He stands at the door and knocks, and that if anyone hears His voice and opens the door He will come in and sup with him, and that to the one who overcomes He will grant to sit with Him on His throne, as He overcame and sat down with His Father on His throne. Such words carry an undying message to those beginning to discover their own nakedness, blindness, and wretchedness. They are an assurance that for these too there is gold to be had, tried in the fire, and white raiment to clothe them, and eye-salve so they may see.

5\. And so the brightest vision of all among these Churches — the only very bright one — ceases to be a vision of some bygone period owing its happiness to a set of circumstances that can never return. The angel of the Church of the Philadelphians had only "a little strength," and because he knew he had only that little, he kept Christ's word and did not deny His name. While those who called themselves Jews refused to believe that the Son of David could be the Son of Man, and claimed an exclusive Messiah to glorify them and condemn the world, this angel would not deny that glorious name; he believed in One who came to die for the world and redeem it.
