Amid all the apparent difficulties and contradictions, he kept the word of Christ's patience, believing that Christ would at last show Himself to be what He had declared Himself to be — that His cross would prove to be the conquering power in the world, the central sun to draw all things to it. Therefore, He who is holy and true, who holds the key of David, who opens and no one shuts, and shuts and no one opens, promises to keep this servant and this Church from the temptation that was to come upon all the world, to try those who dwell on the earth. What that temptation was, and how true the promise "Behold, I come quickly" was, I believe we shall learn as we study this book further. And the more we learn it, and the more we believe it, the less we shall think that any promise of His has worn itself out, or that He Himself has left us; the more we shall long to grasp the full force of the words this Apocalypse was written to explain to the Philadelphians and to us: "Him that overcometh will I make a pillar in the temple of my God; and he shall go no more out; and I will write upon him the name of my God, and the name of the city of my God, which is new Jerusalem, which cometh down out of heaven from my God, and I will write upon him my new name."

## LECTURE IV. THE VISION OF HEAVEN.

*The passage:* After the messages, John looked and saw a door standing open in heaven. The trumpet-like voice he had heard before told him to come up, so it could show him what must happen afterward. At once he was in the Spirit, and he saw a throne in heaven with someone seated on it, radiant like jasper and sardine stone, with a rainbow like an emerald around it. Around the throne were twenty-four seats holding twenty-four elders in white robes with gold crowns. From the throne came lightning, thunder, and voices, and before it burned seven lamps of fire, which are the seven Spirits of God, and there was a sea of glass like crystal. In and around the throne were four living creatures covered with eyes front and back—one like a lion, one like a calf, one with a human face, one like a flying eagle—each with six wings and full of eyes, who never rest from crying "Holy, holy, holy, Lord God Almighty, which was, and is, and is to come." Whenever these creatures give glory, honor, and thanks to the One who sits on the throne and lives forever, the twenty-four elders fall down before him, worship him, and throw their crowns before the throne, saying he is worthy to receive glory, honor, and power, because he created all things and they exist and were created for his pleasure.

The messages to the seven Churches are finished. What those Churches needed was the revelation of a Son of Man living among them—the source of their light, the Judge of their inner condition, the one who cleanses and restores them. But the man who wrote down what the Spirit said to them needed something more to prepare *him* for the crisis that was coming. In the vision of a Prince of the kings of the earth, something was implied that everyone should want to explore, and that those especially had to explore who were to teach their own time and be the prophets of the next generation. If they were satisfied merely to believe in a Friend of Man, they would drag Him down to their own level; or else He would seem too grand and terrible for them to contemplate, and lesser beings, more like themselves in their weakness and mistakes, would become dearer to them than He is. Then, when some great shock disturbed the ordinary course of their lives, they would find they had no firm place to stand. The faith that had for a while raised and purified them would look like nothing but *their* faith, *their* imagination. Everything around them would reel and totter, and they would reel and totter along with it.

I. So the Old Testament teaches us that the preparation for any great turning point in Jewish history was a fresh discovery of God Himself as the foundation of the nation's order and its very existence. I have already spoken in these lectures, and often before in this place, of that revelation to Moses of the "*I AM*" as the one pillar of Israel's law and freedom, the thing that fitted him for his work. Today I must speak of two other passages, which are not only important and interesting in themselves but necessary for understanding the chapter before us. "*In the year that King Uzziah died*," says the sixth chapter of Isaiah, "*I saw also the Lord sitting upon a throne, high and lifted up, and his train filled the temple. Above it stood the seraphims: each one had six wings; with twain he covered his face, and with twain he covered his feet, and with twain he did fly. And one cried unto another, and said, Holy, holy, holy, is the Lord of hosts: the whole earth is full of his glory. And the posts of the door moved at the voice of him that cried, and the house was filled with smoke*." Here a man sitting in Solomon's temple is shown the meaning and reality of the symbols that stood around him. What he saw with his eyes pointed to a living, spiritual world he could not see but which surrounded him. A king was dying or dead; a king was about to succeed who would bring idol worship into that very temple, and in whose reign there would be an invasion by Syrians and a still more dangerous invasion by Assyrians. The earthquake would be terrible; the nation would be shaken to its core. Its inner life would seem to be gone, and from outside the sentence "Cut it down" would seem to be coming. But the outcome of events did not depend on Ahaz, or Shalmaneser, or Sennacherib. The survival of the nation did not depend on the survival of its outward signs and forms. Beneath everything and above everything there was a Divine King and a Divine order. He was holy, whatever else was unholy. There was right at the foundation of things, however much power seemed to trample right down. God had ministers around Him who displayed His nature and carried out His purposes. If Israel utterly failed to be His minister, these would not fail. And in them was a pledge that Israel too would not fail, whatever its kings or priests did. The nation would live on and do its work until the work was done. Knowing this, Isaiah could be a prophet. He could speak to kings, priests, and people about their unbelief in this Divine King and about the miseries their unbelief had brought and would still bring. He could bear witness that there was a Will higher than their self-will, which would in the end prevail against it.

II. Isaiah is often called the evangelical prophet, the prophet of the Christ who was to be born of the Virgin and shown on earth. That makes it all the more important to remember that his prophecy does not begin from that point; its ground is laid in the revelation of a King who lives forever. My other example comes from a later and much darker period. Ezekiel is sitting stunned by the river Chebar. He is a captive among captives. The Babylonian has laid his land waste. The capital drags on a dreary existence under a king whom Nebuchadnezzar has appointed. The temple of his fathers is soon to perish, and he is surrounded by strange sights and strange forms of idolatry. Some of those sculptures whose character we have lately come to know may have been before his eyes; at any rate they were not far off. His people, already used to cruder shapes of Egyptian or Phoenician worship, would be drawn with a strange fascination to the shapes the world's rulers had made. There were all kinds of animal power, with the lion and the eagle standing out above the rest. There was the god-king, with his sharp eye for conquest and destruction. There was the chariot in which he rode to battle or led home his procession of captives. There were the wings that seemed to say he and these animals belonged to a region above the earth. How astonishing the combination of all these forms was! Was not the Lord God of Israel—of whom no image could be made—overpowered, when the rulers of the earth, the conquerors of the chosen people, paid homage to all these images? "*It came to pass in the thirtieth year, in the fourth month, that the heavens were opened, and I saw visions of God. And I looked, and behold a whirlwind came out of the north, a great cloud, and a fire unfolding itself, and a brightness was about it, and out of the midst thereof as the colour of amber, out of the midst of the fire. Also out of the midst thereof came the likeness of four living creatures. And this was their appearance; they had the likeness of a man. And every one had four faces, and every one had four wings. And they had the hands of a man under their wings on their four sides; and they four had their faces and their wings. As for the likeness of their faces, they four had the face of a man, and the face of a lion, on the right side; and they four had the face of an ox on the left side; they four also had the face of an eagle*." A vision of God! Yes—these Babylonian images have not driven Him out of His own creation. They are parts of His own creation. Every one of them bears witness to Him. The lion, the ox, the eagle each display some aspect of His power and His work. This chariot in which the tyrant sits, the chariot that goes out to spread ruin and death, is not, after all, the truly conquering chariot. There are wheels higher than its wheels. There are wheels with a spirit moving in the middle of them. There is an order of life, not of death. And these wheels will at last roll over everything that resists them. Bel will bow down and Nebo will stoop, and those who lead others into captivity will themselves go into captivity. On the throne is the likeness of a man—not of a brute or a tyrant. And just as at the start light was unfolding itself out of a cloud and a whirlwind, so this was the vision's end: "*As the appearance of the bow that is in the cloud in the day of rain, so was the appearance of the brightness round about. This was the appearance of the likeness of the glory of the Lord*."

By such a revelation—the powers of nature and of art rescued from their perversion and restored to their right place in the Divine harmony—by such a vision of a Being at the center of all, terrible not from too much darkness but from too much light—the lonely exile was prepared to endure the troubles of his time, to bear witness to his people about their idolatries and heathenism, and to be the prophet of a rebuilt temple and a renewed society.

III. And so it is with the greater teacher, the last of the Apostles. He had absorbed those earlier revelations, and now needed a still higher one so that he could bear the weight of his present and coming sorrow. For him on the island of Patmos, as for Ezekiel by the Chebar, a door was opened in heaven. And the trumpet-voice he had heard before, waking him to acknowledge the Son of Man walking among the candlesticks, now tells him to come up higher, so he can learn what is to come. That trumpet now, as before, speaks not to his outward ear but to his spiritual ear. At once he is in the spirit, seeking to know what lasts and abides, what will not change when everything he sees changes. "*And behold a throne was set in heaven, and One sat on the throne*." There had been a throne in Isaiah's vision; it spoke of the King who lives on when Uzziah and one king after another die. There had been a throne in Ezekiel's vision; it spoke of a King who rules in righteousness, while the kings of Israel and Babylon ruled in unrighteousness, exalting power over righteousness. St. John's need is greater. The kingdom of Israel is gone. The temple in which Isaiah sat, which in Ezekiel's day was to perish and rise again, had now been finally doomed. And a god-emperor mightier than the Babylonian was claiming the world and its glory as his own. The old Asiatic thrones, the Greek republics, even the Roman republic itself, had all fallen beneath his feet. Was there any throne besides his? Was his the only one, raised to omnipotence? A Person is here—as real as the man on the throne of the Caesars, not an abstraction, not a phantom of cloud or air. But "*He that sat was to look upon like a jasper and a sardine stone*." This is not a revelation of crushing power, but of perfect, untroubled brightness—of light with no darkness in it at all. What had been emerging in Ezekiel's vision through whirlwind and cloud is now shown to the New Testament seer as the home and resting-place of his spirit. Yet he has lost nothing that was given to his forerunner: "*There was a rainbow round about the throne, in sight like unto an emerald*." The sardine stone does not shut its light up inside itself. The light must spread out in different rays; there must be variety within the unity.

IV. I do not want to change these words. I feel how poor every attempt to make them simpler is. I only want us all to let them sink into our hearts and drive out the notions and fancies that lurk there—the dreary ideas of grandeur and divinity we make up for ourselves. We little know how much of these come from the commonest associations of earthly pomp—yes, and of mere brute force. Be sure we cannot get rid of them simply by deciding that our heaven shall bear no likeness to earth. It *will* bear likeness to earth; if you build one heaven wholly cut off from earth, you will end up with another one made from the raw materials of earth, and that is the one you will actually believe in. What you need is not a heaven separate from earth, but one that explains earth's confusions and difficulties instead of being built out of them; one that shows you how the forms of earth are images of heaven's forms, rather than heaven being copied from them. You want a door opened in heaven precisely so that you are not left with an earth given over to oppression and cruelty. Keep this thought in mind as you read the next sentences: "*And round about the throne were four and twenty seats: and upon the seats I saw four and twenty elders sitting, clothed in white raiment; and they had on their heads crowns of gold*." You have often noticed, reading the first book of the Iliad, how closely the council of chiefs at the beginning matches the council of gods at the end. There are quarrels in both; the quarrel on Olympus, you say, is a glorified version of the one among the Greek ships. On the whole I think that judgment is right. I would only ask you to consider what would become of everything you admire, and rightly admire, in Homer if there were *no* such glorified version—if you had only the earthly heroes without the beings they were said to descend from. You would lose the poetry, of course; and I believe you would also lose the poem's truth. The earth would become utterly bleak and bare; you would feel that the men could not be what they longed to be, could not reach the stature they felt they ought to reach, if the sense of higher presences near them were taken away. But if this is the truth, where does the falsehood come in? I have tried to show where. If the standard of the council above is in any way drawn from the standard of the council below—if the poet, wanting to see the gods as the source of the law and justice he finds among men, starts measuring that law and justice by our own—then confusion is bound to creep in. We are deeply grateful to the great poet for letting us see this; his simplicity and honesty are the very things that help us most to detect what keeps him from being wholly simple and true; and we are sure God must have been teaching men who had such glimpses of Him as this Greek and his countrymen had. All the more, then, do we long for God to tell us what He is—whether there is a heavenly order such as men have dreamed of; whether it is unlike everything most precious to us in the order of earth; and if not, whether it must share earth's imperfection.

V. People looking for objections to the Apocalypse may think it a great find that the elders sitting on the twenty-four seats recall an institution of the Jewish commonwealth. To me it is an obvious and very welcome announcement. Instead of the hierarchies of angels with which medieval writers filled the heavenly world, I find here the plain and beautiful revelation of a government and justice that is the pattern for government and justice on earth. Forget the Center of all—the One who sits on *the* throne, who is like jasper and sardine stone—and these twenty-four thrones turn into a polytheistic dream, a new kind of nature-worship or demigod-worship. But join them to that Center, as they are joined in this vision, and a calm sense comes into the mind that these are ministers of the Perfect Ruler, doing His commands and listening to His words, with no caprice or disobedience in them, no fighting against law, but perfect freedom and joy in carrying law out. There is a craving in all of us for such a revelation as this; we grow impatient even with the highest discoveries of science when we think it forces us to worship mere laws of the universe and to deny that those laws may be administered by a Person. That there is really no conflict between these two convictions, I think we shall all one day learn. What we need is the assurance that the robes of these Judges and Rulers are white—that there is on them no stain of indulgence or favoritism; that they wear the golden crowns of wisdom and righteousness, which they win by always looking at Him who is perfectly righteous and wise. Then we can rejoice that they rule the earth, because they have none of earth's baseness.

VI. In the middle of that council of Greek gods sat the Thunderer. A grand image, certainly, and not meaningless—but with very little that is truly awesome. The Israelite was taught that the thunders and lightnings that came from Sinai proceeded from the Lord his God, who had brought him out of slavery. He trembled; but he was told to trust in this God, and those who trusted Him would not be put to shame. From the throne St. John saw, "*proceeded lightnings and thunderings and voices*." Perfect goodness and purity will make war on everything evil and dark. But there were also "*seven lamps of fire burning before the throne, which are the seven Spirits of God*." The consuming fire that is in the lightning has another purpose than destruction. It gives light and gives life. It comes from the Divine Being, the perfect Love; those Spirits are filled with it, and they go out to quicken and inspire all beings that have a will. "*And before the throne there was a sea of glass like unto crystal*." The throne is not only the place from which commands are issued. The glory of Him who sits on it is meant to be mirrored in His creatures; what He is absolutely and wholly, they are to show forth in their various measures and relations.

VII. And now we get the fuller unfolding of that vision which comforted Ezekiel in captivity. Here, in this heavenly world, are the patterns of those different kinds of power to which men have paid homage on earth, which they have taken as varied pictures of the Divine nature. "And in *the midst of the throne, and round about the throne, were four beasts*"—or four living creatures—"*full of eyes before and behind. And the first beast was like a lion, and the second beast like a calf, and the third beast had a face as a man, and the fourth beast was like a flying eagle*." Here are types of powers you have met with in the worship and art of every great nation on earth. The first Asiatic conqueror revered the strength of the lion; it expressed the qualities he most wanted in a king and supposed most typical of a god. The useful animals, for which the calf was both literally and ideally the representative, commanded the awe of the Egyptian, and no less of the Hindu. The Greek, still wrestling with these animal images, nonetheless rose to the idea of a human figure and face that surpasses them. The flying eagle stood to the Roman for his ambition of universal rule. In each case the idea of what was greatest in nature corresponded exactly to the idea of what was greatest in the unseen world. There was indeed, as we saw in Assyrian worship, a confused mingling of these different instincts; none was missing in any people of the earth, and only gradually do we see which was mostly governed by one and which by another. But the great revelation to the last of the seers—the one that was to prepare him for the collapse of his own country's worship and of the witness it had borne against all these idolatries—is that each of these had a meaning; that it was the perversion of a truth; that each animal form had its Divine significance, its heavenly counterpart; that each had its eyes behind, turned to Him who sat on the throne, catching the reflection of His light and purity, and its eyes in front, looking out to the works of His hands. "*And the four beasts had each of them six wings about him; and they were full of eyes within: and they rest not day and night, saying, Holy, holy, holy, Lord God Almighty, which was, and is, and is to come*." Here are the wings that lifted up the living creatures in the old prophet's vision, showing him what the Assyrian sculptor had only dimly sensed—that everything in creation must have a pull upward as well as downward. There is a spirit carrying things toward God, just as there is a nature dragging them toward earth. But here the downward pull is overcome. They keep rising in higher and higher circles; they get nearer glimpses of eternal holiness and truth. The closer they come, the greater the awe, and the greater the sense of distance. There is no ambition to climb, only to behold. Ever more discovery, ever more adoration. To know truth in its many forms; to know it in its essential unity; to enter a love that surpasses knowledge, so that it may flow out through them to the farthest ends of the universe—this is the work, this is the longing, of these heavenly Natures. But this beholding of truth, this entering into the joy of the Lord, does not shut out that other work of judging and governing which our Lord promised His disciples when He said that the one who used his five talents faithfully would rule over five cities. And the work is part of the worship. "*And when these beasts give glory, and honour, and thanks to him that sitteth upon the throne, who liveth for ever and ever, the four and twenty elders fall down before him that sat on the throne, and worship him that liveth for ever and ever, and cast their crowns before the throne, saying, Thou art worthy, O Lord, to receive glory and honour and power: for thou hast created all things, and for thy pleasure they are and were created*."

VIII. It has often been said in Christian pulpits that heaven is just the continuation of worship on earth. Those who have found earthly worship very dreary and unsatisfying have said they would rather have any Greek Elysium or Gothic Valhalla than a heaven like that. I think if we take St. John as our guide—if we accept his revelation as the true one—we can see a truth in what the clergyman says and also a truth in the layman's protest. Everything there is worship, because all are pursuing the highest good in both thought and action; because all are referring their thoughts and deeds to one center, instead of scattering them among a thousand different centers; because each thinker and each doer forgets himself in the object before him and the work given to him. But if the preacher supposes there is any monotony of employment there—that any serious, earnest task that occupied the student here will be dropped or cut off there, that any real work men are engaged in has nothing corresponding to it and fulfilling it there—I would ask him to look hard at this opening of heaven to the Apostle, and correct his own fancies and speculations by it. The four living creatures, we shall find, are busy with all the movements and changes in the moral world; the very word "elders" shows they are exercising the functions and faculties of judgment and direction over spiritual beings and over natural forces. Our worship must always be dreary if it stands apart from our daily life, if it does not interpret and transfigure that life. The heavenly worship is unbroken only because growth in knowledge is unbroken, and because all free action is unbroken. In the many mansions there is room for every form of life; only the shapes of death can be shut out.

IX. So I say we have here the Christian Elysium, or Valhalla, or Paradise—the thing you are all looking for in your calmest and truest moments; when you are most tormented by the discords of the world around you and of your own hearts; when you are most certain there must be a harmony without discords; when you long for room to finish tasks that death will leave undone; when you wish to recover affections that have been broken, to know what you could not know, to work bravely, to rest without stopping work. "I go," said Hooker on his deathbed—and these words move me more than all the ecstatic phrases reported from such moments—"I go to a world of order." It was such a world—as I have said, and as we shall see more fully later—that St. John was driven to seek by the upheavals tearing the world around him to pieces. Is Nero, is Vitellius, the King of kings, the Lord of lords? That was the question he had to face. And this was the answer. He found that the kingdom of heaven, which his Master had spoken of to him as they sat in the boat, and to crowds besides him, was indeed a real kingdom—not far off in some distant star, but bound by the closest ties, by shared pursuits and interests, to everything happening here. It was much to know this; but whoever knows this much will long to know more. What ties this calm life to our restless life? How can one act on the other and reform it? Will He who sits on that heavenly throne restore the children of earth who are trampled under the tyrant on the earthly throne? These questions link today's vision with the one I hope to look at next Sunday—the vision of the book sealed with seven seals, and of the Lamb who prevailed to open it.

## LECTURE V. THE BOOK WITH SEVEN SEALS.

*The passage:* John sees a book in the right hand of the One seated on the throne, written on both sides and sealed with seven seals. A strong angel asks aloud who is worthy to open it, but no one in heaven, on earth, or under the earth can open it or even look at it. John weeps because no one is found worthy. Then one of the elders tells him not to weep: the Lion of the tribe of Judah, the Root of David, has prevailed to open the book and its seals. John sees, in the midst of the throne and the four living creatures and the elders, a Lamb that looks as if it had been slain, with seven horns and seven eyes, which are the seven Spirits of God sent into all the earth. The Lamb takes the book from the One on the throne. Then the four living creatures and the twenty-four elders fall down before the Lamb, each with a harp and golden bowls full of incense, which are the prayers of the saints, and they sing a new song: the Lamb is worthy to take the book and open its seals, because he was slain and by his blood redeemed people to God out of every tribe, tongue, people, and nation, and made them kings and priests to God, and they shall reign on the earth. John then hears the voices of countless angels around the throne — ten thousand times ten thousand — saying that the Lamb who was slain is worthy to receive power, riches, wisdom, strength, honor, glory, and blessing. And every creature in heaven, on earth, under the earth, and in the sea joins in ascribing blessing, honor, glory, and power to the One on the throne and to the Lamb forever. The four living creatures say "Amen," and the elders fall down and worship.

For a time St. John might have been glad to forget everything he had seen of conflict and disorder while he gazed at that vision of peace and order. For he knew it was real. What shifts and changes might be an illusion; this could not be. It was the solid reality lying beneath all those ups and downs. It took nothing from the beholder's mind; instead it gave his mind steadiness. To see a throne on which sat One who was all light and no darkness, all justice and no whim, to see forms reflecting that light and rulers carrying out that justice — this must have brought great calm to a seer who a little before had been looking at the unsteady flickering of those earthly candlesticks, and at the imperfect government of angels who only partly showed the divine righteousness. But if the prophet could forget the earth while he looked at heaven, these heavenly powers could not. He who sat on the throne could not. Were the thrones on earth going to acknowledge this throne, or set up a rule of their own, independent of it and opposed to it? If they were to obey it, what would make them obey? Could a mere decree do it? Could rebel *wills* be made to yield that way?

I. "And I saw in the right hand of him that sat on the throne a book written within and on the backside, sealed with seven seals." It matters little what we call this book. We may call it the Doomsday Book; the book that contains and interprets human history; the book that claims the kingdoms of the earth for God. Each of these names will prove fitting as we go on; the contents of the book will tell us better what it is than any guesses of ours. What St. John wants us to know at once is that the One who looked like jasper and sardine stone held it in His right hand. He knew what was in it. It was the expression of His purpose and will. The belief that there is such a book in such a hand has held up the strongest and sternest minds among men; it has let them bear the world's despair and their own. Those who have spoken of an unbending, unstoppable, irreversible fate have sensed this truth, even when they darkened it most. They meant more than they could say or knew. There was a right hand behind their terrible abstraction; a divine will beneath what looked like the denial of all will. The stoic's mere submission to necessity grows, in the hymn of Cleanthes, into recognition of a Providence over the deeds and thoughts of men; and in the meditations of Epictetus and Marcus Aurelius it even becomes the confession of a deliverer and a friend. But these wise men thought the scroll could never be unrolled. To bear what was assigned them was their only wisdom. A deep wisdom, if only it could be known *what* was assigned; if it could be known what the One who assigns it was doing — so that men might not work merely as servants of a power they must give in to, but as children of a Father training them to be like Himself.

II. It was not the will of the One on the throne, as St. John learned, that the book should stay closed. "And I saw a strong angel proclaiming with a loud voice, Who is worthy to open the book, and to loose the seals thereof?" Often, when students on earth try to look into the puzzles they find around them, behind them, and ahead of them, we stop them with a warning against vanity and presumption. Who can look into the secrets of the All-wise? Who can wrestle with the Almighty? My friends, there may be a right meaning in such warnings; I have admitted there is. In a pagan teacher they should be welcomed with gratitude as signs of an awe he did not learn in an idol's temple — though one would be sorry not to find in him also earnest cries for a solution to the riddle he sometimes calls insoluble. But we ought to ask ourselves seriously whether it may not be an inspiration from above, and not from below, that drives men to strike the bars of their prison and test whether there is not some hidden passage out. That cry on earth is the echo, St. John says, of a voice in heaven. A strong angel called on his own fellows to put out their strength and see whether they could break the seals.

III. "And no man in heaven, nor in earth, neither under the earth, was able to open the book, neither to look thereon."

Many will complain that our translators rendered *oudeis* as "no man" when the scene is in heaven and the ones spoken of are heavenly powers. It was a needless and willful clash with appearances, which they would have been wiser to avoid. But I believe the mistake is not serious at all. *We* set a vast gap between angelic and human natures — partly because the angelic is almost fantastic to us, and partly because we draw our idea of the human from the Fall. The writer of the Apocalypse, as I have said before, often seems to blend the two. God's messenger, whether subject to death or free from it, is still called by one common name. That name names his character and function; whether he fulfills that character and function or not, he can have no higher one — except the filial name that belongs to the beggar in rags as much as to the seraphim. Everything depends on the question of whom we take to be the head of every man — the first Adam or the second.

These remarks are needed to make clear the whole passage before us, even more than this one verse. Whatever superiority angelic powers may have over mortal ones, whatever exemption heavenly powers may enjoy from earth's calamities, that superiority and that exemption did not qualify them to open this book and look in it. The book of God must be read by someone who fully grasped the mind of God. The book of the earth's victory must be read by one who fully grasped the earth's conflict.

IV. "And I wept much, because no man was found worthy to open and to read the book, neither to look thereon." I spoke of the peace that vision of an ordered world must have given the prophet. Thank God, such peace was not all he wanted; if more had not been given, that peace could not have lasted. The pity and tenderness, you will say, of a child of earth showed itself in his weeping; rather, I would say, the pity and tenderness of a child of God. He could not be content until he knew more of the destinies of his own race, because the Father of that race would not let him be content. The question whether justice and truth were to reign *here*, and whether injustice and untruth were to be put down, concerned him most deeply, no doubt. But he learned *how much* it concerned him when he was in the company of those who are above the earth's smoke, when the One who looked like jasper and sardine stone was unveiled to him. Then it became unbearable pain that no one should be worthy to open and read the book.

V. "And one of the elders saith unto me, Weep not: behold, the Lion of the tribe of Judah, the Root of David, hath prevailed to open the book, and to loose the seven seals thereof." "The Lion of the tribe of Judah! the Root of David!" What a drop! A moment ago we were hearing of the heavenly world — the throne, the rainbow, the sea of glass, the seven lamps of fire that are the seven Spirits of God. A great drop, certainly, if there is *no* fellowship between heaven and earth. A great drop, if we tie spiritual power to the great and not to the little. But *no* drop at all, if the very subject of this vision and of this book is the union of heaven and earth, the setting up of heaven's rule over the earth. *No drop*, if spiritual power is made great through weakness, if its true showing is in triumph over animal bulk and vast numbers. The lion was long ago the symbol of the tribe of Judah. That tribe, standing for the nation it belonged to, was raised up to show how one man might chase a thousand and two put ten thousand to flight, because it held a secret of strength that Egyptian and Babylonian kings did not have. David — the youngest son, the shepherd, the stripling, the outlaw — was the proof that God had set His King on the holy hill of Zion. What name could better express the spiritual conqueror than the Lion of the tribe of Judah? What name better points to the true source and ground of all power than the Root of David? Those names belonged to all the early memories of the aged exile; they made him understand that he was never a truer Jew than when he was witnessing for the Prince of all the kings of the earth. They showed him by what divine links the records of that people — which was about to be a people no longer — were joined to the deliverance of all mankind.

VI. For who was the Lion of the tribe of Judah? Where lay that secret of strength that belonged to no other in heaven or on earth?

"And I beheld, and, lo, in the midst of the throne, and of the four beasts, and in the midst of the elders, stood a Lamb as it had been slain, having seven horns and seven eyes, which are the seven Spirits of God sent forth into all the earth."

The Lion of the tribe of Judah is the Lamb that was slain. Here is that revelation of power made perfect in weakness, shown in submission, for which all the earlier history of mankind had been a preparation. Here is that reconciling of the highest with the lowest, of dominion with suffering, of life with death, which alone can explain the riddle of the universe, which alone can satisfy the endless doubts by which men have been and still are tormented — yes, and must be, unless they shut their eyes to facts or sink into dull indifference.

How can that absolute purity and truth, which is like jasper or sardine stone, be brought within a creature's sight? It may be worshiped from afar; it may be seen in some of its outward doings. But that is not enough for beings made in God's image, who themselves demand truth and purity. "In the midst of the throne is the Lamb that was slain." Purity and truth are seen here not in some broken mirror, in scattered fragments, but fully and altogether. In that act of perfect self-sacrifice, of total offering, of surrender to death, all that the angels are seeking comes to light. It is not scaled down or adapted to their needs or their weakness; rather, in meeting that need and weakness, the full, unmeasured divine perfection and glory appear; every veil that hid them is taken away. He is also "in the midst of the elders." Those twenty-four seats, from which go out righteous judgments upon free beings and which direct the movements of natural agents, express the mind that is in the Lamb. Throughout the whole universe the same principle of self-sacrifice, which is the law of His acts, is at work to govern and renew. "He is in the midst of the four living creatures." The lion, the calf, the man, the eagle — the different forms of power that men had worshiped — each had its heavenly original; some aspect of the divine nature was set before each and shown in each. But these creatures have their center here; the Lamb that was slain makes each able to do its own work, makes each effective for the end they are all working toward, and binds them to that throne from which human idolatry cut them off. "He has seven horns." Those powers which, cut off from self-sacrifice, are disordered and clashing, are brought into harmony in Him. Each has its own quality; none clashes with another; there is no sameness in their motions, but a free and living unity. He has the "seven eyes, which are the seven Spirits of God sent forth into all the earth." That sense of an eye watching over the greatest and the smallest *events* in the world's history, which leads us to speak of Providence — a noble word if we let it keep its meaning and did not turn it into a feeble substitute for the living Being who exercises it; and that sense of an eye over the thoughts and intentions of our *hearts*, which leads us to speak of a scrutiny and judgment none can escape — both find their full justification here. Only the language of the Apocalypse is far clearer, brighter, and truer to our actual experience than the language we usually use. We need words that tell us each king and each man is subject to a distinct Providence and a distinct judgment, and yet that it is the same for all and in all. We need words that show the eyes are not only searching and probing, but life-giving; that if they bring the evil in us to light, they also find and give a good that can drive it out. "The seven eyes, which are the seven Spirits of God sent forth into all the earth" give us such words — if we connect them, as the Apostle does, with the One to whom they belong; if we believe that the orderer of our lives, the searcher of our hearts, is the Lamb that was slain.

Here, then, is the New Testament revelation, matching and fulfilling that Old Testament revelation of the Lion of Judah — who was to overthrow the destroying lion, the brute tyranny, of the world — and of the Root of David, who was to set up a kingdom of righteousness in place of a kingdom of self-will. The discovery that the highest Godhead is shown in self-sacrifice; that all power, angelic or human, has this ground; that all power which does not confess this ground is disorderly, rebellious, and doomed to perish — this is the gospel that the Cross of Christ preaches to mankind; and it is this doctrine, in its most sweeping and universal form, that this chapter of the Apocalypse sets before us.

VII. "And he came and took the book out of the right hand of him that sat on the throne."

He is able to unroll the book of God's purposes; to break the seals that have kept it from being understood by men; to show what will last in the universe and what will decay; to show what men may accept and rest in, and what they are to resist to the death. He is able to make clear whether any hope that wise and brave men have ever held for the world is destined to disappointment, or whether they hoped too feebly and the fulfillment will be beyond their dream. Such is the prospect before us. The Apocalypse is going to speak of all these things. If we will attend to its own words and not put ours in their place, I believe it will give us light and satisfaction on all of them.

VIII. But before we go on, let me lead you to think for a few moments about the passage in front of us this afternoon. It may help, I think, to correct an opinion widely spread through the Christian world, which must more or less infect us all, and which must ruin any lessons that St. John's Revelation would teach us. The Lamb is here said to prevail to *open* the book of God's purposes. We often suppose He prevailed by His sacrifice to *change* those purposes. We often say that God's will, or justice, or purity demanded of man something he could not give; that he was doomed to destruction for that failure; that the Lamb stepped in to turn aside this sentence; that He paid the creature's debt; and that so He satisfied the mind of the One on the throne. That many strands are woven into this theory which are drawn from men's practical faith, from their experience of their own needs, and from lessons they have learned in Scripture — this I gladly admit. But I must also insist that this practical faith has suffered, and still suffers cruelly, from the speculations mixed in with it; that men's hearts crave a satisfaction which this scheme of divinity does not give them; and that if they would listen to Scripture's teaching, they would find that satisfaction. How naturally men who are conscious of evil wish to change the purpose of a Power they think is ready to punish that evil; how eagerly they look for mediators they imagine can bring about this change; how they may finally arrive at the idea of a Kehama, who by prayers and sacrifice can bend the gods' will wholly to *his* will — the mythology of all nations proves this abundantly. Christian theology scatters such dark fancies by revealing the Highest Ruler as the All-good, showing the One on the throne as a Being like jasper and sardine stone to look upon; and by revealing the Lamb that was slain as the perfect sharer of His counsels, the perfect doer of His will, the perfect revealer of His designs to mankind, the perfect Redeemer of the world from the rule of the false, hateful, cruel gods men had imagined — gods that upheld all falsehood, hatred, and cruelty in earthly rulers — the perfect Atoner of man with the Father of Light, in whom is no variableness nor shadow of turning.

This, I think you will find, is the spirit of the song the prophet heard on his lonely island. "And when He had taken the book, the four beasts and four and twenty elders fell down before the Lamb, having every one of them harps, and golden vials full of odours, which are the prayers of saints. And they sung a new song, saying, Thou art worthy to take the book, and to open the seals thereof: for thou wast slain, and hast redeemed us to God by thy blood, out of every kindred, and tongue, and people, and nation; and hast made us unto our God kings and priests; and we shall reign on the earth." Here we have a grand confession — from the elders who represent the law of the world, that the Lamb is the author of that law and has perfectly fulfilled it, not set it aside or changed its working in the least; and from the living creatures who represent the divine nature, that the Lamb has perfectly shown it and has in no way given it a new feeling or direction. The bowls full of incense, which are the prayers of the saints, are the cries that have gone up from all the sorrowing and suffering people on earth, praying for deliverance from their oppressors and for their true King to appear. And the song is a thanksgiving that He has appeared; that He *has* consented to die alongside those who were under the sentence of death; that by His own blood, poured out for them, He *has* freed them from their bondage; has made them kings and priests to His Father; and *has* given them a pledge and assurance that the earth shall be possessed by the meek and not by the proud.

IX. And lest we should try to limit the One in the midst of the throne to the benefits He has given the earth — to the redemption He has worked out for the fallen and the sinful — lest this natural tendency, so widespread in Christendom, should spoil the very gratitude that seems its excuse, and should again divide the creatures the Lamb has united — the ear of the prophet's spirit is opened to take in another shout: "And I heard the voice of many angels round about the throne and the beasts and the elders; and the number of them was ten thousand times ten thousand, and thousands of thousands: saying with a loud voice, Worthy is the Lamb that was slain to receive power, and riches, and wisdom, and strength, and honour, and glory, and blessing." That which is the highest act of love — toward whatever persons it was shown, from whatever calamities it saved them — must be the highest showing of the divine character and will; and therefore it must be the cause of delight to all creatures, fallen or unfallen. If the Revelation is true, there can be no break in the sympathies of any part of God's free and intelligent universe. All must have one center, one object, one inspiration, so that each may do its own tasks freely and joyfully, and each may come to a higher knowledge of God's works and of God Himself. Nor these beings only. Free, intelligent beings are but the priests of the whole Creation. The whole of it must share in the redemption; all creation must be forever rising from under the law of death; there must be signs everywhere that the wings are unfolding inside the chrysalis; all must be reaching to enter into the glorious liberty of the sons of God. Not only he who has kept weary watch over man's mortality and hoped against hope amid the wrongs of nations, the sorrows of friends, and the sins of his own heart; but also the student of nature, who finds a wonderful order and beauty throughout the universe and yet is constantly tormented by the strangest sights of death and destruction — he too takes his part in the thanksgiving: "And every creature which is in heaven, and on the earth, and under the earth, and such as are in the sea, and all that are in them, heard I saying, Blessing, and honour, and glory, and power, be unto him that sitteth on the throne, and unto the Lamb for ever and ever. And the four beasts said, Amen. And the four and twenty elders fell down and worshipped him that liveth for ever and ever."

## LECTURE VI. THE SEALS OPENED.

*The passage:* The Lamb opens the seals one by one. At the first, one of the four living creatures says "Come and see," and a white horse appears, its rider carrying a bow and wearing a crown, going out conquering. At the second, a red horse comes, whose rider is given power to take peace from the earth so that men kill one another, and he is given a great sword. At the third, a black horse comes, whose rider holds a pair of scales, and a voice sets prices for wheat and barley and says not to harm the oil and wine. At the fourth, a pale horse comes, whose rider is named Death, with Hell following, given power over a quarter of the earth to kill by sword, hunger, death, and wild beasts. At the fifth, under the altar are the souls of those slain for God's word, crying out how long until God avenges their blood; white robes are given them and they are told to rest a little longer until the full number of their fellow servants is complete. At the sixth, there is a great earthquake; the sun turns black, the moon like blood, the stars fall, the sky rolls up like a scroll, and mountains and islands are moved. All people, high and low, hide in the rocks and beg the mountains to fall on them to hide them from the face of God on the throne and from the wrath of the Lamb, for the great day of his wrath has come, and who can stand?

Much has been said about these seals. Commentators have worked hard to determine which seal was opened in which age, which remain to be opened now or in the future. Since I find no times mentioned in the prophecy, I will speak of none and imagine none. It seems to me that if we follow the words exactly, they will tell us more of what we want to know than if we substitute our own words for them.

I. The Lamb has been revealed at the center of the throne, among the elders and the four living creatures. He is said to have the seven horns of power and the seven eyes of wisdom. As He opens the first seal, the prophet hears something like thunder, and one of the living creatures says, "Come and see." We must assume this divine creature is the Lion of the earlier vision. He represents the side of God's nature we usually set against the lamblike side. But that opposition is not the teaching of the Apocalypse. The One who sits on the throne is fully shown in the One who was slain. The lion's power belongs to him as much as the calf's weakness does. The question to settle is this: Is the root of power—the power that actually rules the world, the power everyone will one day acknowledge—self-will or self-sacrifice? Which of the two expresses God's will and being? Every other question is subordinate to this one.

II. "And I saw, and behold a white horse: and he that sat on him had a bow; and a crown was given unto him; and he went forth conquering and to conquer."

Here another symbol is introduced that will recur throughout this chapter. Man's mastery over the horse—as much for the Arab in the desert as for the Greek of an Ionian city—is the type of his mastery over all animals, the sign and pledge that he himself is of a higher origin. The hero is educated by the Centaur; his own defining title is horse-tamer. In the book of Zechariah—which I will refer to later in this sermon for another purpose, and which is in some ways more closely related to the Apocalypse than any other book of the Old Testament—there is a vision of a chariot with red horses, another with black, a third with white, and a fourth with dappled and bay horses. These are described as the four spirits of heaven, which go out from standing before the Lord of all the earth. The black horses go to the north country, the white after them, the dappled toward the south, and the bay seek to range to and fro through the earth.

Such language belonged, I think, especially to Zechariah's own time—the period after Judea had sunk into a mere part of the great empire, and was rising again to the dignity of a nation. Zechariah had been urging his countrymen to rise and rebuild the temple; he had been showing them that its ordinances, and Joshua its high priest and Zerubbabel its lawgiver, all bore witness that not outward might but God's Spirit is supreme. He had spoken of a form of government founded on the opposite principle, whose first home and full development were in the plain of Shinar. Then he turns to the vision of horses going out into different lands. He could no longer, I think, be content to imagine that only his own land was cared for and watched over by God. He felt more than ever that Judea had its own vocation, that keeping its distinctness was the highest of all duties, as it was the most divine of gifts. But the more he felt its pre-eminence, the more he had to see the other nations as also having their distinct callings and powers, however abused. They might not know the highest God, the source of all power—that was the blessing the Jew was to bring them. But he could only bless them so long as he saw every energy or faculty those nations exercised—even if they made it an excuse for self-exaltation or idolatry—as a divine energy, a "spirit of heaven going forth from the Lord of the whole earth."

So when the Lamb breaks the first seal, and the Lion calls the prophet to come and see what form appears, I think He gives him light on the nature and outcome of conquest—light he especially needed in his generation, as every man has needed it in his own. What did those astonishing conquests mean—of the Asiatic monarchs, of the Macedonians, and still more of the Romans, to whom everyone bowed down? Did they prove, as they seemed to, that a Lion God was reigning, that power came from him, and that all were to be trampled under these white horses' feet? No—it was not so. The power itself was not destructive, not even earthly, but divine. The Lamb was the Lord; the Lion was his servant. In time it would be shown for whom these conquests were won—not for the Asiatic, the Macedonian, or the Roman, not for self-willed arbitrary rule at all, but for Him who was slain and who lives.

III. The Lamb opened the second seal, and another vision came. "I heard the second beast say, Come and see. And there went out another horse that was red: and power was given to him that sat thereon to take peace from the earth, and that they should kill one another: and there was given unto him a great sword." An optimist would turn away from this vision, but a prophet must look steadily at it, for it is the vision of facts. This red horse of tumult, civil war, and mutual hatred does stand before men's eyes, does rise up in every period of the world's history. I need not describe how wildly that horse plunged in the days after Nero's death, before Vespasian was established. I also think there is meaning in which living creature summoned John to look. There is a lion-worship among men and a calf-worship among men. The first goes with admiration of mere conquering strength; the second with prostration, degradation, and moral slavery. Each is evil because it is a divided worship. The triumphant beast is revered by those who feel strong; the feeble beast is revered by those who feel weak. The strength turns to cruelty and tyranny; the weakness turns to meanness. The Lamb will raise men out of both. And this miserable side of humanity, like that splendid one, is a step in the revelation of Him as King of kings and Lord of lords.

IV. "And when he had opened the third seal, I heard the third beast say, Come and see. And I beheld, and lo a black horse; and he that sat on him had a pair of balances in his hand. And I heard a voice in the midst of the four beasts say, A measure of wheat for a penny, and three measures of barley for a penny; and see thou hurt not the oil and the wine."

The third living creature had the face of a man. Here we have no vision of war in either form—neither the grand form we think of with a Nebuchadnezzar, a Timour, a Napoleon, nor the wretched form of countries or families torn apart in some civil upheaval. The images here are all of peace. The sword is exchanged for the scales. Men are busy with buying and selling. They are careful of the oil and wine. So they suppose the qualities of the beast have all been set aside; they are humane and civilized. But alas for the manhood that belongs to this un-brutal state of society! The animal is gone—what has taken his place? The oil and the wine are more precious than the man who uses them; the price of the measure of wheat and barley matters more than the life and heart of those who sow and reap them. We are told John heard these earthly cries—these sounds of commerce—from the midst of the four living creatures. What can that mean? I think it clearly means this, brethren: we imagine a heavenly world that takes no interest in earth's doings, that stands entirely apart from it; but the heavenly world revealed to John enters into all the concerns of men, highest and lowest; it is not indifferent to the needs of the most insignificant creature who feeds or starves below, not indifferent to our sordid ways, but longs to lift us above scorning anything human, however low it looks to vulgar pride, and above revering anything inhuman, however splendid it looks to those same eyes.

V. As in the earlier cases the Lamb claimed the Lion's strength and the Calf's weakness as His own, so now He emphatically claims as His own the face of the Man and all that belongs to the nature He took. The black, red, and white horses are powers and energies from the Lord of all the earth. But can that be said of the next vision? "And when he had opened the fourth seal, I heard the voice of the fourth beast say, Come and see. And I looked and behold a pale horse, and his name that sat on him was Death, and Hell followed with him. And power was given unto them over the fourth part of the earth, to kill with sword, and with hunger, and with death, and with the beasts of the earth."

The living creature with the eagle's wings tells the prophet to look at this ghastly picture. It is one often seen on earth without any heavenly guide to interpret it: Famine and Pestilence traveling together, cutting down hosts of men; the violence of sufferers making their diseases worse; the moral recklessness that accompanies such times, which the great Greek historian describes so wonderfully in his account of the Athenian plague, and which everyone who has recorded such calamities since has noticed. Here, surely, is the pale horse with Death for its rider and Hell following after. That this should be the last of the sights the four living creatures can show—that their work is finished when they have brought us to the mouth of this yawning abyss—might at first seem appalling. But pause a little, and light will dawn on you. This is the downward course of mere animal power. Its various aspects have been shown to us, each standing out clearly and separately; now it is exhausted, having done all it can do. If there is no power but this animal power—even adding to it the power of calculation, of reckoning how much wheat a penny buys, of providing for oil and wine, which we regard as peculiarly human—if this is *the* power of the universe, then we have reached the climax of its history. Then the pale horse and its rider are the last thing we shall look upon. Death and Hell have won. Let us weigh that result well; let us ask ourselves honestly whether the mere facts of earth, supposing all glimpses of any other kind of power be shut out, are not tending toward this end.

VI. I say, brethren, another *kind* of power; for merely to imagine *this* power raised to omnipotence does not remove the confusion but makes it hopeless. Do not suppose for a moment that the Apocalypse is repeating the old story of a contest between the Giants of earth and a Giant of Heaven to see which will prevail. Still further from us be the notion that it describes a struggle between a Prometheus who would seize some of the divine wisdom for man's use and a jealous Jove determined to rule men without sharing his counsels with them. No—understand it well. It is precisely with *this* form of omnipotence, with *this* idea of a jealous will and all the religious and moral horrors that flow from it, that the Lamb is at war. As long as mere power—physical or intellectual—is what men most covet for themselves, they will attribute the same covetousness to the beings they worship; they will find themselves hemmed in by cruel conditions and restraints binding the might that works restlessly and confusedly within them; and in trying to break through these barriers they will destroy the very freedom they want and create the very tyranny they protest against. And so long, all the world's legends and histories will only echo the same cry. There is a pale horse with Death for its rider who closes every vision of good and puts out the light of every sun.

Yes, and there is a darker form behind. You think Hell is an invention of priests. Alas, priests have been shamefully guilty in preaching Hell rather than deliverance from Hell; so far as they have done that, they have violated their commission and borne witness for anyone but the Lamb who conquers death and hell. But Hell is not the priests' invention. It haunts and torments every man. He may picture it under one form or another; and so far as he gives it any form at all, he takes away some of the blank horror surrounding the thought of an impenetrable, solitary void as the home of a living, conscious, thinking creature. Until a man throws off the coil of self, he cannot throw off this coil. The more he wraps himself in self, the more impossible it is for him not to have this pale death-vision before him, and another following it that will swallow up himself, his family, and the universe.

VII. But though the four living creatures have finished *their* task once these four seals are opened, there is another seal for the Lamb to open, and the vision it discloses is wholly unlike the others. "And when he had opened the fifth seal, I saw under the altar the souls of them that were slain for the word of God, and for the testimony which they held. And they cried with a loud voice, saying, How long, O Lord, holy and true, dost thou not judge and avenge our blood on them that dwell on the earth? And white robes were given unto every one of them, and it was said unto them, that they should rest yet for a little season, until their fellowservants also and their brethren, that should be killed as they were, should be fulfilled."

By confining these seals to some particular period in the later history of Christendom, some have been able to limit this description to a single school or sect of Christians who are supposed (and I doubt not did) to have witnessed against the evils around them. But I dare not confine these words even to the Christian martyrs of any age. I understand the vision the Apostle saw to be of all who anywhere—before his time, in his own, or in later times—have believed that there is a power other than self-willed tyrannical power, other than what men worshipped in the lion, the calf, the human intellect, the flying eagle—a power other than death and hell; who, in every anguish and despair from within and without, have held fast their faith and cried to Him who is holy and true to show that He is mightier than those who counterfeit His majesty and abuse it for unholy and untrue ends. From these in every corner of the earth has risen that groan, deep or loud, for deliverance—which He who sits on the throne has understood, even if no one else did, and which has been in His ears a hymn and a confession of the Lamb as the one true Lord and King of the earth. The cry came from beneath the altar. It was inspired by the spirit of sacrifice. It was the expression of a sacrifice of body, soul, and spirit to Him who had shared His own mind with them. The sacrifice was completed when the self-willed tyrants they protested against gave them to the axe or the fire, or made them drink the poison cup. But their cry did not end when they fell by the axe, the fire, or the hemlock. Then it first became distinct and intelligible; then it first joined in clear, full harmony with everything that had ascended from those who had fallen in other lands and other times; then they themselves fully knew that they had not been pleading for their own redemption but for the redemption of the earth from its tormentors. And they were given what they most longed for—the white robe of innocence, purity from all the passion and selfishness that had been mixed into their witness for God and their prayers for men. And they were assured that if they waited, the answer to their prayer would come.

VIII. Here is part of the answer: "And I beheld when he had opened the sixth seal, and, lo, there was a great earthquake, and the sun became black as sackcloth of hair, and the moon became as blood; and the stars of heaven fell unto the earth, even as a fig-tree casteth her untimely figs, when she is shaken of a mighty wind. And the heaven departed as a scroll when it is rolled together; and every mountain and island were moved out of their places. And the kings of the earth, and the great men, and the rich men, and the chief captains, and the mighty men, and every bondsman, and every free man, hid themselves in the dens and in the rocks of the mountains; and said to the mountains and rocks, Fall on us, and hide us from the face of him that sitteth on the throne, and from the wrath of the Lamb. For the great day of his wrath is come, and who shall be able to stand?"

In this grand description most have recognized the signs of a moral and political earthquake, more terrible than any physical earthquake ever was—though it has happened, and will happen again, that physical convulsions keep time with those that shatter nations and fill men's hearts with dismay and anguish. Since the prophet names no time, I will introduce none. The description may fit many periods. It may have been fulfilled for many in the days of Alaric and Attila, of Genghis Khan or Timour, when Constantinople fell, when Napoleon laid the nations low. Do you think it must point to some still more distant day? Have you dreamed it must refer to a destruction of the earth? We shall see whether the Apocalypse leads us to expect any such destruction at any time, or whether instead it tells us of a redemption of the earth, such as the saints beneath the altar prayed for. I believe that if you study this earthquake's description carefully, you will rather be led to think of an even *earlier* fulfillment than those I have mentioned—one that helps us better understand what the others meant. We are told here of the shaking and downfall of *heavenly* powers. What can these powers be? Are they not the demons of the old mythology? Were they not trembling, and indeed falling, at the very time John was on Patmos, before the preaching of a few fishermen and a tent-maker? It was not earth alone that was moved, but heaven too. I do not regard the revolution of the first century as an exception in the history of revolutions in this respect, but as a precedent for them. There has never been a revolution that did not test men's faith and worship as well as their policy and government; never one that did not prove how inseparably these are connected. And I believe there never has been one that did not demonstrate the falsehood of self-will, or arbitrary will, and the truth that all power is centered in Him who gave up all power and took the form of a servant.

There are many excellent men today who will fully agree with me that the Apocalypse points to the deliverance of the earth, not its extinction. But they will say that the great men and captains who called the mountains to fall on them and the hills to hide them were fleeing from the wrath of the Lamb. So the prophet must be describing a manifestation of the Lamb—a manifestation of Him as the actual Lord of the earth. Certainly He is: nothing in human language is more evident. And there is no lesson, it seems to me, so important—none the Apocalypse was so much written to teach us as this: that all false and tyrannical power is a rebellion against the Lamb; that no false and tyrannical power has ever been put down except by a manifestation of the Lamb. May the Spirit of Him who is faithful and true root and ground us in the conviction that the world never has been and never can be governed by oppression and lies; that these have at no time upheld its order but have subverted it; that whether they are enthroned by false religions in heavenly places or seated by usurpation in the high places of earth, they are cursed and doomed; that the very conquests they helped bring about—the civil tumults, mercantile frauds, plagues and famines they promoted—have been their appointed punishments; that the cries of the weak and suffering have in the end proved stronger than they; that the revolutions most terrifying while they lasted, from the crimes that provoked them and the crimes they provoked, from the clash of human evil in its opposite forms, have been the instruments for putting down counterfeit powers and asserting the power that is and must endure; that all righteous men and righteous institutions have been proclaiming who was, is, and shall be the ruler over men; that the full revelation of the Christ who is to come will confirm all the true human authority and all the true human liberty that has ever existed; that He will prove the first to have come from Him, and will claim the second for our race as the reward of His agony and death.

## LECTURE VII. THE SEALING OF THE TRIBES.

*The passage:* John saw four angels at the four corners of the earth, holding back the four winds so no wind would blow on the land, the sea, or the trees. Another angel rose from the east, carrying the seal of the living God, and called out to those four angels not to harm the earth, sea, or trees until God's servants were sealed on their foreheads. He heard the number sealed: 144,000 from all the tribes of Israel—12,000 each from Judah, Reuben, Gad, Aser, Nepthalim, Manasses, Simeon, Levi, Issachar, Zabulon, Joseph, and Benjamin. After this he saw an uncountable multitude from every nation, people, and tongue, standing before the throne and the Lamb, dressed in white robes with palms in their hands, crying that salvation belongs to God on the throne and to the Lamb. All the angels fell on their faces and worshipped God, praising him with blessing, glory, wisdom, thanksgiving, honour, power, and might for ever. One of the elders asked John who these people in white robes were and where they came from; John said the elder knew. The elder answered that they had come out of great tribulation and had washed their robes white in the blood of the Lamb; that they are before God's throne serving him day and night, that God will dwell among them, that they will hunger and thirst no more, that the Lamb will feed them and lead them to living fountains, and that God will wipe away all their tears.

Part of this passage is read as the Epistle on All Saints' Day, and I have spoken of it before in connection with that day. I know of no line-by-line commentary that shows its meaning better. But now I will consider it in relation to the whole subject and purpose of the Apocalypse. The words "after these things" recall the vision we looked at last Sunday. Each of the four seals, as it was broken, revealed some form of *power*—power splendid and victorious; power feeding on itself; power carefully guarding mere possessions and calculating how to increase them; power destroying both things and men. The fifth seal revealed the victims of power crying out for justice, asking when it would be shown that power is the servant and creature of right. When the next seal was opened, the prophet felt the earth shaking, kingdoms falling into ruin, and the great men of the earth begging the hills and mountains to hide them from the righteous Avenger, from the wrath of the One on the throne and of the Lamb.

Yes, from the wrath of the Lamb. For in the Apocalypse he is not shown as stepping in to stop the decrees of righteousness, but as carrying them out and fulfilling them. There is a feeble and utterly confusing notion—cruel as well as feeble, immoral in practice as well as inconsistent in theory—that has crept into all the mythologies and been carried over from them into Christian teaching: the idea of a human benefactor whose mind differs from the mind of the One on the throne, who tries to shield men from the results of God's righteous judgments. That idea is completely opposed to the teaching of St. John the theologian. The Lamb helps the creature *precisely because* he does the Creator's will—because he fights with his weapons against all the usurpers who show they are rebels against God by trampling on man.

Is this upheaval, then, that is making earth and heaven shake—the thrones of the rulers who reign on earth, and the thrones of the gods they worship as the props of their rule in heaven—is it really, as the great and rich men and captains think, the beginning of universal anarchy? The one who believed the Lamb was opening the seals, who had heard the cry from under the altar, knew it could not be so. There *would* be universal anarchy if these kings and great men and captains, these gods on earth and in heaven, were the only supporters of order. But if they were themselves the sources of anarchy—if their disobedience to eternal laws had produced this earthquake and made it inevitable—then those very laws would be asserted and brought to light by the events that made the unfaithful rebels grow pale and hide.

I. The next revelation is of a divine order in the midst of this disorder: "And after these things I saw four angels standing on the four corners of the earth, holding the four winds of the earth, that the wind should not blow on the earth, nor on the sea, nor on any tree." "How foolish!" cries the shallow pretender who wants to be thought scientific; "four angels holding four winds—what ignorance of the laws of Nature!" But this criticism, far from being scientific, is merely the voice of a common feeling that true science would teach us to distrust. How does it interfere with any law that governs the winds—whether we know that law or not—if that law is administered by spiritual powers, living persons? If we insist on tying the acts of such powers to caprice, if we assume it cannot be their special nature to obey and to uphold obedience, we are simply assuming the very thing in question. That assumption is exactly the opposite of the Bible's assumption. In Scripture the righteous angel is precisely the one who carries out the commands of the perfectly righteous and orderly will, listening to the voice of his words. Suppose there is no such will; suppose laws exist without a lawgiver—then indeed all subordinate agents become incredible and impossible. And then the man asks, What governs *me*? And because he finds in himself a will that cannot bend to mere laws and yet must bend to something, he stoops to some tyrannical will. This is the weary circle round which those drive us who despise the old language and have found no new one to replace it. Glorifying laws, they lead us to despise and overthrow law. Aiming at scientific calm, they take away the very thing that protects us from endless restlessness.

What calm it must have given the Apostle's mind, what freedom from restlessness, what a sense of law, that in the very midst of a revolutionary whirl he could see the angels holding the four winds so that nothing would be touched until a purpose affecting the destiny of man was fulfilled. The sea, the earth, and the air are not our masters; we are not the playthings of their whims. They are under the government of the One who made man in his image and redeemed man to be his child. This is the Scriptural philosophy. What other philosophy can you find that equally lifts man above the fear of the elements and all the superstitions that go with that fear, that so bravely equips him to investigate the laws and conditions of a world created to be his home—created by his Father?

II. "And I saw another angel ascending from the east, having the seal of the living God: and he cried with a loud voice to the four angels, to whom it was given to hurt the earth and the sea, saying, Hurt not the earth, neither the sea, nor the trees, till we have sealed the servants of our God in their foreheads."

The distinction between the angel whose task is to seal the foreheads of men and those whose task is to harm the earth and sea is very significant. The latter cannot act without the former; both serve the punishment and education of the free creature. *He* alone can receive the seal of the living God. Every student of the Bible will recognize here principles that work themselves out in every part of its history. They will also recognize in the name "the living God" the characteristic name of the Old Testament. We read that word *living* as if it meant almost nothing; in fact it means the very highest and most practical truth that human language can express.

III. What men are to have this seal set on them? They are called *the servants of God*. And when we ask what that means, we are told that 144,000 were sealed *of all the tribes of the children of Israel*. That general statement is not enough: the Apostle goes on to list the 12,000 of Judah, of Reuben, of Gad, of Aser, of Nepthalim, of Manasses, of Simeon, of Levi, of Issachar, of Zabulon, of Joseph, of Benjamin. We are naturally puzzled by this detailed repetition. Why is it needed? Why—and this surely is the first question, on which the answer to the other depends—why should *Israel* be involved in this vision at all? We might expect that some favored souls would be saved in the coming storm. We might expect the prophet to encourage the Churches of Asia, or elsewhere, with the thought that they or some of their members would be worthy to survive the day of trouble, or be brought through it, or be richly rewarded if taken out of it. But surely Israel had forfeited any claim to special favor. The coming calamity might reach far beyond Jerusalem, but the shock would be most violent there; that city would be swallowed by the earthquake. Our Lord had prepared his Apostles to expect it. Whatever others might suffer, the Jews were surely destined to endure horrors they had never known—horrors no nation had ever known before.

IV. It might be said that, for this very reason, faithful Jews had all the more need to be assured they would be watched over. Perhaps; but if "faithful Jews" means those who had given up their national privileges and merged into the general body of the Church, why are they carefully identified by their national marks? Why are the tribes listed? Why is the same number said to be chosen from each? I take it, my friends, that everything here points to something other than an escape that certain individuals might hope for out of the coming trouble, if they seized the name of Christians and were willing to drop the name of Israelites. Israel—not a few picked men out of it, but the whole nation, the twelve tribes—had been called out to bear witness to a living God. The surrounding nations had bowed to dead gods—gods, the Psalmist keeps saying, that cannot see, or speak, or govern.
