To testify of One who sees the acts and thoughts of men, who communes with their hearts and spirits, who is an actual King ordering their steps and guiding their way, was the task of the family of Abraham, of the people brought out of Egypt, of those over whom David reigned and to whom the prophets spoke. Let me say it again: this work was done by them as a *nation*, as an organic body. Once they lost that organic character, they could no longer do it. As separate individuals they might hold a *religion*, might keep opinions about God different from other men's; but they could not bear witness to One who was upholding their unity, who would not let them be lost in the surrounding world. And so, as I have often pointed out, the holy men and prophets of the Jews, even when their own countrymen persecuted them most, even when they had the bitterest grievances against them, never claimed some private virtue of their own as God's witnesses. They simply asserted the standing of Israelites; they rebuked their fellows for abandoning that standing; their worst dread was that the nation, through its members' indifference, might be cut off; their belief and hope was that, into whatever apparent decline it might sink, its life was immortal, and the living God would himself sustain it. Through all disasters and captivities, in the darkest hours, that faith went with them and carried them straight forward.

I cannot convince myself that St. John's mind was any less filled and penetrated by this truth than the minds of Isaiah, Jeremiah, or Ezekiel; or that anything had happened to make him accept more contentedly than they did the prospect that the nation would be a nation no longer. And yet everything seemed to say this dreadful doom was near. It seemed inevitable that the nation would lose not merely its independence—that was already gone—but its very substance, everything that had marked it as a body called by God to a special service for mankind.

V. Perhaps you will grant that this was an enormous problem for a Jew of the first century to face. But I wish you could see what an enormous problem it is for students of history in this century. What do we mean by the records of the Old World? Surely not the scattered notices we can gather of Babylonian kings, or the names and dates of Egyptian dynasties, interesting as those are. We mean the records of two or three distinct nations: Judæa, Greece, Rome. Everything vital in the ancient world is gathered up in the story and destiny of those nations. Our fathers knew this; with all our added learning, we know it too. Now, Greece as a nation had already sunk; Rome as a nation had died into the empire; Jerusalem was the last remnant of national existence left anywhere in the whole world. It was only a remnant, a token of what had been—but it was that, and while it lasted it was a sign that not everything was imperial.

If this is so, we may be no less interested—we may be even more deeply interested—than St. John himself in that heavenly vision, which told him that the nation, though already shorn of most of its locks, though the last lock might be cut off and it might be doomed for ages to labor in the mill, blind and alone, still had an imperishable life; that those of the nation who confessed a living God and believed he had actually revealed himself in his Son represented its continuance; that, coming from the various tribes, they were witnesses to the enduring, organic reality of those tribes. Their brethren who clung to different sects and schools, who called themselves Pharisees or Sadducees, might be the numerical majority. But they refused to acknowledge the King of the nation. For all practical purposes they refused to *be* a nation. They would become what they had in effect declared themselves to be: a set of units with no principle holding them together—solemn witnesses, in their disorganization, to other lands that only a living God can bind men into one, but very feeble witnesses that a nation once born is never lost, that the gifts and calling of God are without repentance. *That* testimony could be fully borne only by those who held fast to the faith that the Son of David was truly the Son of God, and that David's kingdom would never end while that Son lived and reigned.

VI. In this way I believe the vision of the exile on Patmos confirms the hopes that earnest men—Jews or Christians—have held of the final restoration of the Israelites to more than all the privileges their fathers enjoyed, and refutes the opinion of those who say that Jews are not meant to be different from other people, but, once they confess Christ, must be absorbed into our Christendom. I find it most satisfying to take the language of the prophets even more strictly than those teachers do, and to believe they were not deceived when they said that the hills were less fixed and everlasting than their divine polity. But if we read them thus far literally, we must go further. Last week I hinted that the prophets generally, and those after the return from captivity especially, could not help seeing every nation as having its own function, gifts, and destiny. Whatever was distinct was clearly meant by the living God to be distinct; whatever had done a great work was marked out by God for that work. So, if the Jewish nation has had a permanence in the mind of him who was, and is, and is to come; and if those of the race—wherever and whoever they have been—who have believed in a living God have asserted that permanence; then we may also hold that in his eyes there has been a permanence in the Greek nation through all its ages of humiliation under Christian or Mahometan oppressors; a permanence in the Italian nation amid all its subjection to Papal or Germanic tyrants; a permanence one day to be fully asserted and realized by each. And if we dare to ask, in the case of any of these—or of the Jewish nation—why that full assertion of its life has not come sooner, why what *has* come in each case has so far been so incomplete, the vision we are speaking of gives the answer, and it is one we ourselves need to take to heart as much as any people anywhere.

Look at the history of great national struggles among peoples utterly unlike one another in habits, race, character, and religious profession. Look at the Swiss in the fourteenth century, the Dutch in the sixteenth, the Tyrolese in our own day; see whether the seal of a living God has not been clearly stamped on them; see whether their awakening to conscious national life has not been bound up with a faith in One who cared that men should be alive and free—not a distant Being to be sought out and appeased, but One who was himself seeking his servants, rekindling the dying embers in them, stirring them to repentance and to hope. Consider seriously whether the belief that men form a nation which lives on from age to age, which is affected by the sins and by the righteous deeds of its fathers, which has an inheritance worth striving and dying for, does not imply belief in a living God, and can ever be long divorced from the open confession of a living God. And on the other side, consider whether a people—however great its religious profession, however loud its talk of the immortal individual soul—can really worship a living God while it regards itself nationally as merely a great machine for producing and exchanging goods, or merely as a collection of sects and parties. To talk of nationalities, as newspaper writers or politicians do, is easy; to be a nation—to feel that the word is a reality and not a fiction—is hard; whoever would cultivate that feeling must win it through endurance and sacrifice, must learn to weigh what is substantial against what is accidental, must discover how impotent many fine philosophical theories are. We hold this glorious privilege by inheritance from those who bled for it; if we lose it, we lose all our power over those we govern and all our honesty toward one another. We may lose it; other lands we have spoken of scornfully may earn it as our fathers earned it. If such a calamity is in store for us, and such a blessing for them, it will be because they have become less atheistic than we are, because they are discovering beneath all their superstitions that there is a living God working for them, in whom they may trust—the God our own superstitions are hiding from us.

Another vision followed the one of the sealing of the tribes.

VII. "After this I beheld, and lo, a great multitude, which no man could number, of all nations, and kindreds, and people, and tongues, stood before the throne and before the Lamb, clothed with white robes and palms in their hands; and cried with a loud voice, Salvation to our God which sitteth upon the throne, and unto the Lamb."

The order of these revelations is, I believe, the order of God's own history, and the order in which its truths present themselves to our consciences. The Apostle is thoroughly possessed by the sacredness of the nation; he is sure it endures, that it bears the seal of the living God. And so he can rise to the perception of this society gathered out of all kindreds, nations, and tongues. So he can contemplate a universality and unity that are the exact opposite of imperial universality and unity. So he can see that the family of God is not divided by place or time, nor by the Fall or by death. This Church belongs not to past, present, or future, but is like the One who has gathered it, the same yesterday, today, and forever. It lacks no earthly ties; it is bounded by none. Angels mingle with these citizens of different nations and tribes; their bond is a common worship.

VIII. "And all the angels stood round about the throne, and about the elders and the four beasts, and fell before the throne on their faces, and worshipped God, saying, Amen: Blessing, and glory, and wisdom, and thanksgiving, and honour, and might, be unto our God for ever and ever. Amen."

These words express contentment in the perfectly good will of God; joy that his goodness is joined to perfect wisdom; gratitude that to goodness and wisdom belongs perfect power. This is the thought that gives the angels strength; this is the inspiration of their work. And then follows that memorable passage:

IX. "And one of the elders answered, saying unto me, What are these which are arrayed in white robes? and whence came they? And I said unto him, Sir, thou knowest. And he said to me, These are they which came out of great tribulation, and have washed their robes and made them white in the blood of the Lamb. Therefore are they before the throne of God, serving him day and night in his temple: and he that sitteth on the throne shall dwell among them. They shall hunger no more, neither thirst any more, neither shall the sun light on them, nor any heat. For the Lamb which is in the midst of the throne shall feed them, and lead them unto living fountains of waters: and God shall wipe away all tears from their eyes."

We complain that even these words have grown so familiar to us that they have lost much of their power. No, my friends; it is not familiarity that drains them of power. They are not familiar enough; the picture has been too much a mere fancy in our eyes. We have thought of it as belonging to some distant region wholly cut off from this earth. Let us rise to it as St. John rose to it. Let us think first of our own nation, sealed and set apart by the living God as a witness for him. Let us think of the brave and true men who in past days bore that witness, who did not count their lives dear, so that we might know him better and their land might testify of him and be a richer inheritance for their sons. Let us think of those we have seen struggling and suffering among us, fainting yet victorious, caring above all to show forth the name of Him who is love and to bear his likeness. Let us remember the old men who spoke wise counsels and prophecies to us, the young men who accomplished so little of what they dreamed, the children who left behind them memories of what they might have become. Then reflect: as the nation is immortal, so must those who have lived in it be immortal. These old men, these young men, these children must know us better than they did on earth, because they know God better; must help us better, because they more fully carry out his commands. Then their number will keep growing; those of other lands we have read and heard of will be mingled with them. We shall lose all count of their multitude, yet no single Church, or nation, or person will be lost in it. Each will shine, all will shine, in the light of his tender love—of him who gave his only-begotten Son to take our flesh and to die. Above all, beneath all, for all, will rise the Good Friday prayer: "We beseech thee, Almighty God, graciously to behold this thy family, for which our Lord Jesus Christ was contented to be betrayed and given up into the hands of wicked men, and to suffer death upon the cross, who now liveth and reigneth with thee and the Holy Ghost, ever one God, world without end."

## LECTURE VIII. THE SEVEN TRUMPETS.

*The passage:* When the Lamb opened the seventh seal, heaven fell silent for about half an hour. John saw seven angels standing before God, and each was given a trumpet. Another angel came with a golden censer and much incense, which he offered along with the prayers of all the saints on the golden altar before the throne, and the smoke rose up before God. Then the angel filled the censer with fire from the altar and threw it onto the earth, producing voices, thunder, lightning, and an earthquake. The seven angels prepared to sound. At the first trumpet came hail and fire mixed with blood, cast on the earth, burning a third of the trees and all the green grass. At the second, a burning mountain was thrown into the sea, turning a third of it to blood, killing a third of the sea creatures, and destroying a third of the ships. At the third, a great burning star named Wormwood fell on a third of the rivers and springs, making them bitter, so that many died. At the fourth, a third of the sun, moon, and stars was darkened, and both day and night lost a third of their light. Then an angel flew through mid-heaven crying, "Woe, woe, woe to those who live on the earth, because of the trumpet blasts of the three angels still to sound."

There was an earthquake when the last seal was broken. At that point self-willed power, having passed through its various stages and exhausted itself, was reaching its natural end. There would be a judgment. The cycle might close and a new one begin. But now a doom awaits not the world's city, but God's city — the city that had been set up as a witness to the world *against* its idolatries and *for* the rule of the righteous King. Its downfall seems minor in the eyes of the world's rulers (though not entirely minor even to them), yet to the prophet it is nothing less than the end of an age or dispensation. It is the greatest catastrophe that has yet come upon the universe. Heaven falls silent as it contemplates it.

I. "And I saw the seven angels that stood before God, and to them were given seven trumpets."

Nowhere in the Apocalypse are the references to the Old Testament as numerous as here. To understand these trumpets you must turn to the sixth chapter of Joshua: Jericho was shut up tight because of the Israelites, so that no one went in or out. The Lord told Joshua that He had given Jericho, its king, and its warriors into his hand. The men of war were to march around the city once a day for six days, with seven priests carrying seven rams'-horn trumpets before the ark. On the seventh day they were to march around seven times while the priests blew the trumpets; and when they made a long blast and the people heard it, all the people were to shout, and the walls of the city would collapse flat, and everyone would climb straight up into it.

The story preserved in that sacred book, telling how Canaan was conquered by the small band of former slaves who had come out of Egypt, must have taken a strong hold on the imagination of every Jewish child. A strange sense of an invisible, mysterious power capable of overcoming walls and armed men would have blended with a feeling that their forefathers were righteous men, Heaven's favourites, punishing the wicked and accursed people of Canaan. Rabbinical schooling did little to separate what was right from what was wrong in these early impressions. It hardened the wonder at those ancient events into superstition, and the pride in the glories of the one nation into contempt for other nations. The Jew who broke away from that education — who talked and traded with foreigners, who found himself outwardly inferior to them and inwardly no better, who suspected the same was true of those Joshua led, who came to respect ordinary discipline, material strength, and the resources of an empire, and who learned that the heathen claimed even more miraculous interventions for their ancestors than he did for his — would be likely to distrust in his heart, if not openly scorn, the lessons he had inherited. Living either in that unbelief, or in a violent effort to suppress it and to crush anyone who showed signs of it, was probably the condition of most Jews in St. John's day. But there were people, before and since, who became more truly like little children than these had ever been — precisely because they had put away the merely childish fancies and risen to the thought and reflection of grown men. In the account of Jericho's fall they had found the witness of a God who was no more the God of their fathers than of themselves; a God who, by different methods suited to different periods, was always asserting that material power is subject to spiritual power; who was always dooming to destruction cities built on a false foundation, however strong they looked; who had chosen a poor Syrian tribe — not, as their own lawgiver and prophet kept insisting, for its righteousness, but as a witness of His righteousness — to put down races that defied His laws and defiled the earth with their crimes. And surely, if that were so, He would not treat that chosen tribe on any different principles from those He had applied to the tribes of Canaan.

There were such Israelites. St. John was one of them. And no one knew better than he how much his countrymen had become worshippers of material force and disbelievers in spiritual power. Though he lived away from the holy city, he knew it had become an accursed city, given over to strife and hatred, probably to as much fleshly wickedness as had brought judgment on the Canaanites, and certainly to a spiritual wickedness the Canaanites had never known. Yet he needed a special revelation to convince him that the same sentence carried out on Jericho was now to be carried out on Jerusalem — that the trumpets were sounding around *her*, and that they were blown not by mortal priests but by the angels who stand before God.

This, I take it, is the simple interpretation of the vision, and the one that connects it most naturally with what comes just before and just after. Holding that view, I cannot accept any of the more ingenious explanations that move the trumpets to a later period, or to a series of later periods — even if I thought they could be applied that way more directly to Christian purposes. But I do not think so. Let us hear what these trumpets said to the Israelites; then we shall be able to listen, with cleansed and open ears, to any trumpets that may be sounding warnings to us.

II. "And another angel came and stood at the altar, having a golden censer; and there was given unto him much incense, that he should offer it with the prayers of all saints upon the golden altar which was before the throne. And the smoke of the incense which came with the prayers of the saints ascended up before God out of the angel's hand. And the angel took the censer, and filled it with fire of the altar, and cast it on the earth; and there were voices, and thunderings and lightnings, and an earthquake."

If the earlier words recalled the priests' ministry at Jericho, these recall the priests' ministry in the tabernacle. That ministry, later transferred to the temple, was ongoing. Jews did not just read about it in a book; they saw it with their own eyes. The altar, the sacrifice, the incense, and the daily prayers were bound up with the earliest thoughts of those who lived in Jerusalem, and were probably even more impressive to those who only visited at the great festivals. Like the historical record, these worship-acts must have affected the frequent or occasional onlooker in two ways. The acts of worship spoke of a communion between the visible and the unseen world: God was meeting men at the mercy-seat, and the priest was the continual witness of that communion. But it was the worship of *Jews*. God had spoken to *their* forefathers. *They* had been told what He is and how to approach Him. What an amazing gift! What nation ever received such a gift! The growing boy was formally taught what the child had learned by heart. For him the awe of the service turned into a kind of terror, and what he lacked in genuine sympathy was made up by appeals to his awakening pride: "The heathen know nothing of the right way of serving God; *their* priests are all wrong in the sacrifices they offer; their sacrifices will never be accepted." Then, in countless cases, must have come the mature Israelite's reaction against this indoctrination — or a cold inner indifference to it: "Why is one sacrifice better than another? Why shouldn't their priests be as wise as ours? Why might not both be deceivers alike? How do we know any dealing with beings above us is even possible? How can we tell they are the least bit moved by our offerings? How can we tell such beings exist at all?" And here, too, there was a path leading through the struggles of adulthood back to a truly childlike faith: "My prayers are not only worthless; they are often impossible. I cannot pray. But I want to pray. An irresistible impulse drives me to try. And my sacrifices — what are they? Worse than worthless. They seem wicked. I have no right to offer them. My mind is wrong, and I am trying to bend God's mind to it. And yet — ought I not to make sacrifices? Ought I not to *be* a sacrifice? What if God Himself is moving me to pray, not against His will, but so that His will may be done? What if He is stirring me to sacrifice, not so that I may gain something, but so that I may be freed from the greatest of all burdens — the burden of my own selfishness? What if sacrifice is a surrender? What if He, and not I, is finally the author of that surrender? What if this was the meaning of my nation's worship? What if this is why He ordained the sacrifice, why He made the atonement, why we can only come with our dead offering, confessing our sins? What if the knowledge of this truth is the great blessing given to Abraham's children, the blessing with which we are to bless all the families of the earth?"

Those who had been led this far by an invisible teacher were ready for the mighty revelation of the sacrifice of the Lord of man and the Son of God — the revelation of Him saying, "Lo, I come," not to change your will, but "to do thy will, O God." But another revelation followed from this: the revelation of a Judge. The prayers and sacrifices in the Jerusalem temple had not been in vain. No incense that ever rose to heaven from a human heart had been in vain. The High Priest within the veil had presented every cry and groan to His Father. And what had those cries and groans asked for? That God would *not* judge the earth? That He would leave it to wallow on in crime and misery? Such prayers may have been offered by people kneeling at Jewish or heathen altars, but they were prayers for stones instead of bread — drink-offerings of blood, of bloody men, of which the High Priest says, "I will *not* offer them." The prayers for right and justice did not stop the thunder, lightning, and earthquake; thunder and earthquake were now the answer to them.

When we read our Lord's conversation with His disciples about the last days, we are often tempted to twist His words away from their plain meaning and say they could not refer to a judgment on *that* age and *that* city — even though He so solemnly says they did. Our inclination to trifle even with that divine language is not without excuse. If we suppose Jerusalem had no relation to the rest of humanity — neither to the nations it had been set apart from, nor to the nations that would be born after it had passed away — then we cannot see how words of such sweeping scope, pointing to distress and confusion everywhere, could be fulfilled by the fall of a capital that had lost all its ancient glory and was now merely the home of fierce rebel factions. But if that city had been the divine centre of the old world; if *there* stood the witness to the Name, the Kingdom, and the Will by which the world was governed; then *there* too must lie the key to the world's destinies. The meaning of the earthquake, and its relation to past and future, remains incomprehensible until we grasp what it signified for the city where David reigned and the Son of David was crucified.

III. "And the seven angels which had the seven trumpets prepared themselves to sound."

Everything about this points to the greatness and importance of their task. It is not a Canaanite city that is to fall flat, but the very city that testified that the gods of the Canaanites were not the gods ruling the earth — the city that testified of a King of righteousness and peace.

IV. "The first angel sounded, and there followed hail and fire, mingled with blood, and they were cast upon the earth; and the third part of the trees was burnt up, and all green grass was burnt up."

The book of Joshua gave us hints about the purpose of the trumpets; the book of Leviticus explained the part of the vision dealing with the sacrifices and incense.

Here we are carried back to the plagues of Egypt, which we were reading about before Easter. This passage in Exodus comes at once to the mind of anyone reading the Apocalypse: the Lord told Moses to stretch out his hand toward heaven so there would be hail throughout all Egypt, on man and beast and every plant of the field; and there was hail, with fire mixed in it, over all the land, striking man and beast, every herb of the field, and breaking every tree.

This language, like what we considered earlier, had been put to a fatal use by Jewish self-importance. Pharaoh and the magicians, they told themselves, had been humbled for the sake of *their* forefathers. *Their* lawgiver could do rarer miracles than the so-called wise men of Egypt. The laws of nature had been suspended so that *their* race could be delivered. This passage fed that self-satisfaction: Egypt suffered from the hail and fire, but Goshen was spared. And so the great lesson these plagues carried to the world was entirely missed. It was not the I AM, the Eternal Lawgiver who had revealed Himself to His servant Moses, who was speaking and acting in them, but — as they took it — a capricious being, essentially no different from the gods the magicians honoured. Moses had not claimed the powers of nature that enslaved the Egyptians as the servants of a God mightier than the tyrant, a God who cared to deliver the outcast. He had not taught the Egyptians what such plagues, whenever they occurred, testified about *their* own government. The whole thing was reduced to a mere contest of strength between a Jewish prophet and the Egyptian magi, with both alike setting natural laws aside. Now the old record is restored to its true force. The angel's trumpet proclaims that the plague of hail and fire is a message to Goshen even more than to Egypt. Jerusalem is to hear in that trumpet a sure declaration that the just Judge and Saviour is now judging Israel, so that the world may be saved.

V. "And the second angel sounded, and as it were a great mountain burning with fire was cast into the sea: and the third part of the sea became blood; and the third part of the creatures which were in the sea, and had life, died, and the third part of the ships were destroyed."

The first trumpet, taking the symbols in their simplest sense, indicated a withering of the earth's produce, a decay of vegetation such as some sudden calamity might cause — the result and punishment of neglect and hard-heartedness. The passage I have just read, whatever weight we give to particular phrases in it, points just as clearly to disasters affecting the sea and the commerce of nations. *A third of the ships were destroyed.* That particular kind of suffering could impoverish great trading centres like Alexandria or Ephesus, and would only reach first-century Palestine in its secondary effects. But the angels' trumpets, like our Lord's words, teach the Israelite to link the doom of his own country with the common sufferings of humanity. That city was set on a hill to witness to the world of a God who rules land and sea, who has appointed men to subdue the earth and cross the sea, and who cares more for those made in His image than for all the treasures they dig from the ground or trade in commerce. That message had not been delivered; the Jew had worshipped these treasures as much, cared for man as little, and believed in a just God as little, as the heathen around him. Therefore earth and sea raised their voices against him more than against any others, saying to him — not to Pharaoh or Belshazzar — "Your kingdom has been taken from you; you have been weighed in the balance and found wanting."

VI. "And the third angel sounded, and there fell a great star from heaven, burning as it were a lamp, and it fell upon the third part of the rivers, and upon the fountains of waters. And the name of the star is called Wormwood, and the third part of the waters became wormwood; and many men died of the waters because they were made bitter."

Most, I think, will agree that here we have the hint of some visitation affecting the internal life and health of nations, just as the previous one affected their dealings with each other. I do not know that we would gain much by trying to interpret the specific details more precisely. Wormwood that makes waters bitter and destroys life would be a fitting and by no means exaggerated symbol for miseries that have fallen on countries in East and West at various times; and even without direct evidence, we may well believe such things were among the woes afflicting the world in the disastrous years before Jerusalem's fall. What matters to us is that the calamity was a message to the *Jew*. It told him that he was the Jonah in the ship. It was another trumpet blast declaring that the walls of *his* city were to collapse flat.

VII. "And the fourth angel sounded, and the third part of the sun was smitten, and the third part of the moon, and the third part of the stars; so as the third part of them was darkened, and the day shone not for a third part of it, and the night likewise."

It would be easy to read this as describing a moral darkness rather than a physical one. But I believe that adopting that interpretation would depart from the general purpose of these revelations and break their consistency.

The sounds of these first four trumpets all seem to me to point to the connection between physical life and physical decay on the one hand and moral life and moral decay on the other; to the way physical agents become instruments for punishing moral transgression; to the truth that earth, sea, rivers, sun, moon, and stars obey eternal laws — but laws given them by the One who created voluntary beings and whose highest kingdom is over those beings. These were the truths the Jewish nation had been called out of all nations to declare, by its words, its services, and its continual life. And these are the truths it was now to declare by its ruin and death.

But in saying this, I do not doubt that there are evils far more terrible than any that earth, sea, air, or sun and moon can inflict — or that these worse evils were at work when Jerusalem fell, and were causing her fall. Such darker evils, I believe, are pointed at in the words that follow.

VIII. "And I beheld and heard an angel flying through the midst of heaven, saying with a loud voice, Woe, woe, woe, to the inhabiters of the earth, by reason of the other voices of the trumpets which are yet to sound."

That *these* trumpets point to moral sins, to spiritual wickedness, and to their political consequences, I cannot doubt. Those who live on the earth needed to know that such woes are the greatest woes of all. The heathen needed to know it as much as those to whom belonged the adoption, the glory, the covenants, the giving of the law and the promises, and from whom, as concerning the flesh, Christ came, who is over all, God blessed for ever. And if anyone needs it even more than they, it is those who have inherited a better Covenant, a fulfilled law, a higher promise — the members of Christ's body, the heirs of the Kingdom of Heaven. May He who made the deaf hear enable His Church to receive His message! May He who made the dumb speak enable His ministers to deliver it!

## LECTURE IX. THE LATTER PLAGUES.

*The passage:* The fifth angel sounds his trumpet, and John sees a star fall from heaven to earth. This star is given the key to the bottomless pit; when it opens the pit, smoke pours out like the smoke of a great furnace, darkening the sun and the air. Out of the smoke come locusts with the stinging power of scorpions, but they are told not to harm any plants or trees—only those people who do not have God's seal on their foreheads. They are not to kill these people but to torment them for five months, with pain like a scorpion's sting, so that people will long for death but not be able to die. The locusts look like war-horses, with what seem like gold crowns on their heads, human faces, hair like women's, teeth like lions', iron breastplates, wings that sound like war-chariots, and tails like scorpions with stings, powerful to hurt for five months. Their king is the angel of the bottomless pit, named Abaddon in Hebrew and Apollyon in Greek. One woe is past; two more are coming. The sixth angel sounds, and a voice from the golden altar tells him to release the four angels bound at the river Euphrates, who were prepared for a precise hour, day, month, and year to kill a third of humanity. Their army numbers two hundred million horsemen, with breastplates of fire, jacinth, and brimstone, and horses whose heads are like lions' and whose mouths pour out fire, smoke, and brimstone, killing a third of humanity. Their power is in their mouths and in their tails, which are like serpents with heads that do harm. Yet the rest of humanity, not killed by these plagues, still did not repent of worshipping demons and idols of gold, silver, brass, stone, and wood—idols that cannot see, hear, or walk—nor did they repent of their murders, sorceries, sexual sins, or thefts.

A voice from Heaven had told John that the woes following the last three trumpets would be far worse than those before them. This seemed to me to suggest that they would be of a different kind—more moral and spiritual misery, less merely physical suffering. Let us see whether the description of the first of these woes supports that idea.

I. "And the fifth angel sounded, and I saw a star fall from heaven unto the earth." I don't claim these words settle the nature of the coming calamity. Taken by themselves, they *might* mean some harm to the earth itself, like what we heard when the first trumpet sounded. But I think the symbol feels far less strained, and far more like the ones we meet in prophecy and even in everyday speech, if it means the fall of some intellectual power—the dreadful change that happens when something that ought to give light to the world instead makes its darkness worse.

II. The next words, "and to him was given the key of the bottomless pit," would mean nothing, I think, if the falling star stood for some physical disorder. But suppose some man, or some society of men, who had believed in a righteous and true God of Heaven and had witnessed to Him, became believers in a completely different kind of God—came in practice to acknowledge an evil God. Wouldn't such a description then be fulfilled? A gaping gulf of atheism is uncovered beneath a surface that had been coated with a religious crust, on which fair fruit seemed to be growing. People begin to ask whether the universe has any foundation at all, or whether it is built on rottenness. Most will admit that this pit was opening beneath the *heathenism* of the Roman empire in the first century. But did heathenism hold the *key* of the pit? Was the bottomless abyss laid bare when Jove began to be treated as just another word for the air, or when the whole Pantheon came to be regarded as a gathering of malignant demons? No—to an apostle's eye the worshippers of Jehovah were a far more horrible sight; their loss of faith in the God of their fathers, their putting a dark power in His place, was not only the meaning of the change in heathenism but in some sense its cause. For heathenism's own cause we must look further back. It is set out, I think, in those tremendous words: "If I had not done among them the works which none other man did, they had not had sin. But now have they both seen and hated both me and my Father." The revelation of Christ to them as the brightness of the Father's glory had brought what was hidden in them into full awareness. They had been living in a twilight, with the light still struggling within them. But they saw the Perfect Image and fled from it. That was not their God. What was directly opposite to that image was their God. The witnesses for the deliverer became the witnesses and tools of the destroyer. The star had truly fallen from heaven. And because it was a star meant to light the whole world, the whole world must suffer from its fall. The broken, imperfect faiths of humanity—those still wavering between Ahriman and Ormuzd, still timidly confessing amid every monstrous contradiction and foul deed that good is Divine and must win—all take on darkness from this great apostasy. This is how the bottomless pit is opened; only in this way could it be opened.

III. "And there arose a smoke out of the pit, as the smoke of a great furnace, and the sun and the air were darkened by reason of the smoke of the pit." There is a haze of mist through which we can still see heaven. Through that mist heaven may look to us sometimes like an enlarged earth, sometimes like the faint shadow of what is solid here. But *this* medium is not mist but smoke. A furnace burns beneath it. Nothing can be seen above but wrath and horror. And all of nature takes on the same look. Everything that once seemed beautiful becomes hideous. "The sun and the air are darkened by reason of the smoke of the pit."

IV. "And there came out of the pit locusts upon the earth, and unto them was given power, as the scorpions of the earth have power. And it was commanded them that they should not hurt the grass of the earth, neither any green thing, neither any tree; but only those men which have not the seal of God in their foreheads. And to them it was given that they should not kill them, but that they should be tormented five months: and their torment is as the torment of a scorpion, when he striketh a man."

One of the most vivid passages in all ancient poetry is the prophet Joel's description of the locust army that invaded Palestine in his day. Travellers have confirmed how literally accurate it is; a statistical table or an agricultural report might state the facts of such a disaster more drily, but not more precisely. That description shows better than any other how we go wrong when we talk about "making allowance" for the figurative language of the prophets. We need make no allowances for it. We should study it and interpret it carefully and strictly, and then it will give us a living rather than a dead picture of events that really happened; it will set before us an actual past that can be compared with an actual present.

I point to that passage from the oldest of all the prophets because none helps us better to decide what this passage in Revelation must mean. "A nation," says Joel, "is come up upon my land, strong, and without number, whose teeth are the teeth of a lion, and he hath the cheek teeth of a great lion. He hath laid my vine waste, and barked my fig tree: he hath made it clean bare, and cast it away; the branches thereof are made white." And again: "The field is wasted, the land mourneth; for the corn is wasted: the new wine is dried up, the oil languisheth. Be ye ashamed, O ye husbandmen; howl, O ye vinedressers, for the wheat and for the barley; because the harvest of the field is perished. The vine is dried up, and the fig tree languisheth; the pomegranate tree, the palm tree also, and the apple tree, even all the trees of the field, are withered: because joy is withered away from the sons of men." And once more, in his second chapter: "The land is as the garden of Eden before them, and behind them as a desolate wilderness; yea, and nothing shall escape them."

There can be no mistaking what this language means: its whole force is lost unless it points precisely to the destruction of grass, trees, and crops—the kind of destruction that a swarm of insects, and only insects, could cause. Now notice how carefully the Apostle, clearly with Joel's words in mind, rules out the very things Joel dwells on. *These* locusts were "not to hurt the grass of the earth, neither any green thing, neither any tree." I hardly need point out that such expressions, if they mean anything, must equally stop us from supposing that the locusts figuratively stand for the invasion of some human army. Imagine a horde of Huns or Avars leaving no mark of their march on the ground or on any tree! Imagine it being said of such locusts that they were to torment but not kill their victims! Surely it is our own fault if we refuse to take these hints—if we suppose that a prophet, just because he has a Divine commission to teach us great truths, is more careless with his symbols, and less able to make us understand them, than an ordinary teacher.

The locusts, then, that rose out of the smoke of the pit—those that were to touch only the men without God's seal on their foreheads and to strike them as a scorpion strikes—must point, I take it, to those superstitions, and especially those restless speculations about the future, that beset people from whom all belief in God's goodness has departed; people who feel the world is ruled by an enemy instead of a Father; whose accusing consciences make the worst expectations about their fate seem believable. We can all know how these locusts darken sky and air; how they breed hateful suspicions that poison dealings between people; how they attach themselves to each person's memory of what he has done and what he is; how one moment they tell him that what he is now he will always be, and the next moment urge him to see whether he can escape his own existence, his immortal self. Oh, my friends, these torments really are the torment of a scorpion when it strikes a man; no other comparison could be as true.

V. It is to anguish of this kind that these words apply so exactly and so sadly: "And in those days shall men seek death, and shall not find it; and shall desire to die, and death shall flee from them." A constant craving for death as the end of these scorpion stings, and at the same time a constant horror of death, as though the sting were inside it, and could never be drawn out. The rest of death seems delicious; the man would give worlds for it. "Not to be!" But—does not that really mean "never to have been"? Is there any other terms on which such rest is possible? The conscience answers, "No! The grave must be deep and wide enough to shut in everything I have been as well as everything I am to be, or it is not deep and wide enough for me."

VI. This scorpion-torment is the chief sign of all the superstitions that rise out of the bottomless pit of unbelief. But they have other marks that seem hardly to fit together. The first is this: "Their shapes were like unto horses prepared for the battle." There is haste, eagerness, and terror about them. They *look* as if always getting ready for some encounter, but they never *are* ready. Fear where there is nothing to fear; no fear of the real crisis actually approaching; an inability to measure danger clearly and steadily—here is a token of that day's trouble, as it has been of the like in every later day. Here is the second mark: "And they had on their heads as it were crowns of gold." Fantastic hopes of possible happiness alternate with terror. Gold crowns float before the dupe's eyes; the dealer in omens and enchantments keeps holding them out. A sensual paradise, with no tree of knowledge—or one that may be freely eaten from—seems about to be restored. Here is the third sign: "And their faces were like the faces of men." The various superstitions and dreams take their different looks from the tastes, moods, likings, and dislikes of particular teachers; they all reflect human faces; none has any lasting or Divine substance. Here is the fourth feature: "And they had hair as the hair of women." There was a feminine element in them—not just weakness and timidity, though that was surely present. We have already seen the part the false prophetess played in the church at Thyatira; in the forty years before Jerusalem's destruction, when men like Simon and Elymas were scattered so widely, the Jezebel was found at least as often as the Balaam, one always standing beside the other. And the dreams of the future, along with the immediate fantasies, showed the qualities of both. Again, "The teeth of these locusts were like the teeth of lions." In Joel's bold language these teeth had been given to actual insects; he was comparing things not by their size but by their effects. The prophet of the Apocalypse does the same. However soft an air they put on, however human and feminine they may seem, these superstitions that come out of the bottomless pit are still murderous; they are tearing to pieces whatever righteousness and truth remain on the earth.

Again, "They have breastplates as it were breastplates of iron." Most missionaries could tell what iron breastplates protect the world's cruel superstitions and dark worships. They have learned that fearful truth, I trust, not only from their experience of the heathen but of themselves; they have found what an unnatural and terrible hardness any falsehood we have given in to, and that has wrapped itself around us, can take on. So it need not surprise us that these locusts in John's vision—those that had spread over the earth in Jerusalem's last days and were darkening earth and air—should have this among their signs. And again, "The sound of their wings was as the sound of many chariots running to battle." The Divine seer, like the false prophets, was expecting a battle. And all those various forms of evil that gathered up the falsehoods and false worships spread over the world until then sounded in his ears like chariots hurrying to the fight—the forerunners and heralds of a conflict between the powers of good and evil unlike any there had ever been. But whatever else marks these locusts, we must keep to the end the mark noticed first: "They had tails like unto scorpions, and there were stings in their tails." There may be gold crowns on their heads, but all their power comes from the torments they can inflict on the conscience. That which makes cowards of us all is what makes these thoughts of the future terrifying and real; without it they would be mere lies from the father of lies.

VII. For "they had a king over them, which is the angel of the bottomless pit, whose name is in the Hebrew tongue Abaddon, but in the Greek tongue hath his name Apollyon." Here we reach the root of the matter. Devil worship—the worship of the destroyer—is the source of all the foul and hurtful fancies that fill people's hearts. These men have bowed down to him; they have confessed him as their lord and master. Until now there had always been a protest against his tyranny. Wherever there is a nation, wherever there is law, justice, freedom—even the struggle for law, justice, freedom—he is defied; from there goes out a proclamation: This is not our Lord; we owe him no allegiance. In one nation that protest had been clear, formal, carried on from age to age. In one nation the voice—"I am the Lord thy God, which brought thee out of the house of bondage; I am the Lord God, merciful and gracious; I am the Righteous Judge of the earth"—had been heard through every law and every sacrifice. And now that nation was reverencing Abaddon, or Apollyon, under the name of Jehovah. Was this not a woe that foretold woes to come? Was it not a trumpet announcing that the holy city was to fall?

VIII. "One woe is past; and, behold, there come two more woes hereafter. And the sixth angel sounded, and I heard a voice from the four horns of the golden altar which is before God, saying to the sixth angel which had the trumpet, Loose the four angels which are bound in the great river Euphrates. And the four angels were loosed which were prepared for an hour, and a day, and a month, and a year, for to slay the third part of men."

Doesn't this vision look more like those of the first four trumpets than like the one we have just been considering? An actual river is named, the Euphrates. A third of humanity is said to be killed. Two hundred million horsemen form a huge army. Shouldn't we look for some physical rather than moral calamity in this language? I have no doubt this is most readers' first impression. But I think it can hardly be the final one.

We shall see, I think, that this trumpet follows the fifth in natural chronological order. The bottomless pit is opened; swarms of dark superstitions spread abroad; then comes the removal of a spiritual chain that had until now held an evil host in check. But what of the river Euphrates? On any scheme of interpretation, that river must be symbolic; it cannot point to events visibly happening on its banks or nearby. So the question is: what does such a symbol most obviously and naturally mean? A Jew would always connect the Euphrates with Babylon; he would think of it as the river of the Asiatic monarch. He would take it to stand spiritually for the dividing line between the two great kingdoms—the Babel kingdom and the Israelite kingdom; the kingdom founded on self-will, and the kingdom founded on the God of Righteousness. So he would understand the command to release the angels bound at the Euphrates as the Divine decree that the barriers between these kingdoms—so far as the second was represented in Jerusalem—should exist no longer. The moral basis of the distinction had been destroyed. Jerusalem was now part of the Babel society; may we not boldly say it had become its center and capital?

IX. The year, the day, and the hour of this crisis, we are told, were fixed. The long pent-up evil broke loose. But it had gained no all-powerful might. A higher Will decided when it would become a lie for Jerusalem to keep pretending to be a witness for God, and when it was right that Jerusalem should be declared the great witness for the Devil. That year, day, and hour were not a matter of indifference to the rest of the world. They affected it in ways it did not know. How, in the end, we shall be taught later. How, at once, we learn from this present passage. I find nothing here that makes me doubt that my interpretation of the Euphrates is the right one. The number of the horses is clearly not one that would be used in an account of a real event. It is deliberately chosen to indicate an army that could never have been gathered on any earthly battlefield, yet that carries a wide, mysterious desolation. The description of the horses and riders is of the same kind. The "breastplates of fire and of jacinth, and of fire and of brimstone" point to no armor made in earthly forges; the "fire, and smoke, and brimstone" do not come from the mouths of any animals man can tame.

X. Still, I do not in the least mean to explain away the statement that "men were killed by the fire, and the smoke, and the brimstone that issued out of their mouths." By this I do not mean spiritual death. Wars and fighting came then, as they come now, from the lusts in people's bodies; the brutality, greed, self-glorification, and atheism of a land were then, as now, the causes that destroy hundreds of thousands. The loosing of the angels may have been the removal of restraints from powers of moral evil. But if so, it was necessarily also the removal of hindrances to the murder of people's bodies. The crossing of the Euphrates may have been the breaking of the boundary between a kingdom of righteousness and a kingdom of mere power. But once that boundary is gone, nothing earthly is safe from the violence of power. That strange description in verse 19, of the horses' power—"It is in their mouth and in their tails, for their tails were like unto serpents, and had heads, and with them they do hurt"—means, I take it, a complete union of intellectual with animal force, with all moral purpose shut out. There is the mouth that can speak wisdom; there is the tail that strikes and kills. All the wisdom is serpentine: *the tail has heads*; these obey the lower instincts; to do harm is their only function. Surely when such creatures are turned loose for a little while, they will carry out that function. Woe to the world because of them. Lifted to high places, nothing they have imagined doing will be held back.

XI. Six trumpets, then, have sounded. We ask: what effect did they have on those living within earshot of them? How did they affect the heathen world? How did they affect the Jewish world? The seer answers: "And the rest of the men which were not killed by these plagues yet repented not of the works of their hands, that they should not worship devils, and idols of gold, and silver, and brass, and stone, and of wood: which neither can see, nor hear, nor walk: neither repented they of their murders, nor of their sorceries, nor of their fornications, nor of their thefts." So it was in the beginning, and so it will be to the end. All outward plagues, all outbreaks of moral evil, all apostasies within Divine Societies, were and are God's trumpets; those who acknowledge His goodness and truth will tremble and rejoice that He is speaking to them, calling them to repent, preparing the way to reveal Himself. But these trumpets, however loud and long they sound, rarely move a man who disbelieves in a living and good God to confess Him. The terror in them stuns rather than awakens. The sleeper is half roused from his dream, is bewildered, takes a fresh dose of opium, flees to the gods that cannot see, hear, or walk, and flees from Him whom he has known only in thunder and lightning. The saying is forever true that it is not the fire, nor the earthquake, nor the wind splitting the mountains, but only the still small voice that reaches the heart and makes it bow. Jew and heathen alike hear the notes of coming doom and are unmoved. Both alike shall at last look on Him whom they have pierced, and mourn as one mourns for an only son.

## LECTURE X. THE OPEN BOOK.

*The passage:* John saw another mighty angel come down from heaven, wrapped in a cloud, with a rainbow over his head, a face like the sun, and feet like pillars of fire. He held a little book, open, in his hand. He set his right foot on the sea and his left foot on the land, and cried out with a loud voice like a roaring lion. When he cried, seven thunders spoke. As John was about to write down what the thunders said, a voice from heaven told him to seal those things up and not write them. Then the angel standing on sea and land lifted his hand to heaven and swore by the God who lives forever, who created heaven, earth, sea, and everything in them, that there should be no more delay: in the days when the seventh angel sounds his trumpet, the mystery of God would be finished, as He had told His servants the prophets. The voice from heaven told John to go and take the open book from the angel's hand. He went and asked for it, and the angel told him to take it and eat it — it would be sweet as honey in his mouth but would make his stomach bitter. John took it and ate it, and it was sweet in his mouth but bitter afterward. And the angel told him that he must prophesy again before many peoples, nations, tongues, and kings.

I. In the fifth chapter we were told how the Prophet saw, in the right hand of Him who sat on the throne, a book written on both sides and sealed with seven seals. Those seals have now been broken, and the book is open. The mighty angel whose face is like the sun holds it in his hand. I said before that we could not name the book until we knew something of its contents. Can we name it now? First, we heard that no one in heaven or earth could open it except the Lamb who was slain — the Lamb who joins the highest power to the highest suffering, in whom is all the glory of Him on the throne and all the sorrow of man. He can read it and let us read it. So this is one hint about the book: it must concern the relations between heaven and earth. It must tell what separates them, and whether they can ever be reconciled. Second, when the first four seals were broken, the four living creatures — the lion, the calf, the man, the eagle — each showed a power going out on earth to destroy rather than bless. Each earthly power seemed to correspond to one of these heavenly powers, being an aspect of God that had been cut off from God and twisted into idolatry. So the book must show us how earthly power has become separated from righteousness, and whether it can ever become righteousness's servant. Third, we heard of martyrs beneath the altar crying for deliverance from the self-willed powers, confessing the self-sacrificing power. So the book must be a record of the wars of those who have fought for good and truth against triumphant evil and falsehood, who kept their faith that what looks weakest to men is strongest to God. Fourth, we had the vision of a great convulsion that made all the seemingly supreme powers of the earth tremble, because they knew the avenger had come and the sufferer was truly the king. So the book is meant to interpret those puzzling stretches of human history that show periods of revolution and anarchy. It shows them to be the necessary result of earlier tyranny and defiance of law; it shows there is a Divine purpose in them and a Divine blessing to come out of them. In all this I find everything that fits the Scriptural idea of prophecy, and almost nothing that fits the heathen idea of prediction. The book is an unfolding or discovery of the meaning and purpose of the eternal God. It explains the principles of an unchangeable government. It shows a law at work even in the ups and downs and whims of self-will. So it applies to the ages before John was born; it applies to the time in which he lived. If, as St. Paul and the New Testament writers affirm, the ends of the ages were meeting in that age, the book must have a special bearing on it. By interpreting the century after Christ came in the flesh, it throws light back over all the centuries before He came.

The passages that follow clearly show that this is a book of judgment. And they do not leave "judgment" a vague word, as it often is in our minds. We are told *what* is judged; what *cannot* perish and what *must* perish in the judgment; what the signs of judgment are; how far God judges and punishes men; how far men judge and punish themselves. The subject of the judgment is a Nation. The vision of the sealed tribes taught us that there is an order within the nation to be judged, an order that survives any destruction of its outward forms. The vision of the countless company assured us that the nation's existence does not shut out the existence of a society with no boundaries at all. The book's plain language led us to suppose that the city or polity doomed, around which the angel's trumpets were blowing, was Jerusalem. That would be the natural conclusion if there were no stronger reasons against it. I have pointed to two reasons that have mainly led readers to reject this view and adopt a more far-fetched one. First, they have decided that the Apocalypse was written after Jerusalem's destruction, so the trumpets of coming doom must point to some other city. Second, they have thought the more obvious meaning too poor and thin, one that would strip the Apocalypse of all its instruction for later times.

To the first objection I answer this. Even if the traditional dating of the Apocalypse to the reign of Domitian could be maintained against the judgment of some of the most eminent modern scholars — even if it was not written, as those scholars suppose, in the time of Galba — the argument is still worthless, except to those who confuse prediction with prophecy. The Jews who lived after the temple was destroyed, even after the plough had been driven around the walls and the city's name changed — things that did not happen until Hadrian's time — still needed to understand what that great overthrow meant, and what the Divine signs were that announced it. The Jew needed this light so that he would not be utterly confounded by his ancient records, which seemed to promise the city of David permanence. The Gentile needed it just as much. From Jerusalem had come the message that claimed him as one of God's family. St. Paul, however hated by his own people and even suspected by the Jewish Church, had carried the gifts and offerings of the Gentiles there as a token of their obligation. When the Church lost its Capital, they might easily feel it had lost its Centre. The history of the second century shows us the sea of notions and theories about the visible and invisible world through which the Church had to pass, because belief in such a Centre was so hard to grasp. To know what Jerusalem was to the old world, and what would take its place in the new, might be the most urgent lesson for that time; and if that time and later times did not fully profit from it, perhaps it is all the more necessary for ours.

And so I come to the second objection, which I have noted in earlier lectures but which keeps rising before us and needs to be looked at from several sides. We have our own notions about which events are big and which are small, and about what information we need or do not need for practical guidance. But God's measure of events may be quite different from ours; and our Divine Teacher may know better than we do what we need. To have a set of Scripture passages we can tie to what is happening in the world, and by which we can guess what will happen, may seem to us a great help in living our lives. He may regard the search for these as feeding fever and restlessness rather than calm trust and brave action. He may want to present the Scriptures to us as one living whole, to show how they interpret the life of one nation, and through it the life of all nations. He may want us to see the light they throw on the history and traditions of the heathen — people we assume He was indifferent to, but who were part of the world Christ came to redeem. I have said before that getting a right sense of Jerusalem's place in the old world is needed for a right understanding of the modern world; and I believe St. John will teach us that truth in all his later visions.

II. I cannot think that any preparation for the opening of a book holding such records and lessons could be too vast or majestic. It seems only fitting that a mighty angel should come down from heaven wrapped in a cloud, with a rainbow around his head, to present it as an open scroll. For we have now found the most comforting and satisfying name of all for it — a name St. John himself supplies. It is the book of Unveiling; the Divine mind and purpose are gradually revealed through the working of the ages. And that involves an unveiling of the very name of God, the Father, the Son, and the Spirit. The symbols describing this angel are already familiar. He is wrapped in a cloud; his outward form is hidden, and he is known by the light he brings. There is a rainbow on his head, a face like the sun, and even his feet are of the same kind — only in them there is heat as well as light, a heat that can consume. No part of the universe is untouched by his message: his right foot rests on the sea, his left on the land. His voice is one of terror — not that musical sound made by many waters, but loud as a roaring lion.

III. There is something in the echoes answering that voice which cannot yet be proclaimed. "And when he had cried, seven thunders uttered their voices. And when the seven thunders had uttered their voices, I was about to write, and I heard a voice from heaven saying unto me, Seal up those things which the seven thunders uttered, and write them not."

In a book of discovery, a book that scatters darkness and interprets the confused voices always ringing in our ears with their jarring clashes, there is still a background the spirit acknowledges but cannot reach into; there are still sounds we must be content to believe are the utterances of God's voice, but which we cannot put into our own words. Let this much be granted to those who would represent God's purposes and will as one great riddle that man can know nothing of. Let us be glad that there never has been and never can be any lifting of the veil that would interfere with trust, awe, and adoration. "What thou knowest not now, thou shalt know hereafter" — that is the Divine message every apostle and every man must hear when his Lord stoops to wash his feet; he must hear it again coming from Gethsemane, from Calvary, and from the empty tomb. And so it will be when he passes out of the sight of mortal eyes. Through age upon age there must always be a step of the ladder he has not yet climbed. But through those same ages the voice of God is at the top of the ladder, always calling him to come up higher. And he knows that nothing is kept from him out of grudging jealousy, and that what is hidden must be more good and gracious than what has already been shown. Living under the government of a Being whose delight has been to make Himself known, he can wait in quietness and confidence. If he were living under a Being about whom there was nothing but guesswork and uncertainty, he would only stop being restless when he stopped being a man.

IV. "And the angel which I saw stand upon the sea and upon the earth lifted up his hand to heaven, and sware by him that liveth for ever and ever, who created heaven, and the things that therein are, and the earth, and the things that therein are, and the sea, and the things which are therein, that there should be time no longer: but in the days of the voice of the seventh angel, when he shall begin to sound, the mystery of God should be finished, as he hath declared to his servants the prophets."

I cannot tell whether our translators really thought these words meant what sermons and popular religious books so often make them mean. To the ear there is certainly something very grand in the phrase "time shall be no longer." Such an announcement seems worthy of the Archangel's oath. It is only when we ask ourselves what these sounds actually convey, what we suppose "the end of time" indicates, that we find we are grasping a cloud and, for the sake of that cloud, parting with the most solid realities. Do we really suppose the prophet is saying that a *time* will come when *time* will end and eternity begin? Do we attribute such confused notions to one who tells us in every line of his Gospel and Epistles of an eternal life which was with the Father and has been made known to us — whose Apocalypse means nothing unless it means the unveiling of eternal mysteries to creatures living under the conditions of time? I need hardly point out that if Time had been meant in that abstract, absolute sense, the article would have had to be there. Philology, common sense, and, it seems to me, the highest theology have all been sacrificed here — partly because men could not see why such solemnity should attach to the proclamation that there would be no more *delay* in bringing about the mystery of God; partly because they dimly supposed that the accomplishing of the mystery of God must mean the breaking up of the framework of the universe, which they thought would be aptly described as the end of time. But if the mystery of God that He declared to His prophets is the mystery of the reconciliation and atonement of God with man, the mystery of the Lamb's triumph over the Spirit of disobedience and self-will, the mystery of the conquest of the kingdoms of earth by the kingdom of heaven, then I believe I am not departing from the language of Scripture or of sound theology when I say that *this* mystery is not waiting to be accomplished in some far-off imaginary time when time ceases, but is being accomplished at the very crisis toward which all the earlier words in this vision of the trumpets have been pointing.

Many of the difficulties here arise, in no small part, from certain forms of speech which are useful in themselves and have become habitual, but which we repeat until they confuse and mislead us. For some purposes no distinction is more important than the one between the Old and New Testaments. There is a clear outward boundary between them, the boundary of language: nearly 400 years separate the last of the Hebrew writers from the earliest of the Hellenistic ones. The inward differences seem even more deeply marked. Jews of course have the strongest interest in dwelling on them; for them the New Testament contradicts the Old. Roman Catholics are eager to show how the national life of the Jew is swallowed up in the universal life of the Church. Protestants are anxious to show that we are not under the law but under grace. A large and growing school of liberal writers is even more eager than any of these to insist that the new dispensation has swallowed up or overturned the old. And yet none of them denies, or wishes to deny, the Jewish character of the Gospels, Epistles, or Apocalypse. As little can they deny that the Apostles and Evangelists belonged to the last forty or fifty years of the Jewish Commonwealth. Even if some would assign certain books that go under their names to a later period, all the events those books claim to record certainly fall within that span. Suppose, then, we connect these writers, as they seem to connect themselves, with the earlier prophets and teachers of their land; suppose they did believe, as they say they did, that the events they speak of are the fulfilment of the Law and the Prophets; suppose they did look, as they seem to have looked, for a kingdom of heaven to rise out of the kingdom of David and take the Gentiles into it — would this language *then* sound fantastic or impossible? Would not the destruction of Jerusalem be the winding up of that whole series of events — the incarnation, death, resurrection, and glorification of Christ — which were themselves the finishing of the mystery of God that He had promised to His servants the prophets? I can only ask those who look down on the New Testament writers as ignorant, narrow Jews to consider whether this is not exactly what they *do* say; whether I am not giving the real force and outcome of their words, whether those words are Divine or human, wise or unwise. And I may surely ask those who set great store by the letter of Scripture — though not, in my judgment, too great — who receive its words as oracles, to consider what those words actually are; to consider whether, taken plainly, they do not contain the Gospel of a finished salvation for men; and whether, if so, this was not a fitting occasion for an angel to swear by Him who lives forever, who created heaven and earth and sea and all that is in them — because the glorious purpose for which the Jewish nation had existed was now brought to pass; because the gods of heaven, sea, and earth were now to bow before the one living God, the Lord God of Israel; because a dispensation was about to begin grounded on the regeneration of humanity in the Son of Man; because a Divine Spirit could now stay with men and make His temple in their hearts and bodies.

V. "And the voice which I heard from heaven spake unto me again, and said, Go, and take the little book which is open in the hand of the angel which standeth upon the sea and upon the earth. And I went unto the angel, and said unto him, Give me the little book. And he said unto me, Take it, and eat it up; and it shall make thy belly bitter, but it shall be in thy mouth sweet as honey. And I took the little book out of the angel's hand and ate it up; and it was in my mouth sweet as honey; and as soon as I had eaten it, my belly was bitter."

Nearly every passage of the Apocalypse, as I have already noticed, recalls some passage of an earlier prophet. When Ezekiel, sitting by the river Chebar, had seen that "appearance of the likeness of the glory of the Lord" — which corresponds to the vision in the fourth chapter of this book — he heard a voice saying to him: "Son of man, I send thee to the children of Israel, to a rebellious nation that hath rebelled against me: they and their fathers have transgressed against me unto this very day.... And thou, Son of man, be not afraid of them, neither be thou afraid of their words, though briers and thorns be with thee, and thou dost dwell among scorpions; be not afraid of their words, nor be dismayed at their looks, though they be a rebellious house.... But thou, Son of man, hear what I say unto thee; Be not thou rebellious like that rebellious house: open thy mouth, and eat that I give thee. And when I looked, behold, an hand was sent unto me; and, lo, a roll of a book was therein; and he spread it before me; and it was written within and without: and there was written therein lamentations, and mourning, and woe. Moreover he said unto me, Son of man, eat that thou findest; eat this roll, and go speak unto the house of Israel. So I opened my mouth, and he caused me to eat that roll. And he said unto me, Son of man, cause thy belly to eat, and fill thy bowels with this roll that I give thee. Then did I eat it; and it was in my mouth as honey for sweetness."

Ezekiel's work, as set out there, had to do with the Israelites in Babylon. His message concerned the desolation and restoration of their city, and above all of the Temple. It would not be listened to; the prophet would be scorned and hated by his own countrymen. But the words had to be spoken, whether heeded or rejected, and were to remain for the generation to come. St. John, living in a different age, hears the same tidings of lamentation, mourning, and woe that his predecessor had heard. He is speaking to the same rebellious house. He finds that house under a tyranny essentially like the Babylonian one, and awaiting a still more terrible overthrow. St. John's roll, like Ezekiel's, tells of a temple that is to fall and a temple that is to rise from its ruins. And St. John, like Ezekiel, must eat the roll. Its words are not merely to be read but taken, with all their bitterness and all their sweetness, into his innermost being. In this respect the ages between the seer at Chebar and the seer at Patmos make no difference. Living words — words that are things, words written down only because they have been acted or are to be acted — must be received into the man before he can utter them. He must know them by heart, not by rote. So it was in those old days; so it has been since; so it will be in our times. In easy days the words of all books, and of the Bible especially, become fine subjects for criticism and debate. Eloquent comments are written on them; they can be twisted into all sorts of senses; they can be proved to mean everything, anything, nothing. But in times of stress and anguish they are devoured. They are taken not to soothe the reader nor to condemn people he dislikes; he receives them as they are. They nourish him by their bitter qualities as much as by their pleasant ones; he needs both and accepts both. He asks for no explanation of them; they come already carrying a tremendous explanation of what he was and what he now is; he asks for no evidence about them; they bring it with them.

But why should the book be sweet as honey in the mouth if it is so full of woe? And why should it turn bitter afterward, if it is God's book? Ezekiel and St. John had the same experience in this. Nor, I think, would our own case be different, if we truly ate the words and they really entered into us. There must be an unspeakable sweetness in the actual living taste of a Divine communication — in the assurance, felt in the blood, felt along the heart, that a part of the law by which we are governed has been disclosed to us; that the love beneath all law, working at every moment for the welfare and renewal of the creatures it has called into being, is showing itself in our very selves. Sweet as honey! But then comes the sense of this law defied in the world and defied in ourselves, of that love trampled and resisted in the kingdoms of men and in the kingdom within us. Is there no revulsion in that? Does not the book become the more bitter afterward, in proportion to how delicious it was at first? There may be a return to that early joy; there may be a deeper joy that springs out of conflict and suffering; but whenever the messages and revelations of history and prophecy are fully digested, there must be sorrow. For is not the Man of sorrow the one they speak of, and the one who interprets them?

There was a special cause of sorrow in the case of the Apostle John. He might have hoped the open book would tell him of the judgment on the great Babel-empire of the world. Instead it made known to him the judgment of the chosen witness *against* that empire. The seventh trumpet might have announced the fall of Rome. But no — when it sounds, Jerusalem will fall. And is this, then, the end of everything? Is the mystery of God spoken of by the prophets really satisfied in this ruin? For a moment it seems so: the book is open, the dispensation is closing. But the voices of the seven thunders, which were not to be written down, may still sound after the trumpets have ceased. There may be glimpses into the dispensation still to come. The oppressor may not be meant to reign forever. Only half of the seer's task is done. "And he said unto me, Thou must prophesy again unto many peoples, and nations, and tongues, and kings."

## LECTURE XI. THE TWO WITNESSES.

*The passage:* John is given a measuring reed and told to measure the temple, the altar, and the worshippers, but to leave out the outer court, which is given to the Gentiles who will trample the holy city for forty-two months. God will give power to two witnesses who will prophesy for 1,260 days, dressed in sackcloth; they are the two olive trees and two candlesticks that stand before God. Fire comes from their mouths to destroy anyone who tries to harm them, and they have power to stop rain, turn water to blood, and send plagues.
