When their testimony is finished, the beast from the bottomless pit will make war on them, defeat them, and kill them. Their bodies will lie in the street of the great city—called, in a spiritual sense, Sodom and Egypt, and the place where the Lord was crucified—and people of every nation will look at their unburied corpses for three and a half days and celebrate, exchanging gifts, because these two prophets had tormented them. After three and a half days God's Spirit revives them; they stand up, terrifying those who see them, and are called up to heaven in a cloud while their enemies watch. An earthquake destroys a tenth of the city and kills seven thousand; the rest, terrified, give glory to God. The second woe is past; the third comes quickly. The seventh angel sounds, and voices in heaven declare that the kingdoms of this world have become the kingdoms of God and His Christ, who will reign forever. The twenty-four elders worship, thanking God for taking His power and reigning; the nations were angry, God's wrath has come, the time to judge the dead and reward His servants and destroy those who destroy the earth. The temple in heaven is opened, the ark is seen, and there are lightnings, voices, thunderings, an earthquake, and great hail.

Today we begin what I will call the second part of the Apocalypse. I do not draw this line to fit some theory of my own. The seer is told that when the seventh angel sounds, the mystery of God will be finished, but that he must prophesy again before many peoples, tongues, nations, and kings.

I have taken it that the prophet is speaking of Jerusalem. Six trumpet blasts have announced that it is about to fall. It has not fallen yet. The vision before us mentions a period of 1,260 days, or forty-two months. We usually count about four years between the start of the Jewish rebellion, brought on by the oppression of Florus, and the end of the war under Titus. If I take this stretch of time, or part of it, as the meaning of the 1,260 days, I will at least stay closer to a strict reading than if I turned the days into years—and I will be less likely to be fooled by my own imagination than if I had to guess where those years begin and end in the history of Christendom. I need not describe how that short span was filled with horrors across the whole empire, and with special, indescribable horrors in Jerusalem. Since the disorder was everywhere, the wild fanatics in the holy city could easily convince themselves that the other city, the tyrant city Rome, would fall first. The Capitol was burned before the Temple was burned. The outrages of Vitellius might draw down God's vengeance as much as those of John or Simon. And while the holy place still stood, there might always come some dramatic rescue to save it and destroy the besiegers, like the deliverance that came when Sennacherib besieged the city in Hezekiah's time.

I. "*And there was given me a reed, like unto a rod, and the angel stood, saying, Rise and measure the temple of God, and the altar, and them that worship therein. But the court that is without the temple, leave out and measure it not, for it is given unto the Gentiles: and the holy city shall they tread under foot forty and two months.*"

The parallel here is plain and direct. The prophecy of Ezekiel dwells on the Temple more than any other prophecy does. Its defilement by the idolatry of its worshippers, its coming ruin by Babylon, and what its symbols meant to those living among the idolaters of Chaldea—all this surely filled his soul. Then comes the prospect of restoration. In his fortieth chapter we hear how he is carried in spirit to the land of Israel, where he sees a man with a line of flax and a measuring reed. With these the man measures every part of the city, and especially the various courts of the Temple. The prophet learns what that building is to be—one he cannot see with his eyes, but in which his countrymen will one day worship the God of their fathers.

The command to John to come and measure must at once have brought his predecessor's vision to mind. Is he too a prophet of restoration? Is the Temple to be destroyed and rise again after seventy years? Is there to be a New Jerusalem in his day, as there was after the captivity?

The rest of the Apocalypse, I believe, answers these questions. This chapter introduces the period of 1,260 days. It explains what happens during them and what they mean. It treats them as a time of total darkness and desolation. But it shows that even in that darkest time the old witnesses of God were not extinguished; even then they were doing the work they had always done. The prophet will not clear away a false hope until he has offered something real in its place. Everything within city and Temple is doomed. No fire will come down from heaven to destroy the besiegers. The fall is delayed, but it is certain. Yet those are not wrong who say there is an immortality in the city, its altars, and its worshippers. That immortality will be revealed by the very destruction that is coming. One who once cleansed it of the traders defiling it said, "Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it again." When those who heard Him saw Him rise from Joseph's grave, they understood He had meant the temple of His body. That was one fulfillment; there could yet be another. The stone temple would not have one stone left on another, but the work for which it existed—the work it had already done for the world—could be accomplished. A better resurrection than the one that had comforted Ezekiel was approaching. What it was would be shown ever more clearly to John's spirit. Meanwhile, he was not to measure the courts that Ezekiel had measured; he was to leave them out; the desecration they underwent in the 1,260 days was a sign that the better days, the more divine order, had no connection with them.

II. "*And I will give power unto my two witnesses, and they shall prophesy a thousand two hundred and three score days, clothed in sackcloth. These are the two olive trees, and the two candlesticks standing before the God of the earth. And if any man will hurt them, fire proceedeth out of their mouth, and devoureth their enemies; and if any man will hurt them, he must in this manner be killed. These have power to shut heaven, that it rain not in the days of their prophecy: and have power over waters to turn them to blood, and to smite them with all plagues as often as they will.*"

Those who try to work out what these two witnesses are from sources outside the text have a boundless field for their imagination. I will limit myself to the clues given by the passage itself and by the Old Testament passages it points us to.

The allusion here is to one of the prophets who saw the Temple rising out of its ruins and encouraged those who feared that their own weakness and their enemies' opposition would never let it be finished. In his third chapter, Zechariah has a vision of Joshua, the high priest of that day, standing before the angel of the Lord, with Satan at his right hand to oppose him. "And the Lord said unto Satan, The Lord rebuke thee, O Satan; even the Lord that hath chosen Jerusalem, rebuke thee. Is not this a brand plucked out of the fire?" At first Joshua is dressed in filthy clothes. These are taken away, and he is given the mitre and priestly robes—the full dignity of his office restored. Then in the fourth chapter we read that the angel woke Zechariah as a man is woken from sleep and asked what he saw; he answered that he saw a golden candlestick with a bowl on top, seven lamps and seven pipes to the lamps, and two olive trees beside it, one on each side of the bowl. He asked the angel what these were. The answer is not what we expect: "This is the word of the Lord unto Zerubbabel, saying, Not by might, nor by power, but by my Spirit, saith the Lord of hosts." The prophet is then told that the hands of Zerubbabel that laid the foundation of the house will also finish it. When he asks again about the olive trees, he is told, "These are the two anointed ones, that stand before the Lord of the whole earth."

I believe that if we look closely at this passage, the very things that puzzle us in it—its apparent digressions and inconsistencies—will help us understand the witnesses in the Apocalypse. Joshua and Zerubbabel are clearly the priest and the ruler of the city rising from its ruins. Bound up with these men, who represent the great offices of the commonwealth, is a vision of the Temple itself—the Temple where Joshua will minister and which Zerubbabel will build. But the vision is not of the outward stone building; it is of two of its old symbols, the ones that best express its spiritual character and purpose. At its highest glory and its lowest depression, the Temple has always testified of an everlasting Law coming from the invisible God of righteousness, and of a uniting, living communion between Him and His creatures. The Word from His lips and the Spirit that goes out to give life to the creatures made in His image—these anointed ones who stand continually before the God of the earth—are presented to men in the ordinances of Law and Sacrifice, in the persons of the Judge and the Priest. They had lasted from the earliest times. When Elijah, in Ahab's day, was allowed to bear witness against Baal worship by keeping the heavens from raining during the days of his prophecy; when, centuries before, Moses turned the waters of the Nile into blood so that Pharaoh might know the Lord of the earth to be the Deliverer of captives—they were acting as these witnesses. They were showing that divine power does not dwell with what looks powerful, but with what looks weak. As much as Zerubbabel, they were establishing the maxim, "Not by might, nor by power, but by my Spirit." And so it was now. The witnesses in the midst of Jerusalem testified that a fire would come from the righteous God to sweep away the enemies of righteousness, whoever they were.

III. "*And when they shall have finished their testimony, the beast that ascendeth out of the bottomless pit shall make war against them, and shall overcome them, and kill them. And their dead bodies shall lie in the street of the great city, which spiritually is called Sodom and Egypt, where also our Lord was crucified.*"

A time was to come, however, when these witnesses for the existence of law and of God's care for men were to be silenced. I cannot say whether this refers to some particular event in the siege of Jerusalem that marked the extinction of the last shred of moral restraint and of belief in anything good. Probably it does. Probably the allusion would have made sense to some at that time who needed it. We do not need the specific fact, but the principle it stood for. What matters to us is to grasp that there was a critical moment in the history of that falling city—as there is in every falling city—when its crimes reached their peak and the restraints that had checked them stopped working. The one who kills the witnesses is described here as the beast that rises from the bottomless pit. More about him will be told later. Enough is said here to show that mere brute force, ungoverned lust and self-will, became triumphant over everything opposed to them. To all intents and purposes they were the lords of the Holy City. The Jewish historian would tell us as much. A seer was needed, not to record or repeat the announcement, but to tell us what it implies. The witnesses who perished in Jerusalem were witnesses of God's government and of God's mercy to the world. The killing of those witnesses could not affect Jerusalem alone; it must affect all mankind.

IV. "*And their dead bodies shall lie in the street of the great city, which spiritually is called Sodom and Egypt, where also our Lord was crucified. And they of the people and kindreds and tongues and nations, shall see their dead bodies three days and an half, and shall not suffer their dead bodies to be put in graves.*"

In the first of these verses the place where the witnesses lived is identified as the place where our Lord was crucified. Jerusalem is named just as clearly by that description as by the other—that it is spiritually called Sodom and Egypt. What city, during its year of siege, better matched the idea of a Sodom, where all law and humanity were wiped out, or of an Egypt, where religion had become the great instrument of darkness and oppression, the denial of God, the spur to every crime? When the witnesses' dead bodies are said to lie in the street of this city, I take it to mean that there was the most conspicuous example ever given of the decay and rotting of institutions that had been set up to spread life among the nations. When it says the peoples, tongues, and nations would not let them be buried, I take it to mean that the surrounding heathen preferred to have Jerusalem remain a sink of filth and iniquity—a spectacle of where its grand claim to a special worship had ended—rather than have it swept away at once. For a little while, not much longer than the time the Son of God Himself remained under the power of death and the grave, Jerusalem was to be—so men decreed, so God willed—a sign and witness of the evil possible on earth, which is only possible when it is the corruption of the highest good.

V. "*And they that dwell upon the earth shall rejoice: and make merry, and shall send gifts one to another; because these two prophets tormented them that dwelt upon the earth.*" A sense of release from the restraints of law, from belief in anything invisible, is vividly set out in these words. It has come to different nations at different times; and always, I believe, it has been the result of the moral collapse and degradation of some society that held high trusts and, through its own boasting, sank lower than those it claimed to warn and guide. That there should be this savage glee and mutual congratulation among idolaters who had been tormented by the witness the Jewish law and worship bore against their impurities—and that an actual loosening of all moral ties, greater than ever known before, should be tied to this feeling—we could guess even without evidence; but in fact we have it from the most trustworthy sources about the real shaking of Roman society in the period after Nero's death.

VI. "*And after three days and an half, the Spirit of life from God entered into them, and they stood upon their feet; and great fear fell upon them which saw them. And they heard a great voice from heaven saying unto them, Come up hither. And they ascended up to heaven in a cloud; and their enemies beheld them. And the same hour there was a great earthquake, and the tenth part of the city fell, and in the earthquake were slain of men seven thousand: and the remnant were affrighted, and gave glory to the God of heaven.*" Here is the key to the whole vision. Forms and institutions that were not only ancient and honored but could claim a divine origin and had been upheld by divine power, wither and perish. The world thinks they are gone forever. That is the very moment of their transfiguration and glorification. Then they are found to hold a life that depends on no accidents of time or place.

Everything above and below announces their presence and testifies to their authority. The fears that strike the consciences of those who most defied them are the signs of that presence and authority. The swift disappearance of some power that had claimed independence of them; the confessions, conscious and unconscious, of a God of heaven that follow a stretch of utter indifference and unbelief—these show that the witnesses have ascended. If they lose their local home and name, it is so that they may claim a universal rule.

The truth of these words has been shown at various times, so it is no wonder that one group after another has claimed them for itself. I believe we vindicate them for everyone by taking them as directly referring to the crisis I have described. History tells us that the Roman world, after being shaken from center to edge, was struck with a great fear; that when Vespasian took the throne it once again accepted a just and orderly government—or, as the prophet puts it, did homage to the God of heaven; that there was no such revival in the holy city, but only wilder fury and more utter brutality among those who claimed to honor the God of heaven there; and that the very restoration of order in Rome was therefore the signal for Jerusalem's doom. This, I take it, is what the prophet means by "The second woe is past, and, behold, the third woe cometh quickly." Rome's great earthquake is over; the man who restores and delivers that city is the one destined to destroy this one. For now,

VII. "*The seventh angel soundeth.*" That the fall of the Temple follows immediately upon the trumpet blast I gather not only from everything earlier in this chapter but from its last verse: "And the temple of God was opened in heaven, and there was seen in his temple the ark of his testament; and there were lightnings, and voices, and thunderings, and an earthquake, and great hail." The temple in heaven is the backdrop to the picture of the great fire consuming the temple on earth. The ark and the covenant are seen to be there; the lightnings and thunderings that shatter the one reveal the other. And so it happens that what seemed the most terrible of all disasters is celebrated in these words: "And there were great voices in heaven, saying, The kingdoms of this world are become the kingdoms of our Lord, and of his Christ; and he shall reign for ever and ever." Since last Sunday I spoke of "the finishing of the mystery of God," promised "in the days of the voice of the seventh angel, when he shall begin to sound," I need not dwell long on these words, because, wonderful as they are, they are the necessary sequel to that beginning. If the temple on Mount Moriah bore witness to a Lord God of Israel whom no man had seen or could see, and to an anointed king who would reign over David's house and whose kingdom would have no end, then the fall of that temple either proved that testimony false or declared that the Lord God of Israel is the Lord God of all nations, that the Son of David is the King of men. In words, all Christians admit that the passing away of the Jewish state carried this proclamation with it. There are hymns taught to English children about God now calling the world His own and about heathens tasting His grace. But we have come to treat these as merely rhetorical phrases. We say that in a sense it is no doubt true that God reigns over the nations—meaning that in every practical and important sense it is not true. So when we hear of voices in heaven solemnly declaring that the kingdoms of this world are the kingdoms of God and His Christ, we naturally say they must refer to some quite different event from the fall of Jerusalem, to some time we have never reached. For we reasonably trust that in the language of heaven there are no rhetorical phrases—that its hymns celebrate real, not fictitious, blessings. It would indeed be daring profanity to confuse our idle talk about the world being God's world with the ascription John speaks of in the sixteenth verse.

VIII. "*And the four and twenty elders, which sat before God on their seats, fell upon their faces, and worshipped God, saying, We give thee thanks, O Lord God Almighty, which art, and wast, and art to come; because thou hast taken to thee thy great power and hast reigned.*" But would it be profanity to suppose that they understand the vital meaning of events that we have drained of meaning? Would it be profanity to admit that we have been utterly unthankful for what should have filled us with wonder and gratitude? Would it be profanity to ask whether the Apostles had any message to deliver to the world at all—whether we have any—if the message is not this: that the kingdoms of the world belong to God and His Christ, and not to the tyrants who have claimed them, nor to the devil or his angels?

IX. In any case, I believe we shall find that this and no other is the teaching of the Apocalypse, pressing on us more and more in each vision that follows this one about the overthrow of the holy city and Temple. If the visions were all bright and joyful, we might have good reason to conclude they had nothing to do with the last eighteen hundred years of the world, or with our own day. But they are pictures of fierce conflict. The news that God has taken His great power and is reigning, which stirs such joy in heaven, finds little faith on earth. "The nations," it says, "were angry." They did not like being told of a righteous Judge. They were not eager to acknowledge One who cared for the poor and outcast, or who had taken their nature and died their death. God's government and His Christ's were far too unlike their own for them to want to live under it. But it adds, "Thy wrath is come." Their wishes could not change the fact. They were angry because they felt there was a power over them with which they were out of harmony, and which would prevail against them. And this power, the prophet goes on, is felt among the dead as well as the living. Being the power of Him who was and is and is to come, the power of the Lamb who was slain and lives forever, it cannot be confined by mortal boundaries; it cannot be limited by the sleep that closes off our years.

X. "*It was the time of the dead, that they should be judged, and that thou shouldest give reward unto thy servants the prophets, and to the saints, and to them that fear thy name, both small and great.*" I take the words as I find them; they may shatter some of our notions and theories; they may open up visions of judgment different from those we have inherited or made for ourselves. But if they are revelations of God, we must not shrink from them. We can be sure they will deepen and widen our faith, not shake it; that they will not lessen our awe, but make it more constant and more effective; that if they forbid any thoughts of God inconsistent with belief in His perfect love and full redemption, they will also not allow us to attribute to Him, or to keep in ourselves, the slightest tolerance for evil. The time has come that thou "shouldest destroy them that destroy the earth." This was a cause of the elders' rejoicing when the earthly temple fell and the heavenly temple was opened; and this must reconcile us to the fall of even what is dearest and most sacred to us—what has been dearest and most sacred in God's eyes—if it stops being a witness for Him, if it hides the ark of His testament from men.

## LECTURE XII. THE WOMAN AND THE MAN-CHILD.

*The passage:* A great sign appeared in heaven: a woman clothed with the sun, the moon under her feet, and a crown of twelve stars on her head. She was pregnant and cried out in the pain of childbirth. Then another sign appeared: a great red dragon with seven heads, ten horns, and seven crowns, whose tail swept a third of the stars down to earth. The dragon stood before the woman, ready to devour her child as soon as it was born. She gave birth to a son who would rule all nations with a rod of iron, and her child was caught up to God and to his throne. The woman fled to a place prepared by God in the wilderness, to be fed there for 1,260 days. War broke out in heaven: Michael and his angels fought the dragon, who fought back with his angels but was defeated and lost his place in heaven. The great dragon—the old serpent, called the Devil and Satan, who deceives the whole world—was cast down to the earth with his angels. A loud voice in heaven declared that now salvation, strength, the kingdom of God, and the power of his Christ had come, because the accuser of the brethren, who accused them before God day and night, had been cast down. They overcame him by the blood of the Lamb and by the word of their testimony, and they did not cling to their lives even in the face of death. So the heavens and those in them should rejoice, but woe to the earth and sea, for the devil has come down with great wrath, knowing his time is short. When the dragon saw he was cast down, he pursued the woman; she was given the two wings of a great eagle to fly into the wilderness, to her place, where she is nourished for a time, times, and half a time, away from the serpent. The serpent poured water like a flood after her to sweep her away, but the earth helped her by opening its mouth and swallowing the flood. The dragon, enraged with the woman, went off to make war against the rest of her offspring—those who keep God's commandments and hold the testimony of Jesus Christ.

In the previous vision the earthly temple vanished, and a temple in heaven was opened. The first thing the prophet's spirit sees in that higher region is a woman clothed with the sun, with the moon under her feet and a crown of twelve stars on her head.

It must truly have been a wonder to him. Nothing could more strikingly show the contrast between the old world that was passing and the new world that was beginning. That old world has often been called—whether in praise or blame—a male world. The description fits the Jew at least as strongly as the Roman. It would be very unjust to either to say they lacked reverence for women. Among the Jews it was fostered by all their institutions and prophecies; it colors some of their most striking legends and their most certain records, as it does the traditions the Roman republican cherished. But the man was singled out to be the servant and witness of Jehovah, especially in those acts in which the woman could not share. The fear that some standard of worth other than the male might slip into the State and weaken it was a chief reason the older Romans dreaded the coming of Greek art and learning. For in Greece there had floated a vision of surpassing beauty that could not be embodied in an Apollo alone. Pallas and Aphrodite contended with him for supremacy, though the sternness of the one and the merely passive grace of the other showed how difficult and dangerous it was to lose sight of that other ideal.

What this change means—whether it has necessarily brought effeminacy, whether it marks progress or decline—are questions that have occupied thinkers in every age, never more than now. This heavenly vision, bound up as it is with the whole purpose of the Apocalypse, and beginning a series of visions, may, I believe, shed light on this subject—light we need almost as much for our daily and practical guidance as for the study of history.

I. The wonder the Apostle saw is exactly the wonder that has accompanied Christendom through every stage of its life and growth. The mother and the child, in one form or another, have been present to everyone who has tried to work out what the human is and how it relates to the Divine. All have felt, more or less clearly, "What we are seeking is there; how, we cannot say. Perhaps it cannot be put into words at all. Perhaps the symbol itself is all we need." Romanists and Protestants have felt their difference, their intense opposition, nowhere more strongly than when this subject presses itself upon them—as it always does. They sense that here must lie the very secret of their quarrels. And yet they dimly feel that here too, if they could go a little deeper, might lie the secret of their reconciliation. May not some neglect or reversal of the law that binds this mother to this child explain many corruptions that have made men in the new world long for what seemed the simpler, manlier glories of the old? May not a true grasp of that law vindicate both the old and the new, and prove that the new is truly a blessed unfolding of what was hidden or imperfect in the old? And may not the book that claims to be a revelation of Jesus Christ give us that unfolding?

The woman is clothed with the sun. She has no brightness or glory of her own. Yet she is covered with brightness and glory. She lives in perfect light. She can look up to the source of light. The crown of twelve stars on her head distinguishes the brightness that is around her—which to those looking up from below seems to be hers—from that which she herself sees, from the true Divinity she confesses and worships. The moon at her feet shows that a reflection of this Divinity does come from her, but she must not become enamored of it. If that image attracts her, she will meet the fate of Narcissus: her self-love will destroy her.

Here is a very striking symbol of humanity. Nothing from the old world comes anywhere near it in completeness. That male ideal I spoke of struggles in vain to be more than national. When it aspired to be more in the Greek, it sank into effeminacy and slavery. When it aspired to be more in the Roman, it became imperialism. When it aspired to be more in the Jew, it either narrowed into the tightest sectarianism, or else broke out into this very type of humanity we are now contemplating.

II. But though more perfect than anything the world had yet seen, it is still imperfect. Another element is missing. "And she being with child cried, travailing in birth, and pained to be delivered." Something is needed to connect this female ideal with the male ideal of the earlier ages. That must be expanded as well as this. That must be brought into the closest harmony with this. The woman is not merely looking up to a light above her. She is struggling with a birth. The Divine must be above the human; but it must also come forth from the human. There must be a man-child. All the struggles and conflicts in society and in the individual are the throes leading up to that birth. All the conflicts the individual or society has undergone are conflicts over whether that child shall live or die. For—

III. "There appeared another wonder in heaven. And behold, a great red dragon, having seven heads and ten horns and seven crowns upon his heads. And his tail drew the third part of the stars of heaven, and did cast them to the earth. And the dragon stood before the woman which was ready to be delivered, for to devour her child as soon as it was born." I must remind you again that the last vision of the Apocalypse—if we interpret it strictly, as I try to—points to a definite critical moment in the world's history: as I think, that period of three or four years when Jerusalem was threatened by the Roman armies and Rome herself seemed to be in a death-struggle. Seen from above, what does this crisis mean? It means the struggle over whether humanity shall have its true and rightful king, or whether some other power shall rule over it and receive its homage. That power is pictured as a bloody dragon. The Lamb, you remember, had the seven horns and seven eyes that are the seven Spirits of God going out into all the earth. There we have the symbol of concentrated, harmonized power, coming from one loving Will, aimed at one loving end; the symbol of life in all its measures and forms, coming from the Source of life, to quicken, renew, and unite. Here we have the opposing symbol: seven heads and ten horns—powers intellectual and physical, going out not from one creative mind to achieve a blessed result in various ways, but from a destructive mind, destructive, and therefore broken into different shapes to spread death. This is the dragon that has fought man in every generation of the world—fighting to separate him from the loving, eternal God; fighting to make him see God under divided forms, to make him mistake and pervert God's purposes, to make him build the Divine Spirit out of his own notions instead of letting his notions be shaped and re-formed by the Divine Spirit. This is the dragon, then, who tried to tempt the Divine Son while He was on earth into distrusting and denying His Father, into separating Himself from the creatures whose nature He had taken. And that same dragon is shown here struggling to keep men from confessing the Conqueror of the evil spirit as both the Son of Man and the Son of God.

This dragon is said to draw a third of the stars of heaven and cast them to the earth. Whenever spiritual beings are led to worship themselves, to glory in their own power, to revere power for its own sake, they are dragged down from heaven to the earth. Being stars meant to spread light, they become powers of darkness. All creation is blighted by their influence. Men have believed there was such a fall of spirits. If you ask me how many texts of Scripture they have had to support that belief, I would answer: if they had only this one, it would be all the more remarkable that the faith should be so widespread, should have ruled so many different ages, penetrated so deeply into the heart of nations, taken so many forms of art and literature, and defied so much scorn and disbelief. And this faith, though it may have had various forerunners and foretastes in the ancient world, is strictly a Christian faith. The revelation of our own spiritual capacities and temptations has shown us how the dragon's tail might sweep down the stars of heaven. Every man has known, at some crisis of his life, longings, insights, and hopes that could not belong to an animal but must belong to a spirit. He has felt these were emphatically human longings, insights, hopes. They were not his own. They lifted him above himself. They made him understand what other men and other ages had felt. They found their voice in cries for deliverance and acts of thanksgiving. He knew he had something in him capable of communion with what he could not comprehend. It is possible—we all know it is—to fall from this state of mind, to treat it as utterly fantastic and ridiculous, and at the same time to hate it, as if the sense of it were haunting us and we could not drive it away. And so the thought of how spirits may have fallen connects itself with the resistance we feel when our own spirits are trying to rise. Is there not One who knows all our needs, who is helping us against our enemies, who offers Himself to us as the Divine man, the Lord of our spirits? Is not that the Man-child, feeble yet mighty? Is not the dragon seeking to kill Him as soon as He is born?

IV. "And she brought forth a man-child, who was to rule all nations with a rod of iron, and her child was caught up to God and to his throne." This week we shall be celebrating the mystery of our Lord's ascension. Does that mystery have anything to do with the words I have just read? It seems to me, my brethren, that all the acts of our Lord's life—His Birth of the Virgin, His Cross and Passion and Resurrection—have to do with them. All were steps in the manifestation of the true King and Conqueror of men. All were steps in the redemption of humanity. And the ascension was, if we believe St. Paul, the highest of these acts—the leading of man's captivity captive, the declaration of Christ's royalty. I would never separate that ascension from the one spoken of here. The very worth of heavenly visions is that they bring under one law and principle events that in the records of time are kept apart. In this case I do not think they are separated by any wide interval. The words, "Verily, verily, I say unto you, this generation shall not pass away till all these things be fulfilled," I have taken throughout these Lectures in their literal force. At the very moment when the kingdom of God on earth seemed to be ceasing, when the pledges of any union between heaven and earth were vanishing, then the kingdom of heaven that Christ had preached is actually inaugurated; then the pledges of union are made good. That disappearance of the signs is itself the sign that the representative of humanity is caught up to God and His Throne.

Divines have a technical way of handling this subject. They speak of an actual Christ born into the world, and of a mystical Christ born in the hearts of some men. Philosophical students of history have made use of this distinction, treating the Christian idea as merely an idea, and showing how such an idea had been floating in men's minds in every country and age. It seems to me that St. John the divine understood the whole matter better than these later divines, and that for him the actual Christ and the mystical Christ are never separated. No one is so careful to set forth the Man who sat by the well, ate the Passover supper, whose soul was troubled, whose feet and hands were pierced by nails. No one is so certain that this was the Word, the light of every man, made flesh. So when He had died for the sheep and risen again, He simply returns to His natural state, to the glory He had with the Father before the worlds began. But He returns after making it known to His disciples that He is the Vine and they the branches; that all their life is from Him and in Him; that apart from Him they are nothing and can do nothing. What He had taught them, He sent them to teach the world. Everyone who received the message discovered Christ to be the teacher of his spirit, the source of his life. His conscience had told him of such a one before; now he knew His name. The Jewish prophecies and heathen traditions had said the world must have a Divine king who was also a human king. When the Jewish world passed away, He is revealed as Lord of the universe. The woman has brought forth the Man-child. He is to rule the nations with a rod of iron. Humanity is not entering a merely feminine age—though, seen only in its earthly condition, that is the highest and most universal symbol by which it can be represented. There is to be dominion and government; the Man-child is to rule the nations. There is to be no lazy tenderness. With the rod of His wrath He is to slay the wicked. It is a shepherd's rod, but a rod of iron too; let the tyrants of the earth know it and tremble.

V. "And the woman fled into the wilderness, where she hath a place prepared of God, that they should feed her there a thousand two hundred and three score days." Can all the commentators have been wrong in supposing that the woman here must mean the Church? If not, must I not change the meaning I gave the symbol earlier? I cannot doubt the commentators are right, and that this vision does describe the Church's condition during a period of unusual desolation. But since the Apostle does not use the same images as before, I think we are bound to ask why he changed them, and to learn the new lesson the change must be meant to teach. When he spoke of six candlesticks and said the candlesticks were Churches, I assumed he meant to describe the Churches as lights of the world; when he speaks of a woman without saying he means the Church, I cannot simply swap one name for the other. I must find out from the context and the analogy of Scripture what the symbol naturally means, and then, if the occasion calls for it, apply that discovery to the Church. Doing so, we may hope to reach St. John's own idea of the Church, instead of forcing an idea of our own upon him.

Suppose the twelve hundred and sixty days are that period of darkness when the old witnesses of law and priesthood were prophesying in sackcloth and were about to be destroyed for a time; suppose that was the crisis in which humanity was translated to a new glory, its Divine representative being caught up to the throne of God. Where then should we look for the representative of her new condition upon earth? Not in the city of Jerusalem, which has been denying this glory of man and is to perish in its denial. But those who believe that Jesus is the Christ and that He stands at the right hand of God as their Mediator and Advocate—they, banished from the city, with no country they can call home, no visible fellowship or unity—are nevertheless upholding the true greatness of the race. They are claiming the rights of spirits; they overlook the boundaries of space and time; they have a house not made with hands, eternal in the heavens.

The records of the Church during those particular years—indeed through all the later years of the first century—are especially scanty and confusing. Its numbers must have been greatly reduced; the love of many, as our Lord foretold, must have grown cold. Eusebius gives hints that seem to reflect the confusion of the time in the Church—of a decline that soon followed, especially in Palestine. In them are dim records of a flight to Pella. Bishops are said to have succeeded one another with incredible speed; often they bear heathen names. That the Church survived at all through such a time seemed altogether wonderful to human eyes. No fuller account of its preservation can be given than the one in the text: "She had a place prepared by God"; she was kept alive in the wilderness for twelve hundred and sixty days.

VI. And is that all? Do we only have an account of a few scattered Christians with no visible refuge or home for three or four years? Not quite. We have another record besides this.

"And there was war in heaven: Michael and his angels fought against the dragon; and the dragon fought and his angels, and prevailed not; neither was their place found any more in heaven. And the great dragon was cast out, that old serpent, called the Devil, and Satan, which deceiveth the whole world: he was cast out into the earth, and his angels were cast out with him. And I heard a loud voice saying in heaven, Now is come salvation, and strength, and the kingdom of our God, and the power of his Christ: for the accuser of our brethren is cast down, which accused them before our God day and night. And they overcame him by the blood of the Lamb, and by the word of their testimony; and they loved not their lives unto the death."

That insignificant sight of a handful of poor men, exiles from cities, unrecognized on earth, is suddenly exchanged for this wonderful sight of the hosts of heaven pouring out shouts and congratulations for the greatest victory ever won. What was the victory? The words describe it clearly enough if we heed them. There had been a tremendous barrier between man and God. An enemy was constantly arraigning man as a rebel, and constantly arraigning God as a tyrant. And now the Man-child presented men to God as redeemed, justified, perfected in Him—and presented God to men as their loving Father who had given up His only Son for them. The battle in heaven had this subject and this outcome, no other. Was there truly a bond of peace and fellowship between heaven and earth? Was it truly possible for man to meet God and God to meet man? Was there One in whom both could be well pleased? Was God drawing men to Him or pushing them away?—seeking to save them or to damn them?

Whatever other dreams we may have had about the Devil and his angels being cast out of heaven, this is St. John's account of it. The accuser of the brethren is no longer able to tear the creature away from the Creator. They are made one in the well-beloved Son. "And now is come salvation, and strength, and the kingdom of our God, and the power of his Christ." The two are inseparably, eternally joined. Christ has not exerted any power to change the will of the Father. His power has been shown in fulfilling that will, in making it effective. It is not God who has been keeping men at a distance from Him; it is the enemy of God. He is the condemner; God is the Justifier.

These are not principles that lasted only twelve hundred and sixty days, my brethren. They were proved in those twelve hundred and sixty days; they were established in those days, when the world looked most dreary and hopeless, as the principles on which Christendom and the whole world were standing. For the men of those days overcame the dragon by the blood of the Lamb and the word of His testimony. The blood of the Lamb was a perpetual witness to them that God had reconciled the world to Himself, not counting their trespasses against them. The blood of the Lamb was a living sacrament of a perpetual, living union between the children on earth and their Father in heaven. And the word of this testimony was that God so loved the world that He gave His only begotten Son for it. These were the answers to the accuser, answers that rose above all the arguments and subtleties by which he would try to persuade men that they are an accursed and not a redeemed and blessed race; that death is a sign of separation from God and from one another—as it must seem when we look at any single case—rather than a bond of reconciliation and atonement to God and to one another, as it must be when we look at it in the death of Christ, the Head, Representative, and Redeemer of the whole race. And so these witnesses did not cling to their lives even in the face of death; they could throw their lives away as witnesses for the truth, knowing that the truth was worth more than their lives, and that they could trust their lives with the God of Truth. The male courage of the old time had not departed. The Christian martyrs were to be its guardians and to hand it on.

VII. "Therefore rejoice, ye heavens, and ye that dwell in them. Woe to the inhabiters of the earth, and the seas, for the devil is come down to you, having great wrath, because he knoweth that he hath but a short time."

The dragon was cast down from heaven. Those who claimed their portion in God saw an unclouded face. Those who looked into the eternal world—so full of dark shadows before—now saw that it was full of light. This vision was not for angels, but for sinful, dying men. The kingdom of heaven had been opened to them by Him who had borne sin and overcome the sharpness of death; even in the midst of torments they could dwell in it. The woe and trouble were for those who inhabited the earth and the sea—for those who could not look beyond the visible and temporal. These too had been redeemed and united to the other world. But if they would not acknowledge the reconciliation, if they tried to interpret the universe by the events passing within it, they could find no ground for hope; everything was a reason for despair. It was a time when the devil held sway over the world—a short time, for whatever our unbelief may pretend, such times of ascendancy are short. They bring to an end long stretches of indifference and heartlessness; they act as cures for atheism; they drive men, in spite of themselves, to a good God. But who can tell how dreadful they are while they last? Who can measure their anguish by minutes or by years?

VIII. "And when the dragon saw that he was cast upon the earth, he persecuted the woman which brought forth the man-child. And to her were given two wings of a great eagle, that she might fly into the wilderness, to her place, where she is nourished for a time, and times, and half a time, from the face of the serpent."

The child is beyond the dragon's reach. He has triumphed. But the mother of the child is still exposed to the dragon's violence. That is the formula for the history of the modern world—for the history of humanity since the Son of Man was revealed. Part of the battle, and the most tremendous part, which St. John calls the battle in heaven, had been fought. But the woman's battle—a battle with the dragon in his various forms, now tempting by prosperity, now by adversity, now with one head and horn to the fore, now another—this had still to be fought; and this is the subject of all records from St. John's day to our own.

I do not, however, extend the direct application of this passage beyond the crisis I have already spoken of. The phrase "time, and times, and half a time" is no doubt chosen for some reason—perhaps to signal that these events belong to a definite period but have a meaning that reaches into other periods. Yet the period itself seems to be the same as the 1,260 days; the flight into the wilderness in verse six cannot be distinguished from the one in verse fourteen. What is further said is that the woman is carried into her hiding place on the wings of a great eagle. "The great eagle" would be more literal; one reading is "the two wings of the great eagle." Both the usual emblem of the conquering city and the usage of the Apocalypse allow us to take Rome as the protector here. The eagle that was about to swoop down on the Jerusalem carcass rescued the witnesses for mankind—for the redemption of both Jew and Gentile—from the oppression of the Jewish sects, and from their own false position among those sects. The dwelling in the wilderness seems to imply a suspension of all visible ordinances, all outward fellowship. It was a necessary transition, a time in which the Church was to come to know herself before entering her new calling as herald of a Divine kingdom to the imperial world. But there were dangers besetting her in that solitude.

IX. "And the serpent cast out of his mouth water as a flood after the woman, that he might cause her to be carried away of the flood. And the earth helped the woman, and the earth opened her mouth and swallowed up the flood which the dragon cast out of his mouth."

Jerusalem had been a hot-bed of sects or heresies. We think we have described them all when we name Pharisees, Sadducees, and perhaps Essenes. There must have been many more within these great political divisions, and many outside them. The Samaritans were another element; they had always been more prone than their neighbors to spiritual impostures, and less tied down by orthodox formalities. The Judaism of Alexandria, closely allied with Gentile philosophy, was rich in all sorts of conceptions and imaginings. These turned especially on the nature of the Divine Word who had spoken to the fathers, on the relation He would bear to the coming Messiah, and on the offices, powers, and relations of angels to God and men. What waters these were for the Church to float in, once she was loosed from her old moorings! All simple faith, at a time when faith was most needed, must have been threatened, if not lost, in them. But there was a merciful deliverance from this evil then, as there has been since. The Apostle's words point to it very strikingly. The arguments of learned doctors go very little way toward settling controversies; usually they only increase them and add to the bewilderment of those suffering from them. But the earth swallows them up. They may be very sublime, heavenly speculations—who knows? But they do not meet the daily needs, business, and sufferings of earth. And after all, what we ask for is that which will meet them. Opinions with the clearest warrant of antiquity, or the finest gloss of novelty, must be brought to this test: What can they do for us? You say they are very probable; there is great evidence for them. No doubt; but I want something I can rest on—not something that is very likely, but something that is. Thanks to the good honest earth, which in due time soaks up all watery notions and leaves only what has proved itself substantial.

X. "And the dragon was wroth with the woman, and went to make war with the remnant of her seed, which keep the commandments of God, and have the testimony of Jesus Christ."

The dragon had good reason for his wrath. He had trusted the earth as his friend, and it had proved his foe. The plain solid work and hard suffering that he thought would make men forget the Divine kingdom have really forced them to break through the mists that were hiding that kingdom from them. Other means than these must be tried. The next vision will tell us what they are. But so that we may know why they are used, and what the dragon is trying to destroy, this great simple lesson is left with us for all of us to take to heart. The battle is not with those who hold some system they call Christianity. It is with those who keep the commandments of God and the testimony of Jesus Christ—with those who hold fast the command to love God with heart and soul and strength, and their neighbors as themselves; with those who hold fast the testimony of Jesus Christ, that the God who commands us to love Him has first loved us, has given His Son for us all, has justified and glorified that Son as our Mediator and Advocate at His right hand, and has sent His Spirit of Love to form us after His image. With these the Spirit of Evil fought in the old time, fights now, and will fight until either he or they are vanquished. We have declared this day at God's altar that we are engaged in that struggle, that we have failed in it, and that there is only one strength that can enable us to prevail. And He has said to us this day: You may be more than conquerors through the might of Him who loved you. By the blood of the Lamb, the weakest of you may overcome.

## LECTURE XIII. THE DRAGON AND THE TWO BEASTS.

*The passage:* John stands on the seashore and watches a beast come up out of the sea, with seven heads and ten horns, crowns on its horns and a blasphemous name on its heads. It looks like a leopard, with a bear's feet and a lion's mouth, and the dragon gives it his power and throne and great authority. One of its heads seems to have been mortally wounded, but the wound heals, and the whole world marvels and follows the beast, worshipping both the dragon and the beast and saying no one can compare with it or fight it. It is given a mouth to speak great blasphemies and power to act for forty-two months; it blasphemes God, his name, his dwelling, and those in heaven; it is allowed to make war on the saints and overcome them, and to rule over all peoples. Everyone whose name is not in the Lamb's book of life will worship it. Whoever leads into captivity will go into captivity; whoever kills with the sword will be killed with the sword — here is the patience and faith of the saints. Then a second beast rises from the earth, with two lamb-like horns but a dragon's voice; it wields the first beast's power, makes the earth worship the first beast, works wonders such as making fire fall from heaven, deceives people by these miracles, and tells them to make an image of the first beast that had been wounded and lived. It gives the image life and voice, and has all who refuse to worship it killed. It forces everyone, of every rank, to receive a mark on their right hand or forehead, so that no one can buy or sell without the mark, the beast's name, or the number of its name. Here is wisdom: let the one who understands count the number of the beast, for it is a man's number, and it is six hundred sixty-six.

I. There is something vivid in how this vision is described, if we keep the reading of the first word that our translators adopted. The prophet is carried to the shore of his little island; as he looks at the water, this ugly creature rises out of it.

But there is another reading with more authority behind it, and one that fits the whole thrust of the vision better. It uses the third person instead of the first. It is the dragon, not the prophet, who stands by the seashore. The beast that rises out of the sea is the dragon's own earthly counterpart. In that case the sea, like the beast, carries a symbolic meaning. Symbol and fact are then less confused with each other.

I have said the beast is the earthly counterpart of the dragon; that is the first thing to keep in mind when working out what this beast is. The dragon is above all a spiritual power — the self-willed power, the destructive power. He has been cast out of heaven; he can no longer smear the image of the One who sits on the throne in the eyes of those who dwell there, who see the Man-child at the right hand of that throne. But he can wage war against that Man-child on earth; he can set up a rival dominion against His. Next, remember that such a rival dominion has, according to the Bible, existed in every age. This last book of the Bible views it at one particular crisis, when it had reached its fullest growth.

The language of the previous chapter, taken literally, forces us to think of Christ's actual birth into the world as, in some sense, the start of this crisis. Yet it also fixes a period of forty-two months, or 1,260 days, as the time when the battle was at its peak and its outcome was near. The image of a wild beast who is the counterfeit of a dragon seems to require — nearly all interpreters have thought it requires — fulfilment in some person who would fully embody an evil principle. To reconcile these different requirements, to find something that answers to them all, is the problem. There are other requirements too. There is a description of this beast in the seventeenth chapter, and nothing we conclude here should contradict it. I think it would be out of place to discuss that description now, since it is tangled up with matters we cannot deal with yet. When we come to it in due course, we can consider how far it fits the interpretation I am about to offer.

Seeing that the establishment of the Roman military despotism after the battle of Actium nearly coincides with the birth of the Son of David; and seeing that this despotism, after passing through its stages under Augustus, Tiberius, Caligula, Claudius, and Nero, was put on trial in the three turbulent reigns that followed and emerged in its most brutal form in the person of Vitellius — I take this to be the wild beast in which the dragon saw his own image reflected. The prophet sees it first in the mild form it wore when Octavius rose from being a triumvir to being the patron of the arts, the preserver of order, the object of poetic flattery, when all the names and forms of the Republic were (as the great historian observes) so cleverly kept up to hide the tyranny beneath them; and then in the form it took when that tyranny threw off its disguises and came out as nothing but the will of the soldiers, propped up by nothing but their weapons. Because this beast is shown as having a continuous existence through these reigns, it appears with seven heads. Why seven and not eight, we shall learn later. The ten horns are, I take it, the legions in the different provinces. The real kingship lies in them: the beast keeps its power only through them. Yet it has another power distinct from theirs. On every one of the heads, from the first to the last, was the name *Blasphemy*. Octavius, in becoming an emperor, becomes a god. A poet as restrained and wise as Virgil, so full of the older Italian spirit, would not have given him that title out of mere exaggerated gratitude if it had shocked his countrymen, if they had not accepted it as the natural and necessary meaning of his rise to rule the world. In no case is it truer that "words are things." The name worked its way into all legal forms and into all popular writings. The general of the armies really was the god to whom the Capitoline Jove paid homage. He protected the city that the old republican object of worship had failed to protect. He was to take care of the worship of the gods; and they, in return, were to uphold his throne. But to all intents and purposes he was their master, not they his.

Out of that great sea of troubles — the civil wars after Caesar's death and the wars in which Antony and Augustus defied each other — this beast arose. To say there had never been anything like it in the earlier world would contradict all history, and sacred history most of all. Every military empire had combined these qualities. In each of them the god and the beast had been mingled, and in each the beast finally proved victorious. It would be just as false to say there would be no similar manifestation in ages to come. Perhaps every age might have such a manifestation, each repeating this story with its own variations. But here was the great climax of the ancient world's attempts at universal empire, and here was the pattern for all that would be made in the new. Let no one think he refutes this account of it by showing that it was inevitable — that the Republic could not survive without a head, that the plan of Brutus and those who dreamed of preserving the old order necessarily began in an outrage against order and ended in swapping a noble tyranny for a vulgar one. All this may be granted, and it is fully consistent with the vision. The beast was the natural product of earlier evils. That did not make it any less evil.

II. "And the beast which I saw was like a leopard, and his feet were like a bear's, and his mouth like a lion's; and the dragon gave him his power, and seat, and great authority."

A despotism spotted with the forms of law and freedom; resting on the rough, wild passions of a mob; yet always speaking through its soldiers — this answers to the beast's various outward appearances. Its inner heart is the dragon's.

If the dragon's aim is to destroy the government of the Man-child, here is the government he sets against it; here is his image of a throne above all thrones, and of an unlimited, universal rule.

III. "And I saw one of the heads as if it were wounded to death, and the deadly wound was healed: and all the world wondered after the beast. And they worshipped the dragon which gave power to the beast: and they worshipped the beast, saying, Who is like unto the beast? who is able to make war with him?"

Surely a vivid description of the empire after the death of any one of its successive tyrants, but especially after Nero's death. Then the beast seemed mortally wounded. A power whose malice had seemed limitless, and which had been felt in every province and every household, had actually fallen. Could such a tyranny ever rise again? Had the world not thrown off its chains once and for all? But "the deadly wound was healed." The beast was not really gone; only one of the forms in which its nature had appeared for a while had gone. Another monster was ready to replace the old one — in some ways an even more unqualified display of brutality, a more complete incarnation of the devilish. And what was the result? "All the world wondered after the beast." Tyranny in its vilest form is accepted as the normal, appointed state of things — and more than that, it becomes an object of admiration. It is not only that the neck has grown used to the collar. Men's souls have grown used to it, have learned to love it, are shaped to match it. "And they worshipped the dragon which gave power unto the beast." They believe in the omnipotence of evil; they rejoice in it, they pay homage to it and to nothing else. But evil in its pure essence is too rare and subtle for their everyday devotion. They prefer it in a concrete, earthly form. They "worshipped the beast, saying, Who is like unto the beast? who is able to make war with him?" Can't you hear the words coming across all these centuries from two young Romans chatting as they lounge in the Forum? They once had noble thoughts; they had heard of their fathers' deeds; they had dreamed there might be some possible good for their age. But they have become drunken, dissolute gamblers. And someone even more monstrously drunken, dissolute, and gambling than they are has become their ideal of what is desirable and possible. Who is like him? who can make war with him? These two youths fairly represent the age they live in. There is nothing original about them. They think what everyone else thinks. Their private opinion is simply the public opinion of the city and of the world.

But this wild beast, though from time to time it takes the shape of a particular man, and finally finds one in which little of the human is left, must still be regarded mainly as representing sheer force — especially the force of an unruly army that knows it can bestow the purple on whomever it likes. That is how I understand the next verse.

IV. "And there was given him a mouth speaking great things and blasphemies; and power was given him to continue forty and two months. And he opened his mouth in blasphemy against God, to blaspheme his name, and his tabernacle, and them that dwell in heaven. And it was given him to make war with the saints, and to overcome them; and power was given him over all kindreds, and tongues, and nations." The forty-two months here, as elsewhere, denote, I take it, the whole time of lawlessness before Vespasian's accession and the restoration of regular government. During that time the sufferings of every province in the empire, at the hands of an army that Galba's aged hand tried in vain to restrain, that mocked Otho's softness, and that found nothing but encouragement in the vileness of Otho's successor, must have been utterly intolerable. The blasphemy against God, His name, and His tabernacle was no doubt provoked and sharpened by the belief that the Jews, who had talked of an invisible God, were at once the worst of all peoples and doomed to extinction. Yet I understand their blasphemies to be against all right and order, not specially against the witness for it borne by one class of men or another. Their victory over the saints may mean that whenever they came upon a man who had any reverence for right, they saw in him an enemy, someone to be put down — not by any legal process, but rather by summary execution. And to this desperate military violence and tyranny — which was the proper outcome of the despotism, though it had no real despot, only a figurehead chosen as its instrument — all peoples, tongues, and nations were for a while handed over.

V. "And all that dwell upon the earth shall worship him, whose names are not written in the book of life of the Lamb slain from the foundation of the world." It does not say that all who did not belong to the Christian community would worship this beast of sheer power, or would believe in the prevalence and omnipotence of evil. Very likely many who did belong to that community joined in this general, almost universal worship — subscribing in their hearts, if not with their tongues, to the creed that the dragon was supreme lord. Only those who, whatever name they went by, however little known to one another, were known to God as confessing in their hearts a self-sacrificing King to be the highest King — only those who revered a Prince of Life rather than a destroyer — could resist the temptation to acknowledge as supreme that which to all appearances was proving itself supreme. Their names are said to be written in the book of the Lamb that is slain. Whether we take the prophet to mean that the Lamb was slain from the foundation of the world, or that their names had been written in the book from the foundation of the world, the lesson is the same. An eternal Deliverer is the only refuge from this great world-tyranny. A faith would be useless if it did not look before and after, and connect itself with all mankind. It was necessary to contemplate a Lamb who had always been offering himself up, who came into the world to do what it was His nature and His Father's will that He should do; otherwise there was no sufficient counterweight against the crushing pressure of present wickedness, no sufficient assurance that it might not last forever. There is no need to ask who were or were not armed with this protection. Thank God, the book of life is the Lamb's, and is not open to the inspection or correction of any man or any Church.

VI. "If any man have an ear," the prophet goes on, "let him hear. He that leadeth into captivity shall go into captivity; he that killeth with the sword must be killed with the sword. Here is the patience and the faith of the saints."

A most necessary reminder for that time, and for every time since — worthy to be introduced with the same solemn preparation our Lord used, in the days of His flesh, when He wanted attention paid to His own words. Hear, you suffering people! This dark, devilish tyranny seems as though it were destined to last forever. It has grown worse and worse. Each change of masters has made it worse. Division among the masters seems to be the worst state of all. You do not know whom to ally yourselves with. Each party's success only promises you fresh misery. Wait, I tell you; wait; join no party; enter no conspiracies. If you do, you share their doom. For there is an eternal law that this beast cannot overturn, though he imagines he can. He who leads into captivity shall go into captivity, for a Redeemer, not an enslaver, is Lord of all. He who kills with the sword shall be killed with the sword, for there is a Judge who laughs at men's might and will show how weak it is before Him. This faith you may hold fast, for the Lamb that was slain held it fast while He was on earth. This may be your patience, for it was His. It went with Him through death, and now He is far above all principalities and powers and every name that is named here and in all other worlds.

VII. "And I beheld another beast coming out of the earth, and he had two horns like a lamb, and he spake as a dragon." A form apparently quite unlike the other. He is very soft and gentle. He has horns, but they are not meant to gore. He does not burst out of the stormy sea; he is born of no civil upheaval. He rises gradually out of the earth; he is the natural product of men's thoughts; he sympathizes with their weaknesses. And yet, when he begins to speak, it is like a dragon. Terrible threats come from him. This lamb-like creature wants to inspire fear just as much as the other does.

What is he? What sustained the imperial tyranny of Rome was the religion of Rome. In the old republican days that religion had contained many divine elements: the acknowledgment of fatherly authority, of sacred law, of righteous government in the heart of the city. But there had always been a tendency to turn these merely to the politician's advantage. The augurs were to be consulted, partly to learn the mind of the gods, partly so the people would suppose that mind was known. Sacrifices were offered, partly to confess wrongs done and proclaim the rule of right, partly to see whether wrong could not be made right by compensation, partly to persuade the people that wrong had been made right. There was this mixture of faith and unbelief, of truth and lies. It could not last. The civil wars drove out most of the faith and made the lies both more bearable and more necessary. The empire needed a religion perfectly fitted to its purposes. Then this beast rose out of a darkened earth, to meet men's natural fears and their confused notions about the unseen; holding out fair promises and dreadful predictions; the lamb and the dragon in one, ready to do for the tyrant whatever the tyrant could not do for himself.

VIII. "And he exerciseth all the power of the first beast before him, and causeth the earth and them which dwell therein to worship the first beast, whose deadly wound was healed. And he doeth great wonders, so that he maketh fire come down from heaven on the earth in the sight of men, and deceiveth them that dwell on the earth by the means of those miracles which he had power to do in the sight of the beast; saying to them that dwell on the earth, that they should make an image to the beast, which had the wound by a sword, and did live."

What I said of the first beast applies to this one. He did not first appear in the Roman world. In Egypt, in Chaldea, in every country where arbitrary rule prevailed, some form of false worship, some miracle-working enchanter or soothsayer, arose to prop up that arbitrary rule with unseen tricks and terrors, and to shape the minds of the subjects to fit it. Whenever in later ages the one beast has risen out of the sea and demanded the homage of nations worn out by earlier riots and conflicts, the other has risen out of the earth to secure that homage for it — with the same assumed lamb-like appearance, the same use of the dragon's cunning and the dragon's fury. But the prophet gives us a striking illustration of the whole matter by fixing on the particular moment in the history when the first beast had received the wound by the sword and yet lived. Apparently the imperial tyranny had been struck down by its own instrument. The sword had turned against it. Why, then, did it not die? Another influence was at work that would not let it die. A corrupted religion had so taught men to reverence sheer power, had so fixed the image of that power in their hearts, had so filled and blocked every avenue by which the thought of any other kind of power might have entered, that even when it had been crushed by its own proper masters, it sprang back into fresh life. That is what we are told in the fifteenth verse.

IX. "And he had power to give life unto the image of the beast, that the image of the beast should both speak and cause that as many as would not worship the image of the beast should be killed."

A fearful power! but one given, from time to time, to those who direct the spiritual and moral forces of a country. When some dark form of tyranny has fallen under the weight of its own crimes, by the hand of its own accomplices, they can and do bring it back to life. The sense of the unseen is powerful in all men — powerful even in wrongdoers, if only as dread of what may be coming. If those who claim to commune with the unseen world, instead of rousing men's consciences to a horror and hatred of lies, only fill them with fresh lies — playing with their hopes and their fears, and turning both to evil — they may restore, for a little while, the worst form of brutal power, and help it to destroy all that it hates, all who will not bow before it. That is the reason.

X. "And he causeth all, both small and great, rich and poor, free and bond, to receive a mark in their right hand, or in their foreheads: and that no man might buy or sell save he that had the mark, or the name of the beast, or the number of his name."

The words "he causeth" clearly attribute this to the *second* beast. If it had been the first, we might suppose some outward mark or sign was meant, for that beast deals with the visible and outward. But this one stamps an image on men's souls; it writes a name on all their inward thoughts, which then expresses itself in their ordinary daily acts. Taking these words in this sense, I know none more fearful, and none confirmed by such undoubted evidence from experience. When men read and talk about some great tyrant-power that has established itself in their country or their age, they imagine they are reading and talking about something far removed from themselves. They can comment on it, measure its effects, calculate the odds of its continuing or falling. If anyone complains that it is evil in its origin or immoral in its practice, wise people whisper, "But it does you no harm. You can buy and sell happily under its shadow. Your profits are not seriously reduced. You run no great risk of loss." And all the while these wise people do not realize that they themselves, as much as those they are talking to, have received this power's mark on their foreheads and their right hands; that its image is engraved in their hearts; that in these very conversations they are showing that they bear the name and character of the very thing they are excusing. This vision refers, as I have often said, to a moment when the *effects* of principles were no longer held down by any formal or legal restraints. And then something became visible that had not been visible before. It became clear that men follow, admire, and worship whatever matches some image already *within* them. It could never have come out in palpable form in a single man if hundreds of thousands — if the great majority of society — had not already received the impression. It grows deeper and darker with every fresh act of service and adoration. It is not the ruler's wit or wisdom that holds his subjects in chains. He may have lost his wit or wisdom, and they theirs. But the chains remain, because the subjects are what he is. They carry his name about with them. The God he obeys is the God they obey. What he does on a larger scale, each of them does or tries to do in his own little sphere. There is no bargain they make with one another that does not display the very spirit in which he governs peoples and tongues and nations. For men are bound together in a fellowship even if they become utterly and savagely selfish. They must have a common name, a common image, whether they like it or not. It may be the name of a Father or of a Dragon; it may be the image of a Christ or of an anti-Christ. But in the end, by the laws of the universe, one or the other will show itself — in the small and the great, in the rich and the poor, in the free and the enslaved.

XI. Besides the mark and the name, there is a reference at the end of this passage to the number *of the name*. We ask what that means. The chapter's closing verse gives the answer.

"Here is wisdom. Let him that hath understanding count the number of the beast; for it is the number of a man; and his number is Six hundred threescore and six." Obeying this command, interpreters who thought they had understanding have set to work very diligently to count this number. Since it is said to be the number of a man, their aim has been to find which man is likely meant, and then to see whether his name can somehow be made to add up to six hundred and sixty-six. The traditional method for settling this is to take the numerical values of the Greek letters in the name being tested and add them up. By this method — sometimes helped along by changing a letter, or slipping in an iota subscript when needed — I cannot say how many an eminent man, in one age or another, has been "proven" to be the beast St. John speaks of. It is rash, I know, to break with a rule of interpretation that has such long-standing usage in its favor; but I cannot think that this scheme's success obliges us to pick one out of the crowd of names various commentators have proposed, throwing out all the rest, or entitles us to hazard some new guess in the same line. Since I have suggested that Vitellius was that eighth head of the Roman empire who seemed to bring back all Nero's brutality, I might try whether his name, put into Greek letters, could by some clever counting be made to meet the requirement. But I suspect I would be overlooking other requirements just as necessary. I never heard that the name of Vitellius, or any other name, was ordered to be marked on the right hands or foreheads of the empire's inhabitants, or that no one might buy or sell without bearing it. Among all the acts of reckless and cruel tyranny recorded of Roman emperors or their successors, no hint of this strange and wanton use of power, I believe, has been preserved. But if we insist on an actual word, first turned into letters and then into numbers, we must stick to the text in these details too; we cannot be material one moment and spiritual the next.

It is best, I think, to set these attempts aside once for all, even if we are forced to leave the problem entirely unsolved. If I venture one more try at a solution, I am far from claiming it is satisfactory; I would gladly trade it for any simpler one discovered later. At least it will not clash with what I have already said about the mark and the name. Those, I believe, were stamped in no material letters, then or ever. The name or character of any age's great tyrant, I still assume, is imaged in those who have raised him to his evil height and who keep him there.

In counting the number of this name, I cannot help noticing how constantly the number *seven* appears in this book, and what meaning is always attached to it. Whether the prophet speaks of the seven candlesticks, the seven Spirits of God, the seven eyes, or the seven horns of the lamb, the idea presented to us is perfect unity that involves perfect distinctness.
