To a Jew's mind — and why not to an Englishman's? — the week is the simplest and yet profoundest illustration of this unity: the six days and the one day making the complete whole, the day of rest giving meaning, purpose, and harmony to the others, connecting the order of times and the life of man with the Being who made all things and made man in His own image.

Now suppose we found the number *six* used to denote the beast who embodies the anti-Christian principle — who heads the kingdom opposed to the kingdom of God. We should at once, I think, compare it with this perfect number, and conclude it was the symbol of a society whose head is man, not God — a society made of a mass of atoms without a center, work without a sabbath. Suppose, then, we have not one six but a series of sixes — six in the units, six in the tens, six in the hundreds — may not the meaning be the same? It is as if he had said: "Here is the number that denotes what is divided, as against what is united. Repeat this number as often as you like, you never reach unity. You have lost the secret of unity, the spiritual bond; no material accumulations will replace it. How to find a unity that does not depend on piling up numbers; how to bind the toil of the six days to the rest that completes it and gives it meaning — this you may learn if you will look at the next vision, if you will look at those who surround, not a wild beast, but a lamb, who bear not the name of a destroyer but the name of their Father written on their foreheads."

## LECTURE XIV. THE LAMB AND HIS FOLLOWERS.

(PREACHED ON WHITSUNDAY.)

*The passage:* John saw a Lamb standing on Mount Sion, and with him one hundred and forty-four thousand who had the Father's name written on their foreheads. He heard a great voice from heaven like rushing waters and thunder, and the sound of harpers playing, and they sang a new song before the throne, the four living creatures, and the elders; no one could learn that song except those hundred and forty-four thousand who had been redeemed from the earth. They are undefiled, virgins, who follow the Lamb wherever he goes; they were redeemed from among men as firstfruits to God and the Lamb, with no lie in their mouth, faultless before God's throne. John then saw an angel flying in mid-heaven with the everlasting gospel to preach to everyone on earth, every nation and tongue, saying loudly, Fear God and give him glory, for the hour of his judgment has come; worship the Maker of heaven, earth, sea, and springs. Another angel followed, announcing that Babylon the great city has fallen, because she made all nations drink the wine of the wrath of her fornication. A third angel followed, warning loudly that whoever worships the beast and its image and receives its mark will drink the undiluted wine of God's wrath, and be tormented with fire and brimstone before the holy angels and the Lamb, their torment rising forever, with no rest day or night. Here is the patience of the saints who keep God's commandments and the faith of Jesus. And a voice from heaven told John to write: Blessed are the dead who die in the Lord from now on; the Spirit says they rest from their labors, and their works follow them.

I. This vision brings to mind the one in the seventh chapter. There, as the angels went out with their trumpets to prepare for the fall of the accursed city, another angel was sealing twelve thousand from each tribe of Israel with the seal of the living God. The names of the tribes, so carefully listed there, do not appear again here. But the total number sealed is the same. The place, Mount Sion, ties them to the chosen people. Yet there are two new features: these hundred and forty-four thousand are gathered around the Lamb, and they are marked with their Father's name. I want especially to draw your attention today to the likeness between these visions and the difference between them. Whitsuntide, it seems to me, sheds clear light on the whole subject, and in turn is lit up by the Apocalypse.

(1.) The Feast of Pentecost was strictly a *Jewish* feast, though it gave thanks for the gifts of the common earth. Those who came to it treated Jerusalem as their center. At this Pentecost the Apostles spoke to them of a Son of David, and of David himself. What amazed everyone was that Galileans could speak with Jews living in all the different heathen cities about the works of the God of Abraham. Those who received the Spirit felt they were no longer a divided people scattered among the heathen, but a family that no distance could break apart. They were truly children of Abraham because they were children of God. Though they might be only a remnant of Israel, though most of their countrymen might go on calling the one they revered as a divine King a criminal and blasphemer, they stood for a united Israel. The nation the sects were tearing apart lived on in them.

(2.) And yet the ascended King was shown to be the King of the Jews precisely because he had conquered the death that held all men captive, because he could not remain in one earthly dwelling-place. He must therefore be the King of all men. However slow the Apostles were to learn that they were to proclaim him in that character, however important it was that there should be a time in which they grasped their position as Jews and showed their countrymen what that position meant, events would inevitably break down the barriers they could not break themselves. Their fellowship was essentially a human fellowship.

(3.) It was human because it was divine. Before Christ left them he had said, "I ascend to my Father and your Father; to my God and your God." And now he sent them the promise of this Father. He had himself explained that promise as the gift of another Comforter, who would stay with them forever, a Spirit of Truth, who would teach them about him and about his Father. The new name with which they were sealed was a name that Gentiles as much as Jews had been groping after. The solemn words, "The Lord thy God is One Lord," seemed to set all who had mixed human thoughts and feelings into God at an infinite distance. But now, when the highest showing of God's glory was in One who had taken flesh and become man; now, when a Spirit had been given who could teach them to glorify God most by magnifying his image most; now the hope of the Greek could be fulfilled as much as the hope of the Israelite: the kingdom of heaven could hold both. God's unity repelled no one and drew all.

(4.) These sealed members of the different tribes are now gathered on Mount Sion, around the Lamb. The place shows that they stand for the continuity of the Jewish family. But the Lamb who bore the sins of the world shows that they are no longer members of tribes, that their center is no longer in Jerusalem. The insect has passed through its stages of egg, caterpillar, and chrysalis. It has become winged. It can rise to heaven, or settle on any leaf or flower of earth. The chosen nation has expanded into the Church of the whole universe. It is a witness that men are not fatherless, are not slaves of a beast. It declares that they are redeemed and united in a living and glorified Head.

Perhaps you say the next words contradict this claim. Let us look at them.

II. "And I heard a voice from heaven, as the voice of many waters, and as the voice of a great thunder: and I heard the voice of harpers harping with their harps: and they sung as it were a new song before the throne, and before the four beasts, and before the elders; and no man could learn that song but the hundred and forty and four thousand which were redeemed from the earth. These are they which were not defiled with women; for they are virgins. These are they which follow the Lamb whithersoever he goeth. These were redeemed from among men, being the firstfruits to God and the Lamb. And in their mouth was found no guile: for they are without fault before the throne of God."

Now I will be told that nothing here speaks of a redemption of the world; a great deal speaks of a redemption from the world. These followers of the Lamb stand in sharp contrast to everyone around them. Their great blessing is that the world's habits are not their habits. No one but they can learn or understand their song. The world lies in wickedness, worshiping a beast and his image; they are faultless.

These observations are plain and true. But what conclusions should we draw from them? Many have been drawn. "Christians are redeemed from the earth. What can that mean but that the earth is a vile, unclean thing they have nothing to do with? Christians' habits are not to be the world's habits. What can that mean but that they should keep as much as possible to themselves, and preserve the purity and virginity of their souls by not mixing with the corrupted? They have songs of their own; what can that mean but that God has given them an inheritance shut off from people in general?" In later times these conclusions have seemed so inevitable to many religious people that all over Christendom, Protestant as well as Roman, they have been made the basis of whole systems of action; one sect or order after another has arisen to proclaim them, to denounce earlier sects and orders for neglecting them, and to put them into practice themselves.

To the Christian community shortly after Pentecost, maxims very unlike these seemed inevitable and became the foundation of their conduct. One of the first records of their life is that they ate their food with joy and singleness of heart. This was new to them. Part of the song they had learned was a thanksgiving for the earth and its gifts, which before they had received dully and indifferently. But was separation from other people and suspicion of them a mark of them after their baptism? If it had been, that would have set them apart in no way from the world they had lived in. Each Jewish sect cultivated this separation and cherished this suspicion; the most popular sect earned its religious reputation by how far it kept those who would not accept its rulings. The Christians would at once have been recognized as just another Nazarene sect if they had followed this precedent. They could not follow it, because the very principle of their calling was to follow the Lamb wherever he went. He had not gone that way at all. He had solemnly taken a different road. He had drawn the anger of the Pharisees by rejecting what seemed to them unquestionable rules about how to treat their fellow creatures—by going to sinners and eating with them. If his disciples, after his ascension, were to clean out the old leaven, the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees, they had above all to purge the leaven of exclusiveness; no ingredient had spoiled the pure Passover bread as much as that.

But if they got rid of this leaven, how could they become a pure loaf wholly given to God? Only while they confessed that in themselves, as individuals, no good thing dwelt—nothing that lifted them above the lowest tax-collector or prostitute or heathen in the world; only while they confessed that all their purity was in that Lamb who had taken them into fellowship with himself and written his Father's name on their foreheads; only while they confessed that in him God saw them as faultless, wholly undefiled, and that fault and corruption began when they forgot their relation to him, when they separated from one another and went their own separate ways and interests. What, then, was their *redemption from among men*? Redemption from their self-interest, from their self-worship; the privilege of confessing the Son of Man who had redeemed them and all mankind; the privilege of truly belonging to a human kind, and not being, like the worshipers of the beast, utterly inhuman. I would not change one word or letter of this passage. I insist that it be taken in the strictest possible way. I insist that the weak dodges and evasions that turn "faultless" into "not very faulty," and "undefiled" into "as pure as human weakness allows," be thrown out as irreverent and dishonest. Then, I am sure, this passage will reconcile a host of others where readers imagine a contradiction, using one to water down another until both are emptied of meaning. I am sure it will be seen that the freer Christians are from the corruptions of their age, the less they will dare to limit God's grace; and the more they regard themselves as firstfruits to God and the Lamb, the more they will believe that the whole universe has been offered and sacrificed to God in its High Priest, and that he will claim his right to it, no matter who disputes that right.

This, then, I believe, is what that song is about—the song that began in heaven and was echoed on earth, sung before the throne, the four living creatures, and the elders; the song that was "as it were new"—not actually new, not without earlier versions, not different in spirit from the songs that rose from the hearts and lips of the old prophets when they glorified God under some brute tyranny, amid the sins and decay of their countrymen; but new, because it fulfilled all those older songs; new, because it celebrated the actual reconciliation of heaven and earth that they had only anticipated; new, because the brute power of this day was more openly and formally antichristian than any that came before the Incarnation.

III. "And I saw another angel fly in the midst of heaven, having the everlasting Gospel to preach unto them that dwell on the earth, and to every nation, and kindred, and tongue, and people; saying with a loud voice, Fear God, and give glory to him; for the hour of his judgment is come; and worship him that made heaven and earth and the sea and the fountains of waters." This message, and the ones that follow, are strictly messages from heaven to earth. The first implicitly contains the rest. It is called an everlasting Gospel; good news about an everlasting God; good news to all his creatures everywhere. It is the Gospel that Saul of Tarsus, the Hebrew of the Hebrews, carried to the idolaters of Greece and Asia, feeling as he carried it that he was strictly fulfilling his role as a child of Abraham. The Jew lifted up his voice and said to the world, "You who have worshiped things in heaven, on earth, in the sea, and in the springs of water; you who have bowed to gods in nature and of nature; you who have made gods in your own image, in the likeness of your good and evil desires, your just and unjust ideas, your pure and lustful impulses—fear *the* God who has made everything in heaven, earth, and sea; fear him who made man in his own image; for he has revealed his true image to men; he has revealed the standard of righteousness; the Judge who condemns all unrighteousness; the Redeemer from all unrighteousness." Do you think it is not a Gospel because it begins with "Fear," or because it speaks of judgment? I believe no Gospel but this can satisfy men. They want to know whom they should fear. They must fear an evil being if they do not fear a good one. They cannot trust one they do not reverence. Take away awe, and the one you worship shrinks back to your own size. The misery of men had been that they made a God in their own likeness. To have him revealed as infinitely above them, yet stooping to them, was the overthrow of the superstition that had made them cowards and slaves. And the deliverance would be entirely incomplete if they could not believe that this righteous God was their Judge, the Judge of all the earth, and that they could take refuge in him from the false judgments of all men, and above all from their own judgment, and from themselves.

IV. "And there followed another angel, saying, Babylon is fallen, is fallen, that great city, because she made all nations drink of the wine of the wrath of her fornications."

The Babel, or Babylonian, order was the opposite of the Israelite order. The ruler had no real relation to his people; he was simply a ruler. The gods he honored were *powers*—different, clashing powers; they were to be worshiped and appeased as props of his government, and they cared nothing for those who worshiped and appeased them. The God of Israel, by contrast, was declared to be the King and Husband of his people. The kings who reigned in his name were to care for the people as a husband cares for his wife. When the nation fell, as it kept falling, into idolatry, this was called an act of fornication or adultery, the wife turning away from her real Lord. That language fit the chosen people. Now it could be applied to the world at large. The angel proclaims that the world no longer belongs to Babel. The Husband of the human race has appeared; all idolatry is fornication or adultery. Men can be told that they are spiritual beings, joined to a spiritual Lord, and that in giving themselves to fleshly and visible things they are abandoning their rightful state and yielding to seducers. All this was contained in the revelation of the Son of Man. And so it also contained the downfall of the whole system of idolatry—the whole Babel order. Widely as it had spread, mighty and subtle as its influence was over every tribe and nation, the day the Man-child was born, when the true Head of man, the true bond between heaven and earth was disclosed—that was the day of its doom. Already, in the Roman empire, there were signs of the system cracking and falling to pieces. It could not sustain the tyranny that depended on it, and the tyranny could not sustain it. The cry, "It is fallen! it is fallen!" would be shown in that very age by the upheaval shaking earth and heaven, the seat of the empire and the seats of the gods. And the proclamation was not weaker but stronger because, in that upheaval, the very city that had witnessed against Babel idolatry was itself to perish. For it had let the Babel principle into its own heart. Jerusalem had become emphatically the city of confusion. It had aimed to make a name for itself and had forgotten the name of its God. It had thought its towers were high and reached to heaven; but they were built of brick instead of stone, cemented with slime instead of mortar. The babble of its sects showed that God had come down to confound its pride. It had no language it could understand itself; how could it make itself understood to the world? The Church, which the Spirit had empowered to speak to men of different tribes, in their own tongues, of the wonderful works of God, could not help seeing that the fall of Jerusalem was one sign among many that the thrones of all false gods were to be thrown down:

> "They feel from Juda's land
> The dreaded Infant's hand,
> The rays of Bethlehem blind their dusky eyen."

And this because that Infant came not to destroy but to fulfill; to gather into one all the scattered beliefs and hopes of the world; to overthrow its lies by making good its truths. We must not confuse this voice with the one that follows, closely as they are linked. The Babel society, taken by itself, rests on the worship of a number of persons. But these are always aiming at a mock unity. The beast, described in the last chapter, is the concentration of everything brutal in the others, the exclusion of everything human.

V. "And the third angel followed, saying with a loud voice, If any man worship the beast and his image, and receive his mark in his forehead or in his hand, the same shall drink of the wine of the wrath of God, which is poured out without mixture into the cup of his indignation; and he shall be tormented with fire and brimstone in the presence of the holy angels, and in the presence of the Lamb: and the smoke of their torment ascendeth up for ever and ever: and they have no rest day nor night, who worship the beast and his image, and whosoever receiveth the mark of his name."

The angel's voice sings utter ruin, torment, and endless restlessness to those who carry the beast's image in their hearts. Why? Because the eternal God has shown his image to men, has claimed them as made in that image, and has promised his Spirit to write his name on their hearts. To worship the beast is to degrade spiritual beings into beasts—a degradation carrying an anguish only spirits can feel: a sense of something lost; endless discontent with what has been taken in exchange; the undying worm of conscience; the envy and hatred that possess and rule creatures made for fellowship and love. Something of this misery can be traced in the laments of the companions of Ulysses when they had drunk Circe's cup and been transformed by her wand. The exile's grief is always deepest in proportion to the beauty of the home he has left. The intellectual Greek could perceive the meanness of a swinish existence in a way the savage could not. So it is inevitable that the vision the Gospel gives of a God of eternal goodness and loving-kindness should make the hell of loneliness and selfishness deep and unimaginably dark. Phrases like "the wine of the wrath of God, which is poured out without measure," "tormented with fire and brimstone in the presence of the holy angels and of the Lamb," "the smoke of their torment ascendeth up for ever and ever," "they have no rest day nor night," can easily turn into mere figures of speech the orator toys with at will—that alarm the nerves for half an hour, that fret but do not rouse the conscience, that lazy hearers expect and demand as sounds to break the monotony of an ordinary sermon. For the Gospel of such an orator is not a Gospel of God, but a Gospel about how men may be delivered from God. He has no message about a kingdom of light opened to mankind by the incarnation and death of the Son of God; and so his message about the kingdom of darkness reaches nothing in the hearts and spirits of men and only jangles in their ears. This angel's voice carries real thunder precisely because it is essentially sweet and divine. If God's love were narrowed even a little, the horror of being cut off from that love, of being given over to lust and hatred, would be narrowed by the same amount. If the power of divine love to lift men out of that abyss of horror were reduced by any time limits, then the sense of what spiritual and eternal death means, the dread of it, and the force of those divinely chosen images that describe it, would be reduced by the same measure. In easy times the contrast may not be very obvious; the divine words and their counterfeits may often be swapped for each other and taken to have the same worth. But in such times as St. John describes, when the power of the beast and the power of the Lamb meet face to face; when everyone must choose between them; when all apparent strength is on the side of evil—then the angel's words, which declare that this evil power is not all-powerful but utterly accursed, come out in their clearness and force. The beast with the lamblike face, whose work is to stamp the beast's image with the dragon's horns on men's hearts, may mix eternal and temporal terrors in wild confusion, crushing men's spirits under his shameful yoke more effectively than by any other means. The messenger of God knows in that day that he must set forth, in plain and broad words, the spiritual and eternal ruin bound up in serving a false and cruel power—so that he may turn men to the Conqueror of that power; so that he may convince them that the Redeemer is stronger than the destroyer, and that there are no chains the one can forge that the other cannot break.

VI. The words that follow are familiar to us out of their setting. It matters that we know them in their setting.

"Here is the patience of the saints: here are they that keep the commandments of God, and the faith of Jesus. And I heard a voice from heaven saying unto me, Write, Blessed are the dead which die in the Lord from henceforth: Yea, saith the Spirit, that they may rest from their labours; and their works do follow them."

What has the patience of the saints to do with that announcement about the worshipers of the beast we just heard? My brothers! To believe that the devil is not the lord of the world, that he cannot give its kingdoms to whomever he pleases, is hard at all times, and intensely hard in times when brute and evil power has established its dominance and the world is running after it. Then admitting the enemy's dominion seems only to admit an obvious fact. Indeed, some will insist that holding this belief is a pious duty. "Christ is to reign hereafter; but for now the earth is handed over to the dragon." I think it impossible, while we believe this, not to pay some homage—yes, continual homage—to the power we suppose is on top. And so this book teaches the deep and vital lesson that the patience of the saints consists in steadily believing the angel's message about the beast, and in *not* believing any whispers and suggestions of his about his own authority. In doing this they keep the commandment that says, "Thou shalt worship the Lord thy God, and him only shalt thou serve." In doing this they walk the path of Jesus, who used this very commandment to resist the adversary when he said, "All these will I give thee, and the glory of them, if thou wilt fall down and worship me." Jesus knew that to admit that lie—to confess that these kingdoms were in the hands of the evil one—was to fall down and worship him. That was all the homage the tempter wanted. Christ might have the kingdoms later, if only he would grant that they were Satan's at that moment. He said, "Get thee behind me." In his strength every one of his followers, in the darkest hour, when good seems utterly defeated, is to say the same. And to strengthen them for this effort, another heavenly voice comes, saying, "Blessed are the dead that die in the Lord from henceforth." Why *from henceforth*? Were not the dead who die in the Lord always blessed? If the words had a special bearing on that time, may we dare to use them in our funeral service for any brother or sister whose remains we now bury? I think the words did belong to that time; I think "from henceforth" carried a special comfort for those who, judging by their eyes, would have concluded that the earth was not the Lord's. It reminded them that the earth is only part of the universe; that seventy years do not settle the great questions debated here; that the Lord, who has gone into the grave and hell, has risen on high, to open eternity to men. The voice says to those sinking under the sight the world offers them, "Yes—but at this very moment men are passing into a region where the mists that block our vision here do not exist; where they will know that not the beast, but the Lord God Omnipotent reigns." And so the words became both backward- and forward-looking. "Know from now on that those who fell asleep before Christ came in the flesh are around his throne. Know from now on that you are surrounded by a countless company of every kindred and nation, watching your struggle and helping you in it. Know from now on that this company will gather fresh members from all the ages to come—in lands unknown to you, men trembling at the thought of death will learn to lose their own deaths in his, who died for the world. Remember from now on that the hard labors you or they undergo in fighting the beast, in yourselves or in the world, lead surely to a rest in God. Know from now on that if you or they leave works God set you to do on this earth poor and unfinished, those works will follow you when you have passed through the veil. Know from now on that whatever is begun will be brought to perfection; that new fields of blessed work will open to God's servants; that as they followed the Lamb under the weight of mortality, they will follow him now to fresh victories, until every enemy that has opposed him and tormented his creatures is put under his feet."

## LECTURE XV. THE REAPING AND THE WINEPRESS.

*The passage:* John saw a white cloud, and on it one like the Son of Man, wearing a golden crown and holding a sharp sickle. An angel came out of the temple and cried to him to swing his sickle and reap, because the earth's harvest was ripe; and the one on the cloud reaped the earth. Then another angel with a sickle came from the temple, and another with power over fire came from the altar, calling for the grapes of the earth's vine to be gathered, since they were ripe. The vine was gathered and thrown into the great winepress of God's wrath, which was trodden outside the city, and blood flowed from it as high as the horses' bridles for a distance of sixteen hundred furlongs. Then John saw seven angels holding the seven last plagues, in which God's wrath is completed. He saw a sea of glass mixed with fire, and those who had conquered the beast and its image and mark and the number of its name standing on it with harps of God, singing the song of Moses and of the Lamb: that the Lord God Almighty's works are great and marvellous, His ways just and true; that all should fear and glorify Him, for He alone is holy, and all nations will come and worship because His judgments are revealed. The temple of the tabernacle of the testimony in heaven was opened, and the seven angels came out clothed in pure white linen with golden girdles; one of the four living creatures gave them seven golden vials full of God's wrath; and the temple was filled with smoke from His glory and power, so that no one could enter until the seven plagues were finished.

Someone will ask me: does this vision too belong to the first century? Are we then to give up the fear and the hope that stay in all our minds—the thought that the Son of Man really will come to judge the living and the dead? Is there to be no future harvest of the earth? Will no angel put in his sickle and reap it?

My friends, it seems to me that the expectation of judgment is far too weak in every one of us. The preacher who could deepen it into a firm and lasting conviction would be the greatest of all preachers; and whoever does anything to lessen what there is of it in any person is, to that degree, most harmful. I find that John the Baptist, that our Lord Himself, and that every one of His Apostles was a preacher of *judgment*—a judgment near at hand, one coming upon the very generation they addressed. I think it is because we have not taken their words literally enough that we have been unable to speak in their spirit, to feel and to make others feel that a judgment hangs over us, that the Son of Man really will appear in His Father's glory, and that each person will have the secrets of his heart and the deeds he has done laid bare before him. No book, it seems to me, would bring this truth home to us so well as the Apocalypse, if we read it plainly. While I refuse to twist any of its words out of their obvious sense—accepting them as explaining the sense in which the Baptist, our Lord, and His disciples spoke—I am forced to feel and confess that the judgment is set and the books are open; that nothing can be hidden from Him with whom we have to deal; that every good and evil seed in every person and every society is ripening toward its harvest; that the fruit of the one must be gathered into the barn, and the fruit of the other burned up.

I. We have just heard the message, "*Write, Blessed are the dead which die in the Lord from henceforth. Yea, saith the Spirit, that they may rest from their labours; and their works do follow them*." I took these words to sound very cheering to someone looking out on a world that was worshipping the beast and its image. They assured him that there was a realm where a different worship reigned, and that there was a way from this realm into that one. Then he looks, "*and behold, a white cloud, and upon the cloud one sat like unto the Son of Man, having on his head a golden crown, and in his hand a sharp sickle*." The prophet's first thought might have been that the blessing of the dead consisted in leaving a world where the Son of Man was not *recognized*—no, rather, not *reigning*. This sight scatters that dark delusion. On a cloud drawn up from earth, though its whiteness testifies of light and of heaven, he sees no strange form, but the form of Him who always claimed that human name for Himself, who in His birth, His passion, His resurrection, and His ascension had vindicated it as His own. That the earth was passing through a terrible crisis was plain enough. Wise pagans saw it, and supposed the gods were busy taking vengeance on men and cared nothing for men's safety or rescue. To St. John the Savior of men was revealed as Himself the reaper of the earth. He has won His crown; the divine glory rests on Him. But in His hand is the sign that the work being done on earth is His doing. That was the revelation the exile on Patmos needed. It is the revelation we need. He did not need to be told that a consuming process was going on—everyone could see that. But to learn who was directing that process, and to learn that it was for the destruction of the destroyer, that was precious knowledge indeed. If we accepted it—if we interpreted by it the events passing in our own age, which will make up its history for ages to come—would not that knowledge be worth something to us? The Son of Man sat on the white cloud then; He sits on the white cloud now. If the mists were cleared from our spirits, we should see Him, and that would be an encouragement and a warning in our work such as nothing else could give.

II. "*And another angel came out of the temple, crying with a loud voice to him that sat on the cloud, Thrust in thy sickle and reap, for the time is come for thee to reap, for the harvest of the earth is ripe*." This voice came from the inner temple; it expressed the divine purpose and will. That purpose and will the Son of Man had been fulfilling before the worlds existed. He fulfilled it in the act of creation, when all things came forth at His word, with man at the head of all, and the whole was very good. He fulfilled it in His agony and His cross. He fulfilled it in His triumph over death, and space, and time. And now that purpose and will is to be fulfilled in an act of judgment. All that He has done on earth for men has not been in vain. The resistance to His work has not defeated it, as mortals might conclude. The end of the age has come. The results of the good and evil that have been growing side by side are now plain. "*The harvest of the earth is ripe*."

III. "*And he that sat on the cloud thrust in his sickle, and the earth was reaped. And another angel came out from the altar, which had power over fire; and cried with a loud voice to him that had the sharp sickle, saying, Thrust in thy sharp sickle, and gather the clusters of the vine of the earth; for her grapes are fully ripe. And the angel thrust his sickle into the earth, and gathered the vine of the earth, and cast it into the great winepress of the wrath of God. And the winepress was trodden without the city, and blood came out of the winepress, even unto the horse bridles, by the space of a thousand and six hundred furlongs*."

The reaping of the earth means, I take it, the gathering in of the fruits of all the divine seed that had been scattered over the nations and in men's hearts. Nothing that had been accomplished in society through laws, institutions, family memorials, and traditions, through the voice of prophets and the deeds of patriots; nothing accomplished for the growth and purifying of the individual soul through the countless influences of nature, the voices of mothers, the power of mutual love, through sorrows and separations, reconciliations and hopes; no devout wish that ever rose in prayer and sacrifice, having first been inspired by the very One to whom it was offered—none of this was to be lost. The Son of Man has watched it all and has stored it among His treasures; all has come from Him, and will now be claimed by Him. That this holds true for each individual person we shall be told later. Here the words lead us rather to dwell on the great *human* results of all this divine cultivation—those that are to survive into the coming age, that are to give that age its shape and character. If the reaping of the earth meant the destruction of the earth, it would obviously be pointless to speak of a new age about to begin when the old one passed. But reaping does not mean destruction in ordinary human speech, nor in Holy Scripture. And Scripture does speak of the end of an age, and of a better age to follow. The very lesson this passage teaches—and the whole Apocalypse teaches—is that God's fires are not meant to ruin the earth, which is the work of His hands, but to ruin those who have defiled it by the work of *their* hands.

This is the theme of the words that follow. Not only the earth is God's, but the fire too. The fire is His servant. The message about it comes from the altar. It is to serve, as it always has, both to kindle the sacrifice and to burn the refuse. The vine of the earth here plainly stands for all that is *not* of God's planting—all the thoughts and bloody deeds that have sprung from man's rebellious will, and that have made the earth drunk and foul. The grapes of this vine, like the grain grown from the divine seed, are now fully ripe. When the grain is reaped, it may still have many processes of threshing and winnowing to pass through. The grapes are cast into the winepress. What kind of winepress it is we shall be told more clearly later. But the signs here cannot be mistaken. A terrible conflict between rival factions, coming just before the ruin of a city or an empire, looks at first only like a cause for despair. To see, over a stretch of sixteen hundred furlongs, nothing but blood rising to the horses' bridles—human beings destroying one another, and the innocent animals perishing with them—is a sight to make an ordinary man weep; how much more a seer who believes man was made in God's image and who has proclaimed that God is Love. The sight would be utterly appalling if the seer had not that faith to hold him up. Holding it, he cannot find in the darkest such spectacle the slightest reason for despair. The wrath and fury and wickedness of men, which have been piling up for centuries, have reached their climax, have come to their great day of trial. Now they are to spend and exhaust themselves. Now they are to meet another power. The winepress cannot be regarded merely as one in which the grapes of the earth are crushed. It is the great winepress of *the wrath of God*. The wrath of love, the wrath of goodness, is to meet the wrath of hatred and evil. It is to be proved, on this earth and throughout God's whole creation, which of the two is the mightier.

And so we are prepared for the words that have been, rather awkwardly I think, separated from these and made the opening of another chapter.

IV. "*And I saw another sign in heaven, great and marvellous, seven angels having the seven last plagues; for in them is filled up the wrath of God*." There has been much discussion in the Christian Church about when these seven last plagues began, or are to begin, how long they will last, and whether we are living through one of them now. I cannot regret that such questions have been raised, because I am sure some have been led by them to reflect on the plagues visiting themselves, their families, their country, or Christendom in general, and to ask: Where did these come from, and what message do they carry? Those who have been used to receiving one calamity or another with the thoughtlessness and dullness we are all prone to may well be thankful if, by any means—through some interpreter of prophecy, or through their own ambition to interpret it—they are brought to believe that this or that event may have a divine force in it, may be the sign and witness of God's government. For then will they not go on, step by step, to say: "If this is true in one case, must it not be true in all? Dare I single out any one of the events I have shared in, or read of as affecting my country or mankind, and say: *This* I can attribute to the Lord of heaven and earth, but not *that*; *this* deserves my earnest thought to grasp its meaning, but *those* I may safely pass over?" I can imagine that the very uncertainty of these guesses has led many serious people into a truer state of mind. They could not be sure that any given plague might not fit the description of these Apocalyptic plagues; so why not act on the conclusion that all have the same general character, that not one falls outside the divine orbit, that not one is a stray messenger on an errand of its own? But if any are tempted into frivolous debating about the matches between particular disasters of their own time and those referred to here, until they treat as indifferent and profane the ones where they find no resemblance—that atheistic view at once proves they cannot have sat at the Apostle's feet, cannot have profited by his revelations. It is no answer to say, "But he speaks of seven *last* plagues; we want to know which these are." Certainly he does. And that very word *last*, and its explanation in the phrase "*in them was filled up the wrath of God*," shows that they were the same in kind as everything that came before them; that they marked the winding-up of a period—a period that had been all along under divine cultivation and discipline, not a single event of which an Apostle could ever view except as a sign of a purpose of God or of a struggle against that purpose. Suppose, if you like, that there is to be a winding-up of *our* dispensation; believe, if you will, that it is near. But the more you hold this view, the more you should be afraid of resting anything on guesswork; the more you should wish to be in a calm, steady, habitual state of readiness for what is coming—not as something new or strange, but as the climax of the divine drama that every earlier step has been foretelling.

V. The real lesson we must learn from what we see, and from what we read, and from comparing the two, is set out in the next three verses: "*And I saw as it were a sea of glass mingled with fire: and them that had gotten the victory over the beast, and over his image, and over his mark, and over the number of his name, stand on the sea of glass, having the harps of God. And they sing the song of Moses the servant of God, and the song of the Lamb, saying, Great and marvellous are thy works, Lord God Almighty; just and true are thy ways, thou King of saints. Who shall not fear thee, O Lord, and glorify thy name? for thou only art holy; for all nations shall come and worship before thee; for thy judgments are made manifest*." There was a time when those who stand on this sea of glass and sing these songs had longed for some solid footing of their own; they liked to think that they, at least, were safe on some raft or patch of ground, even if the world were drowned in the waters or burned up in the fire. That craving has been taken from them. The ledges of earth have been swept away, the planks have floated out of reach. Now they stand by faith in God alone. The sea is all they have to rest on; they may drop into the fire at any moment. Their one security is that God will hold them up. They can entrust themselves and the whole universe to Him. And that is their victory over the beast, and its name, and its mark, and the number of its name. Self-seeking, and the distrust that goes with self-seeking—these are the beast's traits, the marks he stamps on his followers. The battle against them has been hard and terrible. Every moment it is renewed; every moment the tyrant threatens them and may prevail again. Their victory lies in their faith, not in themselves. They can trust God on the sea of glass in the midst of the fire. They know the sea is His, and that He made it; that this sea of glass is before His throne and reflects His image. They know the fire is His; that it comes from His altar; that it is the fire of love and will consume whatever is hostile to love.

Therefore these men have the harps of God, and they sing the song of Moses, the servant of God: "*Moses said, I will sing unto the Lord, for he hath triumphed gloriously: the horse and the rider hath he thrown into the sea. The Lord is my strength and song, and he is become my salvation: he is my God, and I will prepare him an habitation, my fathers' God, and I will exalt him. The Lord is a man of war: the Lord is his name. Pharaoh's chariots and his host hath he cast into the sea: his chosen captains also are drowned in the Red Sea*." Here is the song of the old covenant, of the old world. It is the song of God's victory over the oppressor, of God's rescue of the oppressed. It can never become obsolete as long as there are oppressors and oppressed in creation. Woe to those who would take a single word out of it, who would try to make it gentler toward the wrongdoer by making it less of a witness for the one who suffers.

But they also sing the song of the Lamb. That, even more than the other, is a song of redemption. That, even more than the other, is a song of judgment. Moses, the servant of God, declares the great and marvellous works of the Lord—how forever He wars against the tyrant, and forever He wars on behalf of the weak and the crushed. The Lamb of God, the perfect image of the Father, reveals not only His works but His ways; He shows Him as He truly is. The Lamb not only fights the tyrant but submits to the tyrant; not only fights for the weak and crushed but becomes the weakest and most crushed of all. He strips Himself of all power, so that the eternal love of God may be seen through His emptiness. He clothes Himself with power so that it may be seen that all the power of the universe is the power of love, and that it must beat down everything opposed to it. So it is with infinite delight and satisfaction that those who have the harps of God contemplate His judgment. In their minds redemption and judgment have become inseparable. All the plagues falling on the earth are to glorify that Name which alone is holy. All are for the good of the nations; for the nations will at last come and worship before Him; they will renounce the destroyer and confess the deliverer.

VI. "*And after that I looked, and, behold, the temple of the tabernacle of the testimony in heaven was opened: and the seven angels came out of the temple, having the seven plagues, clothed in pure and white linen, and having their breasts girded with golden girdles. And one of the four beasts gave unto the seven angels seven golden vials, full of the wrath of God, who liveth for ever and ever. And the temple was filled with smoke from the glory of God, and from his power; and no man was able to enter into the temple till the seven plagues of the seven angels were fulfilled*." I hardly need to point out to you how tenderly the Apostle lingers over the old names and old symbols so dear to him and to his fathers: how he speaks of the tabernacle of the testimony, bringing back all the memories of the wilderness; of the temple of which no relic was to remain on earth; of the pure white linen and the golden girdles of those priests whose last days had been days of sin and whose end had come. He can bear to let them go, because each has found its meaning, each has been transfigured. The tabernacle of the testimony in heaven is now open. There is no longer a shadow of the divine Presence; there is a full, clear, genuine testimony of what it is. The glory of God is revealed without a veil in the face of a Man. And so there is now an abiding, unchangeable temple, in which no sound of axe or hammer was ever heard, which no Chaldean or Roman can destroy. The linen is truly pure and white, and the girdles of these priests of the inner temple are truly golden. The prophet never needed this vision of their purity and brightness more than now, for they carry with them the seven last plagues. He must have his own faith strengthened—and must strengthen ours—that what we call vengeance is really punishment; that judgments are truly for separating light from darkness; that the kingdom of light will prevail forever, and the kingdom of darkness be overthrown.

On whom the seven vials were poured out, and what their effects were, the next chapter will tell us. What we learn here is that they are full of the wrath of God. I have wanted you to weigh that expression seriously, and to give it all the force it gains from being used by the last of the Bible's seers. Being a Jew himself, he must be taken as the interpreter of how his fathers used it; the man who has set forth all the mysteries of Christian theology may tell us what place it holds in that theology. He does not see less wrath burning through the universe than Moses or David saw; in his visions it reaches more deeply to the center of things and spreads over a wider circumference. Everywhere the consuming fire is at work; it will not stop until its task is done. The wrath is of Him who lives forever and ever. What is true of one time is true of another. The same Being must be carrying on the same war; that winepress which His Son trod in the wilderness, in the garden, on the cross, must be fully trodden. What He died and rose to conquer, all the powers of creation must be fighting against. Let us understand and believe it. They are fighting against it in you and me and in the whole world; whatever in us loves and makes a lie, whatever lie is seizing power over men, the God who lives forever hates with an unquenchable hatred; this—in each human creature, and in the whole of human society—is meeting His fierce and eternal wrath. Would you ask that this wrath be less fierce? Would you like to set a time when it shall stop burning? Then you are praying for your own ruin and the world's; you are praying that you may be left to your own lies—to the father of lies; you are praying that the Infinite Love to which you owe every joy you ever had or ever can have should come to an end and give way to that other wrath which cherishes everything evil and seeks to devour everything good. Let us hope, and steadily believe, that this shall never be. Let us accept the assurance of God's word that it never shall. There are moments—the prophets say so—when the plagues darkening the world seem even to close the temple of God, so that no one can enter it. But they are sent out from that temple; they are meant to clear the air of the mists that have hidden it from men's spirits. It may be that today, as you have listened to the news of the Father of an infinite majesty, of the honorable, true, and only Son, and of the Holy Ghost the Comforter, dwelling in blessed and eternal Unity, other visions have floated before you that have hidden that beautiful and glorious vision. Why, you have asked, am I to be told of an eternal wrath surrounding those who confound the Person or divide the Substance? And if by an eternal wrath—a wrath of God—you have meant an earthly wrath, the wrath of an evil, selfish man, or of an evil spirit, you may well ask how goodness and wickedness, light and darkness, can be brought into such close contact. Or if by those against whom this wrath is aimed you have understood some neighbor of our own day, or someone who left the world in ages past, you may well have trembled lest you should be bringing down on yourselves that dreadful sentence, "With what judgment you judge, you shall be judged; what measure you mete, it shall be measured to you again." But if you have given to eternal wrath the sense it bears in this divine book—the sense that links it with the God who lives forever and ever—then surely you cannot help wishing, for your own sake and for mankind's, that there should be no easing of this wrath until there has perished utterly and forever whatever in men's hearts, or will, or reason tends to confound blessed distinctions or to tear apart what is one. We shall find how much there is in ourselves that leads to that confusion and that division; we shall see how it mixes into all our thoughts, darkening and dividing the forms and workings of the outward world, the dealings of daily life, and our view of the history and character of men, as well as our view of the nature of God. We shall want God's judgment to search us, so that we may see all things as they are in God's divine light, not jumbled together by our fancy, nor torn apart by our failure to trace their inner harmony. We shall be sure that the very same causes that make us refuse justice to the differences of other men, and shrink from fellowship with them, also interfere with our grasp of the perfect Unity revealed in the Persons of the Father, the Son, and the Spirit. We shall not doubt that the full revelation of that Mystery of Love which in words we have confessed today will be a revelation of the glory of God in man, and throughout the whole earth. We shall not doubt that when we fully enter into that revelation, we shall have won a victory over the selfish power that seeks to set heaven and earth at war; that we shall be able to sing the song of Moses and of the Lamb—the song of triumph over fallen tyranny and division, the song of a redeemed and reconciled universe.

## LECTURE XVI. THE VIALS.

*The passage:* John heard a great voice from the temple telling the seven angels to go and pour out the vials of God's wrath on the earth. The first was poured on the earth, and a foul, painful sore fell on the people who bore the mark of the beast and worshipped his image. The second was poured on the sea, which became like the blood of a dead man, and every living thing in it died. The third was poured on the rivers and springs, and they turned to blood; and the angel of the waters said God was righteous in this judgment, because these people had shed the blood of saints and prophets, so now they were given blood to drink, which they deserved; and another voice from the altar agreed that God's judgments are true and righteous. The fourth was poured on the sun, and men were scorched with great heat, and they cursed God's name and did not repent. The fifth was poured on the seat of the beast, and his kingdom went dark, and men gnawed their tongues in pain and cursed God, and did not repent. The sixth was poured on the great river Euphrates, and its water dried up to prepare a way for the kings of the east; and John saw three unclean spirits like frogs come from the mouths of the dragon, the beast, and the false prophet—spirits of devils working miracles, going out to gather the kings of the earth to the battle of the great day of God. ("Behold, I come as a thief; blessed is he that watches and keeps his garments.") They were gathered to a place called Armageddon. The seventh was poured into the air, and a great voice from the temple and the throne said, "It is done." There were voices, thunders, lightnings, and the greatest earthquake ever; the great city split in three, the cities of the nations fell, and great Babylon was remembered before God to be given the cup of his fierce wrath. Every island fled and the mountains vanished, and a great hail fell, each stone about a talent's weight, and men cursed God because of the plague of the hail.

Any reasonably attentive reader will notice a strong resemblance between the events that followed the trumpets and those that follow the pouring out of the vials. When the first four trumpets sounded, the earth, the sea, the rivers, and the sun were struck. Here we read of the same calamities in the same order. The river Euphrates goes with both the sixth trumpet and the sixth vial; the cry "It is finished" went with both the seventh trumpet and the seventh vial. There are several small differences, which I may come to shortly. But there is one large and striking difference that seems to settle the meaning of each vision. When the fifth trumpet sounded, a star fell from heaven to earth, the bottomless pit opened, and locusts came out that did not hurt grass or trees but only tormented—not killed—the men who lacked God's seal on their foreheads, for five months. The fifth vial "was poured out upon the seat of the beast, and his kingdom was full of darkness, and they gnawed their tongues for pain." I tried to show, when I treated that trumpet, that it meant the fall and ruin of a great spiritual dynasty and the moral and spiritual misery that follows such a fall. That this vial means the destruction of a dynasty that is not spiritual at all but essentially brutal—though it may once have leaned on spiritual supports—the words themselves would teach us, even without light from earlier or later passages. Everything so far seems to favor the approach to these prophecies that I have adopted. The trumpets would all be announcements of the fall of the old world's spiritual center; they would point to Jerusalem. The vials would concern what I may call the old world's material center; they would point to Rome. But they would not concern Jerusalem and Rome at different points in history; they would mark a crisis both cities were passing through within the same three or four years.

I. What those three or four years were, and what we know was happening in them, I have already partly considered; the seventeenth chapter will bring the question clearly before us. Today's subject leads us to reflect on the different ways the same events can appear to a prophet's mind. "And the first went and poured his vial upon the earth, and there fell a foul and painful sore upon the men that had the mark of the beast, and upon them that worshipped his image." In the eighth chapter there was no mention of any harm to human beings from the hail and fire mingled with blood cast on the earth; only a third of the trees and green grass were burned. There we were hearing a trumpet; here we are seeing a vial poured out. There the earth was struck; those who had been called to declare who is the Lord of the earth, and had not declared him to men or believed in him themselves, were to read his *Mene Mene* written on their walls. To the others it could not carry that meaning. They only feel the effects of the visitation. It comes to them as a foul and painful sore. They smart under the plague. The lesson reaches them through their bodies. Do not suppose, however, that the message could not have come in this form to the people of Judæa. They too were worshippers of the beast. They were becoming incapable of grasping anything but material losses and sufferings. And there may have been many heathens who saw a divine hand in the affliction and glorified God in the fires. The distinction between the two purposes of the judgment is not changed by how this or that person received it.

II. "And the second angel poured out his vial upon the sea, and it became as the blood of a dead man, and every living soul died in the sea." This, someone will say, is the language of poetry or prophecy, not of fact. I have asked you before whether you are sure the language of poetry or prophecy does not represent facts more truly than the language of statistics. Do you think a count of the number of people who die in a wreck—even the smallest fishing boat—gives you the history of that wreck? Is not a great artist's picture truer? Many of you, I am sure, will know that memorable passage of Jeremy Taylor, where he imagines a man on a battlement of heaven looking down to see how many men and women at that moment lie fainting and dying for lack of bread; how many young men are cut down by war's sword; how many orphans weep over the graves of the fathers who fed them; to hear how many sailors and passengers are at that instant in a storm and cry out as the keel strikes a rock or breaks beneath them; how many people weep from want, are driven mad by oppression, or are made desperate by too keen a sense of constant misery. Surely there was no exaggeration in these thoughts. Such a sight must, at any moment, in Taylor's age or ours, have appeared to anyone standing at that point of observation.

It is not so horrible as the sight of a single den of tyranny that is sometimes laid bare to us just as it is about to pass away. St. John *is* watching the withering of the earth and the destruction at sea from a battlement of heaven. We might describe it in some dry abstraction such as the collapse of commercial prosperity, or the breaking off of trade between nations. To him it is as if the sea became *the blood of a dead man*.

III. "And the third angel poured out his vial upon the rivers and fountains of waters, and they became blood. And I heard the angel of the waters say, Thou art righteous, O Lord, which art and wast and shalt be, because thou hast judged thus. For they have shed the blood of saints and prophets, and thou hast given them blood to drink; for they are worthy. And I heard another out of the altar say, Even so, Lord God Almighty, true and righteous are thy judgments." Nothing puzzles us more often than the sense of divine retribution. Our conscience testifies to it; again and again we are sure we recognize it. To deny it seems like a practical confession that chance rules everything. To deny it makes every calamity inflicted on human beings far more dreadful and ghastly, as if an evil spirit were indulging its malice. Belief in retribution was the support of heathen faith amid all its corruptions; Jewish prophets lived to proclaim it; surely the Gospel cannot have taken the right to it from us. And yet there are our Lord's words about the Galileans whose blood Pilate mixed with their sacrifices, and about those in Jerusalem on whom the tower in Siloam fell. There is the warning to the disciples who assumed the blind man or his parents must have sinned. There is the universal command that overrides and upholds all these particular cases: "Judge not, that ye be not judged." There are the many times we ourselves have broken this command, and have seen others break it, trying to match particular punishments to particular crimes. There are the cruel counter-accusations that stain every page of church history, each party finding the plainest signs of divine vengeance against the other. How can we keep our faith in the exact fitness of the wrongs men do to the corrections God appoints for them, in the face of these warnings from the highest authority and from our own experience?

There are two methods, I believe, that can help us do this if we pursue them faithfully. The first is to fix our minds steadily on the word *Judgment*, to give it its full weight, and to accept no poor substitutes for it. Judgment means, in ordinary use, discrimination—assigning each thing its real worth, separating what is real from what is merely apparent or counterfeit. Mean *that* when you speak of God's judgments. Mean nothing at all that contradicts it. Then you must suppose that he does make his purposes plain, and will make them plainer. You will ask that they be made plain to you. And the more you ask that, and the more you expect an answer, the more horror you will feel at putting your own judgments in the place of God's, your rash and one-sided guesses in place of his revelation. You will feel more each day how you are tempted to do this; how you long to pull the specks out of other men's eyes and forget the beams in your own. You will not plead that you must do this to some degree so as not to hide from men that God is judging them. You will feel that in any degree you do it, you are doing something to hinder the effect of God's judgments, something to make them less believable to your fellow-creatures. The second rule follows from the first. Always try to understand God's retribution first in your own case. There it will become plain to you if you look for it. You will see that the correction was graciously fitted to the faults in your character that needed it. You will see that where you have sown the wind, you have very often indeed reaped the whirlwind. Very often—but not always. It has not been a mere movement from cause to effect. There has been a Judge directing the step from one to the other; who has—oh, how continually!—turned the very evil you did or planned into an instrument of humbling you, and therefore of good. What would naturally have borne only remorse, and so greater sin, has been made the means of leading you to repentance, and so to deliverance from sin. Such discoveries do not force us to imagine exceptions in God's ways. They are the highest examples and illustrations of his ways. They bring out the principle of retribution in its fullest power. So too in great historical crises like the one we are considering, there comes a flash of light from heaven, showing how the rivers and springs of water—those blessed gifts of God that have fed the life and health of nations—are turned into blood, made poisonous and deadly; not by accident, but in obedience to an everlasting law. Those who bore witness against the misuse of these gifts—the saints and prophets who testified that the good things given to men are not to be turned to pride and greed, not to be diverted from human channels into selfish ones—these have been made victims of their protest. Their blood has been mingled with these waters; they have been offered up to appease the selfish gods they said were no gods at all. "And now, O Lord," so sings the Angel of the Waters, "you have claimed these waters as your own. Men who used them for their bloody ends must drink blood. They would not receive the waters as given to nourish the earth; so the waters shall bring them no more nourishment, but death."

IV. "And the fourth angel poured out his vial upon the sun, and power was given to him to scorch men with fire. And men were scorched with great heat, and blasphemed the name of God, which hath power over these plagues, and they repented not to give him glory." Those who try to find which modern plagues correspond to the Apocalyptic ones will never be long without parallels. That these occurred at a certain time, already defined fairly precisely and to be defined more exactly in a later passage, I cannot doubt. But plagues on land, on sea, in the springs, and from excessive heat cannot be confined to one country or one century. Those who think the words carry some more refined, cabalistic sense may justify that sense as best they can and apply it to our practical guidance. Those who think the prophet was shown how all the different parts and powers of the natural world were becoming instruments of punishment against those who had degraded themselves and their fellows into mere servants of these natural powers—so becoming witnesses against idolatry and for the true Ruler of men—will gladly admit that they have not lost that function, and that the purpose they served then may be served by them now. Nor does their failure to bring about any general repentance—the rage and blasphemy they provoked—place them outside our own experience. Are these not rather very solemn signs to us that we must not expect moral renewal, for ourselves or our fellow men, from the terrors inspired by any natural forces? The God of Life speaks through them; he speaks to our spirits. If our spirits hear him, they are bowed; but let the Angel of Death beat his wings ever so loudly, and they will be scared, not changed.

And now we come to that vial I have already spoken of as the object of them all.

V. "And the fifth angel poured out his vial upon the seat of the beast, and his kingdom was full of darkness, and they gnawed their tongues for pain, and blasphemed the God of heaven because of their pains and their sores, and repented not of their deeds." The tribulation announced here in a few quick words is, I believe, the subject of the next two chapters. In those chapters we are told where the seat of the beast is, in language so plain and unmistakable that there has been little—I believe no—difference of opinion about it. Rome, at some period of its existence—Rome under some kind of ruler, imperial or spiritual—has been taken by all readers as fitting the description. I do not think the prophet stopped there. I think he was just as careful in fixing which ruler he meant as in fixing the capital of that ruler's government. For now, though, our business is not with the person but with the nature of his rule. We are to connect it with all that has gone before in this vision. Those who worship the beast and bear his image are those who cannot conceive of anything as almighty and divine except what is earthly, sensual, devilish. The one they worship is the embodiment of the earthly, the sensual, the diabolical. He is the towering, concentrated version of what is scattered and partial in those he governs. Suddenly an eclipse falls on his power, and confusion covers all their plans. There is something—they cannot say what—threatening them and their works. They are in darkness. They cannot feel their way. What is coming? Is it light? Oh, not that! Let that at least stay far from us. We are miserable enough already. Let it not come to torment us more. They gnawed their tongues in pain and cursed the *God of heaven*.

VI. "And the sixth angel poured out his vial upon the great river Euphrates, and its water was dried up, that the way of the kings of the east might be prepared."

In the passage of the ninth chapter that answers to this, four angels that had been bound at the great river Euphrates were set loose. Taking the Euphrates as the prophetic symbol for the boundary that separated the Babel world from the holy city, I supposed that trumpet meant that boundary would now cease to exist; that the work for which Judæa had been marked out as a separate kingdom in the midst of the earth was done; and that whatever witness might in future be borne against the Babel principle would not be borne by a separate nation with a local center. The change was tremendous—a great shaking would come before and after it—but it was a change all the trumpets had been foretelling. Once it was made, the last trumpet would sound; the age's final message would go out. That change and that final message matter as much for the Gentile world as for the Jewish. Seen with regard to the Gentiles, the Euphrates is said to be *dried up, that the way of the kings of the east may be prepared*. The ending of the Jewish state and the Jewish witness might seem to leave the world free to obey its idol rulers, to accept the latest incarnation of evil as its supreme lord. On the great throne of the West sat that incarnation of evil. To that throne the countries of the West bowed; the East had already fallen before it. Was not that kingship the children of Shem had hoped to hold over the earth, in the name of the one Lord God, now passing away forever? No—not passing away! Rather, for the first time beginning to be shown and felt. The Child to whom the Magi came, bringing gold and frankincense, will now begin his march over those western kingdoms the Roman tyrant had claimed for himself. They are not his; they are held by an older title. The West shall pay homage to the East. There shall be a Christendom confessing the God of Abraham as its God, the Son of David as its King.

Does it seem strange that I speak of what is called a vial of wrath as carrying such a meaning as this? You will stumble at every passage in the Apocalypse if you find a contradiction in the idea that the highest blessings are revealed through punishments that look like nothing but curses. This drying of the Euphrates, if it was to make a road along which the divine kingdom would pass into new regions and settle there, could not happen until the Roman state had been shown to be built of brittle clay—until the Jewish state had disappeared. Nor could it happen without another kind of evil affecting the world—especially, I believe, the Christian part of the world. This is shown in the next verses.

VII. "And I saw three unclean spirits, like frogs, come out of the mouth of the dragon, and out of the mouth of the beast, and out of the mouth of the false prophet. For they are the spirits of devils, working miracles, which go forth unto the kings of the earth and the whole world, to gather them to the battle of that great day of God Almighty. Behold, I come as a thief. Blessed is he that watcheth and keepeth his garments, lest he walk naked, and they see his shame. And he gathered them together into a place called in the Hebrew tongue Armageddon."

The message of a divine Spirit, proceeding from the Father and the Son, was what the Apostles delivered to Gentiles as well as Jews. It broke down the middle wall of partition between them; it was, in the language of the Apocalypse, a drying up of the Euphrates.

But that same message stirred up all the ideas about spiritual influences and powers that had been sleeping in men's minds, hidden under their various mythologies and traditions. So, as I noted when speaking of the locusts said in an earlier chapter to overspread the earth, there was a greater outbreak of enchanters and magicians—some heathen, many of them Jewish, and not a few, like Simon, baptized in the name of Jesus Christ—than the world had ever seen before. The craving for glimpses into the invisible world was strong in men's minds; it grew stronger because of their loss of faith in the official augurs and priests who had once fed it; it attached itself both to the old Roman religion and to the modern unbelief. Those adepts who fed and tried to satisfy this craving might turn it to political or merely personal ends; they were almost sure to combine it with foul morals; imperfect spiritual perceptions and intuitions did not raise up, but rather undermined, that plain sense of honesty and justice that had come down, with however many stains and corruptions, from republican times. These, I take it, are the frogs that came out of the mouths of the dragon, the beast, and the false prophet—mixing the coarsest animal wickedness with the subtlest spiritual wickedness. It is no exaggeration and no metaphor to say they went out to the kings of the earth and of the whole world. They were the Roman generals and proconsuls to whom these enchanters applied, into whose ears they whispered their divinations, whose decisions they swayed with their Babylonian calculations. Christian men, even if they did not give in to the fascination themselves, must often have been greatly troubled by reports of predictions fulfilled and wonders performed. If they trusted to their own cleverness in detecting frauds, to their tests for telling true miracles from false, they must have been constantly deceived. They could not help noticing many likenesses between these tricks and what was most precious in their own traditions. The old fences had been swept away; the outward protection that the difference between circumcised and uncircumcised had given was no longer any use. But this defense remained—"Behold, I come as a thief." The Judge stands at the door. The Son of Man, who exposes all tricks and lies, will be revealed. Will this spiritual conceit of mine bear his scrutiny? The prediction may come true; this juggling may succeed. But am I not a child of the light? Do not these works shrink from the light?

And now comes this further assurance. A battle between the powers of light and darkness is close at hand. All these teachers of the kings of the earth are gathering them for that battle. They may be worshipping a beast; but they are spirits, and spiritual powers must rouse them even to satisfy the lowest instincts—a fearful lesson that every age confirms, but that in all the great crises of history stands out and writes itself in letters of blood.

VIII. "And the seventh angel poured out his vial into the air, and there came a great voice out of the temple of heaven, from the throne, saying, It is done. And there were voices, and thunders, and lightnings, and there was a great earthquake, such as was not since men were upon the earth, so mighty an earthquake and so great. And the great city was divided into three parts, and the cities of the nations fell, and great Babylon came in remembrance before God, to give unto her the cup of the wine of the fierceness of his wrath. And every island fled away, and the mountains were not found. And there fell upon men a great hail out of heaven, every stone about the weight of a talent, and men blasphemed God because of the plague of the hail, for the plague thereof was exceeding great."

When the last trumpet sounded, there were voices in heaven saying, "The kingdoms of the world are become the kingdom of our Lord and of his Christ, and he shall reign for ever." The voice, "It is done," which goes with the pouring out of the vial, I have taken to point to the same end and to mean the same thing. An age of the world was ending. The question of that age had been: Whose are the kingdoms of the world? The hour of decision had come. Christ and Antichrist were to meet each other. The place that stands for the conflict is curiously chosen. The name Megiddo is linked with two great triumphs of the Israelites and with two memorable defeats. It is symbolic for both reasons. The battle of Armageddon is one in which the Lord God fights the beast. He is to win the victory. So far it is like Barak's victory over Sisera, or Gideon's over the Amalekites. But it is a victory in which Israel is not to rise, but to perish. So far it corresponds to the overthrow of Saul and of Josiah. The prophet is a patriot to the last. But it is the God of Israel who lives forever and ever; let the seed of Abraham, let his own Church, become nothing, so that he may be exalted. Let the blind and captive Samson pull the pillars of the house down on himself, if only Dagon and his worshippers may fall with him. Of this fall the next chapters will tell us. I receive them as records of a triumph that has been won, and therefore as an unquestionable pledge and assurance of a triumph that shall be won, for God and for

## LECTURE XVII. THE GREAT CITY.

*The passage:* One of the seven angels who held the seven vials invited John to come and see the judgment of the great whore who sits on many waters, with whom the kings of the earth have committed fornication and by whose wine the earth's inhabitants have been made drunk. Carried in the spirit into the wilderness, John saw a woman sitting on a scarlet beast covered with names of blasphemy, with seven heads and ten horns. She was dressed in purple and scarlet, adorned with gold, jewels, and pearls, holding a golden cup full of abominations and filth, and on her forehead was written "MYSTERY, BABYLON THE GREAT, THE MOTHER OF HARLOTS AND ABOMINATIONS OF THE EARTH." She was drunk with the blood of the saints and martyrs of Jesus, and John marvelled greatly. The angel promised to explain the mystery of the woman and the beast that carries her, with its seven heads and ten horns. The beast "was, and is not," and shall rise from the bottomless pit and go to destruction; those on earth whose names were not written in the book of life will wonder at it. The seven heads are seven mountains on which the woman sits, and are also seven kings: five have fallen, one is, one is not yet come and will last only a short while; the beast that "was, and is not" is himself an eighth, yet one of the seven, and goes to destruction. The ten horns are ten kings not yet crowned, who will receive power for one hour with the beast, give their strength to him, and make war with the Lamb, who will overcome them because he is Lord of lords and King of kings, and those with him are called, chosen, and faithful. The waters are peoples, multitudes, nations, and tongues.
