The ten horns will come to hate the whore, strip her, devour her flesh, and burn her with fire, for God has put it in their hearts to fulfil his will until his words are fulfilled. The woman is that great city which reigns over the kings of the earth.

In an earlier chapter we saw a woman clothed with the sun, the moon under her feet. Here we read of a woman with "Babylon the great, the mother of harlots, and abominations of the earth" written on her forehead. Everyone has noticed that the two visions correspond. A Protestant controversialist would say at once that the first describes the true Church of God, kept alive through the ages—hidden from human eyes but beautiful in the sight of her Lord—and that the second points to the Papacy, seated in outward grandeur and power, but marked by God as idolatrous and apostate.

An interpretation that has sunk so deeply into the mind of a nation like ours, shaping our everyday speech and colouring our devotions, is not something we can safely throw away. It must mean something. If we ignore that meaning, we risk missing lessons the prophecy was meant to teach, shrinking it to fit our own private notions, or reducing it to mere antiquarian curiosity.

But there are other uses of the title "great Babylon" which, by the same principle, we should not overlook. It has been applied to the city of London, the very capital of Protestantism, and our consciences have accepted the name as fitting. Again, the most earnest religious teachers have urged their hearers to flee from Babylon and shun the Babylonian harlot, meaning by both phrases that world which Romanists and Protestants alike renounce in their baptism. While we do justice to one of these ways of speaking, we should test whether it is not possible to do justice to all three. If we stay close to the book itself, I believe we can.

I have given you my reasons for thinking that the vision of the woman clothed with the sun, about to give birth to the man-child, is a vision of humanity seen truly: in itself weak and feminine; great only while it looks up to a glory above its own, which becomes its clothing; poor and incomplete until it has found a Son of Man who is also a Son of God, one who does not stay on earth but is carried to heaven's throne, where he is to be acknowledged as our living Head, our Redeemer, our Representative. The discovery of this true Head of Man, and of the glory of Man in him, is what I took the vision to be about. Before this could be discovered there was war in heaven; when it was made, the adversary was cast down from heaven. There was no more darkness over the form of him on the throne. Those who confessed the crucified Man to be at his right hand could overcome all doubts and temptations by the blood of the Lamb and by the word of his testimony. But there was war on the earth. The adversary had not finished his battle here; he was carrying it on furiously. What that battle was, and who would be caught up in it, we were to learn as we went on.

Taking the first vision in this sense, it would, I thought, justify those who said the Church was implied in it. The Church is that body appointed, in its own life and acts, to testify of a Son of Man and Son of God, to say that humanity is complete only in him, to call men to claim the glory he has claimed for them, to tell them that God's face has been fully shown in the only begotten Son and is therefore a face of clear, unclouded light. In its hour of deepest sorrow—which has been its hour of greatest triumph—the Church must represent the woman who sees the sun above her and the moon at her feet. And she must be in danger of looking at the moon instead of the sun, at herself instead of her divine Head.

On the other hand, I have shown you how the image of a woman forgetting her true lord and becoming a harlot runs through all the language of the Old Scriptures, illustrating every national sin. In the Old Testament this harlotry is charged upon Israel—upon Israel alone—precisely because Israel was said to have been claimed as the bride of an unseen, spiritual Lord. The sin that earns this shameful name is idolatry. Israel forgets its unseen King and worships visible things—it becomes a creature of the senses. In doing so it tries to be like the surrounding nations, when it was set in the world to tell those nations that they were made for another service, a nobler fellowship. The punishment for this crime was captivity in the very land that gathered up the idolatry of all the nations, where idolatry had its head and crown, was organised into a system, openly propped up a universal tyranny, was fast turning into the worship of a mortal ruler, and that worship into the worship of what is below humanity.

Babylon, then, seen first on the plain of Shinar—where trust in God was traded for trust in brick walls, where the family rescued from the flood forgot its task of colonising and subduing the earth and tried to make itself a name and build a tower to reach heaven—and seen in all its later tyranny and conquests, was the living type of the idolatrous city. And when the chosen Son was revealed as head not of one nation but of every man, the idolatrous city began to be seen as the mother of harlots: as that which had all along been drawing men away from the worship of a Spiritual Being, their Lord, Friend, and Redeemer, into the worship of many natural things and powers—worshipping not the archetype of man but his mere image, worshipping evil instead of good. From the moment the mystery of God—the mystery of a redeemed humanity—was revealed, Babylon became the mystery of iniquity, representing the degradation and misery of humanity, both socially and individually. The place itself need not stay the same. What was Nineveh under Sennacherib was Babylon under Nebuchadnezzar. The type endured, even when a western city, at a wholly different stage of civilisation, became the ruling power of the earth.

If, then, as we Protestants believe, there is and has been a system spread through Christendom that robs man of his true dignity, blocks his communication with his risen and ascended Lord, and separates heaven from earth by making a mortal man the highest of all rulers—if such a system deserves to be called idolatrous and apostate—then it is a true instinct that links this system with Babylonian harlotry, precisely because our charges against it imply that it is a relapse into the state of the heathen world, that it has canonised the very despotism of sense from which the birth and glorification of the Man-child was meant to free God's creatures made in his image. And if in this city of ours there is, among clergy and laity, idle and busy, noble and tradesman and workman, on one plea or another, on religious or irreligious grounds, in deference to aristocratic or democratic notions, a tendency to revere the visible above the invisible, the passing above the permanent, the customs of society above moral and eternal laws—if this tendency has grown powerful and threatens to become supreme—then it is also a true instinct that recalls the greatness of Babylon when we are tempted to boast of the greatness of London, and warns that London may prove no more lasting than Babylon. A citizen of Paris, Madrid, Vienna, or Venice might rightly feel the same. But our consciences tell us that we have less to do with those cities than with London; their evils are, to some extent, foreign to us, while London's evils are our own. And so it is right that those who teach children or grown men to renounce the pomps and vanities of this wicked world should explain what they mean: "Now, as of old, there are idols' processions and sacrifices offered to worthless things that cannot help or save. Members of Christ, who gave himself away, you are tempted by a society that makes self its centre. Children of God, you are tempted to think yourselves children of Mammon, with everything around you bowing to him. Heirs of the kingdom of heaven, you are led to suppose there are no treasures for you but those that moth and rust corrupt and thieves steal. An evil atmosphere surrounds you. Call your world religious, political, commercial, or fashionable, whatever you like—it is still a harlot world, a world of sense; still a Babylonian world, a world of confusion and bondage." But all these applications assume a conclusion already reached. They would be worth nothing unless there were some type of them in the old world. What that type is we are to learn from the Apostle himself, who will always tell us more than his commentators do.

The first six verses of this chapter refer to the *mystery* of this woman—that is, to the meaning of the symbol, to what belongs not to one age but to all, to the evil that has threatened the heart of human society ever since there was such a thing. They connect with the earlier vision of the seven vials, and they point, as all the earlier chapters have done, to an approaching judgment.

I. "And there came one of the seven angels who had the seven vials, and talked with me, saying, Come here; I will show you the judgment of the great whore that sits on many waters, with whom the kings of the earth have committed fornication, and the inhabitants of the earth have been made drunk with the wine of her fornication. So he carried me away in the spirit into the wilderness; and I saw a woman sitting on a scarlet-coloured beast, full of names of blasphemy, having seven heads and ten horns. And the woman was arrayed in purple and scarlet, and decked with gold and precious stones and pearls, having a golden cup in her hand full of the abomination and filthiness of her fornication. And on her forehead was a name written, Babylon the great, the mother of harlots and abominations of the earth. And I saw the woman drunk with the blood of the saints, and with the blood of the martyrs of Jesus; and I wondered with great admiration."

In one of Zechariah's visions, which resembles this one in many striking ways and clearly presents the mystery of wickedness in contrast to the divine mystery shown in the Temple at Jerusalem, an angel speaks of a "House in the land of Shinar, which shall be established and set there upon her own base." A political order standing on its own foundation, its gods shaped out of human ideas, and by their help becoming a cruel and bloody tyranny—this is the idea of Babylon here and throughout Scripture. We have often been shown the final result. She who would not stand on divine ground must at last be carried by a beast. Humanity has only these two choices. In the end she must confess either a spiritual Lord or a brutal one. On her way to complete degradation she has been drinking the blood of righteous men who witnessed to her of her true greatness and of her fall. All these are called martyrs of Jesus. It does not matter when or where they lived, in what city they made their protest, or in what words or deeds they expressed it. So far as they testified that man is more than an animal, that there is a divine Power seeking to give man his true human rights, that a man is not to save his life but to lose it, not to glorify self but to give himself away—so far they were witnesses for Jesus, and his Spirit and no other was working in them. An apostle must say so. Other men might call them brave or heroic. He must trace everything good in man back to the Lord of Man—all partial light back to the perfect light.

Next we come to the explanation of this mystery as it applies to the particular period the prophecy is concerned with.

II. "And the angel said to me, Why did you marvel? I will tell you the mystery of the woman, and of the beast that carries her, which has the seven heads and ten horns. The beast that you saw was, and is not; and shall ascend out of the bottomless pit and go into perdition; and those who dwell on the earth shall wonder, whose names were not written in the book of life from the foundation of the world, when they behold the beast that was, and is not, and yet is. And here is the mind which has wisdom. The seven heads are seven mountains, on which the woman sits. And there are seven kings: five are fallen, and one is, and one is not yet come; and when he comes, he must continue a short space. And the beast that was, and is not, even he is the eighth, and is of the seven, and goes into perdition." The angel's explanation of the Babylon of that day has, as I noted last Sunday, been accepted by all interpreters as clear and conclusive: it must mean Rome. The only dispute has been, Rome at what period? On that I said the statement seems to me quite as precise. If there are, as I argued in an earlier sermon, grounds for treating the founding of the military despotism under Augustus—together with the divine honours given him as its head—and the Incarnation, or proclamation of Christ's kingdom, as parallel events in the world's history, then Augustus would be the first of the five kings here. Tiberius would be the second, Caius Caligula the third, Claudius the fourth, Nero the fifth. These kings "are not." In the absence of any real evidence for the old tradition that the Apocalypse belongs to Domitian's time, almost any thoughtful critic would take this very passage as reason to place it in Galba's reign. He, the sixth, "was"; Otho, the seventh, "was not yet come." A figure was seen rising behind—an eighth, yet strangely like one of the seven. When Nero died, it looked as if the wild beast into which imperial government had turned itself was dead. Vitellius would show that it still lived and breathed. The world would not only submit to this new apparition but stare after it in blank astonishment, as though beastly tyranny had now proved itself all-powerful, as though nothing else could be meant for mankind—a very natural conclusion, which the facts seemed fully to bear out, and which every attempt to shake off the yoke only confirmed. No one who merely watched passing events and reasoned from them could resist believing that evil would permanently prevail. Only those whose names were in the book of life from the foundation of the world, who saw all things in the light of eternity and watched a book of life unrolling from the beginning, claiming a place in that roll rather than the roll of death—only they could sing of the beast's ruin in his hour of triumph, only they could be sure he would last only a short while. I do not say the tyrant's followers expected his power to last long. Anticipation belongs to men, to spiritual beings; *they* could feel only the leaden weight of the present; *they* could only suppose that a yoke like the one then on the earth must always be there. And so it has been again and again, and so it will be. The revelation may belong directly to the brief reign of Vitellius. But it is the revelation of that state of mind which, at one time or another, overtakes those who have no king but Cæsar, who, being "sensual and dark," must carry the image of a sensual and dark tyranny in their hearts.

III. "And the ten horns which you saw are ten kings, who have received no power as yet, but receive power as kings one hour with the beast. These have one mind, and shall give their strength and power to the beast."

I have already suggested that these horns stand for the military force on which the empire rested. The ten kings would then be the commanders of the legions in the various provinces, who had received no power as yet, though they had just as much claim to the purple, and were just as likely one day to seize it, as the usurper they were content to serve, and for whose sake they set aside their own claims for now. There could not be a truer picture of an organised anarchy. The condition Hobbes imagined society emerging out of was the condition into which it had sunk.

IV. "These shall make war with the Lamb, and the Lamb shall overcome them; and those who are with him are called, and chosen, and faithful."

In old Roman legends, mounted champions recognised as the divine Twins would appear suddenly to encourage a failing army and then vanish when the victory was won. An old Hebrew, an apostle of Jesus Christ, has no sympathy with such legends. He does not want *appearances*. The whole point of the prophecy is to free us from slavery to shapes and apparitions. Those were for the sense-bound idolater. The Christian believed in the crucified Lamb, whom he could not see. Whatever visible armies fight, he sees the real King of kings and Lord of lords on one side and his enemy on the other. What would give him strength in such a crisis as we are reading of is the assurance that unseen hosts are on his side, that Christ leads them, and that therefore the beast will go down to destruction. And the first century was to give the watchword for all centuries to come. Whenever a conflict is being fought in which the order, freedom, and well-being of mankind are at stake, it does not matter how strong the combatants are, or whether they even know in whose name they fight. The victory that puts down the oppressor is the victory of the Lamb. He, with the chosen, faithful, and true, goes out against the beast. *In hoc signo vinces*—"in this sign you will conquer"—is a message to the true warrior. God's Spirit can bring it to him without any cross appearing in the sky.

V. "And he said to me, The waters which you saw, where the whore sits, are peoples, and multitudes, and nations, and tongues. And the ten horns which you saw on the beast, these shall hate the whore, and shall make her desolate and naked, and shall eat her flesh, and burn her with fire. For God has put it in their hearts to fulfil his will, and to agree, and give their kingdom to the beast, until the words of God shall be fulfilled. And the woman which you saw is that great city which reigns over the kings of the earth." When the armies of the Duke of Bourbon entered Rome and, in the name of Charles V.—the very defender of the Church—laid the holy city waste with fire and sword and took its spiritual ruler captive, no doubt the sixteenth-century Reformers thought this language was strikingly fulfilled. The great earthly powers were making desolate and naked the "great city which reigns over the kings of the earth." "God had put it into the hearts of these soldiers of various lands to fulfil his will." Suppose the same drama had been played out more than fourteen centuries earlier—suppose Rome had then been laid waste by military violence and its Capitol burnt—the sixteenth-century Reformers would not have been wrong to say the lesson was repeated in their day; that the same kind of instruments were at work in both cases; that the judgment, whoever carried it out, came from the highest throne of all; that the wicked and cruel tools were working out his great, gracious, and true will. Such thoughts are the only ones that can hold up men's spirits through great crises, the only ones that let them bear the misery they see. But the Reformers spoiled the lesson if they twisted it to any party use—if they read the Bourbon's victory as a judgment in favour of Protestantism rather than a judgment upon unrighteousness, corruption, and hypocrisy wherever it is found. That has been one very harmful result of our way of handling the Apocalypse, which I have tried to illustrate in this sermon. We have found Babylon in the Papacy and forgotten to look for it among ourselves. We have not seen that the harlotry of the senses is at work everywhere, that everywhere men are tempted to become idolaters, that everywhere there are seducers drawing them away from their true Husband and Lord. The far-off evil we have spotted; its punishment we are ready to cheer; but the evil at our own door, in our own hearts, we cannot see. We forget that God shows no favouritism; that Churches which acknowledge Rome's supremacy and those that reject it, individual Papists and individual Protestants, Pharisees and Publicans, will all be sifted by his fan, and must all have their chaff burnt with unquenchable fire.

And this bad result of hasty, one-sided interpretation is closely tied to another. We talk of a Papal apostasy until we forget what the word apostasy even means. We forget that it means standing off from what is our proper ground; that if we revolt, we revolt from a government actually set over us. There could be no apostasy unless a kingdom of God had first been set up in the world. We have paid Popery an honour it has no right to when we have identified it with the Christian Church of many centuries, as if only some tiny body here and there had represented the divine election. It is a most necessary part of faith to believe that a fungus, however widely spread, however closely it seems to imitate the life, is not the tree it is destroying; that the tree is wholly of a good seed. Forget this and all history becomes nothing but a record of foul enormities—a record not of the reign and conquests of the Lamb, but of the Beast. Forget this, and we at once glorify ourselves as though we were better than others, and degrade ourselves by denying the glory and responsibilities laid equally on us and on them. London one moment exalts itself as the great opposite of Rome; the next it sinks into a mere Babylon where evil is the rule and good the rare exception. Whereas we ought to believe that both Rome and London are cities of redeemed men, where God has set up his kingdom, and which the citizens of either turn into a Babylon by disbelieving in their redemption and in his rule, by exalting their own idolatrous, sensual, selfish desires in contempt of their own human greatness and in defiance of his divine purposes.

This, I believe, lies at the root of all our moral evils: that we do not confess humanity to be married to Christ, that all men have been claimed as citizens of his kingdom and not of the Babel kingdom. We do not really confess that God has reconciled the world to himself; and so we do not really reject the world's claim that it is separate from God and can live without him.

From these great errors, which are infecting both our theology and our life, I believe the study of the Apocalypse as it is actually written can be a most effective means of freeing us. It will teach us that the Babel society was always corrupt and adulterous, that it never in any age could be regarded as the true Order of the world. The history and literature of the heathen nations—which our forefathers rightly made a central part of our education—though stained and infected with idolatry, still bear witness to what is not idolatrous, and are perpetual protests against what was corrupting and degrading mankind. They looked forward to the incarnation of the Son of God, who claimed all the life, justice, and self-sacrifice of the old world as his own. The battle of the Lamb against the Beast was to vindicate that truth for the past and to make it the foundation of all the fellowship of men in the ages to come.

## LECTURE XVIII. THE FALL OF BABYLON.

*The passage:* John sees another angel come down from heaven with great power, so bright that the earth is lit up by his glory. The angel cries out that Babylon the great has fallen and become a home for demons and unclean spirits, because all nations were made drunk with her corruption, the kings of the earth joined in it, and the merchants grew rich on her luxuries. Another voice from heaven calls God's people to come out of her so they will not share her sins or her punishments, for her sins have piled up to heaven. She is to be repaid double for her deeds; because she boasted that she sits as a queen, is no widow, and will never see sorrow, her plagues—death, mourning, famine, and fire—will come in a single day, for strong is the Lord who judges her. The kings, merchants, and shipmasters who profited from her stand far off and mourn as they watch the smoke of her burning, wailing that so great a city and so great a wealth came to nothing in one hour; a long list of her merchandise is named, ending with slaves and the souls of men. Heaven and the apostles and prophets are told to rejoice, for God has avenged them. A mighty angel throws a great millstone into the sea to show how violently Babylon will be cast down and vanish, with no more music, craft, millstone, lamplight, or voices of bride and bridegroom, because her merchants were the great men of the earth and her sorceries deceived all nations, and in her was found the blood of the prophets and saints and of all who were slain on the earth.

The language of the previous chapter was exact. The angel spoke of five kings who no longer are, one who now is, a seventh yet to come, and an eighth like one of the seven. It spoke of a city on seven hills that rules over the kings of the earth. Such a description, I thought, must have been meant to fix our minds on imperial Rome at a particular time. The emperor before Vespasian seemed clearly indicated; the setting of the prophecy seemed to be the period between Nero's death and the fall of Vitellius.

The language of *this* chapter is broad and general. It is impossible to limit this description to any disasters that fell on the mere city of Rome—even including, as they did, the burning of the Capitol itself. So I cannot wonder—nor regret—that men in different ages have been sure it did not refer only to some past event affecting others, but concerned them and their own actions, that it was a prophecy about the world they were living in. As I said last Sunday, I believe their instinct is entirely right, and that the more strictly we hold to the *exact* words of the book, the more that instinct is confirmed.

If the Apostle meant Rome, why did he say Babylon? The question is often asked, and the usual answer is surely unsatisfactory. Some suppose that speaking openly would have been dangerous, so he was forced into riddles. Dangerous for whom? He was already on Patmos—would a few more years of life matter so much to an apostle that he would suppress the truth to gain them? Perhaps dangerous because he might have stirred up rebellion against the ruling powers. But whom could he have tempted into such a rebellion? No heathen would have listened. His words were especially discouraging to all Jewish dreams of supremacy. They plainly warned the members of the Church not to take the sword, lest they perish by it. The whole empire, heathen and Jewish alike, was already in chaos. What men wanted was someone to tell them whether the chaos would last forever.

We read of the destruction of Babylon, not Rome, because it was Babylon's destruction that was in the mind of the prophet and of Him who taught the prophet. We read of Babylon, not Rome, because the words were exactly true of the Babel-order of which Rome had become the center, and would not have been true of the mere city, whether considered as walls or as a mass of people. We read of Babylon, not Rome, because the subject is a system that had been at work for generations, not in one country but in all countries, and which was to hear its sentence in that generation. We read of Babylon, not Rome, so that we will not tie that system to one place—however evil the place may be—but may study it in all its forms and aspects, and be certain that wherever it has existed, exists, or shall exist, it is essentially the same.

I. "*And after these things I saw another angel come down from heaven, having great power; and the earth was lightened with his glory. And he cried mightily with a strong voice, saying, Babylon the great is fallen, is fallen, and is become the habitation of devils, and the hold of every foul spirit, and a cage of every unclean and hateful bird. For all nations have drunk of the wine of the wrath of her fornication, and the kings of the earth have committed fornication with her, and the merchants of the earth are waxed rich through the abundance of her delicacies.*"

The earth's darkness is shown to John by a light from heaven. That light does not let him regard any evil as original or as finally victorious. Babylon has fallen from a better and nobler condition; she has *become* a home of demons. The phrase clearly points to moral or spiritual wickedness. The "*unclean and hateful birds*" nesting in the city are not owls and bitterns, but the greed, filthiness, and malice that fill the empty spaces once occupied by better spirits. The ruling city had been corrupting the nations with her pride, ambition, and lust for gold. Kings of West and East had sold their truth and freedom to her; she had degraded and stupefied them with her luxuries; she had made the earth's traders servants of her greed. We all know this was so. If we are afraid to fix the charge on the Roman world of the first century, it is because we suspect the words may apply to worlds we have known more closely. We need not fear that they will lose their force. Let them mean what they meant at first. God will see to it that they mean no less to us.

II. "*And I heard another voice out of heaven, saying, Come out of her, my people, that ye be not partakers of her sins, and that ye receive not of her plagues. For her sins have reached unto heaven, and God hath remembered her iniquities. Reward her even as she rewarded you, and double unto her double according to her works: in the cup which she hath filled fill to her double. How much she hath glorified herself and lived delicately, so much torment and sorrow give her: for she saith in her heart, I sit as a queen, and am no widow, and shall see no sorrow. Therefore shall her plagues come in one day, death, and mourning, and famine; and she shall be utterly burned with fire: for strong is the Lord God which judgeth her.*"

Did this heavenly voice mean that Christians should flee from Rome because it was about to suffer great disasters? Where could they flee? What part of the empire was not exposed to the same disasters? Would they share Rome's sins more by living in her than by living anywhere else? Everyone has felt that the message must mean something other than this. Babylon was in Rome, but it was also wherever men worshipped the things they could see and handle, worshipped their own lusts, worshipped themselves. It was this worship that had brought the evil spirits into the city; from it, and from them, God calls His people out. All were His people—Jews or heathens—who would obey that voice, who would confess a righteous Being to be King and Judge of the earth, who would firmly believe that whatever is not righteous must perish. It is hard to believe that—hard even for those who profess to believe it most, yes, even for those whose reason is fully convinced it cannot be otherwise. Atheism creeps over everyone. The more corrupt a society is, the more its habits and traditions press upon us the feeling that it will go on; it robs us of the heart and hope that ought to rise against it. If the belief of that society were true—that God has abandoned the world to itself, that He is only as just as we imagine Him to be—there would be no escape from this state of things: the worse it grew, the more it would seem guaranteed to last. But He speaks, and He can make Himself heard. He makes men's consciences understand that they will have their revenge for the debasement and degradation these vile doctrines and grovelling practices have inflicted on them—for these have infected everyone within their reach; there is no one whose standard has not been lowered, whose character has not been impoverished, by them. It is a glorious proclamation for mankind: "*Reward her even as she rewarded you, and double unto her double according to her works*"; it is the fitting song of triumph over the fall of a demoralizing tyranny. Do not pity it; it has withered human hearts and souls; let it wither utterly. The punishment must match her luxury and wantonness, must match the carelessness and stupid confidence that centuries of sin have bred in her. "*She sits as a queen*"—what can depose her? She *is no widow*—what does she care for any divine Lord? She "*shall see no sorrow*"—she does not know what sorrow means; she cannot imagine it coming near her. And so the destined fall catches her wholly unprepared: "*in one day there is death, mourning, famine*." The fire that went out from her comes back to consume her, for He whom she thought far away is at hand. She finds that righteousness is not a fantasy but a Person—"*strong is the Lord God that judgeth her*."

III. "*And the kings of the earth, who have committed fornication and lived deliciously with her, shall bewail her, and lament for her, when they shall see the smoke of her burning, standing afar off for the fear of her torment, saying, Alas! alas, that great city Babylon, that mighty city! for in one hour is thy judgment come.*"

The terror of these kings is not about the fall of a particular dynasty—they would be well used to that—nor about some ordinary trouble overtaking Rome, which they might even have welcomed. They have no sympathy for the victim; they "*stand afar off for fear of her torment*." What they feel is the insecurity and instability of everything they had assumed was most secure and stable. Their world is toppling at its foundation. The scheme of society they thought immortal is breaking apart. The outer supports have failed. It seems to have no inner cohesion. The religious ideas it cherished, which seemed to promise more than material help, are thrown aside; men suspect them to be inventions—they never stopped the rulers from doing as they pleased, and they will not stop the rebels from doing as they please. "*Alas, alas, that great city, Babylon!*" It stood on its own foundation, and that foundation has given way.

IV. "*And the merchants of the earth shall weep and mourn over her; for no man buyeth their merchandise any more: the merchandise of gold and silver, and precious stones, and of pearls, and fine linen, and purple, and silk, and scarlet, and all thyine wood, and all manner vessels of ivory, and all manner vessels of most precious wood, and of brass, and iron, and marble, and cinnamon, and odours, and ointments, and frankincense, and wine, and oil, and fine flour, and wheat, and beasts, and sheep, and horses, and chariots, and slaves, and the souls of men. And the fruits that thy soul lusted after are departed from thee, and all things which were dainty and goodly are departed from thee, and thou shalt find them no more at all. The merchants of these things, which were made rich by her, shall stand afar off for the fear of her torment, weeping and wailing, and saying, Alas, alas, that great city, that was clothed in fine linen, and purple, and scarlet, and decked with gold, and precious stones and pearls! for in one hour so great riches is come to nought. And every shipmaster, and all the company in ships, and sailors, and as many as trade by sea, stood afar off, and cried when they saw the smoke of her burning, saying, What city is like unto this great city! And they cast dust on their heads, and cried, weeping and wailing, saying, Alas, alas that great city, wherein were made rich all that had ships in the sea by reason of her costliness! for in one hour she is made desolate.*"

The detail of this description is exactly what gives it its value. Sum up the "*odours, and ointments, and frankincense, and wine, and fine flour*" in some general phrase about the vanishing of material prosperity, and you carry us off into vagueness; the actual cries of the merchants, the shipmasters, and the sailors are no longer heard. I would only weaken the passage by commenting on it. But there are one or two words in the middle of it that throw light on the whole. The trade is in "*beasts, and sheep, and horses, and chariots, and slaves, and the souls of men*." No distinction is drawn between the items; they are all treated alike, as entries on a bill of lading, as things to be bartered. The last of them are valued chiefly as tools for producing and exchanging the rest. They are the least significant part, but a necessary part, of the great system. They must be ground to atoms in the whirl of this social machine; they eat a certain amount of food; one by one they die; their places are filled by others who do the same jobs. And the kings of the earth, and the merchants, shipmasters, and even the sailors, cannot see how it could be otherwise—how society could ever be built on any other principle. To those who profit from the labor and to those who do it alike, it is equally hard to imagine that men could be anything more than hands for reshaping certain things, or heads for directing those hands. It is not only a class of tributaries or serfs that the earth's rulers reduce to this condition; they themselves are tributaries and serfs. The vision is of a state of society in which they have lost any idea that they have any purpose on earth except to combine "*odours, and frankincense, and fine flour, and wine, and sheep, and oxen*" in different ways. It is a world of restless idleness—all wanting to be idlers, all imagining some distant time when they will have so much more of the earth's goods than their neighbors that they can simply eat, drink, and be merry. But these children never reach the end of the rainbow, however hard they run; the serfdom continues; the merriment is always still to come; and their very capacity to enjoy it grows less each day.

V. And therefore, "*Rejoice over her, thou heaven, and ye holy apostles and prophets, for God hath avenged you on her.*"

You, sons of God, who shouted for joy when the earth was made very good, when man ruled over all its bright and glorious things, when man looked up to God, was formed in His image, and worshipped Him—you have seen the spectacle of a wicked world, men bowing down to these things and making gods of them. Will you not shout for joy again, because God is declaring that this reversal shall not stand, because in thunder and lightning He bears witness that His order will be established and that whatever contradicts it will come to nothing? What have you been protesting against, holy prophets, but this worship of things, this degradation of man? What have you been saying but that God will rise up from His place to put down those who make men toil in the furnace, that He will show a man to be more precious than a golden wedge of Ophir, that He will bring forth One who will rule in righteousness and judge the poor, who "*shall reprove with equity for the meek of the earth, who shall smite the earth with the rod of his mouth, and with the breath of his lips shall slay the wicked*"? What have you been announcing, holy apostles, but that the Son of God *has* taken the form of a servant and become man; *has* passed on earth as the carpenter's son; *has* died the death of the slave and the criminal; *has* risen from death in His human body; and in that body *has* ascended on high to fill all things? Did you imagine that in announcing this Gospel you were introducing a new religion to overthrow the old and to glorify your own names? Did you not understand that you were declaring the divine order and economy of the universe? Did you not understand that you were foretelling the utter downfall and destruction of everything—by whatever name it boasts—that reduces man to a slave and an animal, that forbids him to claim his glory as a member of the body of the only-begotten Son of God? Then will you not rejoice? For in this downfall of a brutal tyranny, in this chaos of the ruling city, in this earthquake of all nations, your message is proved true; the kingdom of man's enemy is shown to be rotten; the kingdom of man's Father and Redeemer is shown to be eternal.

VI. "*And a mighty angel took up a stone like a great millstone, and cast it into the sea, saying, Thus with violence shall that great city Babylon be thrown down, and shall be found no more at all. And the voice of harpers, and musicians, and of pipers, and trumpeters, shall be heard no more at all in thee; and no craftsman, of whatsoever craft he be, shall be found any more in thee; and the sound of a millstone shall be heard no more at all in thee; and the light of a candle shall shine no more at all in thee; and the voice of the bridegroom and of the bride shall be heard no more at all in thee: for thy merchants were the great men of the earth; for by thy sorceries were all nations deceived. And in her was found the blood of prophets, and of saints, and of all that were slain upon the earth.*"

The merchants and shipmasters wailed when they saw what was happening in the world. It seemed to them that the system they belonged to, the one they had given their hearts to, was breaking into pieces while another world came into view. The angel who casts the stone into the sea confirms their fear. He too sees in what is happening the pledge of what shall happen; he perceives the doom of the whole Babel system and order in the shock it is passing through at this particular moment. Understanding the nature of prophecy this way frees us from many crude notions about it—notions on which many opinions that disparage the authority of both the Old and New Testaments have been built. "Is it true," we are asked, "that the voice of harpers, musicians, and trumpeters was never again heard in the city of Rome after the first century? Is it true that *no craftsman of any craft was found in her*, and that *the voice of the bridegroom and the bride* was never again heard in her after the upheaval that came upon her in the days of Vitellius, or after any event since? Must not St. John then have been mistaken? Was not the catastrophe he thought was near really centuries away and still unfulfilled? Is it not obvious that he was expecting much more than the destruction of Rome? Was he not looking for the destruction of the world?"

I will take the last question first, and answer it as I have before. If by the destruction of the world you mean the destruction of this earth as it came from God's hands, then St. Paul and St. John did not expect it in their own day, because they did not expect it in any day. The earth was dear to them; they believed Christ had come upon it to redeem it from its curse, to burn away its corruption, to claim it for His Father. If, on the other hand, by the destruction of the world you mean the destruction of human society, I answer that the Apostles' Gospel was about a kingdom of heaven. They took great pains to show that by a kingdom of heaven they did not mean a kingdom somewhere in the clouds, but a society of men built on a principle that does not make society destructive and impossible—a society governed by God and not by the Devil. They did not foretell a destruction of human society in their own day; had they done so, they would have been foretelling that the very kingdom they said would never end was to perish at once. What they did look for was a striking vindication of the law of human society against those who break that law. This they did expect in their own day. They said that if it came in their day, a truth would be revealed for all ages to come: it would be shown that the earth and human society rest on a divine foundation and can rest on no other. One great experiment had been made, and was still being made, to establish it on a brutal or devilish foundation. That experiment would be confounded. The Babel doctrine might have champions, advocates, and apostles by the thousands and tens of thousands in later ages, but it had been proved a lie, and every fresh trial of it would furnish a fresh proof.

And now I come to the particular question about Rome. Beyond all doubt she rose again as an imperial city after the anarchy under Vitellius; she remained imperial from Vespasian to Augustulus; she conferred the title of Caesar in both East and West even after she herself was subject to barbarian masters; the name Eternal City, given her by heathens, was confirmed by popes; her laws and customs were carried into every nation of the West; within her actual walls the voices of harpers and musicians, of bridegrooms and brides, were heard again; all the arts and ornaments of life were there. So it was—and so the words of the prophets and apostles were fulfilled. They said the earth is the Lord's and everything in it; they denied that man's pride and vanity are the source of any outward or inward treasure man possesses; they said that He who gives to all generously and without reproach is the author and sustainer of laws, institutions, and social order, of everything that makes life healthy, convenient, and graceful, of all that lifts us above the beasts that perish. And therefore they said that Babylon the Great—which refuses to confess that the earth is the Lord's and everything in it, which traces all men's possessions to their pride and vanity, which degrades all laws and institutions into servants of arbitrary will, which turns the arts into servants of materialism and sensuality—must sink like lead in the waters; that whenever, in any kingdom on earth, its maxims become fully accepted and all other maxims disbelieved, that kingdom must perish. The voices of bride and bridegroom must cease in it; its music must turn to discord. It may revive, it may pass through many transformations, but the same law will hold in each. God will be its upholder; its own lusts and atheism will undermine it. These lusts and this atheism are most unbearable when they are disguised in the forms of religion, when the name of God is used to cover contempt and denial of His nature, when the spirit of selfishness is confused with His, when the sacrifices that spirit delights in—the sacrifices of the souls and bodies of men—are offered up as if they pleased the God of Truth. In her, it is said, was found the blood of prophets and of all who had been slain upon the earth. This belief in a false and cruel God is responsible for the murder of all brave men who stood firm for the truth, and for the murder of all men whatsoever, in soul and body. All are victims of the Babel system. The blood of all is uncovered when that system falls.

"What matters where, if *I* am still the same?"—the words our poet puts in Satan's mouth. They have a striking force in the sense he meant them. They also have another force. What does it matter where—in what city or land, under what government or what religion—evil is still the same, selfishness is still the same. The Adversary will make a Babylon wherever men will stoop to him. But "What matters where, if *I* am still the same" are also words that He who came to destroy the works of the Devil and to bruise the serpent's head is speaking to our hearts: "Whatever falsehood there is in any society or in any heart, I can overcome it. Out of death I can bring life. Whatever has been turned to Babylon's service—whatever gifts of the earth, whatever human faculties, energies, and affections, in any corner of the universe—is mine. It is the purchase of my blood. I have vindicated it, and I will vindicate it, for the service of the kingdom of God."

## LECTURE XIX. THE WORD OF GOD.

*The passage:* A great crowd in heaven shouts "Alleluia," praising God for salvation, glory, honour, and power, because His judgments are true and just: He has judged the great harlot who corrupted the earth and has avenged His servants' blood. The elders and living creatures worship God, and a voice from the throne calls all His servants, small and great, to praise Him. Then a multitude like many waters and thunder cries "Alleluia, for the Lord God Omnipotent reigns; the marriage of the Lamb has come, and His bride has prepared herself," clothed in fine linen, which is the righteousness of the saints. John is told to write that those called to the marriage supper are blessed—these are the true words of God. John falls to worship the messenger, but is told not to: the messenger is a fellow-servant, for the testimony of Jesus is the spirit of prophecy. Then heaven opens and a rider on a white horse appears, called Faithful and True, who judges and makes war in righteousness; His eyes are flaming, He wears many crowns and a name only He knows, His robe is dipped in blood, and His name is The Word of God. Heaven's armies follow Him. A sharp sword comes from His mouth to strike the nations; He rules with iron and treads the winepress of God's wrath, and bears the name King of Kings and Lord of Lords. An angel in the sun calls the birds to the supper of God, to eat the flesh of kings, captains, and all people. The beast, the kings, and their armies gather to make war against the rider, but the beast and the false prophet are captured and thrown alive into a lake of fire; the rest are killed by the sword from His mouth, and the birds are filled with their flesh.

"We praise God for His mercies, we pray to avert His judgments." This is the idea that even religious people commonly accept. But it clashes oddly with the language of this book. The shouts of praise in heaven, echoed by saints on earth, are for God's judgments. We have seen this before; and nowhere is the lesson taught more forcefully than at the opening of this chapter.

I. "And after these things I heard a great voice of much people in heaven, saying, Alleluia; Salvation, and glory, and honour, and power, unto the Lord our God: for true and righteous are his judgments: for he hath judged the great whore, which did corrupt the earth with her fornication, and hath avenged the blood of his servants at her hand. And again they said, Alleluia. And her smoke rose up for ever and ever. And the four and twenty elders and the four beasts fell down and worshipped God that sat on the throne, saying, Amen; Alleluia. And a voice came out of the throne, saying, Praise our God, all ye his servants, and ye that fear him, both small and great."

Are the thoughts of heaven and earth, then, completely at odds on this subject? Do the twenty-four elders and the four living creatures around the throne rejoice in the very thing that makes mortals weep? Do they give thanks that the prayers of mortals have gone unanswered—that God has carried out His vengeance despite men's efforts to stop it?

Certain phrases in this passage should make us pause before accepting that sad conclusion. One is in the first verse: *salvation* is ascribed to the Lord our God. Now salvation, in some sense, is exactly what all people everywhere are praying for. If we ask not to suffer a plague of endless rain that would ruin the harvest, we are asking for salvation from that plague; if we cry out against battle, murder, sudden death, conspiracy, or rebellion, we ask salvation from those dangers; if we pray against pride, malice, hypocrisy, uncharitableness, against fornication, heresy, schism, and contempt of God's word, we ask salvation from those evils. It does not look, then, as though the heavenly hosts are celebrating the defeat of prayers rising from earth. If they praise God for His judgments, they link those judgments with deliverance.

The second verse explains the first. "He hath judged the great whore, which did corrupt the earth with her fornication, and hath avenged the blood of his servants at her hand." The salvation is from something that has defiled the earth, made it miserable, and filled it with blood. With full delight they watch the smoke of the fire that has consumed the earth's tormentors and destroyers rising for ever and ever. Not for one age but for every age has that fire been kindled; the smoke rising from each age is a pledge and promise for the next. That is why the fifth verse breaks down the barrier between those in the visible world and those in the invisible one. All who fear God, small and great, are asked to join in watching the judgment—all are called to praise for it.

Yet although the Apostle does not present heaven and earth as at odds, but as truly at one, we should not forget that a conflict must exist somewhere. And since all our thoughts, wishes, and prayers are bound to be shaped by it, let us not shrink from pressing the question: Where is it? How does it come about that we speak of praying against what the wisest and holiest seem to pray for? How is it that we dread the very result they long for—which, when they see it, makes them exult?

Suppose I believe I am in a system I am to call generally or universally good, though I am aware that much in it I hate—things that torment me, crush me, devour the life in me. Then of course I do not pray, and cannot. The author of that system—if it has one—has, I suppose, made it as he saw fit. I have nothing to do but endure it as best I can; and my endurance will be mixed with curses that break out of my inmost soul, since its misery has no other outlet. Those curses will be aimed at the system, if I regard it merely as a system; or at whoever set it going, if I think there is such a one. And no arguments proving the folly or hopelessness of rebelling against a state of things to which I am chained—no statistics showing that things follow a certain course, or that after ages everything works out for the good of the whole—will produce anything more than a pretence of contentment in me, so long as I keep the feelings and needs of a human being. The only escape is to drown those feelings and needs. In animal indulgence, in the dullness of a merely sensual life, a man may learn to submit; otherwise he will beat his head against the bars of his prison, if he dreams even a little of escaping it.

How do men rise out of the mire into which they keep sinking deeper? All would say: not by acquiescing, but by *protesting*. They insist that mire is not the element human beings were meant to live in. Though a hundred are willing to stay in it, one or two keep affirming, for themselves and the rest, that this is not their proper state; and if they can see no other, they can at least see there must be another. This protest and affirmation become prayer. They believe that somehow, somewhere, they have a deliverer. If someone has cast them into this misery, someone else must be able to save them out of it. The longing to escape has been put into their minds; the effort to escape has been put into their minds. Whatever the Deliverer's name, and in whatever region near or far He may live, these promptings have come from Him, and He will back them up. They ask Him to save them from the oppressor—they ask Him, if He is strong enough, to judge between the oppressor and them. Whether the Author of the universe is the Oppressor or the Deliverer, the Enemy men must escape or the Friend on whom they can lay all their doubts, fears, and hopes—this is what all past ages were struggling to know. What we say is that the revelation of Jesus Christ was the answer to that question. We who receive Him as the Son of God, one with the Lord of all, do declare that the Creator of the universe is the Deliverer of the universe. Whatever corrupts the earth and human beings we believe comes not from His order but from a breaking of His order. By our loyalty to Him we are bound not to accept abnormalities as if they were laws, but to fight against them. We are bound to believe He is stirring us to fight against them. We have a right to pray against them, whatever they are, our prayers being simply our response to His promptings—cries for His will to triumph over what opposes it. And if we believe He is infinitely wise as well as infinitely good—that we can see only a very little way—then we shall leave our petitions in His hands with no cowardice or distrust. We believe He has ordained summer and winter, seed-time and harvest. Continued rain at this season, experience tells us, is likely to prevent the fruits from appearing in season and to make many starve; that is, we believe it will interrupt or disturb God's blessed order. Does the rain, then, not come from Him? We believe it does. Rain is a gift of God, not a curse of the Devil. So we believe it is sent for some good purpose—we do not know the specific purpose, but for the general purpose of correcting something wrong in how we act or think, of making us less stupid and monotonous in our lives, less dependent on chance, better farmers, less deaf to nature's facts and demands—all this we may safely assume, provided we assume a Fatherly Ruler of the world at all. To ask for the removal of the punishment is therefore, as today's prayer teaches us, to ask for the removal of the cause, whatever it is; to ask for repentance; to ask to be set right. So from whatever starting point, we come back to the conclusion that the source of evil lies in the will; that our wills have set themselves against God's will; that He is working to bring us into our true state; and that all natural instruments are His instruments for this end. Therefore, when people tell us we keep forgetting the permanence of God's nature and character and want to bend the laws to our convenience, we admit the charge is just, and are deeply grateful for any help in making it untrue. So we pray to God at such times as this, appealing to Him as being what He was in ancient generations, in the days of Noah's flood and before—appealing to Him as the God of law and order—appealing to Him against our own ups and downs, irregularities, and worship of accidents; certain that if we do not appeal to Him, if we do not believe in such a God, we *shall* become the plaything and slave of all shifting appearances, unable to recognize anything firm or constant in the visible or the unseen world.

So it is God's judgment we call for in every case where we act in line with what the Bible or the Church teaches. They never teach us to shrink from those judgments. Those texts that seem to justify such shrinking, weighed carefully, point the opposite way; indeed they expose the very frame of mind in us that leads to this distortion and cowardice. Do you think the Psalmist who said, "Judge me, O Lord, and plead my cause," contradicted himself when he said, "Enter not into judgment with thy servant, for in thy sight shall no man living be justified"? I am convinced that one of these prayers explains and confirms the other. He means: "Do not, O Lord, look on me, think of me, judge me as I am *apart from Thee*—a poor, wretched, helpless atom, cut off from all other atoms, having lost the principle of life and unity. Judge me as I truly am, related to Thee, taking refuge in Thee, begging Thee to search whether there is any wicked way in me, to test me and find the ground of my heart, which is in Thyself!" Do you think St. Paul, saying, "Judge therefore yourselves, brethren, that ye be not judged by the Lord," contradicted his other statement in the same letter—"I know nothing against myself, yet am I not hereby justified; for he that judgeth me is the Lord"? I think you will find that the judgment of God he wanted them not to incur was being treated as though they were part and parcel of the evil clinging to them; that he wanted them to judge between that evil and themselves; and that he told them to look boldly up to God, in His Son, to judge them and clear them of that evil. And our failure to do this, brethren—our not believing that God has sent His Son to justify us and free every one of us from our sins, our not believing that we stand in Christ, pure and holy and blameless before Him—is the reason we do not feel about His judgments on the world as the Apostle did, but instead cherish the opposite feeling: a wish that they should not come, or should not reach us. This last is, I think, the worse temper of the two. It is nobler and more righteous to wish that the world should be left in its wickedness and misery—horrible as that wish is—than to wish it to pass through the fires while we get a special exemption. What! has our saintliness come to this? Do we really want to hug our evils and not let them be destroyed? Yet that is what our saintliness will be, if our first, second, or third thought is about ourselves—if our first thought is not the one expressed in this verse:

II. "And I heard as it were the voice of a great multitude, and as the voice of many waters, and as of the voice of mighty thunderings, saying, Alleluia: for the Lord God Omnipotent reigneth." And the second thought is like it: "Let us be glad and rejoice, and give honour to him, for the marriage of the Lamb is come, and his wife hath made herself ready." And the third grows out of the first and second: "And to her was granted that she should be arrayed in fine linen, clean and white: for the fine linen is the righteousness of the saints." Here is the true Divine order. *Hallowed be thy Name*: the Lord God Omnipotent reigns! The eternally righteous Being holds all power. He in whom is light and no darkness at all is shown to be the ground of all things. *Thy kingdom come*: the Son has claimed humanity as His bride. She is no longer the servant of the beast; she has found her true Lord and can see her glory in Him. *Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven*: the Spirit clothes the bride with the graces and beauties of her Lord. Those who have nothing in themselves are able to look up to Him and become bright with His brightness.

III. "And he saith unto me, Write, Blessed are they which are called to the marriage supper of the Lamb. And he saith unto me, These are the true sayings of God. And I fell at his feet to worship him. And he said unto me, See thou do it not, I am thy fellow-servant, and of thy brethren that have the testimony of Jesus. Worship God; for the testimony of Jesus is the spirit of prophecy."

We have already heard of the voice of a great multitude. Now *he* speaks to me. Who? The prophet knows no more than we do. He is sure only that the message is a Divine one. And he is sure it is distinct from that general chorus—it is addressed to him. It must be Divine, for it tells him of what has been the subject of all songs and prophecies since the world began: the union of Humanity with her true Husband—the overthrow of the idolatry that had separated them and degraded her. To this all the creation's hopes, to this all God's revelations, have pointed. And now some are called to celebrate this marriage. They are invited to the wedding supper. The voice pronounces them blessed. To share in such a celebration is to enter fellowship with all blessed and redeemed spirits—with everything gracious, pure, and noble in earth's generations and in the worlds that have never known sin. May not humanity so exalted be an object of worship? Does not the one who brings these tidings represent it? Has not hero-worship now found its justification? Has not the Son of Man destroyed the sin into which earlier ages fell? No—*see thou do it not!* The wedding of Humanity vindicates, rather than reverses, the second commandment as well as the first. Each person is raised by that wedding. Each person content to be a fellow-servant with his brothers, to take his place among those whose nature Jesus bore and whose death Jesus died, shares His glory. But that glory is to look above humanity, as He did. The spirit of prophecy has all along been a testimony to Him—a testimony to a Man who is one with God, who fully asserts God's name, in whom men may draw near to God.

And now He Himself appears—that Son of Man who stood before the Prophet dressed in the robes of the High Priest, walking among the candlesticks; essentially the same, but bearing a different name, carrying out another office.

IV. "And I saw heaven open, and behold a white horse; and he that sat upon him was Faithful and True, and in righteousness he doth judge and make war. His eyes were as a flame of fire, and on his head were many crowns; and he had a name written, that no man knew, but he himself. And he was clothed with a vesture dipped in blood: and his name is called the Word of God. And the armies which were in heaven followed him upon white horses, clothed in fine linen, white and clean. And out of his mouth goeth a sharp sword, that with it he should smite the nations: and he shall rule them with a rod of iron: and he treadeth the winepress of the fierceness of the wrath of Almighty God. And he hath on his vesture and on his thigh a name written, KING OF KINGS, AND LORD OF LORDS."

"In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God." So St. John opens his Gospel. Those who comment on this sentence tell us how what they call the Logos doctrine was spread by a Jew of Alexandria; how it was taken up by the Christian fathers there in the second century; how they, like its first teacher, connected it with ideas drawn from pagan philosophy. Then they ask whether such a deep speculation is likely to have reached a fisherman of Galilee—whether it is not far more probable that what we call his Gospel was a compilation by learned men in a later time. All the learning that can be applied to the history of human thought is valuable, and no speculations about it need cause the slightest anxiety. But, as I have pressed on you before, it may be well worth knowing whether the discovery of the doctrine of gravitation can be traced to Newton, Kepler, or some earlier thinker; but it is rather more important to know whether the doctrine of gravitation is true or false—whether it explains the facts of the universe—rather than merely whether it is contained in a book called *The Principia*. We must apply the same test to the statements at the start of St. John's Gospel. They themselves invite that test. Learned scholar, you know everything Philo or Origen said about the Word of God; but if He has nothing to do with *you*—if you can interpret yourself without Him—you may as well leave Philo, Origen, and the wicked man who forged St. John's name entirely alone. Their doctrine can mean nothing to you, because in that case it is mere fiction. The Gospel assumes that all the light in you, or in any person, comes from His light; that neither you, nor Philo, nor Origen, could have used or understood any words but for this Word; that your darkness, and mine, and everyone's, comes from refusing to acknowledge this light and dwell in it. If this is so, then it seems in no way strange that all prophets should speak of a Word of God coming to them, or that heathens should confess they needed such an enlightener of their hearts and reasons. Nor should it surprise us, at least, that a Jew of the first century, meditating on what he believed had been revealed to the prophets of his land—and on the aspirations and perceptions of men outside their covenant—should have reached a clearer, more definite grasp than his predecessors of some Being related to the Mind of God and to the mind of man, in whom God and man might meet. The question of all questions is whether this Being is really the Enlightener of *man*, or only of wise and select *men*; whether He was made flesh and lived among men to give them power to become sons of God, or whether He was only a spiritual emanation or idea men might dream about, but who could not speak to them, rule them, or judge them. Supposing He really was what St. John says He was, then, I say, the poor Galilean fisherman was exactly the person to whom—for mankind's sake—we should expect Him to reveal Himself, so that He might be shown to be genuinely the Enlightener not of those who bring their own light with them, but of those who confess that, left to themselves, they are blind.

And now this Word of God, declared to be the light of men—the source of good to all and to each—comes out *judging and making war*. Who else can judge but He who has been conversing with every heart, who knows all its passages, who has been carrying on His secret judgments, bringing every man to His bar every day since man began to sin? Who can make war but He who has been striving with each man, each family, each nation—He against whom every idolatrous, malicious, foul thought, desire, word, and deed that has corrupted societies or individuals has fought? We know how dark and bloody men's wars have been; history tells us. What we want to know is whether all has been bloody and dark—whether it is all to end in darkness and blood. Heaven opens, and One is seen on a white horse, who is *Faithful and True*, who judges and makes war in *righteousness*. He sees into the heart of things. Nothing good, nothing of His own creation, can escape those flaming eyes or be lost; nothing evil and corrupting can hide from them and be saved. On His head are many crowns; we cannot measure the extent or variety of His victories. We only know that all His enemies are to be put under His feet. It is not we who gave Him His name; no one knows it but Himself. He knows all our names. Thanks be to God, we cannot fathom the depth of His! His robe is dipped in blood—not only the blood of others, but His own. He has poured out His own blood for men. He will not spare any blood needed for the redemption of the earth, for the destruction of its destroyers. He does not come alone; the armies of heaven are with Him. The Son of God is the Firstborn among many brothers. All the sons of God have received His Spirit and are allowed to share in His work. And that work must be a work of justice and vengeance. A sharp sword goes out of His mouth. The nations have felt the rod of wickedness; they must feel the rod of righteousness. The oppressor must learn there is a power more terrible than his. He has used God's name to strengthen his purposes of fierceness and wrath; he has thought the earth brought forth its fruits only to make him drunk; now he must learn what the winepress of the fierceness of the wrath of Almighty God is.

V. All through this book I have been tracing the record of a mighty catastrophe that was to end that age of the world. The thought of that catastrophe would be utterly unbearable if we could not call it a judgment, if we were not told who the Judge is. It is when I have heard that the Son of Man, the Lamb who was slain, is King of kings and Lord of lords, that I can bear to read the next verses. "And I saw an angel standing in the sun; and he cried with a loud voice, saying to all the fowls that fly in the midst of heaven, Come and gather yourselves together unto the supper of the great God; that ye may eat the flesh of kings, and the flesh of captains, and the flesh of mighty men, and the flesh of all men, both free and bond, both small and great. And I saw the beast, and the kings of the earth, and their armies, gathered together to make war against him that sat on the horse, and against his army. And the beast was taken, and with him the false prophet that wrought miracles before him, with which he deceived them that had received the mark of the beast, and them that worshipped his image. These both were cast alive into a lake of fire burning with brimstone. And the remnant were slain with the sword of him that sat upon the horse, which sword proceeded out of his mouth: and all the fowls were filled with their flesh."

There is no attempt in this passage, or anywhere in Scripture, to disguise the horror and loathsomeness of a battle. We should never shrink from steadily looking at facts, or cover them with pretty sentimental phrases. Such phrases will never change the nature of things. To endure the visible, we must learn to look into the invisible. To bear the darkness over the earth, the veil of the temple must be torn open. We can bear to hear of the flesh of men and animals given to the birds of the air, if the result is that the beast and the false prophet—the military tyranny grinding the nations, the spiritual tyranny preying on their inner life—are cast into the lake of fire. And this the prophet understands as the meaning of the agony and travail through which the world of his day was passing. It was the judgment on the prince of this world; it interpreted all the judgments before it. Every one had been a battle between these same powers, and every one held the pledge of this outcome. Until the Word of God had been fully revealed in the incarnation of the Son of Man, in His Passion, in His return to glory, a heavy cloud still hung over all the doings and destinies of God's creatures. The years since Adam came forth seemed only years of mutual destruction for his race, just as the ages before seemed periods of mutual destruction for the lower creatures. Now, when the Head of the universe had gone through its conflict and misery, a light was thrown back over all the past. His life was seen to be stronger than all the powers of death that had been at work in God's universe.

And so, again, the battle of that age throws light forward into all the battles of later generations. It is hard and sickening to read of Christian men, sons of God, giving their brothers as food to the birds of the air; it is dreadful to think for what ends the kings of the earth and the great captains have slaughtered one another. But do not doubt it: in every one of these fights, some beastly power, some lie that has deceived the world, has been thrown down and cast into the lake of fire. Amid all our reasons for mourning, the spirits of heaven, and those of our brothers who have fought the fight and joined them, have seen reasons for thanksgiving and triumph. Something has been done for the purifying of the earth, for the fuller revealing of the sons of God; new conquerors come from earth's anguish to share in its righteous government; new guests come from the horrible supper of death to sit at the marriage supper of the Lamb; one more step is gained in the triumph of the Perfect Will over rebellious wills; one step more toward that restoration of all things which God, who cannot lie, has promised through the mouth of all His holy prophets since the world began.

## LECTURE XX. THE MILLENNIUM.

*The passage:* John saw an angel come down from heaven holding the key of the bottomless pit and a great chain. The angel seized the dragon—the old serpent, the Devil, Satan—bound him for a thousand years, threw him into the pit, shut it, and sealed it, so that he could no longer deceive the nations until the thousand years were finished; after that he must be let loose for a short time. John also saw thrones, with judges seated on them, and he saw the souls of those beheaded for witnessing to Jesus and to God's Word, who had refused to worship the beast or its image or take its mark; they lived and reigned with Christ a thousand years. The rest of the dead did not live again until the thousand years were over. This is the first resurrection; those who share in it are blessed and holy, untouched by the second death, priests of God and Christ, reigning with him a thousand years. When the thousand years end, Satan is let loose to deceive the nations everywhere—Gog and Magog—and gather them for battle, as many as the sand of the sea. They surround the camp of the saints and the beloved city, but fire from God devours them, and the devil is thrown into the lake of fire and brimstone with the beast and false prophet, to be tormented forever. Then John saw a great white throne and one seated on it, from whose face earth and heaven fled away, leaving no place for them. He saw the dead, great and small, standing before God; the books were opened, including the book of life, and the dead were judged by their works. The sea, death, and hell gave up their dead, all judged by their works; then death and hell were thrown into the lake of fire. This is the second death. Anyone not found written in the book of life was thrown into the lake of fire.

The word "Millennium" brings up unpleasant memories for many educated people. Some think of the Fifth Monarchy men from our civil wars. Others recall speculations they have heard in their own time—speculations that seemed a strange blend of history and fiction, supported by arguments both clever and incoherent. Such people approach this chapter, where the doctrine of a Millennium is stated, with boredom or irritation. They will be inclined to say: "Surely these few words that have prompted such dreams can be passed over as the offhand remarks of a book that is itself rather unintelligible. We are practical men; we want the Bible for practical lessons. If some people have nothing to do, let them busy themselves with these things."

By all means let us remember that we are practical men and that the Bible is a practical book. But I cannot think this way of dismissing passages that, for good or ill, have moved and still move the most earnest men and women is practical at all. Contempt is never practical; it is the child of laziness, and it ends up causing much needless trouble. Questions denounced as beneath our notice prove they have a power we must attend to. Opinions we have despised in others rebound and strike back at us, and probably overthrow us precisely because we ignored them. I am convinced this belief in a Millennium has been a practical belief for many devout thinkers. If it is, as claimed, wrapped in fancies and held on weak grounds, that is a reason to ask where it gets a strength it clearly cannot owe to anything essentially feeble. Suppose there had never been such a tradition in Christendom, no such thought hovering in men's minds age after age—we would all have been the losers. There would not have been less fanaticism; that would simply have taken other forms. But there would have been far less hope, far less confidence in God's promises, and more of a dreary suspicion that the earth is handed over to those who only want to rob and spoil it. All art, all poetry, all politics would have been lower and more grovelling if this idea had not haunted those to whom it came in no formal, doctrinal shape—people who were often least inclined to welcome it.

But as I said about the word "Babylon" in the last chapter, this word points not to one set of opinions but to opposite sets. The objectors to the doctrine are not found only, or mainly, among those who scorn it. Many shrink from it because they hold positive convictions they think they must give up before they can accept it. They say: "We have been taught to look for a heavenly state; you want us to fix our hopes on an earthly paradise. We have been looking forward to a reward for those who serve God faithfully here, once they cross the boundary of death; you point us to a blessed condition that may come to this world in general. Even supposing we could care about that general, indiscriminate happiness here, does any Christian know he will live to reach the appointed moment?"

These are serious doubts—so serious in some minds that they simply ignore or dismiss the part of Scripture that gave rise to them. I cannot regard that as a safe course. It seems to me that the hope of good for this earth is essentially bound up in all God's promises; that we must suppress the plainest statements of Scripture, and kill the best wishes in our own hearts, if we refuse to cherish it. I cannot think that any true belief in a Kingdom of Heaven, or in the gifts God will give after death to those who love Him, would interfere in the least with the other hope. If they have seemed to clash, it must be through some confusion in our minds. And a faithful study of the actual words of the Divine book must be the way to remove the confusion and bring the two into harmony.

The traditions of the early Church show St. John in direct conflict with Cerinthus, who championed a notion of Christ's reign on earth that identified it with a golden age of the god Saturn—a return of an Eden of outward happiness. Though there is no historical foundation for the stories that make John and Cerinthus personal enemies, those stories express an instinct that their doctrines were incompatible, each the undoing of the other.
