That instinct is supported by every part of the Apocalypse. Its subject is certainly the deliverance of the earth—but the deliverance of the earth from the sensual masters who had degraded it, by its true spiritual Lord. That idea of a kingdom of Heaven, unfolding through every part of the New Testament, now reaches its completion. The Lamb fully vindicates His right to rule, against the Dragon, over all God's willing creatures. The unwilling creatures are restored to their proper place when man is restored to his. Remember that this has been the Apocalypse's consistent teaching so far, and now consider the words that open this chapter.

I. "And I saw an angel coming down from heaven, having the key of the bottomless pit, and a great chain in his hand. And he laid hold on the dragon, that old serpent, which is the Devil, and Satan, and bound him for a thousand years, and cast him into the bottomless pit, to shut him up, that he should deceive the nations no more, till the thousand years should be fulfilled: after that he must be loosed a little season."

I want you to notice that in this passage, at least, there is no reference at all to any special earthly happiness the earth or man is to enjoy. Nothing is said about exemption from pain and sorrow for individuals or nations. We have been hearing of a spiritual power who resisted the Lamb, was overcome by Him, and cast out of heaven. We have heard how that power could wage an unusually terrible, though short, war against the inhabitants of the earth. We have heard of armies rising from the bottomless pit to help him—not by destroying trees or grass, but by tormenting the minds and hearts of men. We have heard of a beast to whom the Dragon gave his authority, and of a false prophet who worked wonders in his name. These temporary incarnations of the Evil Spirit have been thrown down. Now it is announced that the Evil Spirit himself will be restrained from exercising the power he has used against human beings. A chain will be put on him. And when we ask what kind of chain, the answer comes at once: he will not be able to *deceive* the nations as he has done. The mischief he has done is moral. He has persuaded men to believe that the Lamb is not King of kings and Lord of lords; that the visible is mightier than the invisible; that he himself is stronger than Christ. The nations will now confess—those who most strongly assented to his lie—that this is not so; that the invisible is mighty over the visible; that Christ is King, not Satan. It is not said his power has ended. He may be, and will be, set loose again. But there will be a stretch of years during which Christ's reign over this planet of ours will not only be proclaimed by a few here and there who stand apart from the world's affairs, but will make itself felt in the world's order, in its most ordinary daily dealings.

One objection to this doctrine, then, is removed by presenting the whole of it instead of just one side—in the Apostle's words rather than our own. You think men are already far too prone to imagine a state of lazy happiness, in which all the fruits of the earth fall at our feet without the labor of heads and hands, in which there is no need of justice, government, or self-sacrifice because everything comes to everyone spontaneously. You think this dream, whether called a dream of heaven or of earth, has weakened thought, courage, manhood, and all the virtues we most prize. The Apostle agrees with you. He declares that the Lamb who sacrificed Himself for human beings displayed the truest, highest, most heavenly state. That spirit who has deceived men with the idea of some good different from and opposed to this—who has held up a vision of selfish enjoyment—the Apostle regards as the great enemy and destroyer of the race. And the thousand years he describes are precisely years in which the earth gains new life, strength, and capacity, just because men are not so much a prey to that delusion as they once were; because a wholly different standard is set before them; because they confess themselves to be under a different law.

But can we accept this idea of a great moral change if we suppose it is caused by a sudden descent of Christ to the earth? I believe, my brethren, that this whole book has been telling us *how* the Apostle connected a moral change in the world with a descent of Christ to the earth. The true Lord of the world showed Himself to the world in poverty and humiliation, in acts of mercy and power, in dying the death of the cross. That was His descent to the earth. And He who descended also ascended, that He might fill all things. Death could not hold Him. He was proclaimed to Jews and heathens as the Conqueror of death, the Son of God, and the Giver of the Divine Spirit to men. Whether He was so or not was to be settled in battle. This book records that battle. Jerusalem, the capital of one half of the old world, and Rome, the capital of the other half, were the fields where it was fought. Other explanations may be given of the outcome. I accept this one: that mortal power was shown not to be supreme over the earth and man; that the power opposed to brute power was vindicated. Christ's descent to the earth, for which all ages had been preparing, was the beginning of this triumph; if He is the Son of God, all that followed was the necessary result of His appearing. The thrones of the earth were shaken to their center so that it might be known who held the highest throne, to whom all the kings of the world owed homage. This manifestation, or appearing, of Christ in the glory of His Father and of the angels was what the Apostles looked for; and this the last of the Apostles was permitted to see through the clouds and darkness that covered the world in his day. But if you ask, not for a manifestation of Christ's royal glory, but for another descent like the one into the manger, I can find no trace of it anywhere—certainly not in this passage about the Millennium, where people have most suspected it. For read the next words.

II. "And I saw thrones, and they sat upon them, and judgment was given unto them: and I saw the souls of them that were beheaded for the witness of Jesus, and for the Word of God, and which had not worshipped the beast, neither his image, neither had received his mark upon their foreheads, or in their hands; and they lived and reigned with Christ a thousand years. But the rest of the dead lived not again until the thousand years were finished. This is the first resurrection. Blessed and holy is he that hath part in the first resurrection: on such the second death hath no power, but they shall be priests of God and of Christ, and shall reign with him a thousand years." Surely, taking these words as they stand, they do not describe a descent of Christ to earth, but an ascent of those who had been beheaded for witnessing to Christ, so they might reign with Him. I do not believe that those who cling with the deepest and truest faith to the idea of a millennium—those to whom it is a strength on their pilgrimage through the world and a comfort under the weight of its evil—would be shaken for more than a moment by noticing this difference, startling as it looks. I am satisfied that what they really mean by a reign of Christ on earth is exactly what the Apostle means, and that if they could give a clear account of their own thoughts and hopes, they would find his words far more satisfying than their own. At first it might distress them greatly to suggest that *they* had put off to a distant future a millennium that *he* believed the world was about to enter in his own day. And if such a reading of his words meant losing any real hope they have held about the future of the earth or of mankind, I would be very slow to offer it, because I would suspect it was false. I am sure the expectations the Bible holds out to us are brighter, not dimmer, than our own; that they include all anyone has imagined; and that the business of every teacher is to strengthen and deepen them, not weaken them. But it seems to me that this passage not only becomes more consistent with the rest of this book and of the New Testament if we understand it of the ages during which the Gospel was establishing itself in the various parts of the Roman Empire, but that by understanding it so, the difficulty that has perplexed so many minds—about the connection between each man's future state after death and the future state of the world at large—is removed, and a brilliant light is thrown on both.

That a vision of the souls of those *beheaded for the witness of Christ* must refer to those who have died, not those who escape death, no one can dispute. What, then, does it seem to tell us? That these witnesses of Christ—who cared so much for the earth when they lived on it, who labored to do it good, and apparently labored in vain, who told it of its true King and of its revolt to a usurper—should, when they were no longer seen, exert an influence over it that had been denied them before, working as the effective servants of Him who gave His life for the world's redemption. This is their high reward, exactly the reward our Lord held out to them in His parable. The one who used the pound well and made it five pounds was to rule over five cities; the one who used the two well and made another two was to rule over two cities. No idleness, no luxurious indulgence was offered them; they would enter into the joy of their Lord; they would have the delight of knowing more and more of His purposes and working more and more in line with them. They would be freed from the miserable vanity and selfishness that had hampered and disappointed their efforts here; they would understand their own fields of labor and cooperate with all who were assigned other fields; they would live as He lives and work as He works, for the good of His subjects; they would judge, as He judges, between right and wrong, between pure and impure.

And here is the distinction between these true servants of God and those who served not Him but His enemy: "The rest of the dead lived not again till the thousand years were finished." No such reward was prepared for them. They had chosen the wages of sin and selfishness, and those wages were death. That is what we are told. I accept these words and those that follow just as they are written. They may conflict with some of our notions about the future. I cannot help that. It is a subject on which our understandings make all sorts of crude, ignorant guesses. If we want a revelation to scatter our darkness, let us be thankful one is given.

"This," we are told, "is the first resurrection. Blessed and holy are they that have part in it." Who, then, have part in it? We have heard the definition of them, and it is surely one that does not *exclude* the first ages of the Christian Church. Our first and most natural thought might be that it refers especially to the times when men were *beheaded for the testimony of Jesus*. So suppose we consider what actually happened on the earth in the centuries after the one in which the Apostles lived. We usually say that in those centuries the Gospel made its way into countries where Greek, Syrian, Egyptian, Celtic, or Gothic idolatry had prevailed. But what do we mean by this phrase? Do we mean that one set of opinions replaced other sets of opinions? If that is all we mean, unbelievers ask, and we are forced to ask ourselves, was the world any better for the change? Was there not as much evil done in the name of Christianity as had been done in the names of the false gods? But when we have worn ourselves out with this thought, there rises before us the clear certainty of moral good and physical changes brought about in the earth—brought about directly by Christian agency. We see—no startling facts on the other side, and no false show of fairness, can keep us from seeing—that we are entering a new stage in the history of our planet. The whole religious scheme that had been bound up with the politics, habits, and amusements of the various nations passes away. Principles once contradicted everywhere in the world, still at odds with its practice, gradually force themselves on men's acceptance as truths. Much as they are disregarded, they more and more prove themselves to be laws that must govern somewhere and ought to govern everywhere. That idea of self-sacrifice, which runs counter to every inclination of every human mind, enthrones itself in human minds as the effective principle—the one thing that can accomplish what nothing else can. In some marvellous way the cross wins recognition from emperors and kings who look as though they must think it the most contemptible of all signs. How do they come to feel its power? By trying their swords against it, by testing whether swords and stakes can put out the confession of it. Those ages therefore exactly match the first part of the description in this chapter. No royal decrees, no acts of priests, will explain the change taking place. It is a change at the very heart of society. The demon is forced to release his grip on minds and spirits that had once recognized him. The new life of the thousand years affects government, education, manners, the cultivation of the soil. But it advances silently, mysteriously, in defiance of appearances. You must study it over the span of centuries to see how complete it is. You must see how the toppling of one established corruption after another shows the permanence of the spiritual rule under which the earth has been brought.

And what is the character of that spiritual rule? We sometimes say nothing is more deceptive than the dream of fame after death. The claim is well founded, for every selfish anticipation is full of delusion. But is there anything more real, more undoubted, than *influence* after death? How it was felt in those ages we all know. The benefactor of a neighborhood was regarded as the saint who, after his death, went on blessing that neighborhood. The tilling of the land is dear to him; he gives healing power to the springs. But were not those ideas full of superstition? Do I think the Bible endorses such superstitions merely because they may have had some good effects? I believe men's thoughts about these saints and this government were full of superstition. I believe the Bible endorses no superstition. I believe no superstition has good effects, only evil ones. And for that reason I do not confuse the thoughts with the reality they pointed to and testified about. This is the mistake of the philosophers who talk grandly about certain ideas that prevailed in certain periods. If by "ideas" they mean men's notions and conceptions, they must canonize very mischievous and dishonorable fancies under that name. But if by "ideas" they mean laws, principles, truths that are not of today or yesterday, then they will admit with me that men's doctrines about departed saints may have pointed to a real rule those saints exercised, of which men were aware in many ways, though they could not reduce it to order and often made it an excuse for shameful idolatry. For, let me say it again and again, this power, if it is of the same nature and quality as the power from which it comes, cannot act on men as if they were stones, or trees, or even mere animals. It must speak to their understandings, affections, and consciences; it must appeal to them as free, responsible creatures. May it not, then, be a great help to us in studying the facts of the world's history—including men's apprehensions about themselves and the powers surrounding them—if we simply adopt the Apostle's statement; if we suppose this dominion of the departed over the earth's condition and destiny is joined, by a Divine and providential link, to the dominion of Him who was the firstborn from the dead, the head of many brethren?

Blessed and holy indeed, if this is so, is the first resurrection and those who share in it. They have passed through one death in the dear power of Him whom that death could not hold. The second death, the death that overtakes the self-seeker, has no power over them, as it had none over Him. His Spirit bore witness with their spirits that they were sons of God, and therefore that their lives, in one world or another, were to be spent in the service of men. And have they not been joined by all whom the same Spirit has taught the same lesson? Do we not know what their occupations must be? Truly the occupations of heavenly creatures, because devoted to helping and comforting those who are walking, and often losing their way, on earth. A light falls on a page of a book—someone seems to be showing you its true meaning. Why not the one who wrote it?—who perhaps understood his own words only imperfectly when he set them down, but who has learned their meaning since. A room brings back the memory of faces once seen there, of voices once heard—why may not those faces be looking at us, those voices giving us reproofs and consolations? If we thought so, we would care little for false messages from the dead; the real ones would be infinitely precious and awful. If we thought so, we would truly feel that the reign of the saints is not yet over—though perhaps a higher reign than theirs has been revealed to us.

For we are told of an end to the thousand years.

III. "And when the thousand years are expired, Satan shall be loosed out of his prison, and shall go out to deceive the nations which are in the four quarters of the earth, Gog and Magog, to gather them together to battle: the number of whom is as the sand of the sea. And they went up on the breadth of the earth, and compassed the camp of the saints about, and the beloved city: and fire came down from God out of heaven, and devoured them. And the devil that deceived them was cast into the lake of fire and brimstone, where the beast and the false prophet are, and shall be tormented day and night for ever and ever."

To those who see the Millennium as the great climax of all human history, this prospect of a dark ending to it—of an overthrow of all its rare happiness—must be very frightening. They naturally shrink from the vision of Satan let loose on the world again, of wild tribes gathered from the ends of the earth to destroy a city where saints had set up their throne. But to those who see this Millennium as the effect of Christ's Death, Resurrection, and Ascension, of the gift of the Spirit, of His victory over Jewish and Heathen enemies, over the rebels of Jerusalem and the tyrants of Rome—to those who regard the Millennium as a real reign, over the earth He had redeemed, of those who believed in Christ, fought on His side, and died in His cause; to those who see it as a state in which this government was making itself felt, but felt by people who kept throwing off its yoke, misunderstanding its nature, and using their acknowledgment of it as an excuse for superstition and crimes; to those who take it as the true explanation of that life-in-death of Christendom which we read of in the records of the modern world; to those who therefore regard it as something immeasurably better than the condition of the old world, and more than any state of mere repose and enjoyment could be, yet immeasurably short of what God's redemption and His promises would lead them to hope for in the end—to these the announcement of a catastrophe that would shake this order, so blessed and so little acknowledged, is a help in interpreting the past and a warrant for confidence in the future. And when we consider the nature of the disaster said to fall on the camp of the saints and the beloved city, the prophet's intention again comes out into light and clearness beside the well-known facts of history. What do those wild invasions mean—the hordes of Huns and Avars, and the Tartar armies under Genghis Khan and Timur, who found a garden before them and left a wilderness behind? They called themselves scourges of God; they believed they had a Divine commission. How did that thought come into their minds? What was their role? They disrupted the civilization and order that had gradually been establishing itself in the world; they seemed to say there was no such thing as lawful government, no sacred yoke of customs and traditions. Sheer force of numbers, sheer strength of arm, apart from all that had turned multitudes and swords into servants of order, seemed once more to prove itself supreme. Yes—and why? So that nations which had sunk into a monotony of customs, laws, and traditions; which had forgotten how these had come to be and what gave them their stability and authority; which had corrupted civilization into mere indulgence, and turned belief in an invisible world into a mere tool for propping up the corruption and tyranny of the visible—so that these nations might be roused to remember what they were and how far they had fallen; that they might learn by bitter experience that selfishness is the author, not of government, but of Anarchy; that the forms, graces, and blessings of civil life are not to be sacrificed to the Devil, as though they came from him, but laid on God's altar, because they are His. Satan is let loose in the strictest sense, so that Satan may be overthrown. That dominion of the upper world over the lower is set at naught so that men may see how real it is, and what a lie society becomes when it ignores the relation between those two worlds and tries to make an earth that is self-sufficient.

That, I take it, has been men's great crime from the beginning. The Tower of Babel set the precedent for it; all the despotisms of the old world were its development; and it is what has made the Church of Christ, the beloved city of the later ages, fouler and more debased than the Jerusalem of earlier ages. That the attempt to put a visible hierarchy in place of an invisible one is denounced in the most terrible sentences of this book, I believe as strongly as those who would simply turn Babylon into papal Rome, or who would deny there has been any Church of Christ, any Communion of Saints, except in some small scattered communities, which they then have to endow with an ideal purity belonging to none of the churches we read of in the apostolic letters.

Take Babylon to represent an evil principle of society that appeared again and again in the old world, and has appeared again under more fearful and complicated conditions in the new; take the holy city to be indeed the Christendom of which we, and all the baptized nations, are members; take the millennial state to represent that new kingdom into which we have been brought—and then all the calamities that have befallen mankind, from the invasions of the wild Gog and Magog hordes on the outskirts of civilization, and from the mobs left untended in particular lands, hold the most fearful lesson and warning for us. And yet they also hold the blessed promise that the borders of the holy city will be widened to receive the very races that have threatened its existence, and that Satan, the common deceiver of all times and lands, will at last be cast into the lake of fire, where the beast and the false prophet—the tyrannical and idolatrous systems of the old world—have already been sent.

And so we are prepared for the vision that closes this chapter.

IV. "And I saw a great white throne, and him that sat on it, from whose face the earth and the heaven fled away; and there was found no place for them. And I saw the dead, small and great, stand before God; and the books were opened: and another book was opened, which is the book of life; and the dead were judged out of those things which were written in the books, according to their works. And the sea gave up the dead which were in it; and death and hell delivered up the dead which were in them: and they were judged every man according to their works. And death and hell were cast into the lake of fire. This is the second death. And whosoever was not found written in the book of life was cast into the lake of fire."

A great white throne, and He who sat on it, before whom *heaven and earth* fled away, and there was no place for them. Remember, it was *heaven* as well as *earth*. If you suppose these words speak of a destruction of the earth, you must suppose they speak of a destruction of the heavens too. If one meaning is impossible, do not contradict everything we have been reading and hearing in this book by imagining the other. Yet the vision is strictly true. In the vision of God Himself all things must disappear—the saints who govern as much as those who are governed. In that perfect light all alike seem lost; *no place is found for them*. And yet it is that very light which brings everything into view. Each one, small and great, stands out clearly and distinctly when He is revealed. Vague thoughts haunt us of great judgments on the world at large in which we individually are forgotten. Or we grow indifferent to the world, thinking only of ourselves and what will become of us. In this Divine light there is no such confusion. God loves the world—how can we separate ourselves from it? God has made each of us a conscious being—how can we lose that being in the vastness of any society? It is a judgment between life and death. Have you chosen life? Have you chosen death? That question reaches every man, whether buried in the sea or in the grave. It reaches, St. John says, into the very depths of hell. And life, he says, conquers. Death and hell are cast into the lake of fire.

Does that mean, my brethren, that by some change of circumstances, some arbitrary decree of God, evil stops bringing its curse and doom—that a time begins when God no longer judges? I can imagine nothing so frightful as that. Evil can never be less accursed until good becomes less blessed. To ask God not to judge is to ask for utter destruction to ourselves. God's victories are victories over the will of men. You and I can only be saved from curses and punishments by being saved from sin. God sent His Son to deliver us from that. If death and hell are cast into the lake of fire, it is because He has done His work; because He has seen the travail of His soul and been satisfied.

## LECTURE XXI. THE NEW HEAVEN AND THE NEW EARTH.

*The passage:* John saw a new heaven and a new earth, for the first ones had passed away and there was no more sea. He saw the holy city, new Jerusalem, coming down from God out of heaven like a bride prepared for her husband. A great voice announced that God's dwelling is now with men: He will live with them, they will be His people, and He will wipe away every tear; death, sorrow, crying, and pain will be gone. The One on the throne said He makes all things new, that these words are true and faithful, that it is done, and that He is the beginning and the end. He will freely give the thirsty the water of life; whoever overcomes will inherit all things and be His son. But the fearful, unbelieving, abominable, murderers, immoral, sorcerers, idolaters, and liars will have their portion in the lake of fire, which is the second death. Then one of the seven angels with the seven last plagues showed John the bride, the Lamb's wife: the holy city descending from God, radiant with God's glory, bright like a jasper stone clear as crystal, with a great high wall, twelve gates with twelve angels and the names of the twelve tribes of Israel written on them (three gates on each side), and twelve foundations bearing the names of the twelve apostles. The angel measured the city with a golden reed: it lay foursquare, its length, breadth, and height equal, twelve thousand furlongs, and its wall a hundred and forty-four cubits. The wall was jasper, the city pure gold like clear glass, the foundations set with twelve kinds of precious stones, the twelve gates each a single pearl, and the street pure transparent gold. There was no temple, for the Lord God Almighty and the Lamb are its temple; no need of sun or moon, for God's glory lights it and the Lamb is its light. The saved nations walk in its light, kings bring their glory into it, its gates are never shut because there is no night, and nothing unclean or false enters it—only those written in the Lamb's book of life.

Before the vision of the great white throne and the One who sat on it, heaven as well as earth had fled away. In that perfect light there seemed for a while to be no place for the spiritual world any more than the material one. But soon that glory, which had cast everything into shadow, brought each thing into full view. The dead, small and great, stood forth; the sea gave up her dead; the thoughts of every person's heart came into judgment. Each one knew what he was. Death and life stood out in dreadful contrast. And death and hell "were cast into the lake of fire; this is the second death. And whosoever was not found written in the book of life was cast into the lake of fire."

Those last words strike one as a very terrible ending to the vision. I believe they are very terrible; and yet, taken together with the words before them, they contain, it seems to me, an infinite comfort. Death and hell mean a separation from God, a denial and contradiction of God. The bottomless pit is Atheism—the condition of being without God. Here we are told that these are conquered by Him; they are cast into the lake of fire. That lake of fire is no longer regarded as outside His dominion, beyond the circle of His grace and love. It is His, to be used for His divine purposes. What those purposes are we have been hearing throughout this book—throughout the Bible, of which it is the climax. Our first thought is that whoever is not found written in the book of life—whoever has chosen death instead of life—must remain forever in that condition. When I reason about the nature of a spirit, arguing about its immortality, I can find no escape from that conclusion, though I feel it withers my energies as well as my hope. But God's revelation gives me what all my speculations cannot. I know that He can act upon spirits; I know that He can and does raise them out of death. I know that the process of rising out of death into the awareness of life is, and must be, agonizing. I know that when a prodigal is coming to himself, his conscience passes through a fire whose intensity none but God knows, though the man knows something of it. If I am told that whoever lacks life is not left in that utter wretchedness, but is cast into a lake of fire—if I am told that death, hell, and whatever divides us from God is cast into that same lake—may I not accept this as a message from above that meets all the needs, fears, and longings of my heart? Must I set up the poor measure of my own intellect against the word of God, merely because that word brings me, as it always does, the most divine consolation in the midst of the most divine warnings?

I am not anxious to answer the argument that such a hope dangerously encourages wrongdoers. The same charge has been made in every age against the teaching that man is justified by faith and not by his own deeds or merits—and rightly made, if the faith is not in a living God who frees the conscience from the guilt, burden, and power of sin, but only in a God who merely cancels the just and merciful punishment of sin. God can take care of His own cause; we take very poor care of it. Left to ourselves, we would always cheat men out of a Gospel, for fear it should make them careless about the Law. And we get the fitting reward of our dishonesty. Men are not able to keep the Law. Its terror has no power to make them obey it—only to make them hate it. They begin to keep the commandment when they trust the One who gave it, when they acknowledge that He is working in them to will and to do according to His good pleasure. Weaken that trust in any way; lead men to think that God is not the same yesterday, today, and forever; make them believe a time will come when He will change as we change—and just so far you are doing the devil's work. Your clever, worldly calculations do not make us better, only more indifferent, more desperate.

There is another assertion of far greater force, if it is taken rightly. We are often told that we must apply the same measures to the blessedness of the righteous that we apply to the misery of the wicked. Yes—and not only to their degrees, but to their kind and quality. If we imagine the misery to be merely material, we will imagine the blessedness to be merely material. If we imagine the misery to be spiritual, we will imagine the blessedness to be spiritual. If we do not tell ourselves exactly what we mean in the one case, we will fall into the same vagueness about the other. If we take all material misery to be an instrument in God's hand for delivering men out of the greater and more terrible spiritual misery, then we will take all material blessedness to be the result of spiritual blessedness. And so we will be able to enter into this twenty-first chapter about the new heaven and the new earth. For we will understand the old heaven to be what we picture while our vantage point is on earth. And we will understand the new heaven and the new earth to be both of them seen as they truly are in the light of God—the heavenly or spiritual as the substance, the earthly as the form or body that has been redeemed into its proper subjection, now able to display the glory it once hid. Think how the earth appeared to a heathen of the ancient world. His own land was the one dear, cherished spot in the midst of it, and everything else was viewed in relation to it. The earth was made up of many different countries, each with a different soil, customs, laws, language, and worship. Then think of the heaven! There dwelt the objects of those worships, the beings to whom the soil owed its richness or barrenness, who were the source of the varieties of customs, laws, and languages. This is the natural way of judging about Heaven and Earth. Do not call it heathenish—it is yours and mine. We start from what is nearest to us, and judge and measure what lies beyond by that standard. Yet we are driven to admit something we cannot see, which nevertheless has power over us and touches us in a thousand ways. What it is we can only guess. The things we do see, especially those objects over our heads that influence all we do in such marvelous ways, shape our guesses. Heaven is felt to come before earth; yet earth appears to come before heaven. How can we descend from the unknown to the known, merely because the unknown seems to be the original? Must we not instead ascend from the known to the unknown?

The Jew was taught that he could not judge the infinite by the finite, or the original by the image. The unseen God spoke to Abraham; the *I AM* unveiled Himself to Moses. The care of the land, and the laws, customs, and sacrifices of the chosen people, were grounded on that revelation. In everything, heaven claimed precedence over earth—the government of earth. But could man be satisfied with the discovery merely of a Name, even of a Government? If he was the image of the One who bore that Name and exercised that Government, must he not behold his Original, must he not meet with Him? The revelation that the Apostles declared to be the necessary sequel and fulfillment of the revelation given to Abraham's family—the one the Jewish rulers and priests rejected—claimed to be exactly this. It claimed to meet the demands of the Gentiles, whose thoughts had risen from earth toward heaven in search of their Ruler, no less than the demands of the Jew, who had confessed that the Ruler of heaven was making Himself known to earth. If that were so—if it were true that the Son of Man, who came down from heaven, ascended up to heaven, and even while on earth was in heaven—then there truly was a new Heaven and a new Earth; and the Spirit of God might enable the purified eye of man's spirit to behold them.

Such a vision was granted to the last Apostle, for his own sake and for ours. It was the result and reward of all those visions of conflict that had come before. That divided heaven of the ancient world had passed away. The narrow heaven of the Jew, still mingled with earth and yet separate from earth, had passed away. This was the Heaven of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit—of God dwelling in His own perfect light, revealing Himself to all in the Divine Word, giving life by His own Spirit. Perfect distinctness; perfect unity. Each Apostle was appointed to bring out some aspect of the mystery. St. Paul, when he was carried to the third heaven, could only wonder and adore, not put into words, what he had seen. But it came out to sustain him in his own weakness; he could speak it in broken words when he cried, "Of him, and to him, and through him are all things, to whom be glory for ever"; when he preached at Athens about the God whom the people "were feeling after, if perhaps they might feel after him and find him, though he was not far from any one of them, since in him they lived, and moved, and had their being"; and more clearly still when he "bowed his knees to the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, of whom the whole family of heaven and earth is named," asking that God would grant the Ephesians to be strengthened by His Spirit in the inner man, that Christ might dwell in their hearts by faith, that they might be rooted and grounded in love, and might know with all the saints the height, length, and breadth, and know the love of Christ that surpasses knowledge—and when, having made this wonderful request for them, he entrusted them to Him who could do "exceedingly abundantly for them above all that they could ask or think." You see how, in this act of adoration, heaven and earth inevitably meet; how impossible it was for St. Paul to enter into the glory of the higher without seeing a new glory resting on the lower. And it was an earth in which there was *no more sea*—no more necessary separation of families, tribes, and tongues; yet no confused mingling of them into a huge Babel despotism. Pentecost had established the fellowship that Babel had counterfeited. All were one in the true Head of the body. One Spirit broke down the barriers of language so far as they destroyed the intercourse of spiritual beings, and upheld the variety of tongues so far as they witnessed to distinct gifts and callings, to distinct grasps of the one Truth, to the different cultivation God had assigned to different parts of His vineyard. And so now, at last:

I. "I John saw the holy city, new Jerusalem, coming down from God out of heaven, prepared as a bride adorned for her husband. And I heard a great voice out of heaven saying, Behold, the tabernacle of God is with men, and he will dwell with them, and they shall be his people, and God himself shall be with them, and shall be their God." John had seen the old Jerusalem—the dear city of his fathers, the witness for the living God to men—vanish in flame and smoke. Behind it and within it was this other Jerusalem; the old walls and battlements had fallen down so that it might be revealed. It is not a material city standing on earth, yet it is joined to the earth as the old city never was—because it descends from God out of heaven; because it is truly the city of God; because it represents humanity in its true, redeemed, bridal condition, united to its everlasting Husband, shining with His brightness. The name Emmanuel, spoken over the cradle of the Child who was King of the Jews, is fulfilled. God is indeed with men. The New Covenant is a reality and not a dream. God has truly said to man in His Son, "I will be to you a Father, and you shall be to me sons and daughters. And your sins and iniquities I will remember no more."

II. "And God shall wipe away all tears from their eyes; and there shall be no more death, neither sorrow, nor crying, neither shall there be any more pain: for the former things are passed away. And he that sat upon the throne said, Behold, I make all things new. And he said unto me, Write: for these words are faithful and true. I am Alpha and Omega, the beginning and the end. I will give unto him that is athirst of the water of life freely. He that overcometh shall inherit all things; and I will be his God, and he shall be my son."

We are all too ready to split the different parts of this passage from one another. The thought of being freed from pain, sorrow, and death is very delightful. Even people who have never felt intense pain, never seen death very close to themselves, think that if only they could be rid of these things they would have the blessedness they are seeking. But those who have endured intense pain, who have felt a little of the horror of death, want something more. Why has the pain been sent to them? Why has death been allowed? They feel that pain is unbearable if it is not a discipline; if it is not meant to make them aware of their own spiritual existence, to make them long for treasures no eye has seen and no ear heard. For them the words about pain, sorrow, and death draw their force from the assurance that God will wipe away every tear from their eyes. They will see an everlasting Friend and Deliverer. He will comfort them. This is the great change they long for: freedom from pain because it has done its work and led them to the source and center of joy; freedom from death because they are brought into fellowship with the One in whom is eternal life. In His presence all things become new, because by His presence all things are restored to their true state—all things seen not marred, distorted, and divided, but healthy, perfect, and harmonious in their Divine Head. "I am Alpha and Omega" is the first note of the Revelation; "I am Alpha and Omega" must be its closing note. The loss of the Son as the ground of all things, and the loss of the Son as the goal and consummation of all things, has brought the earth into darkness, confusion, and death. Those who see Him to be the head and end of creation see it as God sees it—see it without sorrow, pain, or death. They have given up their independence; that giving up is death. They receive continually fresh streams from God; there is the fountain of life. They have overcome their desire to be anything in themselves, anything apart from God; they are content to receive everything from God and to find everything in God, and so they inherit all things. But they can never rise to a higher blessedness than the one granted to them when the Gospel was first preached to them, the one assured to them in their baptism: "I will be their God, and they shall be my sons." This state of being adopted as sons by the One who sits on the throne is the highest state human beings can reach. Christ died, rose, and ascended so that it might be ours; when we fully enter into it, we receive the highest bliss of heaven. But this is also the highest bliss of earth. Earth is taken out of the hands of its murderer. It becomes what its Creator meant it to be.

III. "But the fearful, and unbelieving, and the abominable, and whoremongers, and sorcerers, and idolaters, and all liars, shall have their part in the lake which burneth with fire and brimstone: which is the second death."

All blessedness—oh, when shall we understand this?—consists in acknowledging what truly is; all misery and damnation consists in denying it. That men disbelieved the good and gracious God to be their Father, that they count Him not their Father but their enemy—this is what makes them cowards; this is what makes them dealers with impure and evil spirits; this is what makes them corrupters and destroyers of one another's purity; this is what makes them trifle with each other's life; this is what makes them worshippers of visible things; this is the lie of which all other lies are the offspring. Those spirits which reject truth, which feed on a lie and live in a lie, are in the lake of fire. There is no way to describe their condition but that. A fire is burning in them that nothing can quench. As I said before, if we look at this fire only as reason looks at it, without Revelation, we could find in it only despair. When God enters, despair ceases. He is called in Scripture the God of Hope. Whatever we conceive of as being His must give us hope.

IV. For consider. "There came unto me one of the seven angels which had the seven vials full of the seven last plagues, and talked with me, saying"—What new doom is this angel about to speak of? His task has been to pour out plagues. What is to be their consummation?—"Come hither, I will shew thee the bride, the Lamb's wife." Here is the consummation of the plagues. This is what they have all been leading toward. By them the bride has been disciplined and prepared for her Lord; by them her seducers have been tormented and cast down.

V. What this bride is we have already heard. "And he carried me away to a great and high mountain, and shewed me that great city, the new Jerusalem, descending out of heaven from God, having the glory of God."

Brothers, I believe that we read these words and the ones that follow them in our childhood; that they then made a deep impression on us; that the impression seemed to fade; that we came to regard them as Oriental poetry with nothing to do with the business of life; that, holding that opinion, we dismissed them irritably, as though they were setting before us a phantom world that could never stir our longings or affect our lives; but that, nevertheless, without our knowing it, these early impressions do come back to us in the midst of crowded cities and the hum of earthly voices. When for a moment we contrast them with what we see around us, we begin to suspect that what surrounds us is the phantom world, and that these words may testify of the real one; that what we see is only of today, while this is the permanent and unchangeable; that what we see addresses our eyes and ears, while this reveals the proper home of those to whom the eyes and ears belong—the home we were created to live in, the home our hearts are striving toward, and away from which we must be weary and restless. Oh, that God would give us grace to think in this way of that city the Apostle saw descending out of heaven from God! And I believe He will. I believe that the more we read the history of the world—and see what political orders men have tried to build for themselves, and what promise of good, of real good, there was in them, and what disappointment followed; how each one recognized some Divine power at work in its origin and preservation, and asked that power to keep its members true and the whole society united, and yet what falsehoods were spread in the attempt to make these invisible influences bear on men's visible doings; how old societies fell through weakness, and those that rose in their place were forced to beg help from the traditions they had thrown away; how great experiments for freedom ended in cruel tyrannies; how men have spoken of great ideals and clung to them with earnest faith, and then sighed because the ideal was not real; how the nations of the earth and the Church of God are alike split into factions, each envying and hating the other; how yet the attempts at unity seem worse than the sectarian hostilities they meant to end—the more, I say, we dwell on such sights, the more this vision of an eternal city, founded on the union of God with man, will dawn on us through the mists of the past, through the confusions of the present, through our faint flickering hopes for the future; and the more sure we shall be that God has revealed to us what has been, is, and must be—that which reconciles all ideals, which binds the ideal to the actual, which the world, the flesh, and the devil are always trying to shut out from us, and which they have never been able to shut out; because He who is for us, whose name we are sealed with—the Father, the Son, the Holy Ghost, the God blessed forever—is stronger than those who are against us.

Yes, dear friends, our baptism was truly the witness to us that we are, every one of us, citizens of this new Jerusalem. And we may continually ask the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ to give us the Spirit of wisdom and revelation, so that we may know the hope of our calling and the greatness of our inheritance. This light will come to us through all the turmoils and trials that make us feel our need of it. In hours of unspeakable darkness we shall remember that there is a "light like unto a jasper stone, clear as crystal"; that this light dwells in God Himself and is able to fill the hearts and spirits of men. When we brood on the weakness and insecurity of every material barrier and defense, the thought of "that wall, great and high," surrounding the Divine City will scatter our cowardly fears and assure us that it can be in no danger. When, in the poverty and helplessness of those who seem to be Christ's champions on earth, we cry out for invisible friends and helpers, the "twelve gates with the twelve angels" will rise before us as no imaginary thing but a most real revelation. When we feel it is very grievous to be cut off from the past, to have left its living treasures far behind, we are reminded that "on these gates are names written, which are the names of the twelve tribes of Israel." When we ask whether then we are to be brought back once more to the narrowness of the old Jewish order, which met men only on one side of their lives, which was national and not universal, we are told that in the new Jerusalem "there are on the east three gates, on the north three gates, on the south three gates, on the west three gates." When we imagine that the Church of former days, the Church we read of in the New Testament, is hopelessly divided from the Church of our own day, this cheering message comes to us: "And the wall of the city had twelve foundations, and in them the names of the twelve Apostles of the Lamb."

Everywhere we find theologians going about with measuring rods to determine how far the city of God reaches. Each school and sect has its own rod, and each makes the boundaries narrower. It is some comfort to know that this task is unnecessary; that it was undertaken long ago, by competent hands; and that this is what came of it.

VI. "And he that talked with me had a golden reed to measure the city, and the gates thereof, and the wall thereof. And the city lieth foursquare, and the length is as large as the breadth: and he measured the city with the reed, twelve thousand furlongs. The length and the breadth and the height of it are equal. And he measured the wall thereof, an hundred and forty and four cubits, according to the measure of a man, that is, of the angel." That measure of a man, which is drawn not from his fallen nature but from his angelic nature, is God's measure. The city that lies foursquare is His city. He knows its measure. Perhaps we shall be wiser and safer if we do not make much use of our own measuring rods.

But it is when we think of the materials our human fellowship is made of that we are likely to feel the most despair. We see the corrupt doings of other men; we find a deep root of corruption in ourselves. In stones dug out of the quarries of earth, what can we expect but continual crumbling and decay? Nothing else—if they are only dug out of earth's quarries. But men are not children of the earth; they are children of the light, children of God. When they know this, when they turn to the light, when they receive His brightness and reflect it, then this is the description of them and of the society they form.

VII. "And the building of the wall of it was of jasper: and the city was pure gold, like unto clear glass. And the foundations of the wall of the city were garnished with all manner of precious stones. The first foundation was jasper; the second, sapphire; the third, a chalcedony; the fourth, an emerald; the fifth, sardonyx; the sixth, sardius; the seventh, chrysolite; the eighth, a beryl; the ninth, a topaz; the tenth, a chrysoprasus; the eleventh, a jacinth; the twelfth, an amethyst. And the twelve gates were of twelve pearls; every several gate was of one pearl, and the street of the city was of pure gold, as it were transparent glass."

We sometimes hear of the wealth and extravagance of Nature—her countless forms, her beauties of color and light, which seem to have no eye to look at them. That wealth and extravagance can cause us no grief. It can only encourage us to reflect on the infinite love that has produced this variety of life. But have you never groaned over the wealth and extravagance of human gifts, which seem truly to be thrown away? Religious men have seen the waste and tried to reduce everything to a safe dullness and uniformity. All beautiful talents get abused and perish; why not hide them in the earth? Is that not the way to keep them safe for the hard Master? Oh, think of God's City! Each human character, each human talent, is a distinct pearl, out of which He would build it, with which He would adorn it. Nothing He has given can be lost: all is for use, and grace, and delight. All is meant to reflect a portion of His glory. Only remember that we are nothing in ourselves, that we are created in His image—and then you will understand how each precious stone shall contribute to the glory of the whole; how the purest human gold shall be like transparent glass, through which the Divine beauty shows itself.

VIII. But what has taken the place of that temple in the old Jerusalem, whose destruction was the sign that the age had ended? This has been the question of Christendom in every century since its fall. Can we not have some temple in some earthly city that shall be like that one, or that is essentially the same? Can we not have some earthly city that shall be like the Divine City—the capital of the new world as that was of the old?

This was the question of the early ages and of the middle ages; it is the question that must be settled in ours. The miseries of division are obvious; it is plain to most thoughtful men that none of the little sect-cities men have built is the city that lies foursquare, whose length is as large as its breadth—and that all of them combined into one could never make such a city. So they ask, "Has there not been a Christian order on this earth that had a center? Shall we not attach ourselves to that?" And I know no answer but this: "Oh yes; in God's name let us attach ourselves to the order that has a center. Or rather, let us believe that we are attached to it already. For this is the Divine description of it: 'And I saw no temple there, for the Lord God Almighty and the Lamb are the temple of it.'" These are the centers of the society; from them its worship and sacrifices begin, and in them they end. Whenever we inwardly grasp that this is so—when we confess that it has always been so, only that men have denied it, and have put their own notions and fancies between themselves and heaven, and have tried to separate heaven and earth which God has joined into one—then the remaining words of the chapter will also be received in their fullness: "And the city had no need of the sun, neither of the moon, to shine in it, for the Lord God did lighten it, and the Lamb is the light thereof." Just as truly as the sun and moon give light to the visible earth, so truly is the Lord God and the Lamb the light of Man, the appointed lord of that earth. The Church has existed, and does exist, to bear witness to that truth—to declare that God is light, that in Him there is no darkness at all; to declare that Christ is the Light of the world, that "His life is the light of men." Because she has borne that witness so imperfectly, because she has denied that God is light, and has blocked His light, and has made herself the sun instead of Him, the nations of the earth within reach of her influence have been cold and dark when they might have been living in the full blaze of truth and love. But the words are still true, and shall one day prove themselves true: "And the nations of the earth which are saved shall walk in the light of it, and the kings of the earth shall bring their glory and honour into it." And this because "the gates of it shall not be shut at all by day; for there shall be no night there." And this because "there shall in no wise enter into it anything that defileth, neither whatsoever worketh abomination, or maketh a lie: but they which are written in the Lamb's book of life."

## LECTURE XXII. THE BLESSING AND THE CURSE.

*The passage:* John is shown a clear river of the water of life flowing from the throne of God and of the Lamb. Along it grows the tree of life, bearing twelve kinds of fruit each month, its leaves healing the nations. There will be no more curse; God's throne is there, his servants serve him, they see his face, and his name is on their foreheads. There is no night, no need of lamp or sun, for God gives light, and they reign forever. The angel tells John these sayings are faithful and true, that God sent his angel to show his servants what must soon happen, and that Christ is coming quickly—blessed is the one who keeps these sayings. When John falls to worship the angel, the angel forbids it, calling himself a fellow-servant, and tells him to worship God, and not to seal up the prophecy, for the time is near. Let the unjust and filthy stay so, the righteous and holy stay so; Christ comes quickly with his reward, to repay each according to his work. He is Alpha and Omega, the first and the last. Blessed are those who keep his commandments, who may have right to the tree of life and enter the city; outside are dogs, sorcerers, and the rest. Jesus has sent his angel to testify to the churches; he is the root and offspring of David and the bright morning star. The Spirit and the bride say, Come; whoever is thirsty may take the water of life freely. A warning follows against adding to or taking from the book. Christ says he comes quickly; John answers, "Even so, come, Lord Jesus." Grace be with you all.

The last chapter of Revelation has often been compared to the second chapter of Genesis. There is reason for the comparison, but, as I tried to show you in an earlier sermon, the reason is not that the Bible's final revelation shows a return to the state it described at the beginning. The man formed from the dust, who became a living soul, ate from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil. Everyone in his race has been eating from it ever since, and the evil has produced its natural results—strife, degradation, death. Meanwhile God, the true God, has been offering himself to the knowledge of his creatures; and those creatures, so far as they have known him, have been able to rise above the evil and to see that they were made for fellowship, for continual improvement, for eternal life. When people follow the tendencies of their separate, individual, Adam-nature, they fully experience the curse: they sink into themselves and are solitary even in the midst of society. Claiming their rights as those made in God's image, they have found a second Adam, who is not a living soul but a life-giving spirit. They have left the garden and all its delights as a state fit for infancy, not for maturity. They have seen that labour is better than mere enjoyment, that conquering the thorn and thistle is better than eating everything good and pleasant. They have learned that the way to the tree of life is through death; that when death takes the shape of the Cross, the flaming sword can keep no sinful mortal from approaching it. They see the river that watered the garden turned into a river of the water of life, flowing from the throne of God and of the Lamb.

So it is all progress. There is no falling back to that first condition which, in our weakness and godlessness, we dream about and long for. From the beginning God has been educating his creatures through material things, through signs and sacraments, through the experience of toil and sorrow, through the anguish of sin, through the horror of death, so that they might understand they were born for an inheritance that is incorruptible and never fades—for a kingdom of righteousness, peace, and truth. The son of Zebedee was one of the chosen instruments in carrying on this education. He was taught how all things were created by the Word, for in him was life; how he came to baptize with the Holy Ghost; how those who would see his kingdom must be born of water and of the Spirit; how he would be lifted up as Moses lifted up the serpent in the wilderness, so that those who believed in him should not perish but have everlasting life; how he would give living water, so that those who tasted it would not thirst again; how it would become in them a well of water springing up into everlasting life; how those who thirsted might come to him and drink; and how, when he said this, he was speaking of the Spirit that believers would receive after he was glorified.

I. Those who ponder these passages in St. John's Gospel, and connect them with all that his Epistles say about eternal life, will be ready for the opening words of this chapter. What was shown to John earlier was not a garden but a city, the city of God—that society of men and angels, that union of heaven and earth, presented to us as the new Jerusalem. And now, when we return to the symbols of Eden, it is to remind us that as mere symbols they have passed away, and that we have arrived at the realities they stood for. "He shewed me a pure river of water of life, clear as crystal, proceeding out of the throne of God and of the Lamb." We do not need to find the equivalents for this language; St. John has translated it himself. We know that the Spirit, who proceeds from the Father and the Son, is this River of Life. We know that it does not nourish trees, seeds, and flowers, but the hearts, wills, and reasons of human beings, the mystical body of Christ. And in case there is any doubt about this, we are at once brought back to the city: "In the midst of the street of it, and on either side of the river, was there the tree of life, which bare twelve manner of fruits, and yielded her fruit every month: and the leaves of the tree were for the healing of the nations."

The river of life is what quickens, strengthens, and unites a society of men made to share God's likeness. The tree of life is the one they behold, in whom they see the perfection of their own condition, from whom all living power comes down upon them. Throughout his Epistle and Gospel St. John has been explaining how the Son of God is the source of the various graces that all have received, grace upon grace; how he distributes his twelve kinds of fruit in the proportions and at the times suited to each society and each person; how he shades with his leaves those who do not yet know the taste of the fruit; and how he makes all the outward ministries of his Gospel and Church serve to cure the miseries that selfishness has brought on the nations.

II. "And there shall be no more curse: but the throne of God and of the Lamb shall be in it; and his servants shall serve him; and they shall see his face; and his name shall be in their foreheads."

Men are cursed not because they eat bread by the sweat of their brow, but because they live under a government other than that of God and the Lamb—because they have handed themselves over to tyrants who separate them from the true Master. Where the throne of God and the Lamb is, there is no curse. When people understand that this is the kingdom in which they are living, and are allowed to know what he is—to see him, to become like him—there is no curse. Christ has taken it away. Christ has bound the children forever to their Father in Heaven. So again I say, brothers, we are not reading about some city to be set up on this planet at some distant day; nor about a city on another planet. We are reading of the city whose Ruler is Christ, and therefore of one whose citizens are all who, anywhere, confess him. Our grasp of its nature may be very poor; we must pray God by his Spirit of revelation to deepen and enlarge it. We ourselves, or others, may not admit that any such city exists at all; we must ask that the same Spirit will remove that unbelief. But the truth of things is not changed by our opinion of them. The Apostle was shown what is, not what is not. The muddy garment of decay, which keeps us from knowing our surroundings and ourselves, was stripped from him; and what was uncovered was not some private mansion for him, but that Father's house with many mansions that has been opened to us all, and in which we are all invited to dwell.

III. "And there shall be no night there; and they shall need no candle, neither light of the sun; for the Lord God giveth them light: and they shall reign for ever and ever."

I have pointed you to a few of the familiar sentences in which the Evangelist St. John speaks of Christ as the Life and the Giver of Life. I hardly need remind you of the equally numerous ones in which he speaks of him as the Light of Man, the Light of the World, the Light that shines in darkness which the darkness has not understood. All his other teaching is bound up with this. The condemnation of sin is that light has come into the world and men love darkness rather than light, because their deeds are evil. The one who witnessed to the Lamb that takes away the world's sins is said to have witnessed to the Light, so that all men through him might believe. Our Lord heals the blind man because he is the Light of the World. His charge against the Pharisees is that they will not admit they are blind so as to receive his light. How well the text agrees with this language! Where the Son of God and the Lamb is, there is no night. Those who are actually confessing that throne and kneeling before it are not in darkness but in full noonday. Whenever a human being can scatter some cloud from a neighbour's sky, or show him his path across the earth more clearly; whenever the moral world is cleared of any of the mists that make it dismal; whenever the physical world yields any of its secrets to the humble and faithful seeker—there is a pledge that the visions of this book are not deceptions. And the more thoroughly we are convinced they are not deceptions—the more we act as if God's throne were set up among us, and as if we might turn to him sure that he does not want us to live in darkness but to see the light of life—the more we ourselves will move beyond the circle of night, and draw fallen men out of it; the more we will persuade them that they do not need the candle or the sun, though they may be grateful for both; that whatever brightness comes from either, he sends it, and we may receive it from him through whatever channel it reaches us. Why does he add, "they shall reign for ever and ever"? Is it not because there is an essential, inseparable link between light and rule? The sun and stars were the rulers in the eyes of the old Eastern sages; they are so for the English peasant. Oh, that sages and peasants might learn how much grander and nobler they are than the most glorious objects they can look at! Oh, that they might believe that the Light which made those outer orbs shine can shine inwardly in their hearts and reason—and that when it does shine, and they are willing that it should, they possess an imperishable, ever-growing royalty! The kingdom of darkness must pass away; the kingdom of light is the one Christ established when he rose from the grave.

IV. "And he said unto me, These sayings are faithful and true; and the Lord God hath sent his angel, to shew unto his servants the things which must shortly be done. Behold, I come quickly: blessed is he that keepeth the sayings of the prophecy of this book."

We have met this testimony often before: these "sayings are faithful and true." Why should such repetitions be needed? We can all know why. You and I are constantly tempted to think these are not faithful and true sayings. We suspect they belong to a region that is at least half fanciful; we take them not as explanations of the world's parables but as themselves parables. And we adopt this view all the more readily because, among these solemn statements, there are others we suppose cannot be taken literally: "These things must shortly come to pass. Behold, I come quickly." My chief aim in these sermons has been to show that these sayings too are faithful and true; that if we read them together with the rest, their apparent contradiction disappears. The old Jerusalem did not pass away until the new was revealed. From the beginning of the world all things had been moving toward the revelation of him in whom heaven and earth are joined. That is the revelation granted to the Apostle John—to be, as he says it was, a blessing to the men of his own day, and no less a blessing to men of every later day. Those who kept it would understand what was meant by the earlier movements of the world, and what was meant by the crises and agonies they were passing through. More than that, they would accept the divine kingdom God had set up among men; they would walk in its light; they would not invent some wretched counterfeit of it, nor tear it to pieces. If they "did not keep the saying of the prophecy of this book"—if they treated these sayings as idle tales, or pushed them off into an imaginary future—they would be at the mercy of impostors who would try to do for the world what God had not done; of dividers who would say his Church is only a human creation and so may be pulled down by humans; of unbelievers who would claim he takes no care of his own earth; and of tyrants who would say that what he has neglected they may trample at will.

V. "And I John saw these things, and heard them." The language of the senses is the only real language—the only language—that presents spiritual things to us as they truly are, not altered and disturbed by our reasoning, not as though our perceptions of them were the ground of their existence. So it has been throughout Scripture, and the lesson is pressed home in this closing passage. And there is another lesson we need just as much. The Bible has been throughout a witness against idolatry—and, at the same time, a witness of every man's proneness to idolatry. We speak of Semitic races that had some special leaning toward the worship of the invisible; yet the teachers of that branch of the Semitic race which carried the weightiest and most enduring protest against worshipping the visible declare that their own people were ready to fall into it at every moment. The old Jewish Apostle, seeing and hearing the most divine truths, entering the very heart of the spiritual world, tells us twice that this temptation had not been overcome in him: "And when I had heard and seen, I fell down to worship at the feet of the angel which shewed me these things. Then saith he unto me, See thou do it not: for I am thy fellow-servant, and of thy brethren the prophets, and of them which keep the sayings of this book: worship God."

Triumphant and secure Protestant—you who scorn idolatry, and trample on those who fall into it—has it ever occurred to you that you might not be wholly out of reach of a danger that pursued the beloved disciple to the very end? Has it ever struck you that he might say to you: judge not, that you be not judged; be not high-minded, but fear.

Here, as I noted in an earlier chapter, there is no obvious change of persons. It is not by formal phrases and words that we are taught to tell the angel apart from the one who sent the angel. God himself enabled the prophet to distinguish them; it is he who enables us to distinguish them.

VI. "And he saith unto me, Seal not the sayings of the prophecy of this book: for the time is at hand. He that is unjust, let him be unjust still: and he that is filthy, let him be filthy still: and he that is righteous, let him be righteous still: and he that is holy, let him be holy still. And, behold, I come quickly; and my reward is with me, to give every man according as his work shall be."

The Apostle was told not to store up his prophecy as if its contents belonged to another age rather than his own. "The time was at hand." I do not want to press this statement further than he presses it; but when he is taught that he must draw our attention to it, we must listen. The proof of its fulfilment would not depend on those who understood and received it. The Judge's voice would make itself heard; the unjust and the filthy, the righteous and the holy, would hear it. How would they show they had heard it? The unjust will remain unjust. The sentence would go out: "You have chosen your path, stay in it. You love wickedness—feel and know what wickedness is. You love what is right, pure, and good—feel and know what the right, the pure, and the good is."

When the light of the world shines out fully, each person will be found to have the very thing he has worked for. The wages of sin, and the gift of God—each will be received in full. We keep imagining that this law will somehow be reversed, that somehow we will not reap what we have sown. This delusion clings to us all; we try to convince ourselves that God's grant of pardon licenses it. But God does more than pardon in our poor sense of the word. He gives repentance. All our attempts to measure and limit that grace of his are weak and impious. Yet it is the grace of turning us from darkness to light—not the cruel mercy of letting us go on in darkness, nor the impossible mercy of giving us the blessing of a light we hate. Good and evil were set before man at the first; they are set before him to the last. From the beginning the divine will has been acting on man's will, that he might choose the good and reject the evil. We pray—Christ prays—that this will may prevail entirely. But as long as there is resistance to it, so long there is misery. There has been no change in the divine order, no turning of anything into its opposite. We are reading of a revelation, not of a scheme. God is bringing all contrasts into the open, just as he is bringing everything he has made into harmony.

VII. For the old refrain of the prophecy comes back once more: "I am Alpha and Omega, the beginning and the end, the first and the last." The Revelation to St. John is the revelation of a Person who has been, and is, and is to be—of him who is the living centre of God's universe, in whom past, present, and future meet. Does such a revelation carry us off into some far-off region away from our ordinary tasks and duties? Listen to the next words.

VIII. "Blessed are they that do his commandments, that they may have right to the tree of life, and may enter in through the gates into the city. For without are dogs, and sorcerers, and whoremongers, and murderers, and idolaters, and whosoever loveth and maketh a lie."

Those who remember God's commandments in order to do them; those who live day by day as if under the government of a gracious Father, and as if he were working in them to will and to do according to his good pleasure; those who shun their own evil and turn to him as the source of all good—these gain power over the tree of life. The Apostle's own words are stronger than the more grand-sounding phrase our translators put in their place. In one sense all have a right to the tree of life, since Christ has been set forth as the Life of the world, and has proclaimed himself, in death, the deliverer from death. What we need is not to claim a right but to acquire the capacity to taste and enjoy the tree's fruit. We need the power to give up craving what is our own, the power to stop eating the fruit that grows on our own poor stock. This power is gained in the daily discipline of sorrow, in the effort to do what has to be done, in the struggle not to do the things our evil nature prompts. For there is a constant urging toward what it wants us to do. Around the best man who is striving to live as a citizen of God's city live those who refuse to be its citizens, who would rather be animals, who prefer lying to truth. And the man who keeps God's commands cannot say, like the Pharisee, "I thank thee that I am not one of these." He is continually taught that he has in himself everything that has degraded them into dogs or sorcerers. Only by turning to the tree of life, taking in the food of its fruit, and enjoying the shade of its leaves, can he overcome these temptations, or hope for the day when the city of God will utterly defeat whatever in him or in the universe despises its invitation and lives as a stranger beside it. Thus, in the very middle of the description of the heavenly inheritance—of the highest blessings we can receive or God can give—we are still reminded of what is dark, foul, and terrible. If the highest state were one of mere innocence, with no knowledge of good and evil, this could not be. But if the countless company of angels, and the spirits of just men made perfect, can find no more glorious object of contemplation than the Lamb that was slain; if the Cross is the true tree of life; then the blessedness the New Testament reveals is not one that can ever be separated from the memory of a triumph won over selfishness, and from the desire and hope to share in the battle against whatever remains of it in God's universe.

IX. "I Jesus have sent my angel to testify unto you these things in the churches. I am the root and the offspring of David, and the bright and morning star."

In this complete unveiling, nothing is lost from all the earlier revelation. Jesus the Saviour, born of the Virgin, the root and offspring of David, recalling all that belongs to Jewish life and the old dispensation; the bright morning star, whom the Magi sought in their faithful ignorance—the Light of the whole earth—all these names needed to be repeated, so that the Churches of Asia might receive the testimony sent to them about their King and Brother; that they might send it out into the world; and that we might receive it not as an heirloom handed down from them, but fresh from the lips of the one who is with us as he was with them. Only because they believed he was truly among them could they deliver his message to mankind; only while we believe he is with us can we deliver it. It is a grand, all-embracing message, meeting all the needs of men, both social and individual, reaching beneath all that is evil. "And the Spirit and the bride say, Come. And let him that heareth say, Come. And let him that is athirst say, Come. And whosoever will, let him come and take of the water of life freely." If we could, in even a small measure, believe these words—if we truly owned that the Spirit of the Eternal God was himself speaking them to the thirsty and broken spirits of men in every part of the earth—if we heard them echoed by the Church of all ages—then we might be allowed, each in his own small sphere, and each by the strength and encouragement he can give his brothers in theirs, to take part in the work of breaking the chains that bind our race. And we shall be taught, brothers, in wonderful ways, that this power is given to the weakest, if only they will credit it to the Spirit and the Bride and not to their own might or wisdom. Very feeble words will enter into human hearts and seem to die, and when they have died will bear fruit. For the words are not ours but his, in whom is life, and whose life is the light of men. And this makes the sentences that follow very awful indeed to those who have dared to open their lips in delivering and interpreting the lessons of the Apocalypse or of the Bible.

X.
